# Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ (Chapter 28) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Name: "The Story," and Why That Word Matters**

Al-Qaṣaṣ, "The Story" or "The Narration," takes its name from a single word that appears at the hinge of the sūrah, in verse 25, where Moses, arriving as a fugitive in Midian, "recounted his story" (*qaṣṣa ʿalayhi al-qaṣaṣ*) to the old man who would become his father-in-law. The Arabic root *q-ṣ-ṣ* carries two linked senses that together explain the choice. The first is *to cut or clip* — a *qiṣṣah* is a piece cut out of the continuous fabric of events. The second is *to follow tracks step by step*, as hunters follow the spoor of an animal; the Qur'an itself uses the verb in this physical sense when the two companions of Moses "retraced their footsteps" (*qaṣaṣā āthārahumā*, 18:64). Both meanings are theological. A story is a *selection*, cut out of infinite happening by a choosing wisdom; and reading it is a *tracking*, a following of footsteps laid down by God in history. The story is therefore not told for entertainment. It is told so that the reader may walk where Moses walked and learn what Moses learned.

This is why the sūrah that carries the name opens with the phrase *We recite unto thee in truth* (v. 3), and why, at the other end of the sūrah, believers are praised for turning away from *idle talk* (v. 55). The Qur'an consistently sets the true narrative against the false one: the story that leads somewhere against the chatter that leads nowhere. Al-Qaṣaṣ is named for the former.

**Where and When It Was Revealed**

The overwhelming majority of commentators place al-Qaṣaṣ in the Makkan period, revealed after Sūrah 27, al-Naml, which it closely resembles in both its opening letters and its subject matter. A minority view, recorded by al-Qurṭubī and others, holds that verses 52–55 are Madinan, revealed concerning the People of the Book who embraced Islam in Madinah; and a well-known report holds that verse 85 was revealed at Juḥfah, on the road between Makkah and Madinah, during the Prophet's emigration — a detail to be examined in full at that verse. These exceptions themselves tell the reader something: the sūrah's two poles are the Makkan struggle with the Quraysh and the consolation of a Prophet driven from his home, and both are present in the text.

The Makkan setting determines the sūrah's character. Makkan revelation is concerned above all with the foundations: the Oneness of God, the reality of the Hereafter, the truth of prophethood, and the moral shape of the human being. Legislation is minimal here; argument, parable, and narrative are maximal. A reader who opens this sūrah expecting rules will find instead the education of a prophet, the anatomy of tyranny, the psychology of wealth, and the logic of divine justice — the roots from which all later rules grow.

**The Ṭā Sīn Trilogy**

Al-Qaṣaṣ shares its opening letters, *Ṭā. Sīn. Mīm.*, with the two sūrahs immediately preceding it: al-Shuʿarāʾ (26) and al-Naml (27). Three consecutive chapters, three identical openings, and three long treatments of Moses — a design so conspicuous that the classical commentators remarked upon it. The three sūrahs read almost like a single work in three movements. Al-Shuʿarāʾ presents the drama of the prophets and their peoples, with Moses and Pharaoh as its opening and longest act. Al-Naml turns the lens from confrontation to the inner recognition of truth, and closes with Solomon and the Queen of Sheba. Al-Qaṣaṣ then gives what the other two only partly give: the full biography — birth, exile, marriage, call, and mission — the *making* of a messenger before the mission begins.

**Why Moses, and Why So Often**

Moses is mentioned more often in the Qur'an than any other prophet, and his story is told in more places and at greater length than any other narrative. This is not accidental, and the commentators give the reason plainly: the career of Moses most closely mirrors the career of Muhammad ﷺ. Both were sent to a dominant power that claimed absolute authority; both confronted a ruling elite (*malaʾ*) entrenched in ancestral custom; both were rejected by a people they loved; both were forced to leave their homes; both were given a scripture and a law; both led a community through deliverance and wandering; and both returned in triumph to the city that had expelled them. The promise hidden at the center of this sūrah, *Truly the One Who ordained the Quran for thee shall surely bring thee back to the place of return* (v. 85), was spoken to a man walking away from Makkah under threat of death, and it is the axis on which the whole sūrah turns.

The Prophet ﷺ himself lived inside this identification. When he distributed charity and a man accused him of unfairness, he did not answer with anger but with a comparison:

> *"May God have mercy upon Moses: he was harmed with worse than this, and he was patient."* (al-Bukhārī, Book of Good Manners; Muslim)

And on the Night of the Ascension, when he described the prophets he met in Jerusalem and in the heavens, it was Moses whom he described as weeping, and Moses with whom he conversed at length about the burden laid upon his community — the famous narration of the fifty prayers, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, in which Moses repeatedly sends the Prophet back to his Lord saying, *"Ask your Lord to make it lighter, for your community will not be able to bear that."* The sūrah before us is the written form of that same companionship.

**The Great Themes**

Several interlocking themes run through the eighty-eight verses, and recognizing them in advance makes the details cohere.

First, *the hidden governance of God*. Verses 4–6 declare a divine intention — *We desired to be gracious to those who were oppressed* — and verses 7–13 show that intention working itself out through means that look, from the inside, like catastrophe: a mother commanded to throw her baby into a river, an infant drifting into the hands of the tyrant who ordered his death. The sūrah is the classical proof-text for the doctrine that God's planning (*tadbīr*) operates through, and often against, human planning, and that the apparent disasters of a believer's life may be the very mechanism of his deliverance.

Second, *the anatomy of tyranny*. Verse 4 diagnoses Pharaoh's rule in four clauses: self-exaltation, division of the people into factions, oppression of one group, and the corruption that results. The Qur'an's interest is not merely in Pharaoh as an individual villain but in Pharaoh as a *structure* — the recurring pattern by which power maintains itself. The sūrah traces how that structure argues (v. 36, v. 38), how it flatters itself (v. 39), how it seduces onlookers (v. 79), and how it ends (vv. 40–42, 78, 81).

Third, *the formation of a prophet*. No other sūrah shows so plainly that prophethood is prepared through ordinary human experience: a mother's fear, a young man's anger, a crime, a flight into the desert, hunger, unemployment, a marriage contract, ten years of herding sheep. It is only after this long apprenticeship that the fire appears on the mountain (v. 29). The spiritual lesson the tradition has drawn is that God does not simply *give* a mission; He *forms* the man who can carry it, and the formation is painful precisely because the mission is heavy.

Fourth, *the difference between claimed knowledge and real knowledge*. Moses is given *wisdom and knowledge* (v. 14); Korah claims, *I have only been given it on account of knowledge I possess* (v. 78). The sūrah sets the two side by side, and the earth swallows one of them. The distinction is the distinction between knowledge that recognizes its Giver and knowledge that credits itself.

Fifth, *wealth as a test rather than a reward*. The Korah passage (vv. 76–83) is the sūrah's sustained treatment of money: how it was given, how it was misused, how the crowd envied it, how the people of knowledge answered that envy, and how quickly the whole display vanished. The balanced counsel of verse 77 — *seek the Abode of the Hereafter with what God has given you, and forget not your portion of this world* — is among the most quoted economic and spiritual maxims in the Islamic tradition.

Sixth, *the removal of excuses*. Verses 44–47 construct one of the Qur'an's most careful arguments: you, Muhammad, were not present at these events, and you did not read them in a book, and yet you recite them exactly — therefore your knowledge is revelation. The conclusion is stated in verse 47: God sends messengers precisely so that no one can later say, *If only a messenger had come to us, we would have believed*. The sūrah thus closes the last door of self-justification.

Seventh, *the limits of even the most loving guidance*. Verse 56, revealed concerning the Prophet's own uncle Abū Ṭālib, establishes with piercing clarity that guidance belongs to God alone, and that the most sincere love in the world cannot substitute for it.

**The Architecture of the Sūrah**

The sūrah falls into five panels, though the transitions are woven rather than abrupt:

Verses 1–3 form the threshold: the letters, the Book, and the announcement of the subject.

Verses 4–42 are the Moses epic in eight scenes: Pharaoh's oppression and God's counter-plan (4–6); the infant in the river (7–13); maturity and the killing in the city (14–19); the warning and the flight (20–22); the wells of Midian (23–25); the marriage contract (26–28); the fire and the call (29–35); and the confrontation with Pharaoh, ending in the drowning (36–42).

Verses 43–51 turn from the story to its function: the argument that the Prophet's knowledge of these events is itself proof of revelation, together with the catalog of Quraysh objections and the answers to them.

Verses 52–55 are a brief but luminous digression on those among the People of the Book who recognized the truth when it came, and on the three marks by which such people are known: patience, repelling evil with good, and dignity in the face of abuse.

Verses 56–75 return to the Makkan audience with a series of arguments drawn from human experience — the fear of losing status (57), the ruins of destroyed towns (58), the justice of sending a warner before punishment (59), the worthlessness of worldly goods compared with what is with God (60–61), the scene of judgment where false partners are exposed (62–66), the open door of repentance (67), God's exclusive prerogative to choose (68–70), and the alternation of night and day as a standing sign (71–75).

Verses 76–83 present Korah, the counter-Moses, and draw the sūrah's social and economic conclusion: *That is the Abode of the Hereafter, which We ordain for those who desire neither dominance upon the earth, nor corruption. And the end belongs to the reverent.*

Verses 84–88 close the sūrah with the law of requital, the promise of return, the command to steadfastness, and finally one of the most comprehensive creedal statements in the Qur'an: *And call not upon another god along with God. There is no god but He! All things perish, save His Face. Judgment belongs to Him, and unto Him will you be returned.*

**A Note on Method in Reading the Stories**

The classical scholars were explicit about how this sūrah is to be read and how it is not. Narratives concerning the Children of Israel may be related, but the Qur'an is the criterion by which all such reports are weighed. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Convey from me even if it be a single verse, and relate from the Children of Israel, for there is no harm in that. But whoever lies about me deliberately, let him take his seat in the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 3461)

And he warned the other way as well:

> *"Do not ask the People of the Book about anything, for they will not guide you, having themselves gone astray — and you may deny a truth or affirm a falsehood."* (reported by al-Bukhārī and Aḥmad)

Between these two instructions lies the discipline of the commentator: the narrative detail may be filled in from transmitted reports, but it may never be treated as certain, and it may never be allowed to contradict the text or the creed. Al-Rāzī's practice in this sūrah is the model: he records the popular accounts — that Moses lifted a stone that ten men could not move, that Pharaoh's arrow fell back upon him — and then calmly rules that some of these reports are "not for the sane." The present commentary follows the same rule. Where a detail is Qur'anic, it is treated as certain; where it is transmitted report, it is identified as such; where it is legend, it is named as legend. The Qur'an's own declaration stands above all of it: *We recite unto thee in truth*.

What follows is a verse-by-verse expanded commentary. Each of the eighty-eight verses is treated as a complete, independent discourse — as the verse-by-verse method requires — drawing upon the Arabic language, the classical commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn ʿAjībah, and others), the authenticated words of the Prophet ﷺ with their collections identified, the reasoning that connects each verse to creed and conduct, and its bearing upon ordinary human life. Nothing has been compressed because of the chapter's length; every verse is given the full treatment its own content demands.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:1

> **Ṭā. Sīn. Mīm.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Letters, Three Chapters, One Story**

The sūrah opens, as do twenty-eight others in the Qur'an, not with a sentence but with isolated letters recited by their names: *Ṭā. Sīn. Mīm.* What is singular about this particular trio is that it stands at the head of three consecutive sūrahs — al-Shuʿarāʾ (26), al-Naml (27), and al-Qaṣaṣ (28) — and that all three chapters contain extended accounts of Moses. The reader who recites the Qur'an in order therefore hears the same three syllables three times as a kind of literary drumbeat introducing the longest and most detailed treatment of a prophet's life that the Qur'an contains. The letters are, in effect, the signature of a corpus.

The classical grammarians note how these letters are pronounced. Unlike *Alif* and *Lām*, which open with a vowel, *Ṭā*, *Sīn*, and *Mīm* are recited as open syllables with a prolonged vowel: *Ṭāā, Sīīn, Mīīm*. The recitation is thus melodic and drawn out — a chant that suspends meaning for several seconds at the very moment the reader expects it. This suspension is itself part of the effect. Before any content is delivered, the listener is stopped, made to listen, and made to wait.

**The Sound of the Arabic Language Made Strange**

The most widely held classical explanation — reported from Ibn ʿAbbās and developed by al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, and many after them — is that these letters announce the inimitable character of the Qur'an (*iʿjāz*) by a kind of demonstration. The reasoning runs as follows. The Arabs to whom the Qur'an was first addressed were a people of supreme verbal pride; their poetry was their public record, their weapon, and their measure of a man. The Qur'an's challenge to them, repeated at several points, is to produce something like it:

> *"Say: If mankind and the jinn gathered together to bring the like of this Qur'an, they could not bring the like of it, even if they helped one another."* (Qur'an 17:88)

Now, the letters with which these sūrahs open are not exotic symbols, not a secret alphabet, not foreign characters. They are the ordinary letters of the Arabic alphabet — the very sounds with which every poet, orator, and soothsayer of Arabia composed his work. By placing the raw inventory of the language at the head of the revelation, the text makes an implicit argument: everything you possess is here, in front of you, in your mouths and in your ears — and yet you cannot assemble from it what has just been assembled. The challenge is issued in the medium of the challenged. It is as though a master builder laid his uncut timber before his rivals and said: with these, and none other, this was raised.

The pattern that follows supports this reading. In the great majority of the sūrahs that open with disconnected letters, the very next verse mentions the Book or the revelation — as here: *Ṭā. Sīn. Mīm. These are the signs of the clear Book.* The letters function as a herald's fanfare before the announcement.

**Mystery Honestly Preserved**

Alongside the rhetorical explanations runs a second, older instinct in the tradition, and it is the one the greatest commentators ultimately preferred. The meaning of the letters is known to God; the believer recites them as true speech from God without claiming to possess their interpretation. Al-Rāzī, in the introduction to his discussion of the *muqaṭṭaʿāt*, gathers the many readings and then settles on a principle that is as much spiritual as intellectual: God has placed in His Book things that human reason can grasp and things that it cannot, so that the servant is constantly reminded that his knowledge is partial and that the One who speaks is not on trial before him. He draws the parallel with ritual: the wisdom of prohibiting wine is argued and understood, but the number of circumambulations around the Kaʿbah is obeyed rather than deduced. Both postures — comprehension and compliance — are required of the same soul, and the disconnected letters keep the second posture alive at the threshold of the Book.

This is not an argument for irrationality; it is an argument about the correct posture of a finite mind before an infinite Speaker. Al-Ṭabarī, the most scrupulous of the classical compilers, states the responsible position with precision: the letters are truly from God, they are not nonsense, and several interpretations are possible — but to insist that any one of them is certainly what God meant is to speak without knowledge. It is recorded from Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq that *"every book has a secret, and the secret of the Qur'an is in the openings of the sūrahs,"* and from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib that *"every book has a quintessence, and the quintessence of this Book is its spelled-out letters."* Both sayings identify rather than explain — and identification, in this case, is the point.

**The Readings the Tradition Entertained**

Because the letters invite thought, the commentators produced a garden of readings, none binding, several beautiful, and all to be held lightly.

One early gloss reads them as abbreviations of divine names or attributes, so that *Ṭā* points to *al-Ṭāhir* (the Pure) or to *ṭūbā* (blessedness), *Sīn* to *al-Salām* or *al-Samīʿ* (the All-Hearing), and *Mīm* to *al-Malik* (the Sovereign) or *al-Majīd* (the Glorious). A second reading takes the trio as an oath sworn by God by the components of His own speech — consistent with the Qur'an's habit of swearing by created things (*"By the sun and its morning brightness,"* 91:1) and, by extension, by the elements of the revealed Book.

A third approach, associated with the science of letter-numerology (*jafr*, *ḥisāb al-jummal*), assigned numerical values to the letters and derived chronologies from them. The sober scholars — al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and even the philosophically-minded al-Rāzī — consistently refused to treat such calculations as evidence. Ibn Kathīr's judgment is representative: speculation about the numerical values of the letters is the pursuit of those who have nothing better to do with revelation, and it produces claims about the unseen that no one can verify. The same caution applies, in the view of the mainstream, to attempts to use these letters to predict future events; such attempts have repeatedly embarrassed their authors and damaged the credibility of the religion in the eyes of observers.

**What the Letters Do to the Reader**

Set aside the debates, and something simpler remains, something the plain believer experiences without theory. At the head of a sūrah that will describe a mother's terror, a tyrant's arrogance, a refugee's hunger, and a tower built to climb into heaven, three syllables stand that mean nothing a dictionary can define and everything a reciter can feel. They stop the mind. They remind the reader, in the first breath of the chapter, that this Book is not merely a collection of edifying tales but a discourse whose Author is present in its very sounds.

There is also a practical mercy in their brevity. The disconnected letters are among the easiest things in the Qur'an to memorize and to recite correctly, and they have therefore been the first Qur'anic syllables on the tongues of countless Muslim children across fourteen centuries — a child's first possession of the Book is often these very letters. The one who begins with sound and moves to sense recapitulates, in miniature, the order in which the revelation itself came: a voice in a cave saying *"Recite,"* before there was yet a scripture to recite.

Finally, the letters bind this sūrah to its two sisters. Whoever hears *Ṭā. Sīn. Mīm.* has heard it twice before in the preceding pages, and the ear, trained by repetition, expects the company of Moses. That expectation is rewarded immediately: this is the sūrah of Moses' birth, exile, marriage, call, and confrontation — the story the letters have been announcing all along.

## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:2

> **These are the signs of the clear Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"These" — A Gesture Toward Something Present and Elevated**

The Arabic opens with *tilka*, a demonstrative normally used for what is distant or exalted: *these are* — or, more literally, *those are* — *the signs of the clear Book*. Grammarians have long savored this choice. The Book being indicated is plainly present, held in the hand and heard on the tongue, yet it is pointed to with the pronoun of distance. The commentators resolve the tension in three complementary ways, and all three are true at once.

It is distant because it is exalted — the way a speaker gestures toward someone he reveres without touching him. It is distant because its origin is remote: it did not arise in Makkah on the day it was recited but descends from the Preserved Tablet, *"in a Preserved Tablet"* (85:22), and from the knowledge of the One who spoke it before there was a world. And it is distant because, in the order of revelation, much of it had already been sent down before this verse was recited; the gesture reaches back over years of disclosure.

Al-Zamakhsharī adds a rhetorical observation: the demonstrative functions like a pointing finger at the object itself, as though God were saying *look at this thing, consider what it is, and judge it by what it does to you*. The verse does not argue for the Book's status; it presents the Book and asks to be examined.

**"Signs" — A Word With Three Horizons**

*Āyāt*, rendered *signs*, is one of those Qur'anic words whose range has to be felt rather than translated. It means, in the first place, *verses* — the discrete units of the recited text. It means, in the second place, *proofs* — evidences that establish a claim, as when the Qur'an speaks of the *"sign"* of a prophet's truth. And it means, in the third place, *portents in the world* — the natural phenomena that point beyond themselves:

> *"And among His signs are the night and the day, and the sun and the moon."* (Qur'an 41:37)

All three meanings are active here, and their simultaneity is itself the claim. The units of this Book are not incidental particles of a larger message; each one is itself a sign, a self-sufficient pointer to God. This is why the Qur'an can be recited as worship even by one who does not understand Arabic: the sign operates in the hearing before it operates in the understanding. And it is why the classical scholars insisted that the shortest sūrah is as much the speech of God as the longest — the signhood attaches to the whole and to every part.

**"The Clear Book" — Clarity Given and Clarity Given Out**

The phrase *al-kitābi al-mubīn* packs a deliberate ambiguity into one adjective. The root *b-y-n* means to be distinct, separated, evident. From it come *bayān* (eloquent exposition) and *bayyinah* (clear proof). Now *mubīn* in Arabic can be either passive in sense — *made clear, manifest* — or active — *that which makes clear*. Both apply to the Qur'an, and the commentators insisted on both.

Passively, the Book is clear: its message concerning God, the Hereafter, and the moral life is stated in language that a desert Arab and a modern reader alike can grasp. There is no priesthood required to decode it, no hidden doctrine reserved for an initiated class, no allegory that dissolves the plain sense. The Qur'an says of itself:

> *"And We have indeed made the Qur'an easy to remember; so is there any who will remember?"* (Qur'an 54:17)

Actively, the Book makes clear: it clarifies what human beings could never have settled by themselves. *"And We sent down unto thee the Remembrance, that thou mayest make clear to mankind that which was sent down unto them"* (16:44). Reason can establish that a Creator exists; it cannot establish which acts He wills, what the terms of the Hereafter are, or how a human life is to be ordered. The Book resolves what the mind leaves open, and in that sense it is the *furqān*, the criterion that distinguishes truth from falsehood in matters where the unaided intellect cannot arbitrate.

**The Claim Is Falsifiable, and That Is the Point**

Notice what a statement like this does. *These are the signs of the clear Book* is not a private mystical assertion, safely beyond testing. It is a public, checkable claim about a text lying in front of everyone. If the Book were muddled, self-contradictory, or morally incoherent, the claim would be refuted by the object itself. The Qur'an is willing to stake its authority on that test, and elsewhere issues it explicitly:

> *"Do they not then reflect upon the Qur'an? Had it been from other than God, they would surely have found within it much discrepancy."* (Qur'an 4:82)

This is an unusually honest posture for a scripture. A text that announced itself as clear invites the reader to verify the clarity — to collate its parts, test its consistency, examine its laws for coherence, and compare its account of the world with the world. Nothing in the tradition forbids that inquiry; the tradition exists because of it.

**Why Clarity Carries Consequences**

Clarity is not comfort only; it is also burden. A message that is clear removes excuses, and the sūrah will say exactly that at verse 47 and again at verse 59 — that God does not destroy a people until a messenger has come to them with a message they could understand. The logic is stated with perfect severity a few chapters later:

> *"Messengers bearing glad tidings and warning, lest mankind have any argument against God after the messengers."* (Qur'an 4:165)

The two ideas belong together like two blades of the same instrument. Because the Book is clear, it is a mercy to those who accept it; and because it is clear, it is a proof against those who do not. A person standing before a clear sign is in a very different moral position from a person groping in the dark. This is why the Qur'an rarely describes its rejecters as ignorant; it describes them as *seeing and refusing* — *"they denied them, though their souls were convinced of them, out of wrongdoing and exaltedness"* (27:14).

The reader of this sūrah should therefore register the claim of verse 2 as a contract. If the Book is clear, then the questions it will raise over the next eighty-six verses — about tyranny, wealth, power, the formation of conscience, and the return to God — are questions posed to the reader personally, in a language he understands, with the excuse of obscurity removed.

**Practical Fruit: Reading the Signs Rather Than Merely the Words**

There is a final, quiet implication for how the Book is used. If each verse is a *sign*, then reading is not information transfer but an act of attention to pointers. A sign is not valuable in itself; a signpost is worthless if no one looks where it points. The early Muslims understood this and measured recitation by its effect: the Companion ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd said, describing how they received the Qur'an, that they would learn no more than ten verses at a time and would not move beyond them until they had understood them and acted upon them.

For the reader today, the discipline implied by verse 2 is simple and demanding: do not merely finish the page; ask what each verse is pointing at — in the creed, in the self, in the household, in the marketplace, and in the grave. The Book calls itself clear because it is addressed to a traveler who needs a road, not to a scholar who needs a puzzle. This sūrah, which is about a man who spent ten years in the desert before he was given a mission, is a fitting place to learn that the value of guidance is not in admiring it but in walking it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:3

> **We recite unto thee in truth some of the account of Moses and Pharaoh, for a people who believe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Recite" — God Is the Narrator**

The verse opens in the first-person plural of majesty: *natlū ʿalayka* — *We recite unto thee*. God does not say "I inform you" or "it is reported to you." He says *We recite*, using the very verb (*talā*) that is used for the recitation of the Qur'an itself. The effect is to collapse the distance between the story and the act of reciting it: the same divine speech that is recited in prayer is the speech that now tells the story of Moses.

The commentators draw two conclusions from this. First, the narrative comes to Muhammad ﷺ by revelation through Gabriel, not by his own research, memory, or reading — a point that will become the backbone of the argument at verses 44–46. Second, the story is not the Prophet's composition in any sense; he is the first listener to it, exactly as his community is the second. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this is why the Qur'an says *We recite* rather than *you recite*: the initiative belongs entirely to the Speaker.

The verb also carries a ritual resonance. *Tilāwah* is the word for the Qur'anic recitation that fills a Muslim's day. By describing this narrative as *recitation*, the verse transfers the story from the category of "history" into the category of "worship." One does not read these verses the way one reads a chronicle; one recites them, and the recitation is itself obedience.

**"Some of the Account" — The Discipline of Divine Selection**

The preposition *min* — *some of*, *a portion of* — is small but theologically loaded. God does not say *the* account of Moses and Pharaoh; He says *some* of it. The classical commentators pause here and draw a method from it.

The story of Moses occupies decades: birth, palace, flight, exile, marriage, mission, plagues, exodus, drowning, wandering, the Law, the calf, the long years in the wilderness. Of all this, the Qur'an selects what serves guidance and omits what does not. There is no attempt at completeness, no genealogy, no chronology for its own sake, no inventory of places and dates beyond what the moral point requires. Compare the Qur'anic method with the Book of Exodus, and the difference is instructive: the Biblical narrative is a continuous national history; the Qur'anic narrative is a series of chosen scenes, each illuminating a facet of the same truth.

Al-Rāzī makes the point that this selectivity is itself evidence of the Qur'an's origin. If Muhammad ﷺ had learned the story from a human source, the natural result would have been a more complete and more conventionally ordered account, since teachers transmit what they received. Instead, the same events are retold in different sūrahs with different emphases, each retelling serving the theme of the sūrah in which it appears: Moses' confrontation with the magicians in al-Aʿrāf, his call in Ṭā Hā, his education by al-Khiḍr in al-Kahf, and here in al-Qaṣaṣ the whole arc of his formation from infancy to mission. A story that is retold many times but never repeated identically, and whose every retelling is thematically purposeful, behaves like speech chosen rather than like notes assembled.

This yields a practical rule for reading the Qur'an's narratives, one the commentators state explicitly: the purpose of the story is the lesson, not the details. Where the Qur'an specifies, certainty is available. Where it is silent, silence is the correct response — one neither affirms nor denies, and one never builds doctrine on the unstated.

**"In Truth" — Three Dimensions of a Single Word**

The phrase *bi-l-ḥaqq* modifies the recitation, and the commentators unpack three senses, all intended.

It means, first, *truthfully*: what follows is not legend or parable but what actually happened. The Qur'an's narratives of the prophets are history, and the Qur'an'sGod does not tell fables. The rejection of the story as fiction is precisely the charge the Qur'an answers: *"This is naught but fabricated sorcery"* (v. 36) — and the answer is *bi-l-ḥaqq*.

It means, second, *with justice*: the narration is fair to Pharaoh as well as to Moses. Pharaoh's crimes are named plainly (v. 4), but so is God's graciousness even to Pharaoh's household (v. 9), and the story records that it was Pharaoh's own wife who interceded for the infant. A narration *in truth* does not caricature its villains; it weighs them.

It means, third, *in earnest*: the story is told for a real purpose, not to amuse. The same distinction is drawn elsewhere in the Qur'an between speech that aims at something and speech that merely fills time — the *laghw*, or idle talk, from which believers turn away at verse 55 of this very sūrah.

**"For a People Who Believe" — Why Faith Is Named as the Condition**

The verse closes by naming the audience: *li-qawmin yuʾminūn*, *for a people who believe*. This is not an exclusion — the Qur'an addresses disbelievers constantly and directly — but it does identify who benefits.

The commentators explain the mechanism. Faith is the faculty by which the story is read as a sign rather than as a tale. A believer who hears of the infant in the river perceives the governance of God; the same narrative heard without faith produces, at best, an interesting anecdote. Faith is, in this sense, like literacy: the text is equally available to the literate and the illiterate, but only one of them can read it. The Qur'an states the principle generally:

> *"He sends astray many thereby, and He guides many thereby; and He sends none astray thereby save the iniquitous."* (Qur'an 2:26)

The sūrah itself will demonstrate the contrast within a few verses. The same signs that move the believing household to faith will later be dismissed by Pharaoh's court as *"fabricated sorcery"* (v. 36), and the same Qur'an that brings People of the Book to tears of recognition (v. 52–53) will be mocked by the Quraysh as *"two sorceries supporting one another"* (v. 48). The signal is identical; the receivers differ.

There is also a psychological insight here that modern readers can verify in themselves. Stories work on a person in proportion to his readiness to be addressed by them. The person who has already decided that no one may tell him anything is not unreachable by argument — he is unreachable by *narrative*, because narrative requires a temporary surrender of the self's defenses. Faith supplies that willingness, and it is precisely why the Qur'an repeatedly addresses *those who believe* immediately before or after a passage of great moral weight: it is instructing the posture in which the instruction can land.

**Why Moses and Pharaoh**

Of all the prophet-stories the Qur'an could have paired with this sūrah's themes, it chose the longest one, and the classical scholars give the reason: the career of Moses parallels the career of Muhammad ﷺ more closely than any other. Both were raised in a dominant civilization that claimed to be the measure of reality; both were sent to a ruler who claimed absolute authority; both confronted a council of notables; both were rejected, threatened, and forced from their homes; both were granted scripture and law; both led a believing community out of oppression; both returned in victory to the city that expelled them.

The Qur'an makes the parallel explicit elsewhere:

> *"Truly We have sent unto you a messenger, a witness over you, even as We sent unto Pharaoh a messenger."* (Qur'an 73:15)

The point is consolation (*tasliyah*) and warning at once. Consolation: the Prophet, besieged in Makkah, is told that the man of God has stood here before, and that what happened to Pharaoh's power happened within one lifetime. Warning: the Quraysh are told, without being named, precisely where their behavior places them in the pattern.

The Prophet ﷺ internalized this identification completely. When an ugly accusation was leveled at him over the distribution of charity, his reply was not self-defense but a citation of his brother:

> *"May God have mercy upon Moses: he was harmed with worse than this, and he was patient."* (al-Bukhārī; Muslim)

And on the night of the Ascension, when the prescribed prayers were being lightened, it was Moses who repeatedly urged the Prophet ﷺ back to his Lord, saying *"Your community will not be able to bear that"* — the narration reported by al-Bukhārī and Muslim in the long ḥadīth of the *Isrāʾ*. The Prophet met Moses in the heavens; he also met him in this sūrah, which was being revealed to him in Makkah at the very time of his greatest pressure.

**What This Verse Asks of the Reader**

Three practical attitudes follow from these twelve Arabic words. Recite the narrative as revelation, not as literature — that is, approach it as something addressed *to* you rather than merely *about* others. Accept the discipline of the *min*: where the Qur'an is silent, be silent, and take the lesson rather than the curiosities. And then check the receiver. If these stories have ceased to move you — if Moses' mother, the river, the fugitive at the well, and the drowning tyrant have become familiar furniture rather than live signals — then the defect is not in the narration, which is *in truth*, but in the heart's posture, which the verse names at the end: *for a people who believe*.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:4

> **Indeed, Pharaoh exalted himself in the land, and made its people into factions, oppressing a party among them, slaying their sons and sparing their women. Verily he was among those who work corruption.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Four-Clause Diagnosis of a Tyranny**

In a single verse, the Qur'an performs a political autopsy. Pharaoh's rule is described in four movements, and the order is not accidental: an inner disposition (*exalted himself*), a technique (*made its people into factions*), an action (*oppressing a party*), and an outcome (*he was among those who work corruption*). The verse is thus a complete causal chain from the state of a ruler's soul to the ruin of a society. Read it as a pathology report: the disease begins in the heart, becomes a policy, produces suffering, and ends in corruption.

**"Exalted Himself" — The Root of the Whole Affair**

*ʿAlā fi al-arḍ* — *he exalted himself in the land*. The root *ʿ-l-w* gives the Qur'an the vocabulary of height and superiority, and notably, the same root appears in this sūrah's closing passages with the opposite moral charge: the Hereafter belongs to those *who desire neither dominance (ʿuluww) upon the earth nor corruption* (v. 83). The word links Pharaoh to Korah, and it will be used by God for Himself in the loftiest sense, *al-ʿAliyy* — the Most High. The moral question is never whether one is "high"; it is whether one's height is received or seized.

Two readings of the phrase are recorded. Al-Ṭabarī takes it in its immediate political sense: Pharaoh behaved with tyranny and insolence (*tajabbur*, *ʿutūw*) within his kingdom. Al-Qurṭubī adds the fuller sense suggested by the parallel verse where Pharaoh's claim is quoted verbatim:

> *"And he said, 'I am your lord most high.'"* (Qur'an 79:24)

That is, the exaltation was not merely brutal conduct but a *theological* claim — the ruler positioning himself as the source of authority, law, and meaning. This distinction matters enormously for the reader, because it explains why the confrontation that follows is not a labor dispute or a national liberation struggle but a contest over who is God. Moses is not sent to negotiate better working conditions; he is sent to say *Lord of the worlds* (v. 30) to a man who said *I am your lord most high*.

Theologically, the verse thus identifies the primal political sin as *shirk* relocated from the temple to the throne: the creature claiming the prerogative that belongs only to the Creator. Every subsequent abuse in the verse flows from that single displacement.

**"Made Its People into Factions" — The Technology of Control**

*Wa-jaʿala ahlahā shiyaʿan* — *and made its people into factions, parties, sects*. The word *shiyaʿ* (singular *shīʿah*) denotes a group defined by partisanship and common cause. The commentators stress the verb: he *made* them factions. The division of the population was not a natural or inevitable feature of Egyptian society; it was manufactured.

Why would a ruler manufacture division? The answer is so consistent across history that the verse reads as a law rather than a report. A population that is united can bargain, resist, and judge. A population broken into mutually suspicious groups can be ruled cheaply, because each faction's fear of the others exceeds its resentment of the ruler, and each will seek the ruler's favor against its rivals. Al-Rāzī observes that the tyrant's security depends on this: as long as the groups are quarreling, none of them can combine against him. The strategy is divide-and-rule, and the Qur'an identifies it as a *deliberate act* (*jaʿala*), not a misfortune.

The reader is meant to recognize the pattern wherever it recurs: the privileged class set against the rest, the native-born against the immigrant, the tribe against the tribe, the religious group against the religious group — with the ruler standing above as the indispensable arbiter. The Qur'an's counter-principle is stated flatly and repeatedly:

> *"And hold fast, all of you together, to the covenant of God, and do not separate."* (Qur'an 3:103)

**"Oppressing a Party Among Them" — Weakness as Manufactured Condition**

*Yastaḍʿifu ṭāʾifatan minhum* — the verb comes from *ḍ-ʿ-f*, weakness, and in this form carries the sense of *deeming weak* and *treating as weak*, not merely finding weak. The Qur'an uses the same verb for the arrogance of the powerful toward the prophets' followers: the notables of Shuʿayb's people say, *"We would surely have stoned thee... and thou art not mighty over us except for thy clan"* (11:91); and the people of Ṣāliḥ say of the believers, *"Are these the ones upon whom God has conferred favor from among us?"* (6:53).

The commentators describe the concrete form this took: the Israelites were enslaved, put to servile labor, and forced into the most degrading work — the building of Pharaoh's cities and storehouses. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr report the tasks as construction, agriculture, and public works of the kind that broke bodies and left no leisure for anything else. The spiritual insight attached to this is sharp: a population kept exhausted cannot remember God, teach its children, or imagine a different order. Oppression is not only cruelty; it is a strategy for the occupation of a people's imagination.

There is a second meaning the lexicographers preserve and the commentators note: *istaḍʿafa* also means *to consider weak*, that is, to construct an ideology of contempt. Before a group can be exploited, it must first be redefined as lesser — less intelligent, less civilized, less capable, less deserving. The Qur'an records this mentality in Pharaoh's own words about Moses:

> *"Or am I not better than this one, who is contemptible and hardly makes himself clear?"* (Qur'an 43:52)

That sentence is the perfect specimen of what the verse describes: the oppressor cannot see a human being, only a rating.

**"Slaying Their Sons and Sparing Their Women" — What the Qur'an Does and Does Not Say**

Here the commentarial tradition exercises a discipline worth watching. The Qur'an states the fact — male children killed, females spared — and, notably, gives *no reason* for it. It simply records it as an act of *fasād*, corruption.

The classical commentators, drawing on transmitted reports, proposed that soothsayers or astrologers had warned Pharaoh that a boy born to the Israelites would overthrow him, or that Pharaoh himself dreamt a fire issuing from Jerusalem that consumed Egypt and reached his own house. Al-Rāzī, in a notable passage, questions this entire line of explanation and proposes something more sober and more consistent with the Qur'an's own presentation: the killing was a measure of demographic control and collective punishment against a subject population that had grown numerous — *"lest they multiply and, if war befalls us, they join our enemies"* is the reasoning the Qur'an itself attributes to Pharaoh's court at another point:

> *"We shall slay their sons and spare their women; and truly we have power over them."* (Qur'an 7:127)

Note the phrase *We shall slay* in 7:127: it is a *policy announced in response to Moses' mission*, which indicates that the practice was an instrument of state terror, deployable and repeatable, not a one-time reaction to an omen. Al-Rāzī's preference for this reading is quietly revolutionary: he is willing to overrule inherited commentary where the Qur'an's own internal evidence points elsewhere. The lesson for the student of tafsīr is that the transmitted report is honored but not sovereign; the Book judges the reports, not the reverse.

The sparing of the women is as significant as the killing of the boys. It leaves a people alive but unmanned by design — the next generation of males removable, the women absorbed into the dominant society. It is a slow dissolution rather than an extermination: precisely the sort of measure that keeps a labor force available while eliminating a threat. Weighed morally, the verse simply calls it what it is, and the final clause supplies the verdict.

**"He Was Among Those Who Work Corruption" — *Fasād* in Its Full Sense**

The verse closes with *wa-innahu kāna mina al-mufsidīn*. The root *f-s-d* means literally to rot, spoil, go bad — meat spoiling, water becoming brackish. The Qur'an's moral use is therefore vivid: corruption is not merely wrongdoing but a *decay* that spreads. The word here covers the killing, the enslavement, the division of society, the waste of human potential, the corruption of the moral sense that comes from a whole population's complicity, and the corruption of the land itself, which the Qur'an elsewhere ties to human conduct:

> *"Corruption has appeared on land and sea because of what the hands of men have earned, that He may let them taste something of what they have done, that haply they may return."* (Qur'an 30:41)

Note also the phrasing *among those who work corruption* — not *he was a corrupter* in the abstract. He belonged to a *class*, with predecessors and successors. The verse is written so that Pharaoh is never merely an individual villain from a distant past; he is a type, and the type recurs. The Prophet ﷺ warned his community about the survival of this pattern in a ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, in which he described the kingship that would come after the caliphate and said:

> *"There will be rulers who will not be guided by my guidance and will not follow my Sunnah; there will be among them men whose hearts are the hearts of devils in the bodies of men."*

**The Verse's Logic Made Plain**

Why does the Qur'an open the story this way, with the oppressor rather than the oppressed, and with diagnosis rather than drama? Because the rest of the narrative is unintelligible otherwise. The desperate mother, the basket in the river, the flight to Midian, the confrontation at court, and the drowning in the sea are all *responses* to the four clauses of this verse. The sūrah will spend its next eight verses showing how God answers each clause, one by one: the self-exaltation is answered by an infant raised in Pharaoh's own house; the division is answered by a savior drawn from the despised faction; the slaughter of sons is answered by the survival of the very son the policy was meant to prevent; and the corruption is answered by the sea.

There is also a law of history embedded here, and the Qur'an states it elsewhere with full explicitness:

> *"And We desired to be gracious..."* (v. 5, following)

God's response to oppression is not only deferred to the Hereafter. It operates within history, and often through the most improbable instrument the oppressor can imagine — a child raised at his own table.

**The Modern Mirror**

Read the four clauses against any oppressive system, ancient or contemporary, and they hold. A leadership class that considers itself above accountability; a population deliberately fragmented into hostile identity groups; one segment treated as inherently lesser and kept exhausted and disparaged; policies that harm the vulnerable while being described as necessities of security or economy; and a general spreading rot that eventually touches the oppressors themselves. The verse's value is that it gives the believer a *diagnostic vocabulary* rather than only a moral sentiment. It teaches one to look for the manufactured division, to name the ideological contempt, to trace policy back to the claim to absolute status, and to refuse the lie that any of it is natural.

And it gives the believer the correct emotional posture: not despair, and not a private hatred, but the confidence that a tyranny so constructed has already been read, weighed, and sentenced by the One who says of it, in the very next verse, *Yet We desired to be gracious.*

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:5

> **Yet We desired to be gracious to those who were oppressed in the land, and to make them imams, and to make them the heirs,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The "Yet" That Changes Everything**

The verse opens with a single Arabic conjunction that carries the entire theological weight of the passage: *wa* — rendered here *Yet*. Verse 4 ended with Pharaoh's corruption; verse 5 opens with God's intention. The two are set in direct opposition, and the reader is meant to feel the collision. Pharaoh plots; God wills. Pharaoh slaughters; God confers grace. Pharaoh exalts himself; God raises the despised.

The commentators note that this is not a contest between two comparable powers. Pharaoh *plans*; God *desires and does*. The asymmetry is the whole point, and it is stated with a calm brevity that is more terrible than any threat would be. The tyrant's four-clause program of verse 4 is answered here by a five-clause program of grace, and the reader already knows which one will still be standing at verse 40.

**"We Desired" — Divine Will Described in the Language of Intention**

*Wa-nurīdu an namunna* — *We desired to be gracious*. The verb *manna* comes from a root meaning to bestow a favor, especially one that cannot be repaid. The word appears in the Qur'an both for God's graciousness to His servants and, tellingly, for the *reminding* of a favor in a way that wounds the recipient — *"do not nullify your charity by reminders [of your generosity] and injury"* (2:264). With God, of course, the favor is pure; the human failing of the word's second sense belongs only to creatures. The choice of this verb emphasizes sheer gratuity: God owed the Israelites nothing. He was under no obligation to them by merit; the grace is grace.

That divine will should be described in the language of *desire* is itself a theological statement of the first importance. God is not a passive principle or an unmoved mover, indifferent to what happens in Egypt. He has intentions, and those intentions are particular: toward *these* people, in *this* land, at *this* time. The Ashʿarī and Māturīdī theologians read such verses as establishing the attribute of *irādah* (will) as real, eternal, and distinct from both knowledge and power; the Muʿtazilī attempt to reduce it to something created was rejected precisely by verses like this one, where God's willing precedes and produces events.

At the same time — and here the Sunni position is careful — the divine will is not arbitrary. Al-Rāzī notes that the verse presents God's graciousness as *response*: it is directed *to those who were oppressed*. God's favor here is not random election but a moral choice that honors the condition of the wronged. The Qur'an states the principle generally elsewhere:

> *"And We desired to be gracious to those who were oppressed in the land..."*

and, in the voice of God's covenant with the oppressed:

> *"And those who, when they are wronged, defend themselves."* (Qur'an 42:39)

**"Those Who Were Oppressed in the Land" — God's Side of the Argument**

Al-Ṭabarī identifies them simply: the Children of Israel. But the phrasing matters more than the identification. They are not described by tribe, merit, wealth, piety, or achievement. They are described by what was *done to them*. The verse names them by their wound.

This is one of the most pastorally powerful features of the Qur'an. The divine address consistently seeks out the people whom society has categorized as negligible. The Qur'an uses this very vocabulary of the despised again and again. The *mustaḍʿafūn* — the deemed-weak — appear at the Resurrection as the accusers of the powerful:

> *"And those who deemed themselves weak will say to those who waxed arrogant, 'Nay, it was your plotting by night and day, when you commanded us to disbelieve in God and to set up rivals unto Him.'"* (Qur'an 34:33)

And in another sūrah, the promise made here is universalized:

> *"And We verily wrote in the Psalms, after the Remembrance: 'My righteous servants shall inherit the earth.'"* (Qur'an 21:105)

The logic is not that weakness automatically sanctifies. The Qur'an never teaches that. But it does teach that oppression creates a claim before God, that the cries of the wronged are heard without intermediary — the Prophet ﷺ said to Muʿādh ibn Jabal when sending him to Yemen:

> *"Beware of the supplication of the wronged, for there is no barrier between it and God."* (al-Bukhārī 2448; Muslim 19)

— and that the moral geometry of history is tilted, by God's own declared intention, toward the restoration of the humbled.

**"To Make Them Imams" — From Victims to Guides**

The second grace is the most surprising: *wa-najʿalahum aʾimmah* — *and to make them imams*. Not merely free, not merely safe, not merely compensated, but *leaders* — and leaders in the specifically Qur'anic sense of *imām*, which the classical scholars define with care.

Al-Rāzī glosses *imām* as one who is foremost (*muqaddam*) in religion and in the affairs of the world. Al-Qurṭubī adds the moral dimension: an imam is one who leads in good and calls others to good. The word therefore fuses two things modern societies usually separate — authority and guidance, power and exemplarity.

The Qur'an uses the same term for the patriarchs, and the parallel is the point:

> *"And We made them imams, guiding according to Our Command, and We revealed unto them the performance of good deeds, the establishment of prayer, and the giving of alms; and they were worshippers of Us."* (Qur'an 21:73)

And for Abraham, tested and found true:

> *"He said, 'Indeed, I will make thee an imam for mankind.'"* (Qur'an 2:124)

And as the summit of the believers' own prayer:

> *"And make us imams for the reverent."* (Qur'an 25:74)

Set these together and the theology of leadership in Islam becomes clear. An imam is not one who dominates; an imam is one who is *followed because he is ahead in obedience*. The oppressed Israelites are being promised not revenge but vocation: they were crushed, and from their crushing God will produce people who show the way.

There is a psychological insight worth dwelling on, one the tradition draws out repeatedly. The natural consequence of long oppression is a deformed self-image: the oppressed begin to believe the contempt directed at them. Elevating such a people to leadership therefore requires an *internal* transformation before it can be an external one — they must be healed of the story that they are lesser. The Qur'an's own account shows this healing taking generations, and it was not complete in a single lifetime: the same people who were promised leadership later refused to enter the Holy Land, saying:

> *"Therein are a people of tyrannical might, and we shall not enter it until they leave it."* (Qur'an 5:22)

That is the voice of a people not yet inwardly freed. The verse's promise, then, is both a gift and a task: God makes them imams, but imamate requires them to grow into what God has called them.

**"To Make Them the Heirs" — What Was Actually Inherited**

*Wa-najʿalahumu al-wārithīn* — *and to make them the heirs*. The commentators raise the obvious question: heirs of what?

The most direct reading, and the one that fits the Qur'an's own later report, is that they inherited the land and the civilization of Egypt — not by plundering it after a battle, which they did not fight, but by receiving it after the Egyptians were destroyed. The Qur'an refers to this outcome elsewhere:

> *"Thus it was; and We made the Children of Israel inheritors of it."* (Qur'an 26:59)

and:

> *"And We caused the people who had been oppressed to inherit the eastern parts of the land and the western parts thereof, which We had blessed."* (Qur'an 7:137)

and, describing the abandoned estates of the Egyptian elite:

> *"How many were the gardens and springs they left behind, and sown fields and noble stations, and comforts wherein they had rejoiced! Thus it was; and We made another people inherit them."* (Qur'an 44:25–28)

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note a subtlety in these verses: the inheritance did not pass from Pharaoh to Israel by normal transfer, since Pharaoh's people perished. It passed from God, who owns everything, to a people He chose. This is the meaning of the Qur'an's recurring declaration that God is *the Inheritor* — a theme this sūrah will itself return to at verse 58: *And We, truly, are the Inheritor.*

There is also a broader reading that the spiritual commentators have favored. The inheritance promised to the oppressed is ultimately the earth itself, in the sense of 21:105 quoted above: *My righteous servants shall inherit the earth*. The immediate historical fulfillment becomes a type of the final one, when the meek and the truthful possess what the arrogant only rented for a season.

**Divine Planning Versus Human Planning: The Central Logic**

The deepest logical content of this verse is its placement in a narrative sequence. Verse 4 states Pharaoh's plan; verse 5 states God's; and verses 6–13 show God's plan executing itself *through* Pharaoh's, using the tyrant's own machinery as its instrument.

Trace the mechanism, because it is the heart of the sūrah. Pharaoh orders the killing of Israelite boys. The policy forces Moses' mother to hide her child and then to cast him into the river. The river carries the basket to Pharaoh's own palace. Pharaoh's wife intercedes for the child. The child is raised in Pharaoh's house, at Pharaoh's expense, under Pharaoh's protection, and — by the device of the wet-nurse — is returned to his own mother to be taught and formed by her. The tyrant's decree, designed to eliminate the threat, becomes the cause of the threat's presence in the palace, its education, and its eventual confrontation with the throne.

Theologically this is *tadbīr* — the governance of God that operates beneath and within the choices of creatures without compelling them. Pharaoh was not a puppet; he chose his crimes and is punished for them. But his choices were enclosed within a wisdom that could turn them inside out. The Prophet ﷺ gave the principle its canonical formulation in the ḥadīth of Ibn ʿAbbās:

> *"Know that if the whole nation were to gather together to benefit you with something, they would benefit you only with something that God had already written for you; and if they were to gather together to harm you with something, they would harm you only with something that God had already written against you. The pens have been lifted and the pages have dried."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad, and he graded it ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

The correct human response to such governance is neither fatalism nor anxiety but what the tradition calls *tawakkul* combined with *sabab* — taking the lawful means while resting the heart on the One who disposes outcomes. Moses' mother is the sūrah's own model: she was told to do something terrifying and specific (cast the child into the river), and she was told to trust the promise attached to it.

**Practical Fruit: What This Verse Does to the Lowly and the Powerful**

For anyone who has been humiliated, marginalized, exploited, or written off, this verse is a direct address. God's declared intention is on record: He sides with the wronged. That is not sentimental; it is a statement about how history is governed. It also imposes a shape on the rescued: they are made *imams*, not avengers. The dignity God restores is meant to become guidance for others, and the person who has passed through oppression is uniquely qualified to lead others through it.

For anyone holding power, the verse is a warning so specific it can be read as a checklist. God's counter-plan is aimed precisely at self-exaltation, at the manufacture of division, and at the crushing of a group. No position is secure against a God who can raise the child you ordered killed in your own house. The Prophet ﷺ said, in the ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"O My servants, I have forbidden oppression for Myself, and I have made it forbidden among you, so do not oppress one another."* (Muslim 2577)

The sūrah will show, within thirty-six verses, exactly what happens to a man who did.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:6

> **and to establish them in the land, and to show Pharaoh and Hāmān and their hosts that which they dreaded from them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth Grace: Not Rescue but Establishment**

Verse 5 named three graces — graciousness, imamate, inheritance. This verse completes the set with two more: *establishment in the land*, and the *demonstration* to Pharaoh and his court of the very thing they feared. The progression is deliberate. A people rescued but left rootless can be re-enslaved in a generation. Grace is therefore completed by *tamkīn* — the granting of stable footing, capacity, and durability.

*Wa-numakkina lahum fī al-arḍ* — the root *m-k-n* gives the Qur'an its vocabulary of *place, capacity, settled ability*. The same root appears in the description of Joseph's establishment in Egypt (*"Thus did We establish Joseph in the land,"* 12:21, 12:56), in the description of the believers' condition after persecution (*"those who, if We establish them in the land, perform prayer and give alms and enjoin right and forbid wrong,"* 22:41), and in God's promise to the earliest Muslims.

That last parallel is theologically decisive, because it supplies the *condition* attached to establishment. Power is not granted as a prize but as a trust with a job description:

> *"Those who, if We establish them in the land, perform the prayer and give the alms, and enjoin right and forbid wrong. And unto God belongs the end of affairs."* (Qur'an 22:41)

The commentators therefore read *establishment* here not as mere territory but as *capacity to act rightly on a social scale* — the difference between a people who has escaped and a people who can now build. The Israelites' forty years of wandering after the Exodus were, by this reading, the painful interval between rescue and establishment, and the Qur'an records why it lasted as long as it did.

**The Hāmān Question: A Case Study in Commentarial Honesty**

The second grace is directed at three parties: *Pharaoh, Hāmān, and their hosts*. Hāmān is named in the Qur'an six times (28:6, 28:8, 28:38; 29:39; 40:24; 40:36), always in the company of Pharaoh, and in two of those places it is Hāmān whom Pharaoh orders to build — *"kindling a fire over the clay"* (28:38) and *"build me a tower"* (40:36). The figure is thus presented as Pharaoh's chief executive, the man who turns royal will into brick and mortar.

The identification of Hāmān has generated more modern controversy than almost any other name in the Qur'an, and the classical sources themselves are partly responsible. Many classical commentators transmitted, often uncritically, material drawn from Jewish and Christian lore; al-Thaʿlabī, al-Kisāʾī, and the popular storytellers included reports of a Hāmān who lived centuries before Moses and served a Babylonian king. Skeptical Western scholarship then proposed that the Qur'an had confused the biblical Haman of the Book of Esther with an Egyptian official — a charge that has been repeated in polemical literature for two centuries.

A careful Muslim response, now held by many modern scholars and anticipated in spirit by the more critical classical ones, runs as follows. The name *Hāmān* is most plausibly not a personal name but a *title*, exactly as *Pharaoh* (from Egyptian *per-ʿaa*, "great house") is a title rather than a personal name. The title *Ha-Amen*, "servant of Amun," or the reconstructed Egyptian *ḥm-ntr* (priest of the god), designates the high priest of Amun at Thebes — an office that, in the New Kingdom, was second only to the king in authority and was specifically charged with oversight of building works, quarries, and public monuments. A high priest of Amun would be precisely the person a pharaoh would instruct to fire clay bricks and raise a tower. This reading explains every Qur'anic mention at once, without requiring any confusion with a Persian-era biblical villain.

The methodological lesson here is more important than the identification itself. The classical tradition's weakness was not its scholarship but its occasional willingness to absorb unvetted narrative; the tradition's strength, present in al-Ṭabarī at his best and fully developed by Ibn Kathīr, is its insistence that the Qur'an is the criterion and that *isrāʾīliyyāt* — reports of Israelite origin — are to be neither affirmed nor denied when they are unverifiable. The Prophet ﷺ gave the rule:

> *"Relate from the Children of Israel, for there is no harm in that; but whoever lies about me deliberately, let him take his seat in the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 3461)

and he warned against the opposite error:

> *"Do not ask the People of the Book about anything, for they will not guide you, having themselves gone astray, so that you may deny a truth or affirm a falsehood."* (al-Bukhārī; Aḥmad)

Between permission and caution lies the correct discipline: the Qur'an's account stands on its own, transmitted detail may be mentioned for illustration, and no doctrine may rest upon it.

**"And Their Hosts" — Collective Responsibility**

The verse names not only the two principals but *junūdahumā*, their hosts — the armies, bureaucracies, and supporting classes that gave the regime its reach. The Qur'an is consistent in this: tyranny is never a one-man operation. Pharaoh's *malaʾ*, his council of notables, are named in verse 32 and act separately in verse 20; the magicians are summoned in other sūrahs; and the whole apparatus is drowned together as a unit in verse 40.

The theology of collective responsibility here is precise and worth stating, because it is often misread. The Qur'an holds each soul accountable for its own deeds:

> *"No bearer of burdens bears the burden of another."* (Qur'an 6:164)

But it also recognizes that institutions have moral weight, that a person's participation in an unjust system is itself a deed, and that those who merely execute orders are not thereby excused. The Qur'an's scene of the Hereafter shows the followers and the followed contending with one another (34:31–33), and the followers' plea is exactly the plea of functionaries everywhere:

> *"They will say, 'Our Lord! We obeyed our leaders and our great ones, and they led us astray from the way.'"* (Qur'an 33:67)

The inclusion of *their hosts* in this verse therefore carries a warning that reaches through every level of a regime: the clerks, the engineers, the officers, the propagandists, and the silent beneficiaries. The Prophet ﷺ identified the mechanism with unforgettable clarity:

> *"Whosoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand; if he cannot, then with his tongue; and if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith."* (Muslim 49)

**"That Which They Dreaded" — The Irony at the Center of the Verse**

The final clause is the sharpest: *and to show Pharaoh and Hāmān and their hosts that which they dreaded from them*. The word rendered *dreaded* is from *ḥ-dh-r*, meaning to beware, to be on guard against, to take precautions. They had taken precautions. The killing of the male children *was* the precaution.

Two readings of *that which they dreaded* are recorded, and the difference between them is instructive.

The first, transmitted by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī from earlier authorities, is that it refers to a prophecy or omen — that a boy would be born among the Israelites who would destroy their kingdom. On this reading, God shows them precisely the fulfillment of the thing they were trying to prevent.

The second, which al-Rāzī himself prefers, is far more chilling and requires no omen at all. What they dreaded was the loss of a subject population they had kept subdued by periodically decimating its males. Their dread was simply political: a numerous, unbroken people is harder to rule. And what God showed them was exactly that fear realized — not a foreign army, not a rebellion they could foresee, but a people who walked out of Egypt whole and armed with a Law.

The second reading has a particular power: it shows that a tyrant's paranoia is not a disorder superimposed on his policy — it *is* his policy. He is not afraid despite his precautions; he is afraid *because* of them, because precautions taken against a people deepen the enmity that makes the precautions necessary. The Qur'an describes this spiral of defensive cruelty with psychological precision elsewhere:

> *"And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 3:54)

Note the Arabic convention: the plotting of a creature against truth is called *makr* and is blameworthy; where it is attributed to God in this context, the meaning is that God brings about consequences through means the plotters did not anticipate, and the classical creed is emphatic that no blame attaches to God for this. Al-Rāzī states the rule: God is not described by such names except in a way befitting His perfection, and the intended meaning is requital, not deception.

**The Verse as a Law of History**

Taken together, verses 5 and 6 state a law that the sūrah will then demonstrate in narrative. God's plan for the oppressed includes rescue, vocation, inheritance, stability, and vindication before their oppressors. The vindication is not vengeance but *exposure*: *to show them* — to make them see, with their own eyes, that their precautions were useless and that the God they denied governed the outcome.

That exposure is already visible in the shape of the story. Pharaoh will see the child he ordered killed standing in his own hall, demanding the release of a people, speaking in the name of *the Lord of the worlds*. He will see his magicians fall prostrate. He will see his army destroyed by the very sea he had used as his moat. And at the moment of drowning, the Qur'an records the exact instant of that recognition — which is, in Islamic theology, the least useful moment in the world to acquire a creed:

> *"Till, when drowning overtook him, he said, 'I believe that there is no god but the One in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am among those who submit.' Now? While before this thou didst rebel and wert among the workers of corruption?"* (Qur'an 10:90–91)

**Application: The Believer's Relationship to Fear**

Finally, the verse speaks directly to the management of fear, which is the tyrant's principal instrument and also the believer's principal weakness. Pharaoh's dread moved him to murder. The believer's dread is meant to move him to God, and to nothing else. The Qur'an's psychology of fear is summed up in the declaration made first to Moses and Aaron and then, repeatedly, to every believer since:

> *"Fear not; indeed I am with you both; I hear and I see."* (Qur'an 20:46)

The practical corollary is that a community which lets dread determine its policies will eventually commit the cruelties it dreads being subjected to. The sūrah's cure for that is the knowledge of verses 5 and 6: the outcome is already with God, and the oppressor's precautions are the very means by which God's promise reaches him.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:7

> **So We revealed to the mother of Moses, "Nurse him. But if you fear for him, then cast him into the river, and fear not, nor grieve. Surely We shall bring him back to you and make him one of the messengers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command That Sounds Like Cruelty**

Of all the commands recorded in the Qur'an, this one is among the hardest to read without flinching. A mother is told to nurse her child — and then, if she fears for him, to throw him into a river. The commentators do not soften it. What they do is place it in its proper frame, and the frame is everything.

Note the sequence: *nurse him* comes before *cast him*. The instruction assumes the normal, tender relationship. Nursing establishes the bond, the nourishment, and the first months of life. Only when danger arises (*if you fear for him*) does the second instruction activate. The command to cast him into the river is therefore not a suspension of motherhood but its most extreme expression — it is issued to a mother precisely because she loves her child and because the alternative is certain death for him. She is not being asked to stop being a mother; she is being asked to be one in the only way still available.

The conditional phrasing is also significant. She is not given a fixed timetable; she is given a criterion — *if you fear* — and entrusted with the judgment of when it has been met. The revelation thus honors her perception and her agency. She is not a passive instrument in the story; she is a moral agent whose fear is treated as a legitimate signal.

**"We Revealed" — The Range of *Waḥy* in the Qur'an**

The verb is *awḥaynā*, from *waḥy*. This is the same verb used for the revelation given to Muhammad ﷺ:

> *"It is naught but a revelation revealed."* (Qur'an 53:4)

Its appearance here, concerning a woman who is not a prophet, has generated careful discussion among the scholars, because the creedal distinction between revelation to prophets and inspiration to others is a mainstay of Islamic theology.

The classical lexicographers define *waḥy* at root as *a swift, hidden communication*. From that core, the Qur'an uses the word in several distinguishable senses, and the commentators catalogued them:

> *"And thy Lord revealed to the bee: 'Take for thyself houses among the mountains and among the trees and in what they build.'"* (Qur'an 16:68)

> *"And when I revealed to the apostles [of Jesus]: 'Believe in Me and in My messenger.'"* (Qur'an 5:111)

Instinct to an animal, inspiration to the disciples of Jesus, suggestion to human beings generally, and legislative revelation to messengers — all are called *waḥy* in the Qur'an, because all share the defining feature: a meaning placed in a heart by God, swiftly and imperceptibly, without any creature as its author.

How, then, did it reach Moses' mother? Three views are recorded. Al-Ṭabarī prefers that it was cast into her heart (*ilqāʾ fī al-qalb*) — a direct, immediate conviction that she knew to be from God. Others say it came in a dream, and the exegetes note that the dreams of the righteous are a form of authentic communication, citing Abraham's dream of sacrifice (37:102). A third view holds that Gabriel came to her and spoke.

That last view raised a problem the scholars addressed head-on: if an angel speaks to a person, does that person become a prophet? Al-Qurṭubī's answer is unambiguous — no. He argues that an angelic address does not confer prophethood, and he cites the consensus (*ijmāʿ*) of the tradition that Moses' mother was not a prophetess. The reasoning is that prophethood is not constituted by the mode of communication but by a specific divine commission (*risālah*, *nubuwwah*) carrying law or scripture; a person may receive an angelic message for his own guidance or for a specific act without being sent to anyone. The same reasoning is used in reports concerning Mary, over whom the scholars likewise debated, with the majority holding that she was a *ṣiddīqah* — a woman of perfect truthfulness — rather than a prophetess.

Theologically this verse is therefore one of the proof-texts for a distinction the tradition has always guarded: God speaks to whom He wills in modes He chooses, but the *office* of prophethood is a specific and terminable institution. It ended, by consensus, with Muhammad ﷺ, while inspiration, insight, and true dreams remain open to the righteous:

> *"Nothing remains of prophethood except glad tidings."* They asked, "And what are glad tidings?" He said, *"The true dream that a believing man sees, or that is shown to him."* (al-Bukhārī, Book of Expression of Dreams; the wording is reported in several collections)

**"Fear Not, Nor Grieve" — The Exact Anatomy of Distress**

The promise contains two negations, and the commentators have drawn a great deal from their precision. *Lā takhāfī wa-lā taḥzanī* — *fear not, nor grieve*.

The distinction is this: *khawf*, fear, is distress about what may come; *ḥuzn*, grief, is distress about what has already passed. Between them they cover the whole of time. A mother in this situation has both: she fears what the river will do to her child, and she grieves over the separation that is already occurring. God negates both, and the verse therefore addresses not one emotion but the entire temporal field of human suffering.

The commentators note that this same double negation recurs throughout the Qur'an as the signature of salvation:

> *"Then whoso follows My guidance, no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve."* (Qur'an 2:38)

> *"Lo! The friends of God: no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve."* (Qur'an 10:62)

> *"Truly those who say, 'Our Lord is God,' and then remain steadfast — the angels descend upon them: 'Fear not, nor grieve, but hear glad tidings of the Garden which you were promised.'"* (Qur'an 41:30)

The recurrence is not stylistic habit. It identifies the believer's condition in this life as one in which the two great temporal anxieties — anticipation and regret — are progressively disarmed by trust in God. The Qur'an's psychology elsewhere confirms this: the human being is described as *"anxious"* by nature, *"when evil touches him, fretful; and when good touches him, withholding"* (70:19–21), and the cure is precisely the state of worship and trust that the verse from 41:30 describes.

**"Surely We Shall Bring Him Back to You" — Promise as Anchor**
