The command is followed by a promise: *innā rāddūhu ilayki*. The Arabic is emphatic — *innā* with the active participle *rāddūhu*, "We are indeed returning him." The form conveys certainty and imminence, as though the return were already in progress at the moment of the command.

This is the structural key to the whole story. The command without the promise would be unbearable; the promise without the command would be meaningless. Together they constitute the pattern of every test in the believer's life: an instruction that appears to destroy what is loved, and a promise that what is loved will be restored.

The commentators note that the restoration was not merely to *any* safety but to her: *ilayki — to you*. She was to have her child back, in her arms, as her nursing son, and — as verse 13 will make explicit — with the added gift of knowing, personally and experientially, that God's promise is true.

**"And Make Him One of the Messengers" — The Promise Beyond the Promise**

The verse ends with a promise greater than the one the mother was asking for. She feared for a child's life; she is told she will raise a messenger of God.

The classical scholars pause over this for a reason worth stating. The promise is given *before* Moses has done anything to deserve it, and it is given to the mother as an act of pure grace. It is therefore one of the texts cited in the discussion of whether prophethood can be a "reward" — a topic that arises in this sūrah again at verse 14, where *wisdom and knowledge* are given to Moses and the text adds *Thus do We recompense the virtuous*. Al-Rāzī's careful resolution, repeated by later scholars, is that the *gifts* that prepare a person for prophethood may be described as rewards for his prior righteousness, but the office of prophethood itself is not earned — it is pure divine choice. God said to His final Prophet:

> *"Say: 'Had God willed, I would not have recited it unto you, nor would He have made it known to you.'"* (Qur'an 10:16)

For Moses' mother, the practical effect of this final clause is enormous. She is being told that the child she must throw into a river is not merely going to survive; he is going to matter more than she can imagine. The instruction is thereby transformed from a sentence into a vocation. Every fear she feels is now set against a destiny she has been allowed to glimpse.

**Motherhood in the Qur'an: The Verse in Context**

The tradition has noticed that the Qur'an treats mothers with a specificity and tenderness unusual in ancient literature, and this passage is one of its strongest instances. The Qur'an will describe her state again in verse 10 with a phrase that has become proverbial — *the heart of Moses' mother became empty* — and it will describe the reunion in verse 13 as a healing of her eye and heart.

The broader Qur'anic context confirms the elevated station. The command to honor parents is joined to the command to worship God alone in no fewer than six places:

> *"And We have enjoined upon man [care] for his parents. His mother bore him in weakness upon weakness, and his weaning is in two years: be grateful to Me and to your parents."* (Qur'an 31:14)

And the famous ḥadīth, reported by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Hurayrah, places the mother at the summit of human relationship:

> *A man came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and said, "O Messenger of God, who is most deserving of my good company?" He said, "Your mother." The man asked, "Then who?" He said, "Your mother." He asked, "Then who?" He said, "Your mother." He asked, "Then who?" He said, "Then your father."* (al-Bukhārī 5971; Muslim 2548)

Al-Qurṭubī draws a further connection in his commentary on this passage between Moses' mother and Mary, mother of Jesus: both women received angelic or divine communication concerning a child who would be a sign to the world; both faced a terrifying and solitary trial connected to that child; and both were consoled directly by God. The parallel is developed explicitly in the Qur'an's own account of Mary's labor:

> *"And the pains of childbirth drove her to the trunk of a palm tree... Then he called to her from beneath her: 'Grieve not; thy Lord has placed a stream beneath thee.'"* (Qur'an 19:23–24)

**Living the Verse: The Logic of Risking What You Love for God**

The practical principle this verse teaches is one of the hardest in the religious life, and it is not limited to mothers. It is the principle that the way to preserve what you love may be to place it in God's hands through an action that looks like loss.

The prophets supply the cases. Abraham was commanded to leave his wife and infant in a barren valley:

> *"Our Lord, I have settled some of my offspring in a valley without cultivation, by Thy Sacred House."* (Qur'an 14:37)

and the result was Zamzam and Makkah. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ left his home city under threat of death and was promised return (v. 85 of this sūrah). Joseph's father risked sending his beloved son with his brothers, saying:

> *"God is the best of guardians, and He is the Most Merciful of the merciful."* (Qur'an 12:64)

In each case, the act is not recklessness. It is obedience to a known instruction joined to a known promise, executed while the heart trembles. That last detail matters: the Qur'an does not present Moses' mother as serene. Verse 10 will tell us plainly that her heart emptied with terror and that she came within a breath of betraying the secret. Faith, in this sūrah's account, is not the absence of fear; it is obedience in the presence of fear, sustained by a promise.

The modern application is direct. Every believer eventually faces a version of this command: to give up a job, a relationship, a plan, or a security for the sake of obedience, with no visible guarantee except that God has said *surely We shall bring it back*. The sūrah does not promise that the thing will be returned in the form it was given. It promises something better and stranger — that the lost thing returns transfigured, and that the one who risked it discovers in the process that God keeps His word.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:8

> **Then the House of Pharaoh picked him up, such that he would become unto them an enemy and a sorrow. Truly Pharaoh and Hāmān and their hosts were sinners.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Ironic Sentence in the Qur'an**

*Fa-iltaqaṭahu ālu Firʿawna li-yakūna lahum ʿaduwwan wa-ḥazanan* — *Then the House of Pharaoh picked him up, such that he would become unto them an enemy and a sorrow.* The commentators linger over this sentence because it is, in the plainest terms, a description of a catastrophe that does not know it is a catastrophe. The most powerful household in the known world picks a floating basket out of the water and carries it inside — and the Qur'an tells the reader, before the participants know anything, what the baby will become to them.

The verb *iltaqaṭa* means to pick up something found by chance, something one comes upon unexpectedly — a stray object, a foundling. The word carries no dignity. The future enemy of Egypt entered the palace the way a piece of driftwood enters a room.

**"Picked Him Up" — The Household, Not the Man**

Note that the subject is *ālu Firʿawna*, the House or family of Pharaoh, not Pharaoh himself. The classical commentators infer from this that it was the women of the household — and specifically, according to the biblical account and the reports transmitted in tafsīr, Pharaoh's wife — who found the child and brought him in. This prepares immediately for verse 9, where the wife speaks.

The phrase also establishes an important fact about the story's social setting: Moses was not raised in a private residence but in the *royal household*, which in the ancient Near East meant the center of administration, religion, and military command. He would have had access to the education, the language, the court etiquette, and the political intelligence of the ruling class. The Qur'an will use the fruits of that upbringing later: Moses' confident address to Pharaoh, his understanding of what arguments would reach the court, and his ability to confront the magicians on their own ground all presuppose a man who was formed inside the system he was sent to challenge.

This is a recurring pattern in the Qur'anic treatment of providence. Joseph was raised in the household of the Egyptian official who bought him (12:21). Moses was raised in the household of the tyrant who ordered his death. God places His servants where they need to be formed, and the placement is rarely comfortable.

**"Such That He Would Become" — The Grammar of Consequence**

The particle *li* in *li-yakūna* is the subject of one of the classical disputes in Arabic grammar, and the dispute has theological content. Taken as *lām al-ʿāqibah*, the "lām of consequence," it means: they picked him up, and *the outcome* was that he became their enemy and sorrow. Taken as *lām al-taʿlīl*, the "lām of purpose," it would mean: they picked him up *in order that* he become their enemy.

The overwhelming majority of commentators adopt the first reading, and for a reason that is theologically decisive. To say that Pharaoh picked up Moses *in order to* produce his own ruin would make Pharaoh the agent of a divine purpose and would compromise the justice of his punishment. The second reading is not, however, entirely discarded; some grammarians, including al-Zamakhsharī, note that purpose-language may be used loosely to stress how inevitable and how divinely governed the outcome was — the way one might say of a man who dug a pit for another and fell into it, "he dug it so that he would fall in."

What both readings preserve is the doctrine this sūrah is built upon: God's planning runs beneath human planning and brings about results the planners did not intend, and this in no way removes their responsibility for what they chose. The theologians call the distinction *irādah* (will) versus *riḍā* (pleasure): God willed the outcome without being pleased with Pharaoh's act, and Pharaoh remains fully guilty of every murder. Al-Rāzī states the creedal rule tersely: whatever God wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass, but His will is not His love, and His decree is not His approval.

**"An Enemy and a Sorrow" — Two Words, Two Kinds of Ruin**

The Qur'an uses two nouns, and the commentators distinguish them carefully.

*ʿAduww* — enemy — describes Moses' eventual outward opposition: he would stand against Pharaoh's claim to divinity, demand the release of an enslaved people, and bring signs that overturned Egypt's religious order.

*Ḥazan* — sorrow, grief, distress — describes the inward consequence. The word is etymologically linked to *ḥuzn*, grief, and the scholars note its use here as a description of what Moses' presence eventually cost the Egyptians: the plagues, the loss of the firstborn, the drowning of the army, and the collapse of a civilizational order.

The pairing is psychologically astute. A tyrant can absorb an external enemy; enemies are what armies and walls are for. What he cannot absorb is sorrow — the steady accumulation of loss, humiliation, fear, and dread that hollows out a reign from within. The Qur'an's account of Pharaoh's last days shows exactly this: the magicians defecting, the elite grumbling, the plagues compounding, the population turning. The sūrah names this cumulative ruin in a single word placed at the end of the sentence, and the reader knows it will be collected in full at verse 40.

**"Truly Pharaoh and Hāmān and Their Hosts Were Sinners" — Guilt Affirmed at the Moment of Rescue**

The verse closes by naming the guilt of the household that has just performed an act of mercy. This is an important structural device, and it deserves attention.

The natural human response to the scene — a baby saved from a river by a royal family — is relief, even gratitude toward the rescuers. The Qur'an forecloses that reading instantly: *truly they were sinners* (*khāṭiʾīn*). The rescue does not cancel the crimes; the woman who intercedes is good, but the system she lives within is guilty; and the child who is saved is being saved *from* the household that saves him.

The term *khāṭiʾīn* is from *khaṭiʾa*, to err, to sin deliberately. The commentators note that the Qur'an uses it here rather than a stronger term because the immediate context is their culpability in the oppression of Israel generally, with the specific crime of Pharaoh's own unbelief named separately later. The verse reminds the reader that an oppressor who performs an occasional kindness is still an oppressor, and that gratitude for a particular act must never be confused with approval of a system.

This is a genuinely difficult moral discrimination, and it is one that modern life demands constantly. The Qur'an's model here is precise: name the good act as good (as the tradition does with Pharaoh's wife, who is counted among the believing women), and name the system as guilty. Moses himself will live out this balance: he grows up in Pharaoh's house, presumably with real affection for the woman who raised him and, according to the reports, with genuine status there — and yet when the moment comes, he leaves it and stands against it. Loyalty to persons does not entail loyalty to the structure that formed them.

**The Hidden Providence of Adverse Placement**

The verse teaches a doctrine that the tradition has named *luṭf* — the subtle kindness of God — and has elaborated at length: God arranges the circumstances of a servant's life, including the painful ones, in such a way that the servant is brought toward the good for which he was made.

Consider the mechanics in this story. Pharaoh's decree forces the child into the river. The river, which was meant to be his grave, becomes his transport. The transport delivers him to the palace. The palace, which was the source of the decree, becomes his protector and his school. And his mother, who had to give him up, is paid to raise him. Every element of the catastrophe has been repurposed.

The commentators draw out the lesson with a formula that recurs in the tradition: *how often does God bring about relief through the very thing that was feared, and hardship through the very thing that was desired.* The Prophet ﷺ gave the principle its classic expression:

> *"How wonderful is the affair of the believer! Truly, all of his affair is good, and that is for no one but the believer. If something good befalls him, he is grateful, and that is good for him; and if something harmful befalls him, he is patient, and that is good for him."* (Muslim 2999)

And in the ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me."* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

**The Recitation of History as Comfort for the Oppressed**

Finally, note the audience for whom this verse was first recited. It was revealed in Makkah, to a small, persecuted, economically boycotted, physically assaulted community, and to a Prophet whose own uncle was protecting him while the ruling clans plotted to kill him. The message embedded in the verse would have landed with precision: the machinery that is currently arranged against you has been arranged before, against a better man than you, by a stronger power than yours — and the child the machinery was built to destroy was raised inside the palace of the man who built it.

The sūrah will make the parallel explicit at verse 85, promising the Prophet ﷺ return to the city that expelled him. But the principle is already fully present here, in a basket drifting down a river toward the only place in Egypt where it was safe.

For the modern reader, the verse also settles a question that troubles many people: if God is just, why does evil machinery so often appear to work? The answer the verse gives is not a theory but a narrative. The machinery works right up to the moment it produces the opposite of what it was built for, and the moment of its apparent triumph is often the moment of its defeat. That is not optimism; it is an observation about how the world is governed, offered by the One who governs it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:9

> **And the wife of Pharaoh said, "A comfort for me and for you! Slay him not; it may be that he will bring us some benefit, or that we may take him as a son." Yet they were unaware.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Woman Stands Between a Tyrant and a Basket**

The drama now moves indoors. The basket has been brought into the palace, and the first voice we hear is not the king's but his wife's: *qurrat ʿaynin lī wa-laka* — *a comfort of the eye for me and for you*. The commentators note the speed of her response. Before Pharaoh could give an order, she spoke; and the tradition reads her words as an intercession that saved a life.

This is one of the most remarkable moments in the Qur'an, and the tradition has honored it accordingly. A woman, in the innermost chamber of the most absolute monarchy of the age, defies the standing policy of the state — a policy enforced by armed patrols and mass infanticide — on behalf of an unknown infant belonging to the despised population. She does it in a single sentence, in front of her husband, at the moment of decision. The Prophet ﷺ would later name the spiritual rank of such women in general terms:

> *"Many men attained perfection, but none of the women attained perfection except Āsiyah, the wife of Pharaoh, and Maryam, the daughter of ʿImrān."* (al-Bukhārī 3411; Muslim 2431)

And in the ḥadīth of the Ascension, the Prophet ﷺ described being given a pleasant fragrance and being told:

> *"This is the fragrance of the hairdresser of Pharaoh's daughter and her children"* — a woman who, when combs of iron were heated and brought to her and her children because they would not renounce God, held to her faith, and whose child spoke from the cradle as a sign. (Reported by al-Tirmidhī and al-Ṭabarānī; the account is well known in the commentaries)

Āsiyah — for that is the name the tradition preserves for her — thus stands in the Qur'an as the permanent refutation of two claims that tyrants always make: that resistance is impossible, and that proximity to power requires complicity with it.

**"A Comfort of the Eye" — The Arabic Idiom of Deepest Delight**

The phrase *qurrat ʿayn* is one of the most beautiful in Arabic, and it is worth unpacking. The root *q-r-r* means to be cool, settled, still. A *qurrat ʿayn*, literally "a cooling of the eye," is something that brings such relief and delight that the eye — which in Arabic idiom is the organ most associated with longing, weeping, and strain — becomes cool and still. One weeps until the eye is hot; joy cools it.

The Qur'an uses the idiom in its highest register for the reward of the righteous:

> *"No soul knows what joy is kept hidden for them, as a recompense for what they used to do."* (Qur'an 32:17)

and for the reuniting of families:

> *"Those who say, 'Our Lord, grant us from our spouses and our offspring a comfort of the eye, and make us imams for the reverent.'"* (Qur'an 25:74)

and, in a striking parallel to this verse, for the return of Joseph to his father:

> *"And when the bearer of glad tidings came, he cast the shirt over his face, and he was restored to sight."* (Qur'an 12:96)

The wife of Pharaoh uses for a foundling the very phrase the Qur'an uses for Paradise and for the reunion of the faithful. There is a deliberate irony here that the commentators enjoy: the thing she perceives with such clarity — that this child is a joy and not a threat — is the thing her husband's entire security apparatus was constructed to prevent him from seeing.

**Two Arguments, Ascending in Boldness**

Her intercession contains two distinct pleas, and they are arranged from the safer to the bolder.

The first is *slay him not; it may be that he will bring us some benefit*. This is a utilitarian argument addressed to a utilitarian ruler: the child may be useful. It costs nothing to keep him. The argument is pitched at Pharaoh's level — it does not appeal to mercy but to advantage. The commentators note the rhetorical wisdom in this. She does not open by challenging his theology or his policy; she opens by asking him to suspend a killing for which there is no pressing reason.

The second plea is *or that we may take him as a son* — an argument that goes to the heart of dynastic desire. A royal house without a male heir was a matter of state, and the adoption of a child into the royal family was a known practice in the ancient Near East. By offering Moses as a son, she proposes not merely to spare him but to elevate him into the line of succession.

This is the breathtaking part. The woman is asking the man who ordered the death of every Israelite boy to adopt an Israelite boy as his heir. And she gets her way. The sūrah does not record Pharaoh's reply to this verse, because the outcome is reported in the next: the child stays.

**The Report of Pharaoh's Reply and Its Theological Meaning**

The commentaries transmit an exchange that is not in the Qur'an but has become part of the story's reception: that Pharaoh answered, *"A comfort for you, but for me, no"* — accepting the child's presence while refusing to share his wife's delight. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both transmit the gloss, along with a striking accompanying report attributed to the Prophet ﷺ: that had Pharaoh agreed with his wife's hopeful assessment of the child, God would have guided him as He guided her.

The scholars treat this report as an illustration of a principle, not as a historical guarantee, and the principle is one the tradition holds firmly: guidance is offered at particular moments through particular doors, and a single sincere word of agreement with the truth can be the hinge on which a whole life turns. The converse is also taught: the man who refuses an offered good at the moment it is offered may not be offered it again in the same way. The Qur'an states the mechanism generally:

> *"And when they deviated, God made their hearts deviate."* (Qur'an 61:5)

This does not mean God punishes a first refusal with a second refusal arbitrarily; it means that habitual refusal becomes character, and character becomes destiny. Pharaoh's reply, if the report is sound, is the first small turning of a key that will be turned many more times until it locks.

Note also the gendered contrast that the tradition draws from this episode. The Qur'an pairs Āsiyah's faith with the faith of the "believer from the people of Pharaoh" who concealed his belief (40:28) and with the magicians who believed on the spot and accepted torture (7:120–126). In every case, the people closest to Pharaoh's power are the ones who can see what Pharaoh cannot — a consistent Qur'anic point about how arrogance blinds.

**"Yet They Were Unaware" — Ignorance at Three Levels**

The verse closes: *wa-hum lā yashʿurūn*. The verb *shaʿara* originally means to perceive through the senses, and in Qur'anic usage it means to be conscious, to realize, to have awareness of what is happening.

The commentators offer several referents, and they are complementary rather than exclusive.

They were unaware of the *fate awaiting them*: that the child they were preserving would be the instrument of their destruction. This is the reading that connects directly to verse 8 — *such that he would become unto them an enemy and a sorrow*.

They were unaware of *whom they had taken in*: that this was an Israelite child, the very category their policy targeted, and that the woman they were about to hire as his nurse was his own mother.

And they were unaware of the deeper truth: that God's plan was executing itself inside their palace. This last is theologically the richest, because it identifies the precise nature of the divine stratagem. God did not defeat Pharaoh by overwhelming him from outside; He defeated him by working *within* his own household, using his own decree, his own river, his own palace, and his own affection for his wife.

Al-Rāzī observes that this is the consistent Qur'anic pattern in the punishment of the arrogant: they are destroyed by the very means through which they sought security. Korah is destroyed by the treasure he hoarded (vv. 76–81). Pharaoh is destroyed by the water over which his empire was built (v. 40). The wealthy man of the two gardens is destroyed by his own orchard (18:32–42). The pattern is stated generally:

> *"And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 3:54)

**Adoption in Islam: What the Verse Establishes and What It Does Not**

Because the verse speaks of taking Moses *as a son*, the jurists discussed it in the context of adoption (*tabannī*), and the discussion is worth summarizing because it clarifies an important point of Islamic law.

Before Islam, Arab society practiced full legal adoption: an adopted son took the family name, inherited, and was treated in all respects as a natural son, including in the rules of marriage. Islam abolished this legal fiction while preserving and elevating the *care* of children. The Qur'an states the rule:

> *"Nor has He made your adopted sons your sons. That is but a saying of your mouths... Call them by [the names of] their fathers; that is more just in the sight of God. And if you know not their fathers, then they are your brothers in religion and your clients."* (Qur'an 33:4–5)

Theologically, the underlying principle is that God does not efface realities. A child has one set of parents, and lineage is a fact, not a social arrangement. Islam's alternative to adoption is *kafālah* — fostering and guardianship — which carries full obligation of care, education, and provision, with none of the legal reassignments that Islam abolished.

The Prophet ﷺ gave fostering a status in the Hereafter that is hard to overstate:

> *"I and the one who sponsors an orphan will be like this in Paradise"* — and he gestured with his forefinger and middle finger, holding them slightly apart. (al-Bukhārī 5304)

The story of Moses in this sūrah sits behind all of this. He was fostered by his enemies and nursed by his mother without either party knowing the full arrangement; and the arrangement was made by God, who knows the true father of every child.

**What the Verse Teaches About Intercession**

There is a final, practical lesson. Āsiyah did not have authority, an army, or a public platform. She had a moment, a place, and a sentence. And the sentence saved a prophet.

The tradition has drawn from this a rule of conduct that the Prophet ﷺ stated plainly:

> *"Whosoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand; if he cannot, then with his tongue; and if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith."* (Muslim 49)

and:

> *"Intercede, and you will be rewarded; and God decrees what He wills upon the tongue of His Prophet."* (al-Bukhārī 1432; Muslim 2627)

That second narration is especially relevant: intercession is commanded even though the outcome belongs to God, because the intercessor's duty is the speaking, not the result. Āsiyah spoke; God decreed. And the child lived to stand before the throne and say *Lord of the worlds*.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:10

> **But the heart of Moses' mother became empty, and she would have disclosed it, had We not fortified her heart, that she might be among the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Vindicates Fear**

If verse 7 presents the faith of Moses' mother as obedience, verse 10 presents it as terror — and the two belong together. Between the command to cast the child into the river and this description of her state, the Qur'an does not hide anything. She had done what she was told, and then her heart collapsed.

*Wa-aṣbaḥa fuʾādu ummi Mūsā fārighan* — *and morning found the heart of Moses' mother empty*. The commentators stress the honesty of this report. The Qur'an is not presenting a saint who never wavered. It is presenting a mother who obeyed and then, in the cold light of morning, could hold nothing in her mind but the child she had let go.

This is one of the most pastorally important verses in the Qur'an, because it establishes that the distress following an act of obedience is not a sign of failed faith. The woman who will be called a believer at the end of this very verse is the woman who nearly broke in the middle of it.

**Two Words for the Heart, and Why Both Appear**

The Arabic uses two different words for the heart in one sentence, and the commentators have mined the distinction.

*Fuʾād* in the first clause. The lexicographers describe it as the heart in its outward, containing, affective dimension — the seat of agitation, perception, and strong emotion. It is the word used where the heart is most exposed:

> *"His heart became hollow with fear"* is how the Qur'an describes the terror of the Resurrection: *"their glance returning not to them, their hearts vacant"* (14:43).

*Qalb* in the second clause, from *q-l-b*, to turn, to overturn. It is so named because it constantly turns and is turned. The lexicographers assign to *qalb* the deeper, more essential sense — the kernel of the person, the organ of intention, cognition, and faith. It is the word the Qur'an uses for the seat of belief and unbelief, and it is the word that appears in the famous ḥadīth:

> *"Truly, in the body there is a morsel of flesh which, if it is sound, the whole body is sound, and if it is corrupt, the whole body is corrupt. Truly, it is the heart."* (al-Bukhārī 52; Muslim 1599)

and in the Prophet's supplication:

> *"O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140; Ibn Mājah 3834 — graded ḥasan)

Put the two together and the verse reads with extraordinary precision: her whole emotional being was emptied (*fuʾād*), and God bound fast her essential core (*qalb*). The storm raged at the surface; the center held.

Al-Rāzī adds a caution against over-systematizing: in the Qur'an the two terms are sometimes interchangeable, and both are used for the seat of understanding. Later Sufi literature built a hierarchy out of the vocabulary — *qalb*, *fuʾād*, *sirr* (secret), *rūḥ* (spirit), *khafī*, *akhfā* — mapping the inner life into layers. This is legitimate as spiritual psychology, but the mufassirūn are careful that it is not imposed upon the plain sense of the text.

**"Became Empty" — Four Readings, One Experience**

The commentators record several senses of *fārighan*, and they describe a single experience from different angles.

*Fārigh* means empty. Her heart was emptied of everything except the memory of her son — no other thought, no other concern could occupy her. Al-Ṭabarī transmits this as the primary reading: her heart became empty of all else through her preoccupation with Moses.

A second reading, also from al-Ṭabarī, is that she forgot — or was made to forget — the promise God had given her in verse 7. This is theologically the most difficult reading, and the scholars handle it carefully. It does not mean she disbelieved; it means the promise receded from her active consciousness under the pressure of the trial, so that what remained was raw maternal terror. The phenomenon is recognizable to anyone who has known panic: what you know most certainly can become functionally inaccessible in an hour of crisis.

A third reading, favored by al-Rāzī, takes the word in its idiomatic sense of mental distraction: she was beside herself, unable to think — the Arabic usage in which a person overcome by fear is described as having an empty heart. He compares the usage of 14:43, where the terror of the Resurrection empties the hearts of the guilty.

A fourth, grammatical reading is given by al-Qurṭubī: the verb *aṣbaḥa* can carry its literal temporal sense of "to enter the morning," so that the verse means *morning found the heart of Moses' mother empty* — that is, she had endured the day of separation somehow, but with the dawn, when the reality was unavoidable and the night's distractions gone, her heart gave way. The same temporal nuance appears at verse 18 (*morning found him in the city, fearful and vigilant*) and again at verse 82.

**"She Would Have Disclosed It" — How Close the Story Came to Ending**

The clause is stark: *in kādat la-tubdī bihi* — *she very nearly disclosed it*. The verb *b-d-y* means to reveal, to make apparent, to let out. What would she have disclosed? The commentators agree: that Moses was her son, and that she had put him in the river. Al-Qurṭubī adds a reported phrasing — she would have cried out "O my son!" — an exclamation that would have identified her immediately to a palace actively searching for Israelite boys.

The commentators do not treat this as a sin. Al-Rāzī is explicit: this was the outcry of overwhelming maternal grief, not a failure of faith in any culpable sense. The classical discussions of *ʿiṣmah* ( prophetic infallibility) concern the prophets, not their families, and even for the prophets the tradition distinguishes between a lapse of perfect composure and an act of disobedience.

The narrative point, however, is enormous: the plan came within one cry of collapse. If she had spoken, the palace would have known, the child would have been killed, and the entire sequence of verses 5–9 would have come to nothing by human reckoning. The sūrah is showing the reader how thin the thread is, and how God holds it.

**"Had We Not Fortified Her Heart" — *Rabaṭnā* and the Discipline of Steadiness**

The rescue is described with the verb *rabaṭnā ʿalā qalbihā* — *We bound fast her heart*. The root *r-b-ṭ* means to tie, to bind, to make firm. It is the same root that gives *ribāṭ*, the term for holding a frontier position against attack — and the imagery is apt. A heart under siege was tied down so that it would not bolt.

The same verb appears at a comparable moment in the story of the Prophet ﷺ in the cave:

> *"Then God sent down His serenity upon him and supported him with hosts you did not see."* (Qur'an 9:40)

and in the Qur'an's description of what steadies a believer facing odds:

> *"O you who believe, when you meet a host, stand firm and remember God much, that you may prosper."* (Qur'an 8:45)

The theological point the scholars draw is that steadfastness (*thabāt*) is not a natural possession of the human being; it is given. The human heart is by nature unstable — which is precisely why it is named *qalb*, the thing that turns. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The heart of the son of Adam turns more than a boiling pot."* (Reported by Aḥmad and al-Ṭabarānī)

and he frequently supplicated:

> *"O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."*

That the Prophet ﷺ himself sought what Moses' mother was given is the strongest possible evidence that firmness is a gift requested rather than a trait owned.

**"That She Might Be Among the Believers" — The Purpose Clause**

The verse ends with *li-takūna mina al-muʾminīn*. Why was her heart fortified? So that she would be among the believers. The commentators note the phrasing: she was not merely prevented from speaking; she was brought to a state of faith.

Two readings are recorded, and both are true. The fortification preserved her faith at a moment when terror threatened to undo it — that is, it kept her within the ranks of the believers by not letting panic carry her into an act that would have betrayed the trust. And the fortification *deepened* her faith, because surviving such an hour with one's trust intact produces a certainty that cannot be taught.

The second reading connects directly to verse 13, where the purpose of the entire ordeal is stated: *that she might know that God's Promise is true*. The sequence is therefore: command (v. 7), obedience, terror (v. 10), divine reinforcement, deliverance (v. 13), and then the knowledge of certainty. Faith that passes through that sequence is of a different order from faith that has never been tested. The Qur'an states the general law:

> *"Do people think that they will be left to say, 'We believe,' and they will not be tried?"* (Qur'an 29:2)

and the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The most severely tested of people are the prophets, then the next best, then the next best. A person is tested according to his religion; if he is firm in his religion, his trial is increased."* (al-Tirmidhī 2398; Ibn Mājah 4023 — graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

**The Mother and the Messenger: What Moses Inherited**

The story's economy is worth noting. God did not merely preserve Moses; He preserved him *through* his mother's faith. The woman who was told to throw her child into a river is the woman through whom that child's earliest formation took place — and, by the device of the wet-nurse described in verse 12, she was paid by the state to raise him in the faith of his own people.

The tradition has always drawn from this a principle about mothers and the formation of conscience. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Every child is born upon the natural constitution [fiṭrah]; it is his parents who make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian."* (al-Bukhārī 1358; Muslim 2658)

Moses' mother had, by the arrangement of Providence, a period of unrestricted influence over her son at precisely the age when that influence is greatest — the nursing years. The Qur'an nowhere says what she taught him; it does not need to. The reader has seen her faith at its breaking point and its restoration, and he knows what the child heard in those years.

**Living the Verse: When Obedience and Panic Occupy the Same Heart**

Finally, this verse should be read by everyone who has obeyed God at real cost and then felt the bottom fall out.

The sequence of this story corrects two opposite errors. Against the error of despair, it says: the panic you feel after you have obeyed is not proof that your obedience was a mistake. Moses' mother did exactly what God told her to do, and she still spent a night in agony. Against the error of presumption, it says: the fact that you obeyed once does not guarantee that you will hold steady in the morning. She needed a second grace — *We fortified her heart* — to complete what the first grace had begun.

The practical counsel that follows is the counsel of the whole tradition: obey the instruction, and then ask, continuously and without embarrassment, for the firmness to remain standing after the instruction has cost you something. Obedience is the act of a moment; steadfastness is the gift of a lifetime. The sūrah will show in verse 13 how the gift arrives — not as an explanation of why the trial happened, but as a child returned to his mother's arms.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:11

> **And she said to his sister, "Follow him." So she watched him from afar; yet they were unaware.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Human Instrument in the Plan**

The story's rescue operation now acquires its second human agent. Moses' mother has done the hardest thing asked of her; in verse 11 she does the most intelligent thing available to her. She cannot go to the palace herself — an Israelite woman loitering near the royal household during a purge of Israelite children would be arrested or killed, and would certainly expose everything. So she sends a child.

*Wa-qālat li-ukhtihi quṣṣīhi* — *and she said to his sister, "Follow him."* The verb is *qaṣṣīhi*, from the same root *q-ṣ-ṣ* that gives the sūrah its name: follow his tracks, trace his footsteps, keep him in sight without being seen. The word choice is a quiet reminder of why this chapter is called al-Qaṣaṣ — the whole sūrah is, in a sense, a tracking of the footsteps of God through history, and here a young girl is doing it literally.

The commentators note that the mother's faith is now active rather than merely endurance. A person in despair does not send scouts. By dispatching her daughter, she is cooperating with the promise of verse 7 — *surely We shall bring him back to you* — as though preparing for its fulfillment.

**Who Was the Sister?**

The Qur'an does not name her. The tradition, drawing on transmitted reports, generally identifies her with Miriam (Maryam), the figure who appears prominently in the Exodus narrative and whom the Qur'an refers to elsewhere as the sister of Aaron — a designation that has itself generated discussion among scholars, since the Qur'an uses "sister of Aaron" for Mary the mother of Jesus as well (19:28). The classical resolution of that latter question, given by al-Qurṭubī and others and supported by a ḥadīth recorded by Muslim, is that the Israelites named their children after their righteous predecessors, so that "sister of Aaron" can mean "of the line of Aaron" or "named for Aaron." The Prophet ﷺ said, when told that people called Mary the sister of Aaron:

> *"They used to name themselves after their prophets and the righteous among those who came before them."* (Muslim 2135)

Here in al-Qaṣaṣ, no such puzzle arises: the girl is Moses' sister by birth, and her role in the narrative is definite even though her name is not given.

**The Strategy of the Unnoticed**

Two clauses in the verse describe the operation, and together they form a complete account of how the vulnerable outwit the powerful.

*Fa-baṣurat bihi ʿan junubin* — *so she watched him from afar*. The verb *baṣurat* implies perception with insight and attention, not a casual glance: she observed, she tracked, she understood what she was seeing. The phrase *ʿan junub*, literally "from a side" or "from a distance," indicates that she kept herself removed — present enough to observe, distant enough to be unremarkable.

*Wa-hum lā yashʿurūn* — *yet they were unaware*. This is the second occurrence of the clause in three verses (v. 9 and here), and the repetition is deliberate. In verse 9 it described the palace's ignorance of the destiny it had just carried through its doors. Here it describes the palace's ignorance of the surveillance taking place under its windows. The most absolute regime in the ancient world, with informers, patrols, and a standing order to kill infants, was being watched by a child whom nobody recorded.

The technical term the commentators use is *takhfī* — concealment as a legitimate strategy. The tradition is unanimous that hiding one's true situation from an oppressor is not deception in the blameworthy sense; it is the prudence that God Himself instructed here. The Prophet ﷺ used concealment on the night of the Hijrah, taking an unfamiliar route, hiding in a cave, and having ʿĀmir ibn Fuhayrah graze sheep over the tracks. The principle in Islamic law is that necessity (*ḍarūrah*) permits what would otherwise be forbidden, and that the preservation of life is one of the five universal objectives (*maqāṣid*) of the Law.

**The Social Insight: Power Is Watched by Those It Does Not See**

There is a durable social observation embedded in this verse, and the commentators draw it out. Tyrannical power is, by its nature, observed from only one direction. Those who hold it look down and outward — at rivals, at threats, at the visible. What they do not see are the people they have deemed beneath notice: the women at the wells, the children in the street, the servants in the corridor, the family of the man they killed.

The Qur'an has a consistent interest in these unnoticed observers. The believer from the people of Pharaoh *concealed his faith* (40:28) and was inside the court the whole time. The ants speak in Solomon's army's path (27:18). The hoopoe carries intelligence from a distant kingdom that Solomon did not know (27:22). And here, a girl follows a basket along a riverbank while the machinery of state rolls past her.

The lesson for the reader is two-sided. For those who suffer under power: you are not as invisible to God as you are to Pharaoh, and the unnoticed are often the instruments through which deliverance is arranged. For those who hold power: the people you do not see are real, are watching, and — the Qur'an assures us — are being seen.

**Divine Planning Working Through Ordinary Human Means**

Theologically, this verse is a textbook case of what the tradition calls *al-asbāb* — secondary causes. God had promised in verse 7, *surely We shall bring him back to you*. That promise was absolute and required no human assistance for its fulfillment. And yet the fulfillment comes through a chain of wholly ordinary human actions: a mother's instruction, a girl's walk along the river, her observation, her report, and the suggestion she will make in verse 12.

Why does God work this way? The classical answer, developed at length by al-Ghazālī and by the Ashʿarī theologians, is that the use of means is itself part of what God has decreed, and that He has made the world a place in which human agency is real and meaningful. To refuse the means while claiming to trust God is not piety; the Prophet ﷺ explicitly rejected it. When a Bedouin left his camel untethered saying that he trusted in God, the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Tie it and then trust in God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2517 — graded ḥasan by some, and the meaning is supported by authentic narrations)

Moses' mother, therefore, was not lacking in trust when she sent her daughter to follow the basket. She was exhibiting it in its proper form: taking every available means while resting the outcome entirely on the One who promised.

**The Sister's Role in the Reunion**

The practical importance of her mission becomes clear in the next verse. When Moses refuses all the wet-nurses the palace brings, it is the sister who steps forward with a suggestion. She could only have made that suggestion because she had followed the basket, learned where the child had been taken, and — critically — knew where to find her own mother.

The narrative logic is worth admiring. The mother obeyed God by letting the child go. The daughter obeyed her mother by tracking him. The child's refusal to nurse is arranged by God. And the sister's knowledge, acquired by walking along a riverbank, becomes the hinge on which the whole plan turns.

The tradition has seen in this a model of how families cooperate in righteousness. Each person does what is within her power: the mother prays and endures, the daughter acts and observes, and God disposes. No one in the story does everything, and no one does nothing.

**"Follow Him": A Discipline of Attention**

There is a final, quieter reading that the spiritual commentators have offered, and it is worth recording because it is consonant with the text even if it is not its primary sense. The command *quṣṣīhi* — follow his tracks — describes the discipline of attention that the whole religious life requires. Guidance is rarely given as a map; it is given as a series of footprints that must be tracked from one moment to the next.

The Qur'an uses the same root for a command given to the Prophet ﷺ concerning his own knowledge of earlier messengers:

> *"Those are they whom God has guided; so follow their guidance."* (Qur'an 6:90)

And it uses the root for the act of narrating — *naḥnu naquṣṣu ʿalayka* (12:3) — because narration and tracking are the same activity: you walk behind something and tell others where it went.

For the reader of this sūrah, then, the little girl along the riverbank is a picture of the student of revelation. She does not control the basket. She does not know where it will stop. She simply does not take her eyes off it — and when the moment for action comes, she is the only person present who knows what to do.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:12

> **And We forbade him to be suckled by foster mothers before that; so she said, "Shall I direct you to the people of a house who will take care of him for you and treat him with good will?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal That Broke the Lock**

The crisis is resolved by a detail that, on its face, is domestic rather than political: the baby will not nurse. *Wa-ḥarramnā ʿalayhi al-marāḍiʿa min qablu* — *and We forbade him to be suckled by foster mothers before that*.

The verb *ḥarramnā* is strong and significant. It is the language of prohibition, of a barrier placed. God did not merely arrange that the nurses were unsuitable; He *made forbidden* to the child every breast except his mother's. The commentators note that the Qur'an ascribes the act directly to God, because this is the hinge of the entire rescue, and the Qur'an wants the reader to know who arranged it.

The mechanism itself is utterly ordinary. An infant refuses to feed; the palace tries one nurse after another; each attempt fails; the household grows anxious. Nothing supernatural is visible. And yet this small refusal accomplished what no argument, petition, or protest could have: it drove the palace to *ask for help*, and it created an opening in which a suggestion could be made.

The tradition has drawn from this a rule about how divine intervention typically appears in history. It usually does not appear at all. It looks like a coincidence, a stubborn detail, an unexplained failure in a well-run system. The believer who sees only the surface says "the baby wouldn't feed." The believer who sees the depth says *We forbade him*. Both describe the same event, and the Qur'an supplies the true description of it.

**"The People of a House" — The Sister's Careful Wording**

*Hal adullukum ʿalā ahli baytin yakfulūnahu lakum wa-hum lahu nāṣiḥūn* — *Shall I direct you to the people of a house who will take care of him for you and treat him with good will?*

The commentators admire the phrasing, and there is much to admire. She does not say "my mother." She does not say "an Israelite woman." She does not name anyone at all. She asks a question rather than making a statement, and she offers a *household* rather than a person.

Each choice is protective. A question cannot be rejected as easily as an assertion, because it invites the listener to become the decision-maker. A household suggests reliability and permanence rather than a lone hireling. And the phrase *for you* places the arrangement at their service, not hers.

She then closes with the clause that makes the offer irresistible: *wa-hum lahu nāṣiḥūn* — *and they will treat him with good will, they will be sincere to him*. The word *naṣīḥah* is one of the most comprehensive in the Islamic moral vocabulary. It means sincere, disinterested good counsel and care — desiring another's genuine welfare with no private angle. The Prophet ﷺ made it the definition of the religion itself:

> *"The religion is sincere good counsel (naṣīḥah)."* They said, "To whom, O Messenger of God?" He said, *"To God, and to His Book, and to His Messenger, and to the leaders of the Muslims and their common people."* (Muslim 55)

The sister's promise was therefore not merely that a woman would feed the child, but that the child would be *loved* by those who fed him — and this was the one thing Pharaoh's household could not buy. The palace could provide milk; it could not provide a mother.

**The Irony Layered Upon Irony**

Sit with the scene for a moment, because the ironies are stacked several deep.

The palace is desperately seeking a wet-nurse for an Israelite baby they have decided to raise as an Egyptian prince. The child refuses every Egyptian woman offered to him. A girl appears — unremarkable, unnoticed (*yet they were unaware*, v. 11) — and offers to bring a household that will care for him. The palace accepts. The household is the child's own. The woman who is hired to nurse the son of Pharaoh's house is the woman against whose son Pharaoh's decree was issued.

And then the detail that completes the circle: she is paid for it. The state that was spending resources to exterminate Israelite boys was now paying an Israelite mother to raise one of them in her own faith.

The tradition has relished this, and the classical commentators record the reports with obvious enjoyment. But the theological content is serious: God's governance does not merely defeat the oppressor; it converts the oppressor's own apparatus into the instrument of his undoing. Pharaoh funded the prophet who would destroy his claim to divinity. This is the meaning of the hadeeth qudsee in which God says:

> *"I am as My servant thinks of Me... and if he comes to Me walking, I come to him at speed."* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

**The Doctrine of *Kafālah*: Care With Lineage Intact**

The verse is one of the primary texts the jurists cite in their discussions of *kafālah* and *raḍāʿah* — guardianship and fosterage — and a brief account of that law illuminates the verse.

Islamic law distinguishes sharply between two things that other legal systems have historically merged: the *care* of a child and the *legal identity* of a child. Care is obligatory, rewarded, and transferable. Identity is a fact. The Qur'an states the rule directly:

> *"Call them by [the names of] their fathers; that is more just in the sight of God. And if you know not their fathers, then they are your brothers in religion and your clients."* (Qur'an 33:5)

Fosterage (*raḍāʿah*), however, creates a specific and permanent set of marriage prohibitions. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Fosterage prohibits what lineage prohibits."* (al-Bukhārī 2645; Muslim 1444)

That is, a woman who has suckled a child becomes, for purposes of marriage law, a mother to that child, and her children become his siblings. The Qur'an specifies the relationships:

> *"Forbidden to you are your mothers... and your foster-mothers who suckled you, and your sisters through fosterage."* (Qur'an 4:23)

The jurists derived the conditions from the Sunnah: fosterage is established by five or more definite feedings within the first two years, before weaning — the period the Qur'an itself defines:

> *"Mothers shall suckle their children for two full years, for whoever desires to complete the suckling."* (Qur'an 2:233)

Now apply this to the story. Moses was suckled by his own mother, which creates no new relationship because she was already his mother. But the episode is the classical illustration that fostering is a real, structuring bond and not merely an arrangement of convenience — a child is shaped by the breast that fed him, and the Law recognizes that shaping.

**Why the Qur'an Tells Us This Detail**

None of the story's drama requires the detail of the rejected wet-nurses. The narrative could simply have said *and We returned him to his mother*. The Qur'an includes the mechanism, and the commentators give the reason: the point of the sūrah's first half is precisely to show *how* God's promise executed itself through ordinary events.

This is the sūrah's answer to a question every sufferer asks: if God is going to help, why does He not simply help? Why must it be worked out through baskets, refusals, sisters, and job offers? The Qur'an's answer, given in narrative form, is that the world is a place of means and that God's lordship is exercised *through* the fabric of ordinary causation, invisibly, so that faith is required to perceive it and so that human beings remain genuinely responsible for what they do within it.

If a visible miracle had carried Moses home, there would have been nothing for the mother to endure, nothing for the sister to do, and no faith formed in anyone. As it is, every participant acted, suffered, and waited — and the infant's appetite did the rest.

**The Practical Lesson: Small Obstacles as Vehicles of Rescue**

The verse carries an unusual pastoral counsel. Believers routinely pray for the *removal* of obstacles, and the Qur'anic God often answers by *creating* one. A door will not open, an application fails, a plan collapses, an arrangement that looked perfect falls apart — and the believer's instinct is to conclude that God has abandoned the matter.

This verse says otherwise. The baby's refusal to feed was not an obstacle to the rescue; it *was* the rescue. And the palace's frustration was the mechanism by which the mother was summoned.

The tradition expresses this with the maxim that relief often arrives disguised as a setback, and the Prophet ﷺ gave its theological grounding:

> *"Know that victory comes with patience, that relief comes with distress, and that with hardship comes ease."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad — graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

The discernment required is not to welcome every frustration as divine, which would be presumption, but to refrain from concluding that a blocked path means an abandoned promise. Moses' mother had a promise in writing — *surely We shall bring him back to you* — and every subsequent event, including the frightening ones, had to be read in its light.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:13

> **Thus We returned him to his mother, that she might be comforted and not grieve, and that she might know that God's Promise is true. But most of them know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reunion and Its Three Stated Purposes**

*Fa-radadnāhu ilā ummihi* — *Thus We returned him to his mother*. Six Arabic words, and the whole catastrophe of verses 7 through 12 is resolved. The child is back in the arms of the woman who had been told to throw him into a river.

The verse does not narrate the reunion emotionally. It narrates it theologically, giving three purposes, and each one deserves its own attention.

**First Purpose: "That She Might Be Comforted" — *Qurrat ʿAynin* Again**

The phrase is *kay taqarra ʿaynuhā* — literally, *that her eye might be cooled*. It is the same idiom the wife of Pharaoh used in verse 9 when she pleaded for the child's life: *a comfort of the eye for me and for you*.

The repetition is a deliberate literary link that the commentators point out. In verse 9, the phrase was a plea of uncertain outcome, spoken by a woman who could not know what would become of the child. In verse 13, the identical phrase appears as an accomplished fact for the child's real mother. The plea of one woman became the consolation of another, and the same God arranged both.

Al-Qurṭubī draws the further connection with the story of Jacob and Joseph, where the same idiom governs the entire narrative arc. Jacob wept until his eyes turned white with grief (12:84), and the reunion is described in the same vocabulary as this one. In both cases the Qur'an is making the same point: the cooling of the eye — the end of the weeping, the stilling of the longing — is not a small thing in God's economy. It is named as a purpose of history.

There is a tenderness in this that is easy to miss. God could have stated the purpose of Moses' return as the deliverance of Israel, the confrontation with Pharaoh, or the giving of the Torah — all of which were true and vastly more consequential. Instead, the first stated purpose is *that his mother's eye might be cooled*. The comfort of one grieving woman is written into the text as a reason God did what He did.

**Second Purpose: "And Not Grieve" — The Lifting of the Specific Pain**

The second purpose, *wa-lā taḥzan*, restates and resolves the promise of verse 7: *fear not, nor grieve*. There the negation was a promise about the future. Here it is a report about the present. The grief has been lifted, and the verse records that lifting as one of God's purposes in the event.

The commentators note what this establishes about the divine character. God is not presented here as a sovereign who uses human suffering as raw material for a grand design and then moves on, leaving the wounded to their wounds. He returns, and He returns specifically to heal the wound. Theological language in Islam speaks of this as *luṭf* — the subtle kindness of God, His arranging of matters so that His servants reach their good by paths that also mend them.

The pairing of *taqarra ʿaynuhā* and *lā taḥzan* also completes the temporal arc that verse 7 opened: grief for what has passed is removed, and joy in what is present is given. The mother who had both fear and grief negated in promise now has both negations fulfilled in fact.

**Third Purpose: "And That She Might Know That God's Promise Is True" — The Deeper Intention**

The third purpose is the deepest: *wa-li-taʿlama anna waʿda Allāhi ḥaqq*. The promise of verse 7 — *surely We shall bring him back to you and make him one of the messengers* — had been given, tested, and now fulfilled. She knows now not that God can keep a promise in general, but that *this* God, *this* promise, *this* word spoken to *her*, was true.

The Arabic for knowledge here is *ʿalima*, and the commentators stress the experiential dimension: this is knowledge acquired by living through the fulfillment, not by being told. The tradition has a term for the difference — *ʿilm al-yaqīn* (knowledge of certainty), *ʿayn al-yaqīn* (the eye of certainty), and *ḥaqq al-yaqīn* (the truth of certainty), a threefold gradation named in the Qur'an:

> *"Nay, did you but know with the knowledge of certainty, you would surely see Hellfire; then you would surely see it with the eye of certainty."* (Qur'an 102:5–7)

Moses' mother moved from *ʿilm al-yaqīn* — she believed the promise when she heard it — to *ʿayn al-yaqīn* — she saw it with her own eyes. This is precisely why the trial was necessary. A promise believed in comfort and a promise held through terror produce different kinds of certainty, and only the second can sustain a person through the next trial.

This is also why the sūrah's method matters. God did not simply explain to her that He was trustworthy. He put her in a situation in which trustworthiness could only be discovered by going through. The Qur'an states the law of this elsewhere:

> *"And We shall surely try you with something of fear and hunger, and a loss of wealth and lives and fruits. And give glad tidings to the patient — those who, when affliction befalls them, say, 'Truly we belong to God, and truly unto Him we return.'"* (Qur'an 2:155–156)

**"But Most of Them Know Not" — The Closing Judgment**

The verse ends with a clause that sounds almost abrupt after such tenderness: *wa-lākinna aktharahum lā yaʿlamūn* — *but most of them know not*.

Who are "most of them," and what do they not know? The commentators give two readings, and both fit.

They are the people of Pharaoh — the court, the household, and Egypt generally — who did not know what was happening under their own roof: that the child being nursed at state expense by an Israelite woman was her own son, and that the whole arrangement was the undoing of their king. Theirs was the ignorance described in verses 9 and 11 (*yet they were unaware*), now stated as a generalization.

But the broader reading, and the one with more bite, is that "most of them" refers to human beings generally: most people do not know that God's promise is true, because they do not read events the way this story teaches one to read them. They see baskets, rivers, refusals to nurse, and hired help. They do not see governance. And because they do not see governance, they are at the mercy of whatever the surface of events appears to say.

The Qur'an makes the same complaint repeatedly, in almost identical wording:

> *"They know an outward aspect of the life of this world, but of the Hereafter they are heedless."* (Qur'an 30:7)

> *"And most of mankind, though you strive for it, will not believe."* (Qur'an 12:103)

The verse thus divides humanity into two groups not by intelligence but by interpretive framework: those who read the world with God in it, and those who do not.

**What the Verse Says About Promises**

Theologically, this verse is a locus of the Islamic doctrine of the divine promise (*waʿd*) and threat (*waʿīd*). The scholars of creed established that God does not break His promise:

> *"God — there is no god but He. He will surely gather you unto the Day of Resurrection, wherein is no doubt. And who is truer than God in speech?"* (Qur'an 4:87)

> *"Indeed, God fails not His promise."* (Qur'an 3:9)

The Muʿtazilī theologians drew from such verses a doctrine that God *must*, by His own justice, fulfill His threat to punish the unrepentant sinner. The Sunni position, following al-Ashʿarī and the People of Hadith, is more nuanced and, the tradition argues, more merciful: God's promise of reward is unfailing and never abrogated, but His threat is subject to His forgiveness, which He may extend to whomever He wills. The proof is the vast body of verses and ḥadīth on intercession and pardon, including:

> *"Indeed, God forgives not that partners be ascribed unto Him, but He forgives what is less than that unto whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 4:48)

For Moses' mother, the operative point is simple and is worth carrying: a promise from God is not a probability. And the interval between the promise and its fulfillment is not a period of doubt but a period of formation.

**Application: The Shape of Deliverance**

Two practical conclusions follow, and the tradition states both.

The first is about *timing*. The promise in verse 7 was fulfilled in verse 13 — six verses of fear, separation, near-disclosure, and an infant who would not eat. In the lived experience of the mother, this was days or weeks of agony. There is no shortcut offered in the story, and none should be expected. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Know that victory comes with patience, that relief comes with distress, and that with hardship comes ease."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad)

The second is about *form*. Moses was returned to his mother — but not to the circumstances she had lost him from. He was now a ward of the palace, a child with a future, and she was his paid nurse. Deliverance in this life rarely restores the prior state; it produces a new one, usually stranger and often better than the one that was lost. The believer who insists on restoration in exactly the old form will frequently fail to recognize the answer when it arrives.

There is also a quiet word here for anyone who has cried over a child. The Qur'an names the cooling of a mother's eye as a purpose of God's action in history, and it records it in the same idiom it uses for Paradise. No grief of that kind is beneath the notice of the One who wrote this verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:14

> **When he reached maturity and was firmly established [in life], We gave him wisdom and knowledge. Thus do We recompense the virtuous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Only Narrative Summary**

The story leaps forward in a single sentence. From the nursing infant of verse 13 we are carried, without a word about the intervening years, to a grown man. The Qur'an's economy is characteristic: it does not narrate Moses' childhood, his education, his standing in the palace, or his gradual estrangement from it. It states the outcome and the divine gift accompanying it, and leaves the rest to silence.

The commentators draw the methodological lesson: the Qur'an narrates only what bears on guidance. Everything between verse 13 and verse 15 is passed over in one clause because nothing in it is needed for the reader's instruction.

**"When He Reached Maturity" — *Ashudd* in the Qur'an**

*Wa-lammā balagha ashuddahu* — the word *ashudd* is a plural of intensity denoting the age of full physical and mental strength, when a person's capacities have been drawn taut like a rope at its maximum tension.

The Qur'an uses the term in several precise legal and narrative contexts — most importantly at the age of orphan custody:

> *"And test the orphans until they reach the age of marriage; then, if you perceive in them sound judgment, hand over to them their property."* (Qur'an 4:6)

and in the passages on fulfilling covenants:

> *"And approach not the property of the orphan, save in the best manner, until he reaches his maturity."* (Qur'an 6:152)

The jurists discussed the actual age at length, and al-Qurṭubī summarizes the range handed down: fifteen years in the view of the majority (based on the ḥadīth of Ibn ʿUmar at the Battle of the Trench, when the Prophet ﷺ permitted him to fight at fifteen and not at fourteen — reported by al-Bukhārī and Muslim), with others proposing seventeen, eighteen, or the appearance of physical signs of puberty. The practical rule that emerged in Islamic law is that *bulūgh* (legal maturity) is established by either a definite physical sign or by reaching a set age, varying among the schools from fifteen to eighteen lunar years.

For the narrative, the point is simply that Moses became a man: no longer protected by infancy, now accountable, now capable, now exposed.

**"And Was Firmly Established" — *Istawā*, One Word With a Dozen Meanings**

*Wa-stawā* is one of the richest verbs in the Qur'an, and the commentators have catalogued its range:

> *"Then He turned to the heaven, and it was smoke, and He said to it and to the earth, 'Come willingly or unwillingly.'"* (Qur'an 41:11 — *thumma istawā ilā al-samāʾ*, "then He directed Himself to the heaven")

> *"He it is Who created for you all that is on the earth; then He turned to the heaven."* (Qur'an 2:29)

> *"The Merciful, established upon the Throne."* (Qur'an 20:5 — *al-Raḥmānu ʿalā al-ʿarshi istawā*, the disputed verse of the divine attribute)

The root sense is *to be level, even, balanced, to settle firmly upon*. In the physical description of a human being, it denotes the moment when growth is complete and the frame has settled into its final proportion — no longer growing, no longer unsteady, at full height.

The commentators record several specifications. Al-Qurṭubī and others report the opinion that this refers to the age of forty — the age at which, in the tradition, the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ received the first revelation, and the age the Qur'an names for full maturity:

> *"Till, when he reaches his full strength and reaches forty years, he says, 'My Lord, inspire me to give thanks for Thy blessing wherewith Thou hast blessed me and my parents, and to work righteousness pleasing unto Thee.'"* (Qur'an 46:15)

Al-Rāzī, however, insists on distinguishing *ashudd* from *istawā*, and his reading is psychologically the more interesting. He takes *balagha ashuddahu* as physical maturity and *istawā* as intellectual and psychological maturity — the point at which a person's inward life comes into balance. Al-Ṭabarī transmits other specifications: that *ashudd* is thirty-three and *istawā* is forty.

The spiritual reading, given by Ibn ʿAjībah and developed in the Sufi commentaries, is the most suggestive: *istawā* describes the state of inward equilibrium in which a person is balanced between hope and fear, humility and dignity, dependence and autonomy. It is the condition of the soul when the opposing pulls have been reconciled — and it is, in their account, precisely when *real* wisdom becomes possible. A young man may be brilliant at twenty and unstable at forty; the balance is what makes the brilliance usable.

There is an important implication for how the reader understands Moses' later life. The Moses of verse 15 is strong, educated, morally serious — and not yet inwardly settled. He will strike a man in anger, flee in fear, and spend ten years in the desert. Only after that apprenticeship will the fire appear on the mountain (v. 29). The sūrah's placement is thus exact: *ashudd* and *istawā* describe his arrival at natural maturity, and everything that follows is the further schooling that natural maturity cannot provide.

**"We Gave Him Wisdom and Knowledge" — Two Gifts, Distinguished**

*Ātaynāhu ḥukman wa-ʿilman*. The commentators distinguish the two nouns, though with some overlap.

*Ḥukm*, from *ḥ-k-m*, is wisdom in the sense of sound judgment — the capacity to decide correctly, to put things in their right places, and (in the usage of the Qur'an) authority to judge between people. The same root gives *ḥikmah*, and the Qur'an links the two:

> *"And We gave him wisdom and judgment [ḥukman wa-ʿilman] as a youth."* — the almost identical phrase is used of Joseph at 12:22, and the parallel is deliberate: Moses and Joseph, the two great Israelites of the Qur'an, are both described as receiving judgment and knowledge upon maturity.

Al-Ṭabarī transmits the view that *ḥukm* here means the wisdom a prophet possesses *before* being invested with prophethood, while *ʿilm* means understanding in religious matters. Al-Qurṭubī adds the opinion that it refers to knowledge of the religious tradition of Moses' forefathers among the Israelites — that is, that Moses knew the faith of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob even while living in Pharaoh's palace. This last detail explains much about the next verse: Moses intervenes in the fight because he recognizes a wrong, and he recognizes it because he has a moral framework that did not come from the palace.

**"Thus Do We Recompense the Virtuous" — The Theological Difficulty and Its Resolution**

The closing clause, *wa-kadhālika najzī al-muḥsinīn*, raises a question the commentators address directly: is Moses' gift a *reward* for merit, and can that be squared with the doctrine that prophethood is pure divine election?

The problem is real. Elsewhere in the Qur'an, similar phrasing is used for the reward of good conduct:

> *"Thus do We recompense the virtuous."* (Qur'an 6:84; 37:80, 105, 110, 121, 131; 77:44)

And the Qur'an unambiguously states that God rewards good with good:

> *"Is the reward of goodness aught but goodness?"* (Qur'an 55:60)

But the tradition also holds, on the basis of both reason and revelation, that the office of prophethood is not *earned*. Al-Rāzī states the issue plainly: some commentators concluded from this verse that the gift here cannot be prophethood, since prophethood is never a recompense for merit. The resolution the tradition adopted distinguishes the levels:

The *gifts* that accompany and prepare for a mission — sound judgment, knowledge, protection from error, a good reputation, the love of the righteous — may be described as rewards for prior righteousness, because God has made gratitude and good conduct causes of increase. The Qur'an states this principle:

> *"And when your Lord proclaimed: 'If you are grateful, I will surely increase you.'"* (Qur'an 14:7)

But the *office* itself — that God should send this person rather than that one, to this people at this time — is a pure act of divine choice, and no creature has a claim upon it. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Say: 'Had God willed, I would not have recited it unto you, nor would He have made it known to you.'"* (Qur'an 10:16)

and God said to him:

> *"And We have not sent thee, save as a mercy unto the worlds."* (Qur'an 21:107)

The two principles are therefore held together: human righteousness has real effects and is genuinely honored, and yet no one stands before God with a claim. The believer works, hopes, and trusts, and is taught to say with the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds."* They said, "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said, *"Not even me, unless God envelops me in His mercy and grace."* (al-Bukhārī 5673; Muslim 2816)

**Al-Muḥsinūn: The Rank of *Iḥsān***

The word translated *virtuous* is *muḥsinīn*, from *iḥsān* — the word the Qur'an uses for the highest grade of religious excellence. The classical definition is enshrined in the ḥadīth of Gabriel, in which the Prophet ﷺ was asked about *iḥsān* and replied:

> *"It is that you worship God as though you see Him; and if you do not see Him, then indeed He sees you."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8)

And the Qur'an pairs it with justice as the summary of the moral life:

> *"Indeed, God commands justice and excellence [iḥsān], and giving to kinsfolk."* (Qur'an 16:90)

Note that *iḥsān* is not merely "doing good works"; it is doing them with an excellence that goes beyond the required measure, with the consciousness of being seen. Moses, at this stage of the story, is counted among the *muḥsinīn* — a designation that will matter when the reader reaches verse 15 and has to weigh his conduct there.

**Practical Fruit: The Formation That Precedes the Mission**

The verse carries a lesson about preparation that the whole sūrah illustrates and that modern life constantly forgets.

Moses received the palace's education and then something better: judgment and knowledge from God. He received the advantage of position — raised in the house of the king — and then the disadvantage of exile, hunger, and ten years of hired labor. And he did not receive the mission until both had done their work.

The pattern is consistent in the Qur'an's treatment of those whom God sends. Joseph was tested with the pit, slavery, and prison before the storehouses of Egypt. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was an orphan, a shepherd, and a merchant before the cave of Ḥirāʾ. The tradition has distilled from this the principle that capacity is built before it is used, and that the interval between being ready and being sent is not wasted time.

Applied to ordinary life: the years in which a person appears to be only preparing, or only waiting, or only working for someone else's flock, may be the exact years in which *ḥukm* and *ʿilm* are being given. The sūrah's own hero spent a decade of them in Midian before a single word of revelation came to him.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:15

> **He entered the city at a time of heedlessness among its people. And he found therein two men fighting—one from among his own faction and the other from among his enemies. Then the one from among his own faction sought his aid against the one who was from among his enemies; so Moses struck him with his fist and put an end to him. He said, "This is the work of Satan! Truly he is a manifest enemy, leading astray."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Scene of Moses' Adult Life**

The sūrah has spent thirteen verses getting Moses to adulthood. The first thing it shows him doing as a man is killing someone.

The shock is deliberate. The reader has seen the infant in the river, the mother's terror, the sister's tracking, the reunion, and the gift of wisdom and knowledge — and now, in a single verse, the hero of the story commits a fatal act of anger. The Qur'an does not soften it, does not excuse it in the narrator's voice, and does not narrate it as a heroic exploit. It reports it and then immediately quotes Moses' own horrified response.

**"He Entered the City at a Time of Heedlessness"**

*Wa-dakhala al-madīnata ʿalā ḥīni ghaflatin min ahlihā*. The commentators offer two readings of *time of heedlessness*.

The first, transmitted by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī, is the siesta: the middle of the day, when the streets emptied and people were indoors sleeping through the heat. This reading explains how a fight in the street could proceed with few witnesses, and why Moses' intervention went unobserved — until it did not.

The second, preferred by some later commentators, is that the people were heedless of *Moses* — that is, they no longer knew who he was, or did not remember that he had begun to side with the Israelites against the regime. This reading presupposes a backstory the Qur'an does not narrate: that Moses' sympathies had become known and that he had been cast out of favor.

Both readings are possible, and the difference does not affect the moral substance. What the phrase establishes is that Moses was acting in a moment when normal oversight was relaxed — a detail that matters because the whole subsequent crisis arises from the collapse of that secrecy.

The identity of *the city* is disputed in the commentaries: the capital where Pharaoh resided, or specifically Heliopolis, or Memphis. Al-Rāzī notes drily that these specifications cannot be verified from revelation, and the point is not worth pursuing. What matters is that it was an Egyptian city where an Israelite could be beaten in the street with impunity.

**"Two Men Fighting" — The Social Setting in One Clause**
