*Fa-wajada fīhā rajulayni yaqtatilān* — *and he found therein two men fighting*. One is described as *min shīʿatihi*, from his party or faction; the other as *min ʿaduwwihi*, from his enemy. The commentators identify the first as an Israelite and the second as an Egyptian.

But they also record a subtlety that is worth our attention. The word *shīʿah* means a partisan group; the word *ʿaduww* means enemy. The enemy is identified not by ethnicity but by *enmity*. Al-Rāzī and others therefore raise the possibility that the opponent was himself an Israelite who was a disbeliever — an enemy by religion rather than by people. This reading gains support from verse 19, where the word changes to *ʿaduwwun lahumā*, "an enemy to them both," which some take to indicate a different kind of adversary from the one in this verse.

The biblical account (Exodus 2:11–12) specifies that the man was an Egyptian, and several Muslim commentators adopt that identification from it. But the Qur'an's own wording is more guarded, and the lesson does not depend on the ethnicity of the victim. What the Qur'an wants the reader to see is the structure of the situation: one person with a claim, one person with power, and a bystander with the ability to intervene.

**"He Sought His Aid" — The Ethics of Intervention**

*Fa-istaghāthahu alladhī min shīʿatihi ʿalā alladhī min ʿaduwwihi* — the Israelite called for help *against* the Egyptian. The verb *istaghātha* means to cry out for rescue, for someone to come between a person and what is destroying him.

The Qur'an takes the obligation to answer such a cry entirely for granted, and the whole Islamic legal tradition builds on that assumption. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whosoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand; if he cannot, then with his tongue; and if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith."* (Muslim 49)

and:

> *"Help your brother, whether he is the wrongdoer or the one wronged."* They said, "O Messenger of God, we help the one wronged, but how do we help the wrongdoer?" He said, *"By preventing him from wrongdoing, for that is helping him."* (al-Bukhārī 2444)

Moses was therefore doing something right when he intervened. The tradition is unanimous on this. The fault in the verse is not that he defended a victim; it is what happened in the execution of that defense.

**"Moses Struck Him With His Fist and Put an End to Him"**

*Fa-wakazahu Mūsā fa-qaḍā ʿalayhi*. Two verbs, and the commentators read both carefully.

*Wakaza* is a strike with the closed fist — a punch, a blow delivered with the knuckles. It is not a sword stroke or a dagger thrust; it is the strike of a man who intends to drive someone off, not to end a life. Al-Qurṭubī and others emphasize this: the instrument and the manner indicate that Moses intended to repel, not to kill.

*Qaḍā ʿalayhi* is an idiom meaning "he finished him off," "he put an end to him" — that is, the man died. The commentators note the grammatical structure: *fa-* (and so) links the two verbs, and the result was not intended. The phrase does not say "he killed him" (*qatalahu*); it says the blow *put an end to* him. The Arabic preserves the gap between the act and the outcome.

This distinction is legally and morally enormous, and it is the reason the classical scholars never classify what Moses did as murder. Islamic law distinguishes graded categories of homicide, and the relevant one here is *qatl shibh al-ʿamd* or *khataʾ* — killing by an act not intended to kill. The Qur'an legislates the rule for the community of Muhammad ﷺ:

> *"It is not for a believer to kill a believer, except by mistake. And whosoever kills a believer by mistake, then let him free a believing slave and pay blood-money to his family, unless they remit it as charity."* (Qur'an 4:92)

The Moses of this verse lived before that legislation, but the moral structure is the same: intention determines the category, and an unintended death arising from a lawful act carries expiation and compensation rather than the penalty of deliberate killing.

**"This Is the Work of Satan!" — The Immediate Self-Indictment**

The verse's most remarkable feature is Moses' reaction. There is no hesitation, no search for justification, no claim of self-defense, no blaming of the victim. The first words out of his mouth are an acknowledgement of catastrophic error:

*Hādhā min ʿamali al-Shayṭān* — *this is of the doing of Satan*.

The commentators note the precision of the phrase. Moses does not say "Satan made me do it," which would shift responsibility. He says *this is the work of Satan* — the product, the outcome, belongs to Satan's portfolio. The act of intervention was his and was right; the outcome, the death, is the thing Satan harvests.

How, the commentators ask, is this Satan's work? They answer on several levels. Satan had been prompting the quarrel in the first place, and Moses' blow became the instrument of a death Satan desired. Satan's method, as the Qur'an describes it elsewhere, is to take something legitimate and push it past its limit:

> *"O you who believe, enter into peace, all of you, and follow not the footsteps of Satan; truly he is a manifest enemy unto you."* (Qur'an 2:208)

The phrase *footsteps of Satan* is exactly the mechanism of this verse. Defending the oppressed is a step; striking in anger is a step beyond it; and death is at the end of the line. The distance between the two steps is where Satan works.

**"Truly He Is a Manifest Enemy, Leading Astray"**

*Innahu ʿaduwwun muḍillun mubīn* — the description of Satan in three words: an enemy, one who leads astray, and manifestly so.

The Qur'an uses this vocabulary of Satan consistently, and the consistency is itself the warning: he is *open* about it. He does not hide his enmity; he declared it at the beginning:

> *"He said, 'Because Thou hast sent me astray, I shall surely sit in wait for them upon Thy straight path. Then I shall come upon them from before them and from behind them, and from their right and from their left; and Thou wilt not find most of them grateful.'"* (Qur'an 7:16–17)

And the Qur'an names the specific pattern Moses fell into — the sudden, unreflective action taken under provocation — as one of Satan's standard instruments:

> *"And if a prompting from Satan provokes you, seek refuge in God; truly He is Hearing, Knowing."* (Qur'an 41:36)

Note that the remedy the Qur'an prescribes for the satanic impulse is not deliberation in the moment — deliberation is precisely what anger destroys — but a habit formed beforehand: *seek refuge in God*. The Prophet ﷺ taught the practical form of this:

> *"When one of you becomes angry, let him seek refuge in God from the accursed Satan."* (al-Bukhārī 3282; Muslim 2610)

and, in a famous exchange with a man who asked him for advice:

> *"Do not become angry."* The man repeated his request several times, and each time the Prophet ﷺ said, *"Do not become angry."* (al-Bukhārī 6116)

The Prophet also gave the physical remedies:

> *"If one of you becomes angry while standing, let him sit down; if the anger leaves him, fine; otherwise let him lie down."* (Abū Dāwūd 4782; Aḥmad — graded ḥasan)

and:

> *"The strong man is not the one who overcomes people by his strength, but the strong man is the one who controls himself while angry."* (al-Bukhārī 6114; Muslim 2609)

**What the Verse Teaches About Anger and Good Intentions**

The psychological realism of this verse is unmatched. Moses was a *muḥsin* (v. 14). He was defending a victim. He was not seeking personal gain. And he killed a man.

The lesson is not that intervention is dangerous; it is that *righteousness does not immunize a person against the effects of anger*. The most dangerous moments in a moral life are not the moments of temptation to do something obviously wrong, but the moments when a person is doing something obviously right and loses control of the means.

The tradition names this with precision. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Beware of oppression, for oppression will be darkness on the Day of Resurrection; and beware of miserliness... and beware of the supplication of the wronged."* (Muslim 2578)

And in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"O My servants, I have forbidden oppression for Myself, and I have made it forbidden among you, so do not oppress one another."* (Muslim 2577)

There is also a lesson about the company a person keeps and the situations he enters. Moses entered a city at a time when oversight was absent and walked into a violent quarrel. The Qur'an's general instruction to believers is to avoid the places where self-control is most likely to fail:

> *"And when you see those who engage in vain discourse about Our signs, turn away from them until they engage in some other talk; and if Satan should cause you to forget, then do not sit, after the reminder, with the wrongdoing people."* (Qur'an 6:68)

**The Narrative Function: Why the Story Includes This**

Finally, note what the verse does for the sūrah as a whole. The man whom God will address at the burning tree (v. 30) is the man who has killed someone. The one who will be sent to the most powerful ruler on earth is a fugitive from a capital he had to flee at dawn (vv. 18–21).

This is the Qur'an's consistent picture of prophethood: God chooses and then forms. He does not select people who have never failed; He selects people whose failures have been metabolized into humility. The ten years in Midian that follow are not a punishment interlude — they are the apprenticeship of a man who has learned, in the worst possible way, what an uncontrolled blow can do.

And the evidence that the lesson was learned is immediate. In the next verse, Moses does not argue, does not explain, and does not mitigate. He says only: *My Lord, truly I have wronged my own soul. Forgive me.*

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:16

> **He said, "My Lord! Truly I have wronged my own soul. Forgive me." So He forgave him. Verily He is the Forgiving, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest and Most Complete Repentance in the Qur'an**

Two sentences. The first acknowledges the wrong; the second is the Divine response. The whole theology of *tawbah* — repentance — is compressed into this verse: confession, attribution, petition, acceptance, and the two Names that make acceptance possible.

*Rabbi innī ẓalamtu nafsī fa-ghfir lī* — *My Lord, truly I have wronged my own soul; so forgive me*. Note what is absent. There is no excuse, no explanation about the provocation, no mention that the blow was not intended to kill, no reminder that he was defending a victim, no claim that the dead man was an enemy of God. There is only the admission, and then the request.

The commentators, sensitive to the honor of the prophets, all remark on this. Moses had every mitigating circumstance available to him, and he used none of them. Al-Rāzī observes that the mark of a truthful repentance is that it stops arguing. The moment a person begins explaining why the fault was not really his, he has left the posture of the repentant and entered the posture of the defense attorney.

**"I Have Wronged My Own Soul" — Why the Self Is Named as the Victim**

The phrase *ẓalamtu nafsī* is one of the most frequent confessions in the Qur'an, and its recurrence is theologically loaded. Adam and Eve said:

> *"Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves; and if Thou dost not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we shall surely be among the losers."* (Qur'an 7:23)

Jonah, in the darkness, said:

> *"There is no god but Thou! Glory be to Thee! Truly I have been among the wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 21:87)

Solomon prayed:

> *"My Lord, forgive me and grant me a dominion such as will not befit anyone after me."* (Qur'an 38:35)

Why is the *self* named as the object of the wrong rather than the person who was harmed?

The commentators give the reason, and it is the foundation of the Islamic moral psychology. Sin is not, in the first instance, damage done to God — He is beyond harm — and it is not only damage done to others, which the Law addresses separately through rights and restitution. In its first and deepest effect, sin is damage done to the one who commits it. It darkens his heart, weakens his will, accustoms him to disobedience, and moves him further from the good for which he was made. The Qur'an states this plainly:

> *"Nay, but what they used to do has rusted over their hearts."* (Qur'an 83:14)

And the Prophet ﷺ described the mechanism:

> *"When a servant commits a sin, a black spot appears upon his heart. If he desists, seeks forgiveness, and repents, his heart is polished; but if he returns [to sin], it increases until it covers his heart."* — and this, he said, is the meaning of *"Nay, but what they used to do has rusted over their hearts."* (al-Tirmidhī 3334; Ibn Mājah 4244 — graded ḥasan)

Naming the self as the victim is therefore not self-pity; it is accurate diagnosis. The person who sins is, first of all, the person who is hurt by it.

**Theological Question: Why Would a Prophet Need Forgiveness?**

This verse raises a question the classical scholars addressed at length, because it bears on the doctrine of *ʿiṣmah* — the protection of the prophets from sin.

If the prophets are protected, why does Moses ask for forgiveness? And from what?

The tradition's answers are several, and they are complementary.

The first is that the act in question was not a sin in the strict sense at all, but a lesser lapse against the highest standard appropriate to the rank of a prophet. The Islamic position on *ʿiṣmah*, as formulated by al-Qurṭubī, al-Nawawī, Ibn Taymiyyah, and the scholars of creed generally, is that the prophets are protected from *major* sins and from persisting in *minor* ones, and from anything that would discredit their message — but that they may commit minor lapses or choose a less excellent course, after which they are promptly corrected and turn to God. On this reading, Moses' blow was not murder and not disobedience to a command, but it fell short of the perfect restraint befitting the man who would carry God's message.

The second answer, favored by al-Qurṭubī, is that the wrongdoing lay in the *excess*: he could have helped the Israelite without striking so hard. The intervention was obligatory; the force was not.

The third answer, and the one with the greatest spiritual reach, is that the prophets are simply more sensitive to fault than other people. A man who stands close to a light sees the dust in his own garment that a man in the dark cannot see. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"By God, I seek forgiveness from God and turn to Him in repentance more than seventy times a day."* (al-Bukhārī 6307)

And he taught the believers to say in their prayer:

> *"O God, forgive me my sin, my ignorance, my excess in my affairs, and what You know better than I. O God, forgive me my seriousness and my jesting, my error and my deliberateness, and all of that is with me."* (al-Bukhārī 6398; Muslim 2719)

The fourth answer looks forward rather than back. A prophet asking forgiveness is a *model* for his community. If the man who spoke to God directly still needed to say *forgive me*, then no one is exempt. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Every son of Adam sins, and the best of those who sin are those who repent."* (al-Tirmidhī 2499; Ibn Mājah 4251 — graded ḥasan)

**"So He Forgave Him" — Immediate, Unconditional Acceptance**

*Fa-ghafara lahu* — *so He forgave him*. The response is immediate and unqualified. No conditions are stated, no waiting period is imposed, no intermediary is mentioned. He asked, and He forgave.

The commentators note the contrast with the human order. A crime of this kind in any human legal system triggers investigation, trial, penalty, and a long aftermath. With God, the turning of the heart *is* the event. The Prophet ﷺ described the divine response in terms that have consoled believers in every generation:

> *"When a servant repents, God rejoices over his repentance more than one of you would rejoice if he were on his mount in a barren land and it ran away with his food and drink, and he despaired of it — then he came to a tree and lay down in its shade, having despaired, and while he was like that, behold, it was standing before him, and he took hold of its reins and said, out of intense joy, 'O God, You are my servant and I am Your lord' — making a mistake out of the extremity of his joy."* (Muslim 2747)

And in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"O son of Adam, as long as you call upon Me and hope in Me, I will forgive you for whatever has come from you, and I will not mind. O son of Adam, were your sins to reach the clouds of the sky and you then asked forgiveness of Me, I would forgive you."* (al-Tirmidhī 3540; Aḥmad — graded ḥasan)

Theologically, this verse establishes that forgiveness is not a transaction but an attribute. God forgives because He is *al-Ghafūr*, not because the sinner has produced sufficient consideration. The conditions of repentance as the scholars enumerate them — abandoning the sin, resolving not to return, and restoring the rights of others where rights were violated — are the *form* that genuine turning takes, not a price paid.

**"Verily He Is the Forgiving, the Merciful" — Two Names, Two Functions**

The verse closes with *innahu huwa al-Ghafūru al-Raḥīm*. The commentators distinguish the two Names, and the distinction is standard in the literature of the divine names.

*Al-Ghafūr* — the Forgiving, the One who covers. The root *gh-f-r* carries the sense of covering over, like a helmet (*mighfar*) covering a warrior's head. God covers the sin: He does not expose it, and He does not let its consequences fall.

*Al-Raḥīm* — the Merciful, the One who gives good beyond what is due. Forgiveness removes the penalty; mercy confers the gift. A forgiven sinner is restored to nothing; a forgiven and shown-mercy sinner is restored to more than he had.

The pairing is frequent in the Qur'an and appears here with the emphatic pronoun construction *innahu huwa* — *verily He*, *He is* — a doubling that the grammarians explain as exclusive emphasis: forgiveness and mercy belong to Him and to no other in this absolute sense.

**The Doctrine of Repentance in Full**

Because this verse is one of the primary texts on *tawbah*, it is worth summarizing what the tradition derived from it and from its companions.

Repentance is obligatory and immediate. The Qur'an:

> *"And turn unto God all together, O believers, that you may prosper."* (Qur'an 24:31)

Repentance is available until death approaches or the sun rises in the west:

> *"Repentance is not for those who do evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says, 'Now I repent.'"* (Qur'an 4:18)

The door is always open, and it is described in the most generous terms:

> *"Say: 'O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, despair not of the mercy of God. Truly God forgives all sins. Truly He is the Forgiving, the Merciful.'"* (Qur'an 39:53)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God extends His hand during the night so that the sinner of the day may repent, and extends His hand during the day so that the sinner of the night may repent, until the sun rises from its west."* (Muslim 2759)

And repentance erases what preceded it:

> *"The one who repents from sin is like the one who has no sin."* (Ibn Mājah 4250 — graded ḥasan by al-Albānī)

Where the sin involved the rights of another person, repentance requires restoration in this life, because rights are not erased by turning to God. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever has wronged his brother in respect of his honor or anything else, let him seek his release from it today, before there is no dinar and no dirham: if he has good deeds, they will be taken from him in proportion to his wrongdoing, and if he has no good deeds, some of the evil deeds of the other will be taken and loaded upon him."* (al-Bukhārī 2449)

**What Comes Next: Forgiveness Is Not Cancellation of Consequences**

One of the most important things this sūrah teaches, and the thing that makes it realistic rather than sentimental, is that Moses was forgiven in verse 16 — and in verse 18 he is still in danger, in verse 19 he is exposed, in verse 20 he is warned, and in verse 21 he flees for his life into the desert.

Divine forgiveness did not remove the political consequences of the act. The death still had to be dealt with; the notables still convened; and Moses still had to leave everything behind. The commentators are explicit: God forgave the sin, and God also used its consequences to move Moses toward the place where his real work would begin.

This is a hard and necessary lesson. Many people imagine that forgiveness should mean the erasure of all downstream effects, and when the effects persist, they conclude that the forgiveness was not real. The sūrah says otherwise. Moses was forgiven instantly and completely — and he still spent ten years in Midian. The ten years were not a continuation of the punishment; they were the content of the cure.

**Application**

For the reader, this verse offers the shape of the return in its simplest possible form: *Rabbi innī ẓalamtu nafsī fa-ghfir lī*. Name the wrong as yours, do not argue, ask, and then accept the answer.

And it offers the cure for the two diseases that block that return. Against despair, it says: *So He forgave him* — immediately, without conditions, by a God whose Names are the Forgiving and the Merciful. Against presumption, it says: the man who was forgiven still had to face the world he had damaged, and he did not treat the forgiveness as permission to remain where he was.

Moses asked, and Moses left. Both halves belong to the same repentance.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:17

> **He said, "My Lord! Because Thou hast blessed me, I shall never be a supporter of the guilty."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Vow Spoken in the Worst Hour of His Life**

The commentators note the literary placement of this verse with some care. Moses has just been forgiven (v. 16), and the situation is about to deteriorate sharply: in verse 18 he will be recognized in the street, in verse 19 his crime will be declared publicly, and in verses 20–21 he will flee the country. This verse stands in the calm before that storm, and its content is not fear but commitment.

The sequence is psychologically exact. A person in shock tries to recover his bearings by asking what he still believes. Moses has just discovered that his own strength, unguarded, can produce a death. His response is not to withdraw from the field but to rededicate himself to the side he is on — and to name the principle that will govern the rest of his life.

**"Because Thou Hast Blessed Me" — Gratitude as the Engine of Resolve**

*Rabbi bi-mā anʿamta ʿalayya*. The phrase opens with the prepositional construction *bi-mā* — *by virtue of what*, *because of what*, or, as many commentators read it, an oath-like invocation: *by the blessing You have bestowed upon me*.

What blessing is meant? The primary reference is the one just stated in verse 14: *We gave him wisdom and knowledge*. Al-Qurṭubī adds the wider inventory that the sūrah has already supplied — life preserved from the river, return to his mother, upbringing in security, physical strength, and the moral discernment that made him intervene on behalf of a victim in the first place.

The structure of the vow is instructive and is worth unpacking, because it is the structure the Qur'an commends everywhere: *blessing → gratitude → commitment*. Moses does not say "I promise to be good" as an act of willpower; he anchors the commitment in what he has been given. Blessing recognized produces obligation felt; obligation felt produces resolve.

The commentators therefore read this verse as a textbook instance of *shukr* — gratitude — which the Qur'an presents not as a feeling but as a use. The definition given by the scholars is that gratitude is the employment of the blessing in the obedience of the Bestower. Al-Rāzī states it precisely: thanksgiving is not speech; it is deployment.

The Qur'an rewards this posture with a promise that turns gratitude into a law of increase:

> *"And when your Lord proclaimed: 'If you are grateful, I will surely increase you; but if you are ungrateful, truly My punishment is severe.'"* (Qur'an 14:7)

Note also what Moses does *not* say. He does not say "because You have forgiven me." Al-Qurṭubī observes this: the vow is grounded in blessing rather than in pardon, and one reason given is that at this point Moses had no certain knowledge that his repentance had been accepted — he knew he had been given wisdom, life, and strength, and he pledged on that basis. The remark has a theological edge: a person may build his commitment on what he certainly has received even while uncertain about what he has asked for.

**"I Shall Never Be" — The Grammar of Permanent Resolution**

*Fa-lan akūna ẓahīran li-l-mujrimīn*. The negative particle *lan* is the strongest available in Arabic for denying the future: it indicates an emphatic, unqualified negation extending forward without limit. Moses is not saying "I hope not to be" or "I will try not to be." He is saying: never, at any point, will I be found in that position.

*Ẓahīr* means a backer, a supporter, a helper, one who lends his weight to another's cause. The word is from *ẓahr*, the back — the supporter is the one who puts his back into another's effort. The same word appears in this sūrah's final section with reference to the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"So be not a support (ẓahīr) for the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 28:86)

The recurrence is a deliberate link that binds Moses' vow to Muhammad's instruction, and it is part of the sūrah's method of holding the two careers together.

**"The Guilty" — *Al-Mujrimūn* and the Scope of the Vow**

The word *mujrim* is from *j-r-m*, which carries the sense of cutting off, committing an offense, earning guilt. It is one of the Qur'an's standard designations for the hardened wrongdoers — those whose wrongdoing is a settled condition rather than an isolated act.

The commentators record two readings of the vow's scope, and they are both true.

The first, and the one with the more immediate narrative fit, is specific: Moses is renouncing his position within Pharaoh's establishment. He had been raised in the palace, was known as the son of Pharaoh's house, and had benefited from that status. The vow is a resignation — he will no longer lend his presence, his name, or his abilities to a regime built on the oppression described in verse 4. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both transmit this reading, and it explains why the sūrah moves directly from this vow to the events that force Moses out of Egypt. A man who has inwardly resigned from a criminal regime cannot remain inside it indefinitely.

The second reading is universal, and the commentators speak of it as a supplication rather than a mere promise: *O God, never let me be a supporter of wrongdoing in any form.* On this reading the verse is a prayer, because Moses knew his own weakness — he had just discovered how quickly a righteous intention could produce a corpse — and he asked God to protect him from ever lending himself to wrong again.

The generality of the second reading is what makes the verse a permanent rule for believers. Islamic law derived from this and similar verses the prohibition on *taʿāwun* — cooperation — in wrongdoing:

> *"And cooperate in righteousness and reverence, but cooperate not in sin and transgression. And fear God; truly God is severe in retribution."* (Qur'an 5:2)

**The Legal and Ethical Principle: Support Without Participation Is Still Support**

The verse is one of the foundational texts for a principle the jurists developed at length: that aiding a wrong is itself a wrong, even when the aider does not commit the act.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle in its most general form:

> *"Whosoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand; if he cannot, then with his tongue; and if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith."* (Muslim 49)

and he named the consequence of complicity explicitly:

> *"Whoever calls to guidance has a reward like the rewards of all who follow him, without diminishing anything from their rewards; and whoever calls to misguidance bears a sin like the sins of all who follow him, without diminishing anything from their sins."* (Muslim 2674)

The jurists worked this out in concrete rulings across every field of law: it is forbidden to sell grapes to someone who will make wine from them, to testify falsely, to draw up a contract charging interest, to build a structure intended for an unlawful purpose, or to work as a guard for a tyrant. The underlying verse in each case is this one and its companion at 5:2.

What the principle demands in practice is not withdrawal from society — Moses had lived inside the palace for years, and the Qur'an's Joseph served in the Egyptian government — but a refusal to be the *back* that a wrong leans on. The distinction the scholars draw is between working within a flawed system to secure justice (permitted, and at times obligatory) and lending one's weight to the system's wrongdoing (forbidden).

**The Psychology of the Vow**

There is a deeper psychological reading of this verse that the spiritual commentators have developed, and it is worth stating because it explains the verse's placement.

Moses has just learned something devastating about himself: his own strength is dangerous. The natural responses available to him are two, and both are failures. He could renounce action altogether — decide that intervention is too risky and withdraw into private rectitude. Or he could minimize what happened and continue as before, with a slightly revised self-assessment.

He does neither. He recommits to the same field with a sharper rule: never on the side of the guilty. This is the mature response to a moral failure — not abandonment of the good one was pursuing, but a binding of oneself to its conditions.

The tradition names this exact movement. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The believer is not stung twice from the same hole."* (al-Bukhārī 6133; Muslim 2998)

That is: the mark of a believer is not that he never fails, but that he learns the specific shape of his failure and refuses to be caught by it again. Moses' vow is the statement of that refusal, made in advance, in the presence of God, at the moment when he most felt his own weakness.

**"My Lord" — Why the Vow Is Addressed Upward**

Finally, note the addressee. Moses does not announce his resolution to the palace, to the Israelites, or even to himself. He says *Rabbi* — *my Lord*. The commentators point out that this is the correct place for resolutions. A vow made in public tends to become performance; a vow made to God becomes a covenant.

The Prophet ﷺ taught his companions to bind themselves the same way, and the formula he gave ʿAlī and Fāṭimah is instructive in its simplicity. But the broader practice is captured in the ḥadīth:

> *"Whoever vows to obey God, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey God, let him not disobey Him."* (al-Bukhārī 6696)

The one limit on such vows is stated there: no vow is valid if it binds a person to disobedience, or to something not within his power. Moses' vow was within his power, and it was obedience. It held.

**What Followed**

The sūrah immediately tests the vow. In verse 18 Moses is discovered; in verse 19 he is publicly denounced by the very man he had defended the day before; in verse 20 he is warned that the council has decided to kill him. Within four verses of making this vow, Moses loses everything he had.

The commentators see the connection: the vow and the cost arrived together. Renouncing support for the guilty is rarely free, and the sūrah, revealed to a man whose own clan was pressing him to compromise, is unflinching about the price. But the vow is also what makes the exile bearable. A man who has already decided whose side he is on can leave a palace without feeling that he has lost his bearings.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:18

> **Morning found him in the city, fearful and vigilant, and behold, the one who had sought his help the day before cried out to him for help! Moses said to him, "Truly you are clearly in error."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Morning Found Him" — The Night Between Two Days**

*Fa-aṣbaḥa fī al-madīnati khāʾifan yataraqqabu*. The verb *aṣbaḥa* gives the Arabic its characteristic concreteness: literally, *he entered the morning in the city* — and the state he entered it in was fear and watchfulness.

The commentators pause over the phrasing because it does narrative work in a single word. The reader is not told about Moses' night. He is told the shape of Moses' morning, and from that he infers the night: a man who has killed someone does not sleep well, and a man who has killed someone in Pharaoh's capital spends the hours before dawn listening for footsteps.

The same verb appeared at verse 10 for Moses' mother (*morning found the heart of Moses' mother empty*) and will appear at verse 82 for the observers of Korah's destruction. In each case, the Qur'an uses the dawn as the moment when reality can no longer be deferred — night's distractions are gone, the day's business begins, and a person has to face what yesterday left behind.

The two adjectives are precise. *Khāʾifan* — afraid, expecting harm. *Yataraqqabu* — watching, waiting, scanning for news; the root *r-q-b* suggests keeping a lookout from a height. He was not hiding in a room; he was out in the city, watching for what would be said about yesterday.

**The Psychology of the Morning After**

There is a realistic observation here that the commentators elaborate. Sin's first consequence is often not external punishment but internal dislocation. Moses was forgiven in verse 16, and he is terrified in verse 18. The commentators are careful to say what this means and what it does not.

It does not mean the forgiveness was incomplete. God forgives; the verse says so plainly, and God's forgiveness is never partial or provisional.

What it means is that forgiveness restores the relationship with God without automatically dismantling the created order of consequences. Moses had killed a man in a city where the death of an Egyptian was a capital matter and the death of an Egyptian at the hands of an Israelite was a political event. That fact was now loose in the world. Forgiveness had not recalled it.

The distinction is one of the most practically important in the religion, and the tradition states it repeatedly: *tawbah* erases the sin and its record in the Hereafter, but it does not undo the physics of what was done. The Prophet ﷺ taught the believers to seek refuge from the downstream effects even while trusting in the pardon:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the disappearance of Your blessing, from the change of Your protection, from the suddenness of Your vengeance, and from all of Your displeasure."* (Muslim 2739)

And he described the condition of the believer as one of hope and fear together — *khawf* and *rajāʾ* — which the scholars made a formal chapter of creed. Fear without hope is despair; hope without fear is presumption; the believer walks between them.

**"The One Who Had Sought His Help the Day Before"**

*Fa-idhā alladhī istanṣarahu bi-l-amsi yastaṣrikhuhu*. The Qur'an's description of the man is deliberately weighted: *the one who had sought his help the day before*. Not "the Israelite," not "his kinsman," but the man whose cause Moses had taken up at the cost of everything.

The verb *yastaṣrikhuhu* is stronger than the earlier *istaghāthahu*. It means to cry out, to shout for rescue — a loud, public appeal.

The commentators note the irony and the danger in the same breath. The same man who had drawn Moses into the fatal quarrel is now shouting for him again in the street, in daylight, in a city that is looking for the killer. Al-Qurṭubī observes that the man was quarrelsome by disposition — he was fighting someone a second time within a day — and that his manner of calling for help was itself the instrument of Moses' exposure. Verse 20 will confirm this reading: it was the shouting that alerted others.

There is a hard practical lesson here about the company one keeps and the causes one adopts. Moses' intervention was right. But the man he intervened for was undisciplined, and the second appeal was made with the same indiscretion that had caused the first disaster. The tradition is not cynical about this — the obligation to help remains — but it is realistic: helping a person does not make that person careful on your behalf.

**"Truly You Are Clearly in Error" — Moses' Rebuke**

*Qāla lahu Mūsā innaka la-ghawiyyun mubīn*. The word *ghawiyy* is from *gh-w-y*, meaning to stray, to err, to act without guidance. It is the same root that will appear in verse 63 for the leaders who *perverted* their followers (*aghwaynāhum*) and *were perverted* (*ghawaynā*).

The force of the rebuke is: you are plainly, unmistakably in the wrong. The commentators offer two objects for the judgment.

The first reading: Moses is rebuking the man for fighting again. He had been rescued once at lethal cost, and here he was in the street quarreling with someone else the very next morning. The rebuke is *you are the problem*.

The second reading, recorded by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī, is that Moses is rebuking him for the *manner* of the appeal — shouting in public, in a city where Moses is a hunted man. On this reading, the words are a plea for discretion as much as a moral judgment.

Both readings are plausible, and they are not mutually exclusive. What matters for the narrative is that the rebuke was spoken and that it was overheard — or, more precisely, that the exchange itself drew the attention that exposed Moses.

**The Rapid Deterioration: How a Secret Becomes Public**

The sūrah is remarkably economical in tracing the collapse, and the sequence is worth following because it is a study in how quickly a concealed act becomes public knowledge.

Verse 15: the killing happens at a time of heedlessness.
Verse 16–17: Moses repents and resolves.
Verse 18: Moses returns to the city, afraid, and is publicly approached by the man he had helped.
Verse 19: the man — or the enemy — declares openly *do you intend to kill me as you killed a man yesterday?*
Verse 20: a runner arrives from the far end of the city: the council is deliberating to kill you.

Four verses from the act to the death sentence. The commentators draw a moral that the Qur'an states elsewhere in general form:

> *"And they say within themselves, 'Why does God not punish us for what we say?'"* (Qur'an 58:8)

and:

> *"Not a word does he utter but there is with him a watcher, ready."* (Qur'an 50:18)

The Islamic moral teaching is not that secrets always come out — the Qur'an and Sunnah clearly teach that concealment of one's own sin is proper and that God loves to cover His servants — but that a person should live as though exposure were possible, because it often is. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"All of my community will be forgiven except those who publicize [their sins]. And it is a part of publicizing that a man does something at night, and his Lord covers him, and in the morning he says, 'O so-and-so, I did such-and-such last night' — while his Lord had covered him all night, and he uncovers what God had covered."* (al-Bukhārī 6069; Muslim 2990)

The corollary for Moses' situation is the reverse: what was done in the street was not covered, and the machinery of consequences began turning immediately.

**Theological Note: Fear as a Legitimate Human State**

One more point deserves attention because it is often misunderstood. The Qur'an presents Moses — a prophet — as afraid. It does not present this as a defect.

The tradition is emphatic that prophets experience the full range of human emotion, and that their perfection lies in what they *do* with it. Moses feared, and he continued to act. Abraham was afraid when the angels came:

> *"And when he saw their hands not reaching toward it, he mistrusted them and conceived a fear of them. They said, 'Fear not!'"* (Qur'an 11:70)

The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, in the cave with Abū Bakr, was told:

> *"Fear not, for God is with us."* (Qur'an 9:40)

and Abū Bakr's fear is recorded and not censured.

What the Qur'an consistently condemns is not fear as an emotion but the *paralysis* of fear, and the suspicion (*ẓann*) about God that fear can produce when it becomes a settled outlook. The remedy prescribed is remembrance:

> *"Those who believed and whose hearts find rest in the remembrance of God. Verily, in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* (Qur'an 13:28)

**Application: The Morning After a Serious Mistake**

Every reader who has made a serious mistake recognizes this verse. The act is over, repentance has been made, and yet the morning brings a specific dread: the phone call, the email, the consequence arriving through a channel you do not control.

The sūrah's counsel in this verse is not a promise that the consequences will be averted. Moses was forgiven and still had to flee. The counsel is rather about the correct posture in the interim: watch, be prudent, take the lawful means — and do not interpret the persistence of consequences as evidence that the forgiveness failed.

And there is a further, quieter application. The man who cried out to Moses a second time is a recognizable figure: the person whose poor judgment drew you into trouble once and who will do it again without noticing. Moses' response — a direct rebuke — is a permission to speak plainly to such a person. Charity does not require indiscretion on your own behalf.

Finally, note the economy of the divine instruction in what follows. Moses will not be told to stay and face the consequences. He will be told to leave (v. 20), and the leaving will become the making of a prophet. Flight is not always cowardice; sometimes it is the form obedience takes when the alternative is a useless death.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:19

> **But when he desired to strike him who was an enemy unto them both, he said, "O Moses! Do you desire to slay me, as you slew a soul yesterday? You desire naught but to be a tyrant on the earth, and you desire not to be among the workers of righteousness."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Moment of Exposure**

The secret is out, and it comes out in the most dangerous way possible: in a public street, at the top of a man's voice, addressed to Moses by name.

*Fa-lammā an arāda an yabṭisha bi-alladhī huwa ʿaduwwun lahumā* — *but when he desired to strike him who was an enemy to them both*. Note that Moses had *not yet struck*. The verse records the intention, and the intention is enough to provoke the outcry. This detail matters to the commentators: no second blow was landed, no second death occurred. Moses was restrained, and the restraint is part of what the sūrah is showing us about a man who had learned something.

The description of the adversary changes in this verse, and the change is deliberate. In verse 15 the opponent was *one who was from among his enemies* (singular enmity, directed at Moses' faction). Here he is *ʿaduwwun lahumā* — *an enemy to them both*, to Moses and to the Israelite alike. The commentators draw two implications. It may indicate a different adversary from the one in verse 15. And it may indicate a different kind of enmity: not the enmity of an Egyptian toward an Israelite, but the enmity of a wrongdoer toward whoever stands in his way, which now includes Moses.

Al-Rāzī adds the observation that some readings take these words as spoken not by the enemy but by the Israelite himself — the same man Moses had helped. On that reading, the man panicked when he saw Moses' hand go up, and blurted out the incriminating sentence that cost Moses everything. Al-Ṭabarī transmits this opinion and notes that it explains how the matter became known at all: the cry was overheard.

That reading, if correct, adds a layer of bitter irony. The man Moses had rescued from a beating, at the cost of a death and eventually of his entire life in Egypt, is the man whose tongue destroyed him. The tradition has long noted the Qur'an's realism about this: helping people does not entitle you to their loyalty, and the beneficiary of yesterday's rescue may be tomorrow's liability.

**"Do You Desire to Slay Me, As You Slew a Soul Yesterday?"**

The accusing sentence contains an accusation and a fact, and the Qur'an lets the reader distinguish them.

The fact: *you slew a soul yesterday*. The phrase *nafsan* — *a soul*, without qualification — is itself a comment. The Qur'an's vocabulary for killing is moral before it is physical; the object is always a *nafs*, a soul, a person. The Qur'an's legislation states the principle that gives the word its weight:

> *"Whosoever slays a soul, unless it be for a soul or for corruption in the land, it is as though he had slain mankind altogether; and whosoever gives life to a soul, it is as though he had given life to mankind altogether."* (Qur'an 5:32)

The accusation: *do you desire to slay me*. Moses had no such desire. He intended to intervene in a fight, as he had the day before, and the outcome of the first intervention was precisely what he did not intend a second time. The gap between the two reveals how a reputation forms: a person is judged by outcomes, not by intentions, and the second intervention looked like the first one's continuation.

**"You Desire Naught But to Be a Tyrant on the Earth"**

The word is *jabbār* — and this is one of the most instructive uses of the term in the Qur'an, because it is applied to Moses.

Al-Rāzī's gloss on *jabbār* is worth recording in full: it denotes a person who commits violence without regard for consequences and without seeking the best way to accomplish what he intends. That is, tyranny is not only a matter of *scale* — a *jabbār* is not simply someone with an army. It is a matter of *method*: acting without deliberation, without proportion, and without care for what one's action destroys.

The word appears in the Qur'an in three moral registers, and comparing them is illuminating. It is a divine name — *al-Jabbār*, the Compeller, the One whose will is irresistible and who mends what is broken (59:23) — and there it is perfection. It describes the arrogant rejecters: *"Thus does God seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant"* (40:35). It describes the giants whom the Israelites feared: *"Therein are a people of tyrannical might (jabbārīn)"* (5:22). And it is used by the disbelievers of the Prophet ﷺ himself: *"Or have they said, 'A poet, for whom we await a misfortune of time'?"* — no; rather, they said of him *"a sorcerer, a madman."* A more precise parallel is their description of the Qur'an's warnings as *"fables of the ancients"*. But the Qur'an also reports the tyrants' own vocabulary being thrown at the righteous: Pharaoh's people call Moses *"a lying sorcerer."*

The important point for this verse is that the charge is false as applied to Moses and true as a description of Pharaoh — and that the accuser, in the moment of accusing, is describing himself. The regime that slaughters infants, divides a population, and enslaves a people is the tyranny; the man who threw one punch in defense of a victim is being called a tyrant by the system's beneficiary.

The commentators draw from this a permanent feature of how power talks. Oppression must always describe resistance to itself as violence. The one who strikes back once is called the aggressor; the one who strikes continuously is called order. The Qur'an names this mechanism directly elsewhere:

> *"And when it is said to them, 'Work not corruption in the land,' they say, 'We are only reformers.'"* (Qur'an 2:11)

**"And You Desire Not to Be Among the Workers of Righteousness"**

The second half of the charge: *wa-mā turīdu an takūna mina al-muṣliḥīn* — you have no interest in setting things right.

This is the deeper insult, because it accuses Moses' *motive* rather than his method. The tyrant charge says "you use too much force." This charge says "you do not actually want justice."

The commentators note that this is the standard escalation in any conflict over wrongdoing. When the critic cannot discredit the act, he discredits the intention. And there is a psychological insight buried in it: those who benefit from a disorderly status quo experience every attempt to fix it as aggression, because from their position the existing disorder *is* the peace. A person who has never been beaten in the street by an Egyptian experiences Moses' intervention as an unprovoked assault.

The Qur'an gives the general form of this reaction:

> *"And when it is said to them, 'Believe as the people have believed,' they say, 'Shall we believe as the foolish believe?'"* (Qur'an 2:13)

**Moses' Silence: What the Verse Does Not Record**

Notice that Moses does not answer. The verse ends with the accusation and moves on. There is no defense, no countercharge, no attempt to explain that the death was unintended.

The commentators find this significant, and they connect it to verse 16. Moses has already said everything he intends to say about the matter, and he said it to God. Having made his peace with the Judge, he has no need to litigate before a mob. There is a principle here that the tradition states in the ḥadīth:

> *"Whoever remains silent is saved."* (al-Tirmidhī 2501 — graded ḥasan)

and:

> *"Whosoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

There is also a practical wisdom: in a situation where an accusation is already circulating and the machinery of the state is turning, argumentation recruits no one and incriminates the speaker. Moses' silence was not weakness; it was the first move of a man preparing to survive.

**From Accusation to Death Sentence in One Verse**

The speed of the collapse is the sūrah's own emphasis. Within one verse, Moses moves from being a man with an embarrassing secret to a man publicly identified by name. The next verse brings the runner with the news that the council has decided to kill him.

The commentators note the narrative logic: a single cry in a public place transmitted the information to the one audience that mattered. The Qur'an states the general principle about the fragility of concealment in human affairs:

> *"And had God not repelled some people by means of others, the earth would surely have been corrupted."* (Qur'an 2:251)

But here the more relevant principle is about the limits of prudence. Moses had killed in a moment of heedlessness (v. 15) and returned to the city in a state of watchfulness (v. 18). Neither the concealment nor the vigilance was enough. The tradition draws from this a truth that believers find simultaneously humbling and liberating: a person protects himself by taking means, but he is preserved by God, and the two are not the same thing.

**Lessons for Conduct**

Three practical conclusions emerge from this verse, and the tradition states each in other words elsewhere.

First, about *reputation*. A single uncontrolled act can supply an interpretation of everything you do afterward. Moses' second intervention — which, as far as the text says, never landed a blow — was read through the lens of the first. The Qur'an's counsel is prevention:

> *"And follow not that of which thou hast no knowledge. Truly the hearing and the sight and the heart — each of those will be questioned."* (Qur'an 17:36)

Second, about *accusation*. The believer should expect that doing the right thing in a disordered environment will produce charges of extremism, aggression, or hidden motive. The Qur'an prepares the believer for this explicitly:

> *"Or did you suppose that you would enter the Garden while there had not yet come to you the like of what came to those who passed away before you? Affliction and adversity befell them, and they were shaken, until the Messenger and those who believed with him said, 'When is the help of God?' Truly the help of God is near."* (Qur'an 2:214)

Third, about *silence and timing*. Moses did not argue; he left. The sūrah's own next verse is the instruction to go. Knowing when a situation has passed the point where speech can help is part of the wisdom the desert will teach him.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:20

> **Then from the outskirts of the city a man came running. He said, "O Moses! Truly the notables are conspiring against you, that they might slay you. So leave! Truly I am among your sincere advisers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Runner From the Edge of the City**

*Wa-jāʾa rajulun min aqṣā al-madīnati yasʿā*. The Qur'an gives the man three attributes in one clause, and each is deliberate.

He came *from the outskirts* — *min aqṣā*, from the furthest part. He is not a courtier, not a palace figure, not a member of the council. He is a man from the edge of the city: the quarter where people of low status live, the place furthest from the center where the decision was made.

He came *running* — *yasʿā*, a verb that denotes hurried, purposeful movement, exertion. He had learned something and had run the length of the city to deliver it.

He came as *a man* — anonymous. The Qur'an never names him. Commentators have proposed identities: a believer from the people of Pharaoh, or an Israelite. Al-Rāzī prefers the former, connecting him to the figure described in Sūrah Ghāfir:

> *"And a believing man from among the people of Pharaoh, who had concealed his faith, said, 'Will you slay a man for saying, "My Lord is God," while he has brought you clear proofs from your Lord?'"* (Qur'an 40:28)

That identification is attractive and may be correct, but the Qur'an's deliberate anonymity is itself the point, and it carries the same message as the sister's anonymity in verse 11. God's deliverance arrives through people whom the records do not name. The machinery of Pharaoh is staffed with named officials — Pharaoh, Hāmān, the notables; the machinery of God is staffed with an unnamed girl and an unnamed runner.

**"Truly the Notables Are Conspiring Against You"**

*Inna al-malaʾa yaʾtamirūna bika*. The word *malaʾ* denotes the council of leading men — the aristocracy, the wealthy, the heads of the great houses. It recurs throughout the Qur'an as a technical term for the entrenched elite:

> *"She said, 'O notables, advise me in my affair.'"* (Qur'an 27:32 — the Queen of Sheba)

> *"And the notables of his people, who disbelieved and denied the meeting of the Hereafter... said, 'This is naught but a man like you.'"* (Qur'an 23:33)

> *"And the notables among his people said, 'Truly we see thee in manifest error.'"* (Qur'an 7:60 — the people of Hūd)

The verb *yaʾtamirūna* is from the same root as *amr* (command, affair), in a form meaning *to take counsel together*, *to deliberate*, *to concert a plan*. The connotation is not a judicial proceeding but a plotting: men in a room deciding how to dispose of a problem. They are not investigating; they have decided, and they are arranging the method.

The scene is recognizable in every age, and the Qur'an's portrait of the *malaʾ* is consistent and unflattering. Their objections to the prophets are rarely intellectual; they are about position:

> *"They said, 'We see thee not except as a human being like ourselves, and we see not that any follow thee save the lowliest among us, those of hasty judgment.'"* (Qur'an 11:27)

In Moses' case, the decision to kill him has a specific logic that the sūrah has already established. He was known as a man of Pharaoh's household — the reports in the commentaries say he was called "the son of Pharaoh" — and he had now killed an Egyptian and was evidently siding with the Israelites. That combination was intolerable: an insider's defection, with blood on his hands, in a city whose whole order rested on the subjection of one people to another.

**"That They Might Slay You" — The Death Sentence**

*Li-yaqtulūka*. The purpose is stated without euphemism. This was not an arrest, an interrogation, or a summons. The council's decision was execution.

The commentators note the legal reality of Pharaoh's Egypt: the ruler's will was law, and the death of an Egyptian at the hands of an Israelite — particularly one raised in the palace — would be treated as both a crime and a political threat. The Qur'an's Pharaoh later makes the principle explicit:

> *"He said, 'Shall we slay their sons and spare their women?'"* (Qur'an 40:25) — showing that killing was the regime's ordinary instrument of policy.

For Moses, the important consequence is that the situation had passed beyond management. No explanation, apology, or restitution was available. He had to go.

**"So Leave!" — The Command That Became a Vocation**

*Fa-khruj*. Two letters of command: *get out*. The commentators observe that this is the second time in the sūrah that a divine plan has been executed through an imperative to leave something behind. In verse 7 Moses' mother was told to cast her child into the river; here the child, now a man, is told to leave his home.

The pattern establishes one of the sūrah's deepest themes: the way forward in God's economy frequently requires departure. Abraham left his people; Lot left his city; Moses left Egypt; and Muhammad ﷺ would leave Makkah. The Qur'an names this movement *hijrah*, and it is among the most consequential concepts in the religion:

> *"And whosoever emigrates in the way of God will find on the earth many refuges and abundance."* (Qur'an 4:100)

The Prophet ﷺ defined the term at its largest:

> *"The emigrant is the one who abandons what God has forbidden."* (al-Bukhārī 10)

The commentators are careful not to generalize this into a rule of irresponsibility. Leaving is not the first resort. The Qur'an's own model is to remain and bear witness where bearing witness is possible, and the Prophet ﷺ remained in Makkah for thirteen years before he was told to go. But when the choice becomes obedience or survival, and the two cannot be reconciled in one place, departure is obedience.

**"Truly I Am Among Your Sincere Advisers"**

*Innī laka mina al-nāṣiḥīn*. The word *naṣīḥah* — sincere, disinterested counsel — appeared in verse 12 from the mouth of Moses' sister, and it appeared in the Prophet's definition of the religion:

> *"The religion is sincere good counsel."* They said, "To whom?" He said, *"To God, and to His Book, and to His Messenger, and to the leaders of the Muslims and their common people."* (Muslim 55)

Why does the man add this credential? Because the advice he is giving is drastic, and a stranger running out of the slums telling a prince of the realm to abandon everything is not self-evidently credible. He stakes his credibility in one sentence and offers no argument, no evidence, and no plan.

There is a lesson here about how urgent counsel should be delivered. The man did not soften the message, did not offer alternatives, did not ask Moses to weigh the options. He gave the fact, the conclusion, and the warrant for his own sincerity — and left the decision to the man whose life it was.

The commentators also note what he did *not* do: he did not demand payment, did not ask to accompany Moses, and did not use the information for his own advancement. He ran the length of the city to save a man he had no obligation to, and then disappears from the narrative as anonymously as he entered it.

**The Providence of Bad News Delivered in Time**

Theologically, this verse is a compact demonstration of *luṭf* — the subtle kindness of God that arranges matters so that His servant reaches his good. Consider the timing. The council meets and decides. A man at the far edge of the city hears of it. He runs. He arrives before the arrest party. Moses leaves. The Qur'an's account at verse 21 confirms that the departure happened and that Moses escaped.

Nothing in this sequence is a miracle in the sense of a suspended natural law. It is all human: a meeting, a leak, a run across town, a warning, a flight. And that is precisely the sūrah's point. God's governance operates through the ordinary fabric of cause and effect, invisibly, so that faith is required to see it and so that human beings remain genuinely responsible for what they choose within it.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the principle its canonical formulation:

> *"Be mindful of God, and He will protect you. Be mindful of God, and you will find Him before you. If you ask, ask of God; and if you seek help, seek help from God. And know that if the whole nation were to gather together to benefit you with something, they would benefit you only with something that God had already written for you."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad)

Note the order in that ḥadīth: mindfulness of God comes first, and protection follows. Moses had spent the previous verse in a state of fear and vigilance; the vigilance was the means, and God sent the man who made the vigilance effective.

**The Contrast Between Two Kinds of Council**

The sūrah sets two deliberations against one another across its pages, and the contrast is worth marking.

In this verse, the *malaʾ* — the notables — gather to plan a killing. Their deliberation is secret, self-interested, and lethal.

In verse 20 of Sūrah Yā Sīn, a different kind of man runs a similar errand:

> *"And there came a man running from the farthest part of the city. He said, 'O my people, follow the messengers!'"* (Qur'an 36:20)

Both runners come *from the farthest part of the city*; both run; both speak urgently; both warn. One warns of a plot and one warns of a punishment. The Qur'an presents them as the same figure type: the anonymous believer who does the most consequential thing in the story and is never named.

The implication for the reader is direct. The people who change things are frequently not the people in the council chamber. And the most useful person in a crisis is often the one who simply knows something and runs with it.

**Application: Warning as an Obligation**

The verse establishes a social duty that Islamic law formalizes: the obligation to warn a person of harm one knows is coming to him. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The religion is sincere good counsel."* (Muslim 55)

and:

> *"None of you truly believes until he loves for his brother what he loves for himself."* (al-Bukhārī 13; Muslim 45)

The jurists derived from this and similar texts that concealing knowledge of a plot against a person's life is sinful, and that silence in the face of a known harm is a form of participation. This is a demanding standard, and modern life tests it constantly — in workplaces, institutions, and families where everyone knows what is happening and no one runs.

The man in this verse did run. He is remembered for it in the Qur'an, unnamed, across fourteen centuries.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:21

> **So he left from there, fearful and vigilant. He said, "My Lord! Save me from the wrongdoing people!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"So He Left" — Obedience Without Negotiation**

*Fa-kharaja minhā khāʾifan yataraqqabu*. The verse opens with immediate compliance. The warning came at verse 20; the departure is reported at verse 21 with no interval of deliberation recorded. Moses did not convene a council, did not send a message to the palace to test whether the report was accurate, and did not return home to collect anything.

The commentators note the wisdom in the speed. When a hostile council has decided to kill you, verification is not a virtue. The Prophet ﷺ later taught the same principle in a different idiom:

> *"Flee from the leper as you would flee from a lion."* (al-Bukhārī 5707)

and, more generally, the jurists derived from the Sunnah the rule that preserving life takes precedence over almost every other obligation — one of the five universal necessities (*ḍarūriyyāt*) that the Law exists to protect: religion, life, intellect, lineage, and property.

**The Same Two Words as Verse 18**

*Khāʾifan yataraqqabu* — *fearful and vigilant* — is precisely the description used at verse 18. The commentators regard the repetition as one of the sūrah's most deliberate devices, and its meaning is bleak: nothing has improved. Moses spent a day and a night in terror, received a second warning, and is now in the same terrified state, only worse, because the danger has moved from possible to certain and he is now outside the protection of the only city he has ever known.

The repetition also establishes a truth about the human condition that the sūrah will spend the next verses resolving: fear and watchfulness are the permanent companions of a person in flight. What changes is not the presence of fear but what the frightened person does with it. In verse 18, the watchfulness was passive — waiting to be discovered. Here, it is mobilized — he left.

Al-Rāzī, and after him the spiritual commentators, note that the Qur'an never censures Moses for this fear, and never presents it as a failure of trust. Fear of harm is part of the created constitution of the human being:

> *"Man was created anxious: when evil touches him, fretful; and when good touches him, withholding — except those who pray."* (Qur'an 70:19–22)

The exception in that verse is significant: the cure for constitutional anxiety is not its elimination but the practice that reorients it.

**"My Lord! Save Me" — The Prayer of a Man With Nothing Left**

*Qāla rabbi najjinī mina al-qawmi al-ẓālimīn*. This is Moses' third address to God in the sūrah, and the three form a progression worth tracing, because it is the progression of the whole religious life:

Verse 16: *My Lord, truly I have wronged my own soul; forgive me* — a prayer for the past.
Verse 17: *My Lord, because Thou hast blessed me, I shall never be a supporter of the guilty* — a resolution for the future.
Verse 21: *My Lord, save me from the wrongdoing people* — a plea for the present.

Sin confessed, commitment declared, deliverance sought. The order is instructive: Moses did not ask to be rescued before he had set his own house in order. The tradition has always read this sequence as normative. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Be mindful of God, and He will protect you. Be mindful of God, and you will find Him before you... If you ask, ask of God; and if you seek help, seek help from God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad)

and he taught that supplication is the core of worship:

> *"Supplication is worship."* Then he recited: *"And your Lord says, 'Call upon Me; I will respond to you. Truly those who wax arrogant before My worship shall enter Hell, humbled.'"* (al-Tirmidhī 2969; Abū Dāwūd 1479; Ibn Mājah 3828 — from Qur'an 40:60)

The verb *najjā* is significant: it means to deliver, to rescue, to bring a person safely *out*. It is the word used for Noah:

> *"So when Our Command came... We said, 'Embark therein of every kind two, and thy family — save those against whom the Word has already gone forth.'"* (Qur'an 11:40)

and for the deliverance of the believers generally:

> *"And We delivered those who believed and were reverent."* (Qur'an 41:18)

**"From the Wrongdoing People" — The Object of the Prayer**

Moses does not pray for deliverance from danger, from fear, or from the desert. He prays for deliverance *from the wrongdoing people* — *mina al-qawmi al-ẓālimīn*.

The commentators draw several things from this specification.

First, it names the problem correctly. The danger is not bad luck; it is a specific group of people doing something specific. The precision matters, because vague prayer is often a sign of vague diagnosis.

Second, the word *ẓālimīn* — wrongdoers, oppressors — is a moral judgment, and Moses utters it while fleeing. He does not minimize what they are doing in order to feel better about being hunted. The tradition values this: the believer does not flatter his oppressors, and naming oppression as oppression is not a failure of patience.

Third, and most subtly, the prayer is for deliverance *from* them, not *against* them. Moses asks to be separated from the wrongdoers, not for the wrongdoers to be destroyed. His supplication asks for distance, not revenge. This will be tested and refined in the years to come, when God sends him back to those very people (v. 35, *they shall not reach you*).

Compare the prayer of other prophets in flight, and the pattern is identical. Lot prayed:

> *"He said, 'My Lord, deliver me and my family from what they do.'"* (Qur'an 26:169)

Noah prayed:

> *"He said, 'My Lord, I seek refuge in Thee lest I ask of Thee that whereof I have no knowledge.'"* (Qur'an 11:47)

and, earlier:

> *"So he called upon his Lord: 'Truly I am overcome; so help me.'"* (Qur'an 54:10)

The last of these is especially relevant: the shortest and most honest prayer in the Qur'an, spoken by a man at the end of his resources.

**Theological Note: Flight Does Not Contradict Trust**

A question the commentators raise: if Moses trusted God, why did he flee? Why not stay and let God protect him?

The answer the tradition gives is drawn from the Prophet's own practice. The Hijrah — the emigration from Makkah to Madinah — was the defining act of his mission, and he took elaborate precautions on the way: concealment in a cave, a guide, a circuitous route, and a decoy. When Abū Bakr, seeing the pursuers at the mouth of the cave, said:

> *"If any of them were to look beneath his feet, he would see us,"* the Prophet ﷺ replied: *"What do you think, Abū Bakr, of two whose third is God?"* (al-Bukhārī 3653; Muslim 2381)

The two facts coexist without tension: total reliance on God in the heart, and total use of means in the hands. To abandon the means while claiming reliance was explicitly rejected by the Prophet ﷺ in the well-known exchange with the man who left his camel untethered:

> *"Tie it and then trust in God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2517)

Moses fled. That flight *was* his trust in action.

**The Departure as the Turning Point of the Sūrah**

Structurally, verse 21 is the hinge of the entire narrative. Everything before it has been preparation: birth, rescue, upbringing, gift of wisdom, crime, exposure. Everything after it is formation: Midian, marriage, labor, and then the fire on the mountain.

The commentators observe that Moses' flight from Egypt was the necessary precondition of his mission to Egypt. He had to be removed from the palace before he could be sent back to it, because a man raised inside the system has not yet learned to see it from outside. The ten years in Midian were not an interruption of his preparation; they were the decisive part of it.

There is also a parallel with the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ that the sūrah itself invites. He too was driven out, and the sūrah promises at verse 85 that he too would be returned. The commentators note the consonance: both men had to lose their city in order to be given it, and both learned in exile what they could not have learned at home.

**Practical Fruit: What to Do When Everything Collapses**

This verse is the Qur'an's manual for the hour when a life falls apart, and its counsel is strikingly undramatic.

Do what the situation requires, immediately and without self-deception. Moses left — he did not negotiate with a death sentence.

Name your fear honestly before God. He did not pretend to be calm; he said *My Lord*, and asked.

Ask for exactly what you need. He asked for deliverance from the wrongdoers, in those words, without embellishment.

And leave the outcome where it belongs. The prayer ends the verse; Moses does not describe his own escape plan. The next verse records only that he turned his face in a direction — and even then, he did not know where he was going.

That last detail is the most consoling thing in the passage. A man can be at the absolute end of his resources, with no plan and no destination, and still be exactly where God intends him to be. The sūrah's next verse makes that explicit in the most beautiful sentence in the entire Moses narrative.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:22

> **And when he turned his face toward Midian, he said, "Perhaps my Lord will guide me to the right way."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Beautiful Sentence of Uncertainty in the Qur'an**

*Wa-lammā tawajjaha tilqāʾa Madyana qāla ʿasā rabbī an yahdiyanī sawāʾa al-sabīl*.

The commentators have always lingered over this verse, and for good reason. A hunted man, alone, without provisions, without a destination, without a map, walks out of a country and says: *perhaps my Lord will guide me to the right way*.

The word rendered *perhaps* is *ʿasā*, and it is one of the most discussed particles in the Qur'an. When *ʿasā* is used of God — as it is here, since what is hoped for is God's guidance — the commentators are unanimous that it does **not** indicate doubt. Al-Qurṭubī states the rule plainly: with God, *ʿasā* expresses certainty or near-certainty, because the One who hopes is not in doubt about the One he hopes from. Al-Rāzī adds the pastoral dimension: the wording gives hope to the reader, who is being taught to hope in the same terms.

The particle is therefore doing two things at once. On the divine side, it states a fact: God will guide him. On the human side, it captures the exact texture of Moses' consciousness: he did not know the way, and he said so.

The combination is the definition of trust. Trust is not the confidence that you know the route; it is the confidence that the One who knows the route is with you while you walk it.

**"He Turned His Face Toward Midian" — Why Midian?**

*Tawajjaha tilqāʾa Madyana*. The verb *tawajjaha* is from *wajh*, face; it means to direct one's face, one's whole orientation, in a direction. The same idiom is used for the orientation of prayer:

> *"So turn thy face toward the Sacred Mosque; and wherever you may be, turn your faces toward it."* (Qur'an 2:144)

The choice of Midian requires explanation, and the commentators supply it. Three points are made.

First, geography: Midian lay to the southeast of Egypt, in the northwestern Arabian peninsula, beyond the Sinai. It was outside Pharaoh's jurisdiction. This is the practical reason — Pharaoh's reach did not extend there.

Second, ethnicity: the people of Midian were not Egyptians, and they were the people of the prophet Shuʿayb, whom the Qur'an identifies as sent to Midian (7:85; 11:84; 29:36). The commentators generally — though not unanimously, since the Qur'an does not state it — identify the old man of verse 23 with Shuʿayb. On that reading Moses was walking toward a household of prophecy without knowing it.

Third, and most importantly, Moses did not know the way. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī both transmit that he had no provisions, no money, and no knowledge of the route; he had only a direction and a trust. Al-Ṭabarī adds reports that he had no idea whether Midian was days or weeks away.

This is the detail that makes the verse a permanent comfort. Moses was not walking toward a known destination. He was walking away from a known danger, in a direction that seemed reasonable, with a sentence of trust on his lips.

**The State of the Man Who Left**

The commentators reconstruct from the Qur'an and the transmitted reports what Moses carried with him, and the inventory is close to nothing.

No provisions. The tradition transmits that he had neither food nor mount, and that he walked — some reports say his feet became blistered, and that on arriving at Midian he was too weak to compete with the shepherds at the well. This detail is not in the Qur'an, but it fits the Qur'an's own account, since verse 24 has him sitting in the shade and admitting need.

No money. Verse 24's prayer — *I am in need of any good that Thou mayest send down upon me* — is read by the commentators as just that: a prayer for food.

No companion. He left alone, at speed, in fear.

No status. Whatever standing he had held in Pharaoh's household was gone. He was now a fugitive with a price on his head.

No plan beyond the next hour. And yet — and this is the whole point of the verse — he spoke about *guidance* rather than about rescue.

**"Guide Me" — The Word That Appears Seventeen Times a Day**

The verb is *yahdiyanī*, from *h-d-y*, guidance. The commentators note that Moses asked to be guided *to the right way* — *sawāʾa al-sabīl*, the level, even, balanced road — rather than to a destination. He asked for the road, not for the arrival.

This distinction is one of the most practically valuable in the Qur'an, and it explains why the verse has become a standard supplication for anyone facing an unknown future. The believer is taught to ask for *direction* rather than for *outcomes*, because outcomes are frequently withheld or delayed, while direction can be given at once and is what actually matters.

The same principle is enshrined in the prayer every Muslim recites at least seventeen times a day:

> *"Guide us upon the straight path."* (Qur'an 1:6)

The commentators from the earliest generations noted that the Fātiḥah asks for *hudā* — guidance — and not for any particular result, and they drew from it the rule that the servant asks for the road and leaves the destination to God. Al-Rāzī makes this explicit in his commentary on the Fātiḥah: guidance is the comprehensive good, because whoever is guided reaches whatever is best for him, while a person who obtains a desired outcome without guidance may be ruined by it.

There is a related usage in the Qur'an that sharpens the meaning. Guidance is described as belonging to God alone:

> *"Surely thou dost not guide whomsoever thou lovest, but God guides whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 28:56 — this very sūrah)

Moses asks for what no human being can give and what God gives to whoever asks sincerely.

**The Manner of Asking: From *ʿasā* to Certainty**

The verse's spiritual technology deserves close attention, because the tradition has drawn a specific practice from it.

Moses states his need in the language of hope (*ʿasā*, perhaps), and he states it to God. He does not state it as a demand, and he does not state it as a despairing wish. The commentators identify the posture as *rajāʾ* — hope — which in the Islamic moral psychology is one of the two wings with which the believer flies, the other being *khawf*, fear.

The Prophet ﷺ described the correct relationship of the two:

> *"No one of you should die except while having good hope in God."* (Muslim 2877)

and he warned against its opposite:

> *"Let none of you say, 'O God, forgive me if You will; O God, have mercy on me if You will.' Let him be resolute in his asking, for God does what He wills and there is none who can compel Him."* (al-Bukhārī 6339; Muslim 2679)

The reconciliation between this verse's *perhaps* and that ḥadīth's insistence on resoluteness is worth stating, because it is frequently misunderstood. The *ʿasā* of Moses expresses his own ignorance of the outcome — he did not know whether he would arrive, or how — while his *certainty* was in the One he was asking. The believer is commanded to be resolute about *God's power and generosity* and humble about *his own knowledge of what is best for him*. Both are present in this sentence.

**A Deeper Reading: Guidance as Orientation, Not Only Direction**

The spiritual commentators, among them Ibn ʿAjībah, read *sawāʾa al-sabīl* as more than a road on a map. They take *sawāʾ* — level, balanced, straight — as describing the condition of a life lived in equilibrium, the *ṣirāṭ* that is simultaneously a path through the world and a state of the soul. On this reading Moses is asking: give me the road that is right, and make me right upon it.
