The reading is not imposed on the text; it is drawn from the Qur'an's own usage, where the straight path is defined by its destination and its company:

> *"The path of those upon whom Thou hast bestowed blessing, not of those who incur wrath, nor of those who are astray."* (Qur'an 1:7)

and where guidance is described as something given *after* a person turns:

> *"And those who strive in Our cause, We shall surely guide them upon Our ways."* (Qur'an 29:69)

The last verse is the key to this one. Moses *turned his face* and then asked to be guided. The striving and the guidance are ordered: the servant moves, and God directs the movement.

**Practical Fruit: The Prayer for Anyone Who Does Not Know What Comes Next**

This verse has been used by Muslims in exactly that way for fourteen centuries: the supplication of the person setting out, uncertain, without a plan. It is the sunnah of Moses, and the tradition has always treated the supplications of the prophets as a treasury.

What makes it usable is its honesty. It does not ask for a specific outcome, a specific city, or a specific provision. It asks for the road. And it asks in the awareness that the asker does not know the road.

The situations it fits are the common ones: a person leaving a marriage, a country, a career, a certainty; a young person standing at the edge of adulthood with no map; a family forced out of a home; anyone who has had to walk away from something and has not yet arrived anywhere.

And the verse's answer is immediate in the narrative. Moses says *perhaps my Lord will guide me* in verse 22; in verse 23 he arrives at water and people; in verse 25 he is given shelter and safety; in verse 27 he is offered a marriage and a home. The guidance arrived in the form of a well, two women holding back their flocks, and a job — none of which Moses would have thought to pray for, and all of which were better than anything he would have specified.

That is the final lesson. God answers the prayer for the road with the road, and the road is usually not the one the traveler would have chosen. Moses asked for guidance; he was given Midian. And Midian was where he became Moses.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:23

> **And when he arrived at the wells of Midian, he found there a community of people watering [their flocks]. And he found beside them two women holding back [their flocks]. He said, "What is your errand?" They said, "We water not [our flocks] until the shepherds have driven [theirs] away, and our father is a very old man."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arrival: Water, Crowd, and Two Women Standing Aside**

*Wa-lammā warada māʾa Madyana wajada ʿalayhi ummatan mina al-nāsi yasqūna*. The verb *warada* means to arrive at water — to come down to a watering place. For a man crossing desert without provisions, this is the word of survival. He had asked for the road; the road led to water.

The scene the Qur'an sets is vivid and social. There is a *ummatan mina al-nāsi* — a crowd, acompany of people — watering their animals. And beside them, *min dūnihim*, apart from them, two women *tadhūdāni* — holding back.

The commentators give three reasons for the women's position, and they are not mutually exclusive: they lacked the physical strength to push their animals through to the water; they did not want their flocks to mix with the men's flocks; and they were avoiding the crowding and jostling of the men for reasons of modesty. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both record all three.

Al-Ṭabarī transmits an alternative reading of *tadhūdāni*: that the women were holding *the other people* back from their own flocks, keeping their animals separate. The difference is minor, but it changes the picture from passive waiting to active management.

**The Social World of the Well**

The commentators note what the scene reveals about the setting. Water in that environment was a common resource but a contested one; strength determined access; and those without a man to push for them waited until everyone else had finished. The two women were, in the economy of the well, at the bottom.

This is important because it explains the shape of the whole episode. Moses did not arrive at a palace. He arrived at the one place in the region where the powerless were visibly waiting their turn, and where a stranger with strength could change the queue. The well is where the sūrah's Moses — who has just spent years as a prince and days as a fugitive — begins his real education.

There is also a quiet providence in the detail that he arrived at *water*. Al-Rāzī points out that of all the places in Midian, the well is where a stranger must go — and where a stranger without a tribe must nevertheless appear. God led him to the one location where his presence was inevitable and his service useful.

**"What Is Your Errand?" — A Question, Not a Rescue**

*Qāla mā khaṭbukumā*. The commentators note the restraint of Moses' question. He did not push through the crowd and water their flocks uninvited. He asked.

The word *khaṭb* means matter, affair, situation — a serious business, not a casual one. The question carries concern rather than curiosity, and it respects their agency: he is offering, not taking over.

The traditional reading of this moment, found in al-Qurṭubī and the later commentaries, emphasizes the etiquette involved. A strange man approaching two women alone in a public place is a fraught situation in any society. Moses' conduct is measured: he asks a question about their situation rather than about themselves, he keeps the exchange brief, and he lets them explain.

The Prophet ﷺ later set the standard for exactly this kind of interaction, and the commentators cite it here:

> *"No man is alone with a woman except that Satan is the third of them."* (al-Tirmidhī 1171; Aḥmad — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

Islamic law's rules on *khalwah* (seclusion with a non-maḥram) and on lowering the gaze are not restrictions imposed on this story from outside; they are the codification of the behavior the Qur'an itself models here. The interaction is brief, purposeful, conducted in public, and concluded quickly.

**"We Water Not Until the Shepherds Have Driven Away" — An Answer With Three Facts**

*Qālatā lā nasqī ḥattā yuṣdira al-riʿāʾu*. The women answer with a complete explanation in a single sentence, and the commentators admire its efficiency.

It contains three pieces of information, and each is functional rather than self-dramatizing:

They do not water while the men are there. The reason is not stated but implied — they are holding back out of modesty and lack of strength.

They wait until the shepherds *yuṣdirūna* — drive their flocks away, finish and depart. That is, they will wait until the well is empty.

And their father is *shaykhun kabīr* — a very old man. This is the reason for the whole situation: the person who would normally perform this labor cannot do it.

Notice what the answer does not contain. There is no complaint about the men, no appeal for help, no request, and no self-pity. They state the facts and let the stranger draw his own conclusion. The commentators from al-Qurṭubī onward note this as a mark of dignity: they explained their situation without asking him for anything.

**"Our Father Is a Very Old Man" — The Detail That Explains Everything**

The phrase *wa-abūnā shaykhun kabīrun* carries the episode's social logic. In that time and place, watering flocks was heavy physical work requiring strength and the ability to assert oneself at a crowded well. An elderly father with daughters and no sons was in a structurally vulnerable position.

The commentators note the connection to verse 26, where one of the daughters will say to her father, *hire him*, and to verse 27, where the father offers a marriage. The household had a labor shortage and no male heir; the arrival of a strong, capable, well-mannered stranger was, in that setting, an event of enormous practical consequence.

There is also a parallel with the structure of the Moses story itself that the tradition has drawn out. Moses' own father had been unable to protect him, and his mother had been commanded to place him in the water. Here, another elderly father is unable to perform a necessary task, and another household is in need. The man who was once the helpless infant is now the one with strength to offer — the completion of a long arc of formation.

**Why the Qur'an Tells Us the Women's Words**

The inclusion of direct speech for two unnamed women is itself significant, and modern readers should register it. In a seventh-century Arabian context, and in a narrative set in antiquity, the Qur'an gives these women a voice, an explanation, and an active role in the story's outcome. One of them will speak again at verse 26 with a recommendation that determines Moses' next decade.

The commentators note that the women's conduct is presented entirely positively: modesty in the presence of men, honesty about their situation, refusal to beg, and diligence in doing what work they could. Their father, for his part, sent them to do necessary labor rather than keeping them secluded to the point of helplessness — a balance the jurists have often cited in discussions of women's work and public presence in Islam.

The tradition's general principle, drawn from this and similar texts, is that necessity and dignified conduct govern, and that the Islamic regulation of gender interaction aims at restraint and respect rather than at exclusion from public life. The women of this verse are in the public square, conducting business, speaking to a stranger about a practical matter, with brevity and propriety.

**The Identity of the Father: Shuʿayb or Another?**

The Qur'an does not name the father, and the commentators discuss the identification at length.

The majority position, transmitted by al-Rāzī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr, is that he was Shuʿayb, the prophet sent to Midian, whom the Qur'an names elsewhere:

> *"And unto Midian [We sent] their brother Shuʿayb."* (Qur'an 7:85)

> *"And unto Midian [We sent] their brother Shuʿayb."* (Qur'an 11:84; 29:36)

The biblical account names him Jethro/Reuel, priest of Midian. Muslim commentators generally equate the two.

The minority position, and the one that better respects the Qur'an's silence, is that he was simply a righteous elder of Midian — possibly a descendant of Shuʿayb's community, possibly Shuʿayb himself. Al-Rāzī states the caution plainly: the Qur'an does not say, and the identification rests on transmitted report rather than revelation.

The safe and scholarly position, and the one this commentary adopts, is that the Qur'an's silence is intentional and that the identification is probable but not certain. What the Qur'an *does* establish about the man is more important than his name: he was old, he was righteous, he was generous, he was fair in his contracts, and he judged a stranger on the evidence of his conduct (vv. 25–27).

**The Theological Reading: Provision Arrives at the Moment of Service**

There is a pattern in the Qur'an's narratives of exile that this verse implements, and the tradition has named it: the fugitive arrives at water, performs a service, and is given a home.

The same structure appears in the story of Hagar and Ishmael — a mother and child in the desert, water appears, and a settlement grows. It appears in the story of the Prophet ﷺ at Madinah — a stranger arrives, and the Ansār compete to host him. And it appears here.

The mechanism is worth stating plainly, because it is the opposite of what a fugitive expects. Moses arrived destitute and did not ask for anything. He saw a need within his capacity and met it. And the meeting of that need is what opened the door to everything that followed — safety, employment, marriage, and eventually the fire on the mountain.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle generally:

> *"Whoever relieves a believer of a distress of the distresses of this world, God will relieve him of one of the distresses of the Day of Resurrection. And whoever eases the difficulty of one in difficulty, God will ease for him difficulty in this world and the Hereafter. God is in the aid of His servant as long as the servant is in the aid of his brother."* (Muslim 2699)

And, in the negative formulation:

> *"He who does not show mercy to people, God will not show mercy to him."* (al-Bukhārī 7376; Muslim 2319)

**Application**

Three things are worth carrying from this verse.

First, *arrival*. Moses asked for guidance and was led to water. Guidance frequently arrives as an ordinary place with ordinary people doing ordinary business, not as a sign in the sky. The believer learning to recognize guidance should look for the well in front of him.

Second, *attention*. Many people were at that well. Only one noticed the two women standing apart. The moral life consists substantially of noticing who is being left out of the queue — in an office, a family, a community, a conversation — and asking, *what is your situation?*

Third, *service before request*. Moses did not petition for help; he looked for what he could do. This is the sūrah's own cure for the helplessness of exile, and it is available to anyone. The person who has lost everything can still ask what someone else needs.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:24

> **So he watered [their flocks] for them. Then he turned toward the shade and said, "My Lord! Truly I am in need of any good that Thou mayest send down upon me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Deed: One Sentence, No Commentary**

*Fa-saqā lahumā*. Four letters in Arabic: *so he watered for them*. The Qur'an does not describe the effort, does not mention the crowd, does not say whether the shepherds objected, and does not report any words exchanged.

The commentators note the economy and draw their own conclusion from it: the act was significant enough to change Moses' life and is recorded in six letters because its significance lies in the act itself, not in its drama. Al-Rāzī observes that the Qur'an's silences are as instructive as its statements — had there been an altercation, or had Moses used his status to intimidate, the narrative would have had to mention it. What is left is a man who saw a need, had the strength to meet it, and did so without negotiation.

The preposition *lahumā* — *for them* — is worth noting. He did not merely water the flocks; he watered them *on their behalf*, as their agent. The service was done in their interest and at their request implied by their situation.

**The Popular Accounts and the Discipline Applied to Them**

The commentaries transmit a well-known elaboration: that a heavy stone covered the mouth of a well, requiring ten men to lift it, and that Moses lifted it alone. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī all record versions of this report, and all treat it with caution.

Al-Rāzī's handling is the model of critical method in the tradition. He records the story, notes that it is not in the Qur'an, observes that the plain sense of the text requires no miracle at all — Moses was a strong man who could move animals to water, which is exactly what the women's situation called for — and concludes that the addition is unnecessary. He states the principle that the Qur'an's account should not be burdened with reports whose chains cannot be established and whose content is not needed.

This is the correct discipline, and it is worth stating because the same material circulates widely in popular storytelling. The Qur'an's Moses does not need to lift a stone to be impressive. His greatness in this verse is that he was hungry, frightened, and destitute, and he served two strangers anyway.

**"Then He Turned Toward the Shade"**

*Thumma tawallā ilā al-ẓilli*. After the exertion, he withdrew to whatever shade was available. Al-Ṭabarī transmits that it was the shade of a tree; al-Rāzī infers from the detail that it was the hot part of the day — which is consistent with the account elsewhere that the people of that region watered at midday.

The commentators note the physical realism. This is a man who has walked out of Egypt without provisions, has just performed hard labor, and is now sitting alone in whatever shelter he can find. Nothing about the scene is glamorous. The Qur'an's heroes are not depicted as immune to hunger, heat, or exhaustion, and this is part of what makes them usable as models.

**"My Lord! Truly I Am in Need of Any Good That Thou Mayest Send Down Upon Me"**

*Qāla rabbi innī limā anzalta ilayya min khayrin faqīr*. This is one of the most frequently quoted supplications in the Islamic tradition, and it deserves close reading, because its phrasing is unusually rich.

The construction is front-loaded: *limā anzalta ilayya min khayrin* — *for whatever good You send down to me* — comes before *faqīr* — *I am in need*. The effect in Arabic is to place the emphasis on the *source* and the *character* of the good before naming the need. He is not saying "I am hungry"; he is saying "whatever good comes down from You, I am the one who needs it."

Three features of the wording deserve attention.

*First, the indeterminacy of the request.* Moses does not name what he wants. He says *any good* — *min khayrin*, an indefinite "some good of whatever kind." The commentators praise this. A specific request presumes to know what would be best; a general request leaves the determination to the Giver. The verse therefore teaches the etiquette of asking: state the need, not the specification.

*Second, the word faqīr.* The root *f-q-r* denotes poverty, need, and — in the usage of the lexicographers — a state of essential dependence upon something else. It is used both materially and spiritually. The same word appears in the Qur'an's classification of the recipients of charity (*"The alms are only for the poor [fuqarāʾ] and the needy [masākīn],"* 9:60) and, more significantly, in the verse that defines the human condition before God:

> *"O mankind! You are the poor (fuqarāʾ) in relation to God; and God — He is the Self-Sufficient, the Praised."* (Qur'an 35:15)

The word therefore carries a theological weight beyond hunger. Moses is describing himself as a creature before his Creator: needy by nature, dependent by definition. The Sufi tradition built an entire vocabulary on this verse — *faqr*, spiritual poverty, is the recognition that one has nothing of one's own — and the commentators record the saying that *al-faqru fakhrī*, "poverty is my pride," attributed to the Prophet ﷺ (though its chain has been debated by the ḥadīth scholars, its meaning is anchored in verses like 35:15).

*Third, the word anzalta — "send down."* The verb is used for revelation, for rain, and for provision sent from above. By using it for his need, Moses places his hunger in the same category as revelation and rain: something that descends from God and cannot be produced by human effort. The Qur'an uses the same verb for material provision:

> *"And He sends down for you from the sky provision."* (Qur'an 40:13)

**Why the Prayer Is Made in Public and Not Demanded From Anyone**

An important moral detail: Moses is sitting in the shade, near people, and he prays to God rather than asking the shepherds or the women for food.

The commentators note this as a mark of his dignity (*ʿiffah*) — the self-restraint that keeps a person from begging even when he is desperate. The tradition extols this quality in the strongest terms. The Prophet ﷺ described it in his famous description of those who will be shaded on the Day of Resurrection, and he said:

> *"The upper hand is better than the lower hand; the upper hand is the one that gives, and the lower hand is the one that receives."* (al-Bukhārī 1429; Muslim 1033)

and he praised those whose need is invisible:

> *"One of you takes his rope, goes to the mountain, brings a bundle of firewood on his back, and sells it — and thereby God preserves his honor — that is better for him than asking people, whether they give him or refuse him."* (al-Bukhārī 1471)

The Prophet ﷺ also made the supplication against the opposite condition:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from poverty, scarcity, and humiliation; and I seek refuge in You from wronging others or being wronged."* (Abū Dāwūd 1544 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The lesson is not that asking for help is forbidden — it is not, and necessity permits it — but that the believer's first movement in need is toward God, and that dependence on people is to be avoided where one has any alternative.

**The Structure of the Supplication and Why It Is Still Used**

The tradition has treated this verse as a model *duʿāʾ* for anyone in need of anything — provision, employment, a spouse, a child, relief from a difficulty — and the reasons are visible in the text.

It opens with *Rabbi*, the intimate vocative used by the prophets throughout the Qur'an. It acknowledges the state without dramatizing it. It declines to specify the form of the answer. And it attributes the good entirely to the One from whom it descends.

The Prophet ﷺ taught the same structure in a general supplication:

> *"O God, I ask You for guidance, piety, chastity, and sufficiency."* (Muslim 2721)

and he taught that God is ashamed to turn away empty hands:

> *"Your Lord is generous and shy; when His servant raises his hands to Him, He is shy to return them empty."* (Abū Dāwūd 1488; al-Tirmidhī 3556 — graded ḥasan)

And the conditions of an answered supplication are stated in the well-known ḥadīth:

> *"Then mention [the case of] a man who has traveled a long distance and is disheveled and covered with dust; he stretches his hands toward the sky, saying, 'O Lord! O Lord!' — while his food is unlawful, his drink is unlawful, his clothing is unlawful, and he has been nourished with what is unlawful. How, then, can he be answered?"* (Muslim 1015)

Moses at the well is the exact opposite of that man: a traveler, disheveled, hungry — and nourished by nothing unlawful. The commentators note that he was the ideal figure of the answered supplicant: in need, honest about it, and asking the One who sends down.

**What Happened Next: The Answer's Form**

The answer came within one verse, and in a form Moses could not have specified. He asked for "any good" and was given: an invitation to a meal, safety from his pursuers, employment, a wife, and a decade of stability.

The tradition draws a specific lesson from this pattern. The believer who prays for "some good" receives good; the believer who prays for a specific thing may receive it and be ruined by it, or may be refused and given something better without recognizing it. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"There is no Muslim who supplicates to God with a supplication that contains no sin and no severing of kinship ties, but that God gives him one of three things: He hastens his answer in this world, or He stores it for him in the Hereafter, or He averts from him an equivalent evil."* They said, "Then we shall ask much." He said, *"God is more [generous than that]."* (Aḥmad 11133; al-Ḥākim — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**Application: The Service-and-Need Sequence**

Finally, note the order of verse 24, because it contains the whole practical wisdom of the passage.

Moses served first (*he watered for them*) and asked afterward (*I am in need*). He did not calculate that helping these women might lead to an invitation; he helped, and then, alone in the shade, he stated his need to God.

That sequence is the opposite of the transactional instinct. It is not "I will help you so that you will help me." It is "I will help you because you need it, and I will tell God about my own need, and leave the connection between the two to Him."

For anyone in a precarious position — unemployed, newly arrived, without resources, starting over — this is the sūrah's instruction: do the good within reach, state your need to God honestly, and refuse to beg from those whom you have just served. The connection will be made, as it was made here, by the One who sends down.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:25

> **Then one of the two came to him, walking bashfully. She said, "Truly my father summons you, that he might render unto you a reward for having watered [our flocks] for us." When he came and recounted his story unto him, he said, "Fear not. You have been saved from the wrongdoing people."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Approach: "Walking Bashfully"**

*Fa-jāʾathu iḥdāhumā tamshī ʿalā istiḥyāʾin*. The Qur'an describes how she walked, and the description has been discussed by commentators in every generation.

The phrase *ʿalā istiḥyāʾin* — literally "upon modesty," or "with bashfulness" — conveys a walk marked by *ḥayāʾ*, the quality the Islamic tradition regards as the distinguishing mark of faith. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Modesty (ḥayāʾ) is a branch of faith."* (al-Bukhārī 9; Muslim 35)

and:

> *"Modesty brings nothing but good."* (al-Bukhārī 6117; Muslim 37)

The commentators describe what this walking looked like: a lowered gaze, an unhurried step, a covered bearing, no flirtatiousness, and no unnecessary words. Ibn Kathīr transmits the report attributed to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb that she walked with her garment drawn to her face — not out of aversion, but out of the natural reserve of a modest woman approaching a strange man on an errand.

It is important to state what this verse is **not** saying. The bashfulness here is not weakness, not shame, and not oppression. This is the same woman who, in the next verse but one, will speak to her father with the confidence of someone making a hiring recommendation and will state her judgment about a man's qualifications in two precise words. Modesty and competence are not opposites, and the Qur'an's depiction of her holds both together without strain.

**Why She Came and Not Her Sister**

The Qur'an says *one of the two*. The classical commentators generally identify her as the elder, and some transmit that the two daughters drew lots or that the father sent the one who had spoken with Moses. Others note simply that she was the one delegated, and that the errand was a family matter requiring only one person.

The structural point is worth making: the woman who came is the one whose judgment will govern the next decade of Moses' life (v. 26). The Qur'an gives that role to a person who was, three verses earlier, standing aside at a well because she lacked the strength to push through a crowd of shepherds.

**"Truly My Father Summons You" — The Invitation**

*Inna abī yadʿūka*. Note the formality and the clarity of the message. She does not invite him on her own behalf, does not extend a personal invitation, and does not leave him guessing about the terms. She conveys a summons from her father, the male head of the household, on a matter of business.

The commentators note the propriety of the entire arrangement: the father initiates; the daughter conveys; the stranger is invited to a household rather than to a private meeting; and the purpose is stated openly — *that he might render unto you a reward*.

The word *ajr* — reward, wage, recompense — is significant because it establishes the moral frame of what follows. This is not charity; it is payment for work done. Islamic law places enormous emphasis on the obligation to pay a worker, and the Prophet ﷺ stated it in the severest terms:

> *"Give the worker his wage before his sweat dries."* (Ibn Mājah 2443 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

and, in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"God says: 'There are three whose adversary I shall be on the Day of Resurrection... a man who hires a worker, takes full work from him, and does not give him his wage.'"* (al-Bukhārī 2227)

The old man's instinct — to pay for the service rendered before any relationship developed — is therefore a mark of his righteousness, and it sets the moral tone of everything that follows in the marriage arrangement.

**The Reports of Moses' Conduct on the Walk**

The commentaries transmit a famous detail: that Moses asked the woman to walk behind him and indicate the direction, so that he would not be walking behind her and looking at her; and that the wind moved her garment and he told her to direct him by throwing a stone to indicate turns. These reports appear in al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, and elsewhere, and they are transmitted from Ibn Masʿūd and others.

Al-Rāzī's handling is again instructive. He records the reports, notes that they are not from the Qur'an, and observes that the plain text already establishes everything necessary: a modest woman approached, delivered a message, and left. The additional detail, whatever its chain, is consistent with the Qur'an's depiction of both parties but is not required by it.

What the reports do establish, and what is uncontested, is the principle that the Qur'anic model for interaction between men and women in such circumstances is one of restraint, purposefulness, and brevity.

**"He Recounted His Story Unto Him" — *Al-Qaṣaṣ* Appears in the Text**

*Fa-lammā jāʾahu wa-qaṣṣa ʿalayhi al-qaṣaṣa*. Here is the word from which the sūrah takes its name, used twice in one phrase: *he recounted to him the story* — *qaṣṣa ʿalayhi al-qaṣaṣ*.

The commentators note that the phrase is emphatic and complete: the telling and the thing told share a root, which in Arabic produces an intensive, comprehensive sense. Moses told him the whole account.

What did he tell him? The Qur'an does not say, but the content is inferable from what he had to explain: who he was, why he was in Midian, that he had killed a man in Egypt, that the authorities were seeking him, and that he had fled. He told a stranger that he was a fugitive from a capital crime.

This is a remarkable act of candor, and the commentators remark on the discernment behind it. Moses had observed the household enough to judge its character — the father who sent his daughters to work rather than to beg, the daughters who waited their turn with dignity, and the immediate offer of payment for a service rendered. A man with those qualities was worth telling the truth to.

There is a practical wisdom here about disclosure. Concealment from an oppressor is obligatory; concealment from a potential ally is often the very thing that prevents the alliance. Moses had to make a judgment about which kind of person he was facing, and the household's conduct gave him the evidence.

**"Fear Not. You Have Been Saved From the Wrongdoing People"**

*Qāla lā takhaf najawta mina al-qawmi al-ẓālimīn*. The old man's response is immediate and specific, and it answers the exact prayer Moses had made in verse 21: *My Lord, save me from the wrongdoing people*. Between the prayer and its answer, eight verses; the words of the answer, spoken by a stranger in Midian, echo the words of the request.

The commentators note two things about the assurance.

First, its ground: *Pharaoh has no authority in this land*. Al-Ṭabarī transmits this as the plain meaning — the jurisdiction of Egypt ended at the border, and Midian was outside it. The old man was stating a political fact, not merely offering comfort. He knew who Pharaoh was, and he knew what Pharaoh's reach was and was not.

Second, its authority: how could he know? The commentators answer that a stranger arriving destitute from Egypt, with a story of flight, was plainly beyond the reach of anyone's extradition in that era; and — more importantly — the Qur'an presents the old man as a man of discernment and, on the majority reading, as a prophet. His assurance carries the weight of knowledge, not merely of optimism.

The word *najawta* is the same root as the verb in Moses' own prayer (*najjinī*). The answer matches the request exactly.

**Theological Reading: Answered Prayer Disguised as Human Kindness**

This verse is one of the clearest examples in the Qur'an of how divine answers are delivered. Moses asked God for deliverance; God delivered him through a well, two women, an invitation, and a conversation with an old man.

The tradition draws a constant lesson from this: God's answers arrive through created means, and to credit only the means is to miss the Giver, while to despise the means is to misunderstand how the world is governed. The correct posture is to receive the means with gratitude to the One who sent them.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the complementary principle:

> *"Whoever is not grateful to people is not grateful to God."* (Abū Dāwūd 4811; al-Tirmidhī 1954 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

Moses' response in the narrative is to accept the household's offer, work faithfully for a decade, and marry into the family. Gratitude for the means is expressed by using them.

**Practical Fruit: How a Person Is Judged in This Story**

It is worth noticing what qualified Moses for shelter, and it was not his past.

He was a fugitive, a killer by accident, a man without money, references, or a tribe. What he had was: strength used in someone else's service, restraint in his conduct with women, honesty about his situation, and a visible trust in God.

The old man judged him on the evidence of one afternoon's conduct and one conversation. The commentators note the parallel with the hiring practices the Prophet ﷺ endorsed, in which reliability (*amānah*) and competence (*quwwah*) are the two criteria — the very two words the daughter will use in verse 26.

And there is a lesson here for anyone starting over in a new place with a damaged history. Moses arrived with the worst possible résumé and the best possible conduct, and the conduct carried him. The tradition has never tired of pointing this out: what you are in the place you arrive at matters more than what happened in the place you left.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:26

> **One of the two said, "O my father! Hire him. Surely the best [man] you can hire is the strong, the trustworthy."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Words That Became a Standard**

*Qālat iḥdāhumā yā abati istaʾjirhu inna khayra mani istaʾjarta al-qawiyyu al-amīn*.

With this sentence, one of two women standing at a well in Midian established the criterion by which appointments have been judged in the Islamic tradition ever since: *al-qawiyyu al-amīn* — the strong, the trustworthy.

The commentators have always noted the compression. She did not praise Moses' appearance, his lineage, his wealth, or his prospects. She named the two qualities that determine whether a person can be entrusted with work, and she named them in the order that matters: capacity first, integrity second — because capacity without integrity is dangerous, and integrity without capacity is useless.

**Why Her Testimony Carried Weight**

The question the commentators raise is obvious: how did she know? She had met this man for a few minutes at a well.

The answer they give is that she had observed exactly the things that reveal character, and that the observation of a single afternoon can be more reliable than years of reputation.

She had seen his *strength* (*quwwah*): he had moved their flocks to water and managed animals in a crowded place — labor she and her sister were unable to perform and that the shepherds had been unwilling to let them perform. This was not abstract physical power; it was strength exercised, and exercised on their behalf.

She had seen his *trustworthiness* (*amānah*): he had spoken to them briefly and with restraint, had asked about their situation rather than about themselves, had performed the service without bargaining for payment, had gone to the shade rather than pressing his claim on them, and had not followed her to the house but answered a summons. The commentators add the transmitted report — whose chain al-Rāzī questions but whose moral content is undeniable — that he asked her to walk ahead of him and indicate the way, so that he would not be looking at her as they walked.

The lesson the tradition draws is about the epistemology of character. Character is not hidden; it is displayed in exactly the situations this narrative describes: when someone has power over you and does not use it, when there is no one watching, when there is nothing to gain, and when a service is rendered without immediate compensation.

**"O My Father" — The Etiquette of Advice to a Parent**

*Yā abati*. The commentators note the form of address: a term of tenderness and respect, not a challenge. She was recommending a course of action to her father about the management of his household and his property — a matter in which he was the decision-maker — and she did it with deference.

The verse therefore models something the tradition values: a person may offer counsel to a parent or a superior, provided the counsel is offered with respect and left to the other's judgment. She does not say "I have decided to hire him"; she says *hire him*, and gives her reason. The decision remained her father's, and he acted on it.

The Islamic etiquette of *naṣīḥah* — sincere counsel — is unconditional in its object but highly structured in its manner. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The religion is sincere good counsel."* They said, "To whom, O Messenger of God?" He said, *"To God, and to His Book, and to His Messenger, and to the leaders of the Muslims and their common people."* (Muslim 55)

The scholars note that counsel given in public can become humiliation, whereas counsel given privately and respectfully is *naṣīḥah* in its proper form. This verse, with its intimate address and its single sentence of reasoning, is the Qur'anic model.

**"Hire Him" — The Recommendation and What It Rests On**

*Istaʾjirhu* — from *ajr*, wage; the verb means to employ someone for a wage. Her proposal is a business proposition, and its logic is economic: the household needs labor, this man has demonstrated capacity and reliability, therefore employ him.

This is worth noting because of what it is not. It is not a proposal that her father adopt him, or shelter him out of charity, or marry him to her. It is a recommendation of employment, made on the merits, by a person with knowledge of the household's needs.

The commentators draw from this the legitimacy of a woman conducting and opining on business affairs. The jurists discussed the verse in the context of women's legal capacity, and the conclusion of the schools is that a woman of sound mind has full capacity to contract, to own, to trade, and to give testimony in matters within her competence. The Prophet's first wife, Khadījah, was a merchant who employed the Prophet ﷺ before she married him — a fact the tradition has always set beside this verse as its living commentary.

**The Two Criteria in the Prophetic Tradition**

The pairing *al-qawiyyu al-amīn* became canonical in Islamic administrative thought, and it is directly attested in the Qur'an elsewhere. When Joseph sought office in Egypt, he stated his qualification in the same two terms:

> *"He said, 'Place me over the storehouses of the land; truly I am a knowing guardian (ḥafīẓun ʿalīm).'"* (Qur'an 12:55)

And when the daughter of the righteous man in Sūrah al-Kahf advised her father about Moses (in the account of Moses and al-Khiḍr), the structure of the recommendation is similar.

The Prophet ﷺ made the principle explicit in his appointments. He said:

> *"Whoever is appointed over the affairs of the Muslims and appoints a man while he knows that there is someone better for the Muslims than him, he has betrayed God and His Messenger and the believers."* (Reported by al-Ḥākim and al-Bayhaqī; the meaning is supported by authentic narrations)

And he stated the negative rule with characteristic severity:

> *"When a matter is entrusted to someone other than its people, then await the Hour."* (al-Bukhārī 59)

The scholars glossed "its people" as those who possess both competence and integrity. The caliph ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, whose administrative practice the jurists cited constantly, wrote to his governors that he had appointed them not because of their lineage or wealth but because of what he had seen of their reliability, and he instructed them to appoint on the same basis.

The modern application is immediate. The verse is the Islamic standard for every hiring committee, every appointment, and every election: capacity and integrity, in that order of inquiry, with neither substituted for the other. Integrity without capacity produces a pious failure; capacity without integrity produces a competent disaster.

**Why Integrity Is the Rarer of the Two**

The commentators note a sociological observation embedded in the verse's reception: strength is common, and trustworthiness is rare. Moses' strength had been visible in a single act at a well; his *amānah* required the whole sequence of his conduct to establish.

The tradition expanded this into a full moral psychology. *Amānah* in Islam is not limited to financial honesty; it is the comprehensive virtue of returning to each thing what is due to it. The Qur'an uses the word for the trust that the heavens, the earth, and the mountains refused to bear:

> *"Truly We offered the trust (al-amānah) to the heavens and the earth and the mountains, and they refused to bear it and were afraid of it; but man bore it. Truly he has proven himself unjust and ignorant."* (Qur'an 33:72)

And the Prophet ﷺ made its absence a sign of the collapse of the religious sense:

> *"When the trust is lost, then await the Hour."* It was asked, "How is it lost, O Messenger of God?" He said, *"When a matter is entrusted to someone other than its people, then await the Hour."* (al-Bukhārī 59)

and, in a famous narration:

> *"The hypocrite has three signs: when he speaks, he lies; when he promises, he breaks his promise; and when he is entrusted, he betrays the trust."* (al-Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59)

**A Note on the Woman's Role in the Narrative**

It is worth pausing on what the sūrah has done in three verses. Two women appear at a well, marginalized by strength. One of them speaks two sentences of direct speech in the entire Qur'anic account. And those two sentences determine the next ten years of the life of one of the greatest messengers of God.

The tradition has consistently read this as elevating rather than incidental. The woman's judgment was sound, her reasoning was precise, her address to her father was respectful, and her recommendation was accepted. Nothing about her gender qualified or limited the value of her counsel.

The Prophet ʿĀʾishah, from whom a vast body of the religion's law and practice is transmitted, is the standing proof in the Islamic tradition that women's judgment in public and religious matters is authoritative; and this verse is one of the texts the tradition has set beside her example.

**Application**

For employers and those who appoint: the verse supplies the specification. Do not hire on affinity, charisma, or credentials alone. Ask whether the person can do the work, and then ask whether the person can be trusted with it. The second question is harder to answer and more important.

For employees and those who seek responsibility: the verse supplies the résumé. Be strong — acquire real competence, and be able to demonstrate it. And be trustworthy — because a person of average ability and absolute reliability is more sought after, and more blessed, than a brilliant person who cannot be trusted.

And for anyone in the position of advising: this verse shows how it is done. One sentence, one reason, addressed respectfully, with the decision left to the one who bears responsibility for it. The daughter of Midian spoke for perhaps ten seconds. She was right, and she was heard.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:27

> **He said, "I desire to marry you to one of these two daughters of mine, on condition that you hire yourself to me for eight years. But if you complete ten, that will be of your own accord. And I desire not to be hard upon you. You shall find me, if God wills, to be among the righteous."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Proposal: Marriage and Labor in One Contract**

The old man's response to his daughter's recommendation is swift and generous. He does not merely hire Moses; he offers him his daughter in marriage, with the hire as the bridal gift.

*Innī urīdu an unkiḥaka iḥdā ibnatayya hātayni ʿalā an taʾjuranī thamāniya ḥijaj* — *I desire to marry you to one of these two daughters of mine, on condition that you hire yourself to me for eight years.*

The commentators note the structure and its social logic. The household needed labor; Moses needed safety, a home, and a lawful family; and the father had two daughters and no son. The arrangement met every need at once, and it did so through lawful contract rather than through dependency or charity.

This is one of the clearest and most frequently cited texts in the Islamic law of marriage, and it establishes several principles at once.

**The Mahr as Service: A Valid and Ancient Form**

The bridal gift (*mahr*, *ṣadāq*) in this contract is *service for eight years*. Islamic law permits the *mahr* to be anything of value that is lawful, known, and capable of being delivered — money, property, a benefit, or, as here, work.

The Qur'an establishes the *mahr* as the wife's right:

> *"And give the women their bridal gift as a free gift; but if they, of their own accord, remit unto you a part of it, then enjoy it with right good cheer."* (Qur'an 4:4)

The Prophet ﷺ validated a *mahr* consisting of teaching the Qur'an. A woman offered herself to him, and he said:

> *"I have married her to you for what you have of the Qur'an."* (al-Bukhārī 5087; Muslim 1425)

— that is, for teaching her the Qur'an. And the jurists derived from these texts the rule that anything of legitimate value may serve as *mahr*: the majority permit service, as in this verse, some requiring that the service be specified and bounded.

The point of the institution is worth stating plainly, because it is frequently misunderstood. The *mahr* is not a bride-price paid to a family; it is a gift given to the wife herself, which becomes her exclusive property and over which no one — husband, father, or anyone else — has any claim:

> *"And if you desire to exchange one wife for another, and you have given one of them a treasure, take not aught from it."* (Qur'an 4:20)

In this verse, the service is rendered to the father rather than to the bride, which the jurists explain on the basis that the daughter, being part of the household whose labor was being lost, benefited from the arrangement, and that the father acted as her representative with her consent — consent that is explicit in the account, since it was her recommendation that initiated the whole matter (v. 26).

**The Term: Eight Years, With Ten as an Option**

*Thamāniya ḥijaj; fa-in atmamta ʿashran fa-min ʿindika* — *eight years; but if you complete ten, that will be of your own accord.*

Two details deserve attention.

First, the term is *specified and bounded*. The contract is not open-ended, and the word *ḥijaj* (years, from *ḥajj*, a completed circuit) makes the unit unambiguous. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The Muslims are bound by their conditions [in contracts]."* (al-Bukhārī, in a *muʿallaq* report; Abū Dāwūd 3594)

Islamic law's treatment of contracts requires that the subject, the term, and the compensation all be known; ambiguity in any of these invalidates the agreement.

Second, the *option of extension runs one way only*. The eight years are owed; the additional two are voluntary — *min ʿindika*, "from yourself," meaning an act of generosity on Moses' part, not an obligation. The father explicitly declines to bind him to the longer term.

This is the clearest possible expression of the verse's governing spirit, and it is why the verse is cited in the law of contracts: the stronger party here is the father, who has a citizen's security and a fugitive's dependence, and he uses his advantage to *reduce* what he could demand.

**"I Desire Not to Be Hard Upon You"**

*Wa-mā urīdu an ashuqqa ʿalayka*. The root *sh-q-q* means difficulty, hardship, splitting apart — the same root as the Qur'an's description of the religion's ease:

> *"God desires ease for you and desires not hardship for you."* (Qur'an 2:185)

> *"He has chosen you and placed no hardship (ḥaraj) upon you in the religion."* (Qur'an 22:78)

The commentators read this clause as the father's promise about the *quality* of the years, not merely their number: he will not load the work beyond what is reasonable, he will not exploit Moses' vulnerability, and he will not use the marriage arrangement to extract labor beyond the contract.

This is a striking thing for a man to say to a penniless foreigner he has just met, and the scholars have always treated it as the mark of *iḥsān* — excellence — in contracts: not merely to abide by the letter of an agreement, but to intend the other party's ease within it.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God has prescribed excellence (iḥsān) in all things."* (Muslim 1955)

and:

> *"May God have mercy on a man who is easy-going when he sells, when he buys, and when he seeks payment."* (al-Bukhārī 2076)

**"You Shall Find Me, If God Wills, to Be Among the Righteous"**

*Wa-satajidunī in shāʾa Allāhu mina al-ṣāliḥīn*. The father's closing words are a promise qualified by the conditional phrase — *if God wills*.

The commentators note the etiquette here, and it is the etiquette the Qur'an commands explicitly:

> *"And say not of anything, 'Truly I shall do that tomorrow,' without adding, 'if God wills.'"* (Qur'an 18:23–24)

The qualifier is not a formality. A promise about future conduct is a promise about something entirely outside the speaker's control, since a person cannot guarantee his own character or even his own survival until the moment arrives. The Prophet ﷺ said, on the authority of Sulaymān, that he had said he would visit his wives in turn and then added *in shāʾ Allāh* — and the ḥadīth records the Prophet's comment on the importance of the exception (al-Bukhārī 5240; Muslim 1474).

There is also a substantive point about *ṣalāḥ* — righteousness. The father is not promising to be generous; he is promising to be *righteous*, which in the Qur'anic vocabulary means to be in right relationship with God and therefore right in one's dealings. He grounds his promise in his religion rather than in his temperament.

**How Many Years Did Moses Serve?**

The question is discussed at length in the commentaries, and it is genuinely open.

The transmitted reports divide into two camps. Some hold that Moses completed the full ten years — the longer and more complete term — and this is reported from Ibn ʿAbbās. Others hold that he completed eight and that the additional two were not served.

Al-Rāzī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr all transmit the famous exchange in which a Kharijite questioned Ibn ʿAbbās about exactly this point, seeking to trap him in an inconsistency, and Ibn ʿAbbās replied that the Prophet of God fulfilled the longer and more complete of the two terms. The story is well known in the commentarial literature and is reported through several channels.

Al-Ṭabarī, characteristically, records the disagreement and declines to resolve it definitively, noting that the Qur'an itself says only *when Moses had completed the term* (v. 29) without specifying which term.

The spiritual reading, common in the tradition, is that Moses served the full ten — because a righteous man does not stop at the minimum with a father-in-law who has given him his daughter, his safety, and his home. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The best of people are those who are best in repaying [kindness]."* (al-Bukhārī 2304; Muslim 1601)

**Marriage in the Islamic Frame: What This Verse Shows**

Several features of the Qur'anic model of marriage appear in this one verse, and they are worth marking because they constitute the tradition's answer to a great deal of modern confusion.

Marriage is a *contract* (*ʿaqd*), entered by offer and acceptance, with terms.

The woman's consent is integral. Here the daughter's recommendation preceded the offer, and the Qur'an presents the arrangement as one in which she is a participant, not an object transferred.

The *mahr* belongs to the wife, is specified in advance, and may take any lawful form.

The family is involved, but the involvement is protective rather than coercive — the father acts on his daughter's recommendation rather than over her objection.

And the purpose is a household, not a transaction: the word *unkiḥaka* — *I marry you to* — is used, not *I give you*.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the criteria for choosing a spouse in a well-known ḥadīth:

> *"A woman is married for four things: her wealth, her lineage, her beauty, and her religion. So choose the one with religion — may your hands be rubbed with dust [i.e., may you prosper]."* (al-Bukhārī 5090; Muslim 1466)

— and, correspondingly:

> *"When someone whose religion and character you are pleased with comes to you [seeking marriage], then marry him; if you do not, there will be tribulation in the land and widespread corruption."* (al-Tirmidhī 1084; Ibn Mājah 1967 — graded ḥasan)

The old man of Midian had exactly the evidence that ḥadīth requires: he had seen Moses' religion (his prayer in the shade, his trust, his restraint) and his character (strength used in service, honesty about his past, no bargaining).

**Application**

For anyone entering a contract: the verse sets the standard in both directions. Be precise about terms, and then be easy within them. The two are not in tension. Ambiguity is the enemy of justice; generosity within clarity is the perfection of it.

For anyone in a position of advantage: notice what the father did. He had every structural advantage over a destitute foreigner, and he used it to shorten the term, promise leniency, and offer his daughter. The Qur'an holds this up as the behavior of *al-ṣāliḥūn*.

And for anyone who has been given refuge: the proper response to the household that sheltered you is the full measure, not the minimum. The tradition's report that Moses served ten rather than eight is the tradition's own answer to the question of how a fugitive should treat the family that took him in.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:28

> **He said, "So let it be between you and me. Whichever of the two terms I complete, let there be no enmity toward me. And God is Guardian over what we say."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses' Acceptance: Three Clauses**

*Qāla dhālika baynī wa-baynaka* — *He said, "That is [settled] between me and you."* The demonstrative *dhālika* is the language of contract: the terms have been stated, and they are now agreed.

Moses' reply contains three elements, and each is a distinct legal and moral act.

**First: "Whichever of the Two Terms I Complete"**

*Ayyama al-ajalayni qaḍaytu*. Moses restates the two terms explicitly — eight years or ten — and confirms that the choice is his, as the father had stipulated.

The commentators note this as a model of contractual clarity. A party to an agreement should restate its terms in his own words, so that any divergence in understanding surfaces immediately rather than years later. The Qur'an's general instruction on contracts is famously detailed:

> *"O you who believe! When you contract a debt for a fixed term, write it down... and let not the scribe or the witness refuse... And have witnesses when you conclude a contract."* (Qur'an 2:282)

That verse, the longest in the Qur'an, legislates documentation and witnessing precisely because memory and goodwill both fail. In Moses' case there was no writing mentioned — the agreement was oral, before the old man's household — and Moses supplied the stronger guarantee, which is the subject of his third clause.

**Second: "Let There Be No Enmity Toward Me"**

*Fa-lā ʿudwāna ʿalayya*. The word *ʿudwān* is from *ʿ-d-w*, transgression, aggression, hostility. The meaning, as the commentators explain, is: whichever term I fulfill — whether I stop at eight or complete ten — you will not hold it against me, demand more, or treat my choice as an injury.

Al-Rāzī reads the clause as a request for the *spirit* of the agreement, not merely its letter: do not let the contract's optional clause become a source of resentment if I choose the lesser option. This is the anxiety of every party who has been given a choice by a stronger party: is the choice real?

The father had already anticipated it. His *I desire not to be hard upon you* (v. 27) was precisely the assurance Moses now seeks. The two verses together form a complete picture of a just contract: the stronger party promises restraint, and the weaker party names his vulnerability and asks for it to be honored.

This is the kind of exchange that prevents a decade of resentment. The commentators note the practical wisdom: ambiguity about whether a favor is truly optional is one of the most common sources of ruin in relationships between families, employers and employees, and business partners. Moses named it in advance, in one sentence, at the time of the agreement.

**Third: "And God Is Guardian Over What We Say"**

*Wa-Allāhu ʿalā mā naqūlu wakīl*. The word *wakīl* means a trustee, a guardian, an agent charged with the oversight of something — and it is one of the divine names:

> *"God is the Guardian (al-Wakīl) over all things."* (Qur'an 39:62)

> *"And sufficient is God as a Guardian."* (Qur'an 4:81)

The commentators note what Moses is doing here, and it is a complete theology of contract in a single sentence.

He is making God the *witness* to the agreement. No scribe, no document, no third party — because the witness is the One who cannot forget, cannot be bribed, cannot die before the term expires, and cannot be intimidated.

He is also making God the *guarantor*: if either party violates the agreement, the other has a recourse that does not depend on courts, kin, or power. The weaker party in every contract — and Moses was unmistakably the weaker party here — is thus placed on equal footing with the stronger, because the enforcer is omnipotent.

And he is invoking God as the *overseer of the intention*, not only of the words. *ʿAlā mā naqūlu* — *over what we say* — covers the utterance, and the One who hears it knows what each party meant by it. The tradition has always read this as including the unstated intention, since God knows what is in the breasts (cf. v. 69 of this sūrah).

The Qur'an commands the same practice generally:

> *"And fulfill the covenant of God when you have covenanted, and break not your oaths after you have confirmed them, having made God a surety over you. Truly God knows what you do."* (Qur'an 16:91)

> *"O you who believe! Fulfill your contracts."* (Qur'an 5:1)

**The Islamic Law of Contracts Drawn From This Verse**

This brief exchange is one of the primary narrative sources for several rules the jurists derived.

*Freedom of contract within the lawful.* The parties set their own terms — duration, compensation, and optional extension — and the Law's role is to enforce them, not to substitute its own. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The Muslims are bound by their conditions [in contracts]."* (Abū Dāwūd 3594; the meaning is foundational in all schools)

*The prohibition of ambiguity (gharar).* The term must be known. The Prophet ﷺ forbade sales involving uncertainty, and the jurists extended the principle to hire, marriage, and partnership.

*The obligation of fulfillment.* Breaking a contract without cause is a grave matter. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"There are three whose adversary I shall be on the Day of Resurrection... a man who gives his word to another and then breaks it..."* (al-Bukhārī 2227)

and:

> *"The signs of the hypocrite are three... and when he covenants, he betrays."* (al-Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59)

*The permissibility of hiring labor for a fixed term (ijārah).* The verse is a direct textual basis for employment contracts, and the jurists discuss at length its implications: the employer owes the wage, the worker owes the work, and neither may be extended without agreement.

*The permissibility of a mahr consisting of service*, as discussed at verse 27.

**The Report About the Staff**

Al-Ṭabarī transmits a detail attached to this verse: that it was at this point that Moses was given his staff, with some saying the old man gave it to him, and others that Gabriel brought it later. A related and widely transmitted story has the old man telling Moses to choose a staff from a house, and the same staff falling into his hands repeatedly, at which the old man said to his daughters, "This is a prophet."

Al-Rāzī records these accounts and dismisses them as popular lore. His reasoning is straightforward: the Qur'an attributes Moses' staff to him without explaining its origin, and the origin is not needed for the guidance the story carries. The legendary elaborations of the staff — that it was Adam's staff, brought from Paradise by Gabriel — belong to the category of *isrāʾīliyyāt* that the tradition transmits without affirming.

The discipline is worth restating because it will recur at verse 31, where the staff becomes a sign: the Qur'an's account of the miracles is clear and sufficient, and the commentarial embellishments, however edifying, are not revelation.

**Theological Note: Contracts in the Sight of God**

There is a doctrine embedded in this verse that the tradition has made much of. A contract made between two human beings, in a remote place, with no state to enforce it, is made *before God*, and the moral obligation it creates is not diminished by the absence of enforcement.

This is the foundation of the Islamic insistence that religious obligation and legal obligation are not separate spheres. A Muslim who could break an agreement without legal consequence is not thereby released from it, because the Witness he invoked is also the Judge.

The Prophet ﷺ expressed this in the strongest terms:

> *"The most deserving of conditions to be fulfilled are those by which you make lawful what is private [i.e., marriage]."* (al-Bukhārī 5158; Muslim 1418)

And he said:

> *"The covenants of the Muslims are one; the least of them may grant protection on behalf of all of them."* (al-Bukhārī 6755; Muslim 1370)

**Application: The Value of Naming the Vulnerable Point**

The most transferable lesson in this verse is Moses' second clause. He had been offered generous terms by a good man, and his response was not simply to accept them but to name the point at which the arrangement could go wrong.

In practice, this is the discipline of the difficult conversation held *before* rather than *after*. In a business partnership: what happens if one of us wants out? In a family arrangement: what if the loan is not repaid on schedule? In a marriage: what are we each expecting from the other's family? In employment: what exactly does the role include, and what would count as overreach?

Moses said it in five words: *no enmity toward me*. He did not accuse the old man of intending any. He simply placed the point on the table, in the presence of the Witness, at the moment of the agreement — and then, by all accounts, served him faithfully for a decade without a recorded dispute.

And the final clause tells the reader where the real security lay. Not in the old man's goodwill, though it was real; not in Moses' strength, though it was considerable. *God is Guardian over what we say.*

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:29

> **Then when Moses had completed the term and set out with his family, he perceived a fire on the side of the Mount. He said to his family, "Stay here. I perceive a fire. Perhaps I will bring you some news therefrom, or a firebrand, that haply you may warm yourselves."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ten Years of Silence**

*Fa-lammā qaḍā Mūsā al-ajala* — *then when Moses had completed the term*. The Qur'an passes over a decade in a subordinate clause.

This is one of the most remarkable silences in the Qur'anic narrative, and the commentators have drawn a great deal from it. Between verse 28 and verse 29, Moses spent years herding sheep in Midian. No revelation came. No miracle occurred. No voice from a tree. He worked, he married, he had children, he grew older, and he served his father-in-law.

The tradition sees a deliberate design in this. The man who would stand before Pharaoh and demand the release of a nation spent the preparatory years doing the least glamorous work available, in obscurity, for someone else's flock. The commentators note that this is the Qur'anic pattern for every messenger the tradition reveres: David was a shepherd; the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was a shepherd, and he said:

> *"God did not send any prophet but that he tended sheep."* His companions asked, "And you?" He said, *"Yes, I used to tend them for the people of Makkah in return for some carats."* (al-Bukhārī 2262)

The Prophet ﷺ also gave the reason why such work forms a person:

> *"No one eats better food than that which he eats from the work of his own hands; and the prophet of God, David, used to eat from the work of his own hands."* (al-Bukhārī 2072)

Sheep-herding in particular is the apprenticeship the tradition singles out, and the commentators on this verse cite the ḥadīth:

> *"There was no prophet who did not tend sheep."* (al-Bukhārī 2262)

The spiritual logic is discernible. A shepherd learns patience with slow, unintelligent creatures; constant vigilance; the discipline of returning what strays; and long solitude in which a person is left with nothing but his own thoughts and God. Every one of those qualities was required of the man who would spend forty years with the Children of Israel.

**How Long Was the Term?**

As discussed at verse 27, the commentaries divide over whether Moses served eight years or ten.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both record the view, transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and expressly attributed by some to the Prophet ﷺ, that Moses fulfilled the longer and more complete of the two terms — that is, ten. Some reports add that he stayed a further ten years afterward, though al-Rāzī notes that this rests on weak transmission.

What the Qur'an itself establishes is only that *he completed the term* — whichever term he had undertaken — and that he then left. The lesson does not depend on the number: he finished what he had contracted to do, and he finished it completely before he moved on.

**"And Set Out With His Family"**

*Wa-sāra bi-ahlihi*. The verb indicates travel, journeying. He was not alone now: he had a wife and, on the standard reading, children. The fugitive who arrived at Midian with nothing was leaving with a household.

Why leave? The Qur'an does not say, and the commentators offer reasons: a desire to return to Egypt now that the old king's decree might have lapsed or the king himself died; a longing for his mother and his people; or simply the completion of a phase. Some transmit that a longing for his family in Egypt came over him.

The tradition's consensus is that the departure itself was lawful and natural — a man completing his contract and moving on — and that God used an ordinary journey for the greatest appointment of Moses' life. The commentators note that revelation did not come to him in a retreat or a temple, but on the road, in the middle of an ordinary family journey, at night, in the cold.

**"He Perceived a Fire on the Side of the Mount"**

*Ānasa min jānibi al-Ṭūri nāran*. The verb *ānasa* is significant: it means to perceive something with a sense of relief or reassurance — to notice, to sense the presence of. It is not the ordinary verb for seeing. A traveler in the dark who sees a fire feels hope before he feels curiosity.

*Al-Ṭūr* is the mountain — generally identified with Mount Sinai, the mountain of the covenant, which the Qur'an names elsewhere with solemn emphasis:

> *"By the Mount, and by a Book inscribed."* (Qur'an 52:1–2)

> *"And by the Mount of Sinai."* (Qur'an 95:2)

The location matters. Moses had left Egypt years before; now he was at the mountain of divine encounter. The commentators note the providence: he thought he was going somewhere else, and he was brought here.

**"Stay Here. I Perceive a Fire"**

*Imkuthū innī ānastu nāran*. His words to his family are practical and unhurried. He does not say "I have seen a sign" or "something is happening." He says: wait, I see a fire.

Three purposes are given, and they ascend in usefulness:

*Perhaps I will bring you some news therefrom* — *laʿallī ātīkum minhā bi-khabarin*. Fire means people, and people mean information: which way to the road, where there is water, what lies ahead. A family traveling in the dark needs a guide.

*Or a firebrand* — *aw jadhwatin mina al-nāri* — a burning brand, a piece of fire, something with which to start their own fire.

*That haply you may warm yourselves* — *laʿallakum taṣṭalūna*. The verb is from *ṣ-l-y*, meaning to warm oneself at a fire, to be exposed to its heat. The commentators note the detail: it was cold, they were traveling at night, and a family with children needed warmth.

The entire statement is natural to the point of ordinariness, and this is exactly its value. Moses was not expecting a theophany. He was cold, he had a family, he saw a light, and he went to get fire.

The tradition draws the moral that the greatest moments in a life arrive unannounced and in the course of ordinary errands. The Prophet ﷺ was in a cave on an ordinary retreat when Gabriel came to him. The Qur'an's consistent pattern is that God meets people where they actually are, doing what they are actually doing.

**Why the Fire?**

The commentators ask why God chose fire as the medium of the first encounter, and their answers are worth summarizing.

Fire is what a traveler in the dark needs — it is the form of help Moses was looking for, so the encounter begins as mercy before it becomes command.

Fire is the element that transforms what it touches, and the encounter transformed Moses.

Fire is the thing that, in the ancient world, was most commonly worshipped — and by appearing in fire while declaring *I am God*, the encounter subverted the natural human impulse to worship the medium.

And fire is the classical Qur'anic image of the divine light:

> *"God is the Light of the heavens and the earth. The likeness of His Light is a niche wherein is a lamp... Light upon light!"* (Qur'an 24:35)

The commentators note the connection with the parallel accounts in the Qur'an, where the same scene is narrated with slightly different emphases:

> *"Has there come to thee the story of Moses? When he saw a fire and said to his family, 'Stay here; I perceive a fire; perhaps I can bring you a burning brand therefrom, or find guidance at the fire.'"* (Qur'an 20:9–10)

> *"And when thy Lord called unto Moses: 'Go to the wrongdoing people, the people of Pharaoh. Will they not be reverent?'"* (Qur'an 26:10–11)

> *"When Moses said to his family, 'Truly I perceive a fire. I will bring you news therefrom, or I will bring you a flaming brand, that haply you may warm yourselves.'"* (Qur'an 27:7)

That the same event is narrated four times in the Qur'an, each time with a different emphasis serving its sūrah, is itself among the things the tradition cites as evidence of the Qur'an's rhetorical method: selection and emphasis rather than mere repetition.

**Theological Note: The Call That Interrupts a Life**

The moment at which Moses' life changed was the moment he was not looking for it. He was looking for fire.

The commentators have drawn from this the principle that divine appointment comes by God's choice and timing, not by human preparation alone — though preparation is real, as the ten years in Midian show. Moses was *ready*, and he was *not expecting*. Both are true.

There is also a pastoral word here for anyone who feels that the years of quiet labor have been wasted. The decade in Midian was not a parenthesis in Moses' story; it was the content of his formation. The tradition has always read this verse as the answer to the question of why God makes His servants wait: the waiting is not doing nothing.

And finally, note that Moses went toward the fire *for his family*. He was looking for what they needed — news, warmth, light. The commentators observe that the man who would be sent to a people was, at the moment of his call, serving his own household. The mission began in the ordinary obligations, and God met him in the middle of them.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:30

> **And when he came upon it, he was called from the right bank of the valley, at the blessed site, from the tree, "O Moses! Truly I am God, Lord of the worlds!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene: A Voice, a Valley, a Tree**

*Fa-lammā atāhā nūdiya min shāṭiʾi al-wādi al-aymani fī al-buqʿati al-mubārakati mina al-shajarati an yā Mūsā innī anā Allāhu rabbu al-ʿālamīn*.

The Arabic is dense and deliberate, and the commentators have parsed it closely. The call came *from the right bank of the valley* — *al-wādī al-ayman*, the wadi on the right, the side the Qur'an names elsewhere as the place of the covenant. It came *at the blessed site* — *al-buqʿatu al-mubārakah*, a patch of ground set apart by blessing. It came *from the tree* — *min al-shajarati*.

The prepositions have generated careful theological discussion, and the discussion matters.

**"From the Tree" — The Creedal Question**

Does *min al-shajarati* — *from the tree* — mean that God spoke from inside the tree, or from beside it?

The exegetes are unanimous in rejecting any reading that makes the tree the speaker or God's location. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Rāzī all state the rule: God created the speech in a place — the tree or its vicinity — and Moses heard it, but the speech is God's speech, not the tree's. The preposition *min* here indicates the *place from which the sound was heard*, just as one says "I heard the voice from the house" without implying that the house speaks.

The parallel verse removes all ambiguity:

> *"And when he came to it, he was called: 'Blessed is whosoever is at the fire and whosoever is around it! And glory be to God, Lord of the worlds!'"* (Qur'an 27:8)

That verse distinguishes the fire from God with total clarity: blessing is invoked *upon the one at the fire* — Moses — and then glory is ascribed to God alone. The Qur'an is scrupulous at exactly the point where a careless reader might slip into locating the Creator in His creation.

The commentators thus read the tree and the fire as *created signs* — the place where an uncreated speech was made audible. This distinction is the foundation of the Islamic doctrine of the divine attributes, and it was drawn with care long before the later controversies.

**The Divine Speech: The Great Theological Battleground**

This verse became one of the central proof-texts in the most consequential doctrinal dispute in Islamic history: the *miḥnah*, the Inquisition over whether the Qur'an is created or uncreated.

The argument, in outline, runs as follows.

The Qur'an says of Moses:

> *"And unto Moses God spoke directly (kallama Allāhu Mūsā taklīman)."* (Qur'an 4:164)

The Arabic is emphatic: the verbal noun *taklīman* is an intensive cognate accusative, a construction the grammarians call *mafʿūl muṭlaq* used to stress the reality and directness of the act. God spoke to Moses with actual speech, without intermediary.

Now: is that speech created or uncreated? The Muʿtazilah argued that the speech heard by Moses was created at the moment it was heard, on the grounds that to posit an eternal speech alongside God's essence would compromise the absolute oneness of God. The People of Ḥadīth, led by Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal, argued that Speech is an eternal attribute of God, that God speaks whenever He wills with a speech that is His own, and that to call it created is to say that God acquired an attribute He did not previously have — which is to attribute imperfection to Him.

Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal's position, and the position that became normative for Sunni Islam, was:

> *"The Qur'an is the Speech of God, uncreated; whoever says it is created is a Jahmī, and whoever says the Qur'an is created is an unbeliever."*

The Sunni formulation, refined by later scholars including Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim, states it with precision: God is described by Speech as an eternal attribute (*ṣifah qadīmah bi-l-nawʿ*), while individual divine utterances occur by His will and power at particular times (*āḥād al-kalām*, the individual instances, occur in time). God spoke to Moses with a real voice that Moses really heard; the voice is created in the sense that the sound is an event, but the Speech of which it is an utterance is God's own attribute and is not a creature.

The practical creedal rule for the ordinary believer, which the scholars insisted upon, is simpler than the debate: God truly speaks; the Qur'an is truly His speech, not the speech of Gabriel or of Muhammad ﷺ; and the *how* of it is not known to us and must not be imagined:

> *"And they ask thee concerning the Spirit. Say: 'The Spirit is of the command of my Lord; and you have been given but little knowledge.'"* (Qur'an 17:85)

Imām Mālik's famous response to the question of how God is established upon the Throne applies equally to every divine attribute:

> *"The establishment [upon the Throne] is known, its modality is unknown, belief in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation."*

**"I Am God, Lord of the Worlds" — The Content of the First Declaration**

The first words Moses heard were not a command, a threat, or a commission. They were an identification: *innī anā Allāhu rabbu al-ʿālamīn* — *truly I am God, Lord of the worlds*.

The commentators note the structure of the declaration and what it accomplishes.

*Innī anā* is a double emphasis — "truly I, I am" — the strongest possible form of self-identification in Arabic. This is not a messenger speaking; it is the King Himself.

*Allāh* is the proper name of the Divine Essence, the name that belongs to no other and that no Muslim will hear as anything but the name of the One God.

*Rabbu al-ʿālamīn* — *Lord of the worlds* — is the decisive phrase for everything that follows in this sūrah. *Rabb* means lord, sustainer, owner, the one who brings a thing to its completion by stages. And *al-ʿālamīn* is the plural of *ʿālam*, worlds — all created orders: the world of humans, of jinn, of angels, of animals, of the heavens and the earth, and every order of being known and unknown.

The phrase appears 42 times in the Qur'an; it opens the Fātiḥah (*Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds*), and it appears at the climax of this sūrah's confrontation narrative. Here it does something specific and polemical. Moses is about to be sent to a man who said:

> *"I am your lord most high."* (Qur'an 79:24)

The declaration *I am God, Lord of the worlds* is the direct answer to that claim, given to Moses before he ever reaches Egypt. The mission is defined at the moment of the call: Moses is not being sent to negotiate, to reform, or to petition. He is being sent to announce, to the most powerful man on earth, that the lordship that man claims belongs to the Lord of the worlds.

**"The Blessed Site" and the Sacred Geography**

*Al-buqʿatu al-mubārakah* — the blessed patch of ground. The commentators discuss what the blessing consisted of: that God spoke there, that Moses was commissioned there, that it was the site of the covenant, and that the region itself was fruitful.

The Qur'an names this geography with emphasis elsewhere:

> *"By the fig and the olive, and by Mount Sinai, and by this secure land."* (Qur'an 95:1–3)

> *"And We called unto him from the right side of the Mount, and We brought him near in communion."* (Qur'an 19:52)

The tradition's theology of sacred place is worth stating precisely, because it is frequently misunderstood. Islam does not hold that the Divine is present in a location; God is exalted above place, and the Qur'an is emphatic:
