> *"There is nothing like unto Him; and He is the Hearing, the Seeing."* (Qur'an 42:11)

What Islam holds is that God *chooses* places, times, and objects for His favors, and that honoring what God has honored is obedience, not attribution of divinity to the thing honored. This is why the Ḥaram in Makkah, the mosque in Madinah, and the Aqṣā in Jerusalem are venerated, and why the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Do not undertake a journey except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque [in Makkah], this mosque of mine [in Madinah], and the Aqṣā Mosque [in Jerusalem]."* (al-Bukhārī 1189; Muslim 1397)

The valley of Ṭuwā — named in Sūrah Ṭā Hā as the place of the call:

> *"Verily I am thy Lord; so take off thy shoes. Truly thou art in the sacred valley of Ṭuwā."* (Qur'an 20:12)

— is blessed in exactly this sense: chosen, not indwelt.

**"O Moses!" — Addressed by Name**

The call opens with a personal address: *yā Mūsā*. The commentators note the intimacy of it. The Creator of the worlds, speaking from a tree in a valley in a desert, addresses a fugitive shepherd by his name.

The tradition has drawn from this a consolation that has sustained believers in every generation: God knows His servants individually and calls them individually. The Qur'an states it generally:

> *"And He is with you wherever you are; and God sees whatsoever you do."* (Qur'an 57:4)

and the Prophet ﷺ said in the ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me."* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

There is also a striking correspondence between this call and the call of the final Prophet ﷺ. Moses heard *O Moses* from a tree; Muhammad ﷺ heard *Read, in the name of thy Lord* (96:1) in a cave. Both were addressed by name or with a direct command; both were alone; both were not looking for it. The tradition has always held the two calls together as the two poles of the same prophethood.

**Practical Fruit: Where God Meets People**

Several conclusions emerge that are directly usable.

God meets people in the middle of their obligations. Moses was fetching fire for his family. The encounter was not conditioned on retirement from the world but occurred within service in it.

God meets people where they actually are, not where they think they should be. Moses was on a road at night, cold, uncertain. He was not in a sanctuary.

The first word from God to a person is often an identification of who is speaking, because everything else depends on it. Before any command, God declared who He is.

And the content of that declaration is the answer to whatever claims lordship in a person's world. Modern readers do not face a pharaoh claiming divinity, but they face systems, appetites, ideologies, employers, and reputations that make the same claim in quieter register. *Rabbu al-ʿālamīn* is the corrective: Lord of all the worlds, and therefore of the one that is currently claiming to be absolute.

**A Note on the Narratives of the Staff**

Because this is the scene where the staff's origin is often discussed, al-Rāzī's caution deserves repeating. The commentaries transmit that Moses' staff came from Paradise with Adam, or that it was selected by miraculous means from his father-in-law's house. Al-Rāzī records and declines these; Ibn Kathīr transmits them while noting their *isrāʾīlī* provenance. The Qur'an itself tells us nothing about the staff's origin, and its silence is the proper limit of the believer's knowledge. What the Qur'an *does* establish is the function: the staff became a sign by God's command (v. 31), and the sign belongs to God, not to the wood.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:31

> **and, "Cast thy staff!" Then when he saw it quivering like a serpent, he turned in retreat without returning. "O Moses! Approach and fear not! Truly, thou art of those who are secure.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Sign: An Ordinary Object Transformed**

*Wa-an alqi ʿaṣāka*. The command is attached to the declaration of verse 30 — *and [it was said], "Cast thy staff!"* — as though the identification and the sign were one continuous utterance. The Lord of the worlds announces Himself, and immediately demonstrates that the natural order belongs to Him.

The commentators note the choice of object. The staff was the most ordinary possession a shepherd had — a stick, used for leaning, for guiding animals, for striking at scrub. It was the tool of his trade, the very instrument of the ten years of obscurity he had just completed.

There is a deep logic in this, and the tradition has made much of it. God did not give Moses a new and exotic instrument; He transformed the one Moses already carried. The message embedded in the sign is that the ordinary material of a faithful life — the work, the skills, the habits formed in obscurity — is what God takes up and turns into a proof.

The same pattern marks the other great Qur'anic signs. The fire that was meant to burn Abraham became coolness and safety (21:69). The sea that protected Pharaoh became a road for Moses and a grave for his army (26:63–66). The staff that Moses leaned on became a serpent. In every case, God demonstrates lordship not by introducing foreign material but by commanding what is already in His servant's hand.

**"Quivering Like a Serpent" — *Jānn* and Its Implications**

*Fa-lammā raʾāhā tahtazzu ka-annahā jānnun*. The verb *ihtazza* means to shake, to quiver, to move with rapid vibration. The simile *ka-annahā jānn* — *as though it were a jānn* — is the phrase the commentators discuss most in this verse.

*Jānn* is from the same root as *jinn*: it denotes a small, swift snake, and it is also used in Arabic for a class of jinn. The lexicographers note both meanings, and the exegetes have taken the word here in its primary sense — a slender, quick-moving serpent — while noting the resonance with the unseen world.

Why a snake? The commentators give several reasons, and they are complementary.

Egypt was the civilization of the serpent: the cobra was the emblem of Pharaoh's crown, the symbol of royal and divine authority in Egyptian iconography. The sign therefore aimed directly at the thing Pharaoh's power was symbolized by. What Pharaoh wore on his brow as a claim to divinity, Moses could command on the ground.

The serpent was also the central instrument of Egyptian magic. The Qur'an records that Pharaoh's magicians threw down ropes and staffs that appeared to become snakes:

> *"They said, 'O Moses, either you throw, or we shall be the first to throw.' He said, 'Nay, you throw!' And lo, their ropes and their staffs appeared to him, by their sorcery, to be crawling."* (Qur'an 20:65–66)

Moses' sign was therefore given in the precise idiom of the court he was being sent to — a demonstration that what the magicians counterfeited, God performed. The confrontation at verses 36–40 is prepared here.

And the transformation itself — a dead stick becoming a living, moving creature — is the most compact possible demonstration of the claim made in verse 30: that the One speaking is the Lord who gives life and takes it, who commands the nature of every thing.

**"He Turned in Retreat Without Returning"**

*Wallā mudbiran wa-lam yuʿaqqib*. The commentators note the humanity of the reaction with some relief. The man who would stand before Pharaoh fled from his own staff.

*Wallā mudbiran* — he turned his back, retreating. *Wa-lam yuʿaqqib* — and did not come back, did not turn around to look, did not wait. The verb *ʿaqqaba* in this form means to return upon one's steps, to double back.

Al-Ṭabarī reads *lam yuʿaqqib* as *he did not wait* — he left immediately and at speed. Others read it as *he did not look behind him*.

The commentators are careful to say what this flight was not. It was not disobedience and it was not disbelief: Moses had just heard the living God identify Himself. It was *the natural fear of a human being confronted with the disruption of the natural order*. The Qur'an records the same reaction in the parallel accounts, where God reassures him:

> *"He said, 'Take it and fear not; We shall restore it to its former state.'"* (Qur'an 20:21)

This is an important theological datum. The prophets are not portrayed as fearless by constitution; they are portrayed as obedient *in* their fear. The tradition has always insisted on this, because a prophet who could not feel fear would not be a model for human beings, and because the excellence of the prophets consists precisely in obeying what is frightening.

The Prophet ﷺ experienced something analogous at his first revelation, and the ḥadīth records his fear without any attempt to soften it:

> *The angel came to him and said, "Read." He said, "I am not a reader." He said: "The angel took hold of me and pressed me until I could bear no more, then released me and said, 'Read.'"...* — and ʿĀʾishah's narration continues that he returned to Khadījah with his heart trembling, saying, *"Wrap me, wrap me."* (al-Bukhārī 3; Muslim 160)

The greatest of the prophets was afraid at the moment of the call. The sūrah's Moses, fleeing from a stick, is his brother.

**"O Moses! Approach and Fear Not!" — The Reassurance**

The call comes again, and it is structured as command followed by reassurance: *aqbil wa-lā takhaf*.

*Approach* — turn back, come near. The commentators note that God did not simply reassure him from a distance; He required him to walk *toward* the thing that frightened him. The pedagogy is exact: the cure for a fear that must be overcome is not its removal but the approach toward it.

*Fear not* — the standard Qur'anic reassurance given to the prophets and the righteous at moments of encounter, and one of the most frequent phrases in the Book:

> *"Fear not; indeed I am with you both; I hear and I see."* (Qur'an 20:46 — to Moses and Aaron)

> *"Fear not, for God is with us."* (Qur'an 9:40 — to Abū Bakr in the cave)

> *"Fear not, and grieve not, but receive glad tidings of the Garden which you were promised."* (Qur'an 41:30 — to the believers at death)

> *"And We said, 'Fear not, O Abraham.'"* — the angels' words when they came to him and he drew back from them (cf. 11:70; 15:53)

The recurrence establishes the shape of the relationship: God does not promise His servants a life without terrors; He promises them His presence within the terrors. The *lā takhaf* is never "you will not be afraid"; it is "you have no cause to fear what you think you have cause to fear."

**"Truly, Thou Art of Those Who Are Secure"**

*Innaka mina al-āminīn*. The word *āminīn* is from *a-m-n*, the root of safety, security, trust, and faith (*īmān*). It means those who are safe, protected, granted security.

The commentators note the shift in the verse's register. Moses had been reassured (*fear not*), and is now *classified*: he belongs to a category of people who are safe. The phrasing is not "you are safe now" but "you are of those who are secure" — a statement about his standing rather than only about the moment.

Who are *al-āminīn*? The commentators give the answer from the Qur'an's own usage. They are those whom God has taken into His protection:

> *"And whoever enters it is secure."* (Qur'an 3:97 — of the Sanctuary)

> *"And God sets forth the parable of a town that was secure and at ease, its provision coming to it in abundance from every place."* (Qur'an 16:112)

> *"Those who believe and do not obscure their faith with wrongdoing — for them is security, and they are rightly guided."* (Qur'an 6:82)

That last verse is the definition. Security belongs to those who believe and do not mix their faith with *ẓulm* — and the Prophet ﷺ explained *ẓulm* there as *shirk*, on the basis of the verse:

> *"Truly, associating partners with God is a tremendous wrong."* (Qur'an 31:13)

When that verse was revealed, the companions were distressed, asking who among them had not wronged himself, and the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"It is not as you think. It is only as Luqmān said... 'do not obscure your faith with wrongdoing' — that is, associating partners with God."* (al-Bukhārī 32; Muslim 124)

Moses, standing in a valley at night before a staff that had become a snake, was classified among the secure: a man of pure monotheism, and therefore a man under protection.

**The Two Signs as a Unit**

Verses 31 and 32 together present the two signs Moses was given — the staff and the hand. The commentators note that they were given together, at the moment of the call, rather than only at the confrontation with Pharaoh, and they explain why.

The signs functioned first for *Moses*. Before they were proofs to anyone else, they were proofs to him: they established, beyond any possibility of doubt, that the Voice he was hearing was the Lord of the worlds. A man who is about to be sent to the most powerful ruler on earth with nothing but a message needs an unshakable certainty about the source of the message.

This is the pattern consistently reported in the tradition. Every prophet was given a sign to establish his own certainty before he was sent to others. And the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was given the Qur'an itself, whose inimitability is at once the proof to him and the proof to his people:

> *"Say: 'If mankind and the jinn gathered together to bring the like of this Qur'an, they could not bring the like of it.'"* (Qur'an 17:88)

**Practical Fruit: What God Does With What You Already Carry**

The most applicable lesson in this verse concerns the staff.

Moses was not given a new implement for the greatest work of his life. He was told to throw down the one he already had — the tool of his trade, the symbol of his decade in obscurity, the ordinary thing he leaned on.

The tradition reads this as a standing principle. God transforms what is already in the servant's hand: the skill he has acquired, the discipline he has formed, the experience he has survived. The person who has spent years doing something obscure and unglamorous should not assume those years are unusable for what comes next. Moses' decade with sheep produced the staff, and the staff became the sign before Pharaoh.

There is a second, harder edge to it. *Cast it* — the command is to let go of it. What God transforms, He first asks for. The thing that has become indispensable, the instrument of your competence and your identity, must be thrown down before it becomes a proof. That is the structure of this sign, and it is the structure of most callings.

And there is a third: the transformed thing was frightening to the man who had carried it. What God does with your life may be unrecognizable to you at first, and your first instinct may be to run. The response given to Moses is the response given to everyone: *approach, and fear not — you are among those who are secure.*

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:32

> **Insert thy hand in thy bosom; it will come forth white, without blemish. And draw thine arms to thyself against fear. These are two proofs from thy Lord to Pharaoh and his notables. Verily they are an iniquitous people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Sign: A Hand Made Light**

*Usluk yadaka fī jaybika takhruj bayḍāʾa min ghayri sūʾin*. The second sign is given as a command and a promise in the same breath: put your hand inside the opening of your garment, and it will come out *white, without blemish*.

The word *jayb* denotes the opening at the breast of a garment — the collar, the breast opening. The commentators note the practical detail: Moses did not have to go anywhere or fetch anything. The sign was performed on his own body, with the garment he was wearing.

*Bayḍāʾa* — white, luminous, radiant. And the crucial qualifier: *min ghayri sūʾin* — *without blemish*, *without harm*, *without disease*.

**Why "Without Blemish" Matters**

The commentators are unanimous on the point of this qualifier, and it is theologically essential.

Whiteness of the skin can be a symptom — leprosy (*baraṣ*), vitiligo, or other conditions that in the ancient world were causes of ritual isolation and social horror. If Moses' hand had emerged white *with* a blemish, the sign would have been indistinguishable from a disease, and Pharaoh would have had a pretext for dismissing it — or worse, for treating Moses as one of the afflicted people Egypt expelled.

So the Qur'an specifies, in every account of this sign, that the whiteness was not pathological:

> *"And draw thy hand to thy side; it will come forth white, without blemish — another sign — that We may show thee some of Our greatest signs."* (Qur'an 20:22–23)

> *"And he drew forth his hand, and lo, it was white to the beholders."* (Qur'an 7:108; 26:33; 27:12)

The translators and commentators render the effect as *radiant* rather than merely pale — a light issuing from the hand. The parallel phrasing "white to the beholders" indicates that the luminosity was perceptible to onlookers, not a private experience of Moses'.

Theologically, the sign is a demonstration that the God who spoke from the tree also commands the properties of the human body. Where the staff sign showed power over inanimate matter and over life, the hand sign showed power over the servant's own flesh — a reminder that the body Moses would use to confront Pharaoh belonged to the One who made it.

**"And Draw Thine Arms to Thyself Against Fear"**

*Wa-ḍmum ilayka janāḥaka mina al-rahbi*. This clause has attracted a good deal of commentary, and the readings divide into two, both transmitted by al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī.

*The physical reading.* After two terrifying signs — his staff transformed and his hand made luminous — Moses was afraid, and he was told to draw his arms to his sides as a means of steadying himself. The gesture is the opposite of the fear-response, which throws the arms out and away from the body. Al-Qurṭubī notes that pressing the arms against the chest was understood as a means of allaying terror, and he records reports that when Moses performed it, the fear left him.

The commentators also note the resonance of the word *janāḥ* — literally "wing," used in the Qur'an for the wing of a bird and, metaphorically, for the embrace of care:

> *"And lower unto them the wing of humility, out of mercy."* (Qur'an 17:24)

> *"And lower thy wing to the believers."* (Qur'an 15:88)

On that reading, the instruction carries a second sense: gather yourself in, compose yourself, bring your scattered faculties back under your own care.

*The fear of what?* Al-Rāzī says the fear was of the serpent; al-Qurṭubī says it was of Pharaoh, whom Moses now knew he was being sent to. Both are plausible, and the verse's placement — immediately before the statement of mission — favors the second: the signs have now been given, the destination has been named, and the natural human reaction to that combination is terror.

Either way, the practical instruction is the same and is one of the most useful in the Qur'an: when fear scatters you, gather yourself physically and draw in. The tradition has noted the correspondence with the Prophetic guidance on anger and panic, which prescribes a physical change of posture:

> *"If one of you becomes angry while standing, let him sit down; if the anger leaves him, fine; otherwise let him lie down."* (Abū Dāwūd 4782; Aḥmad)

The wisdom in both is identical: the body is not incidental to the state of the heart, and God's remedy for a frightened prophet included a posture.

**"These Are Two Proofs From Thy Lord"**

*Fa-dhānika burhānāni min rabbika*. The word *burhān* is stronger than *āyah*. It denotes a conclusive proof, a demonstration that settles the matter — the word used in logic and theology for apodictic evidence.

The dual form — *two proofs* — is explicit, and the commentators note that this is the Qur'an's own confirmation that the staff and the hand constitute a matched pair, given together, for a single purpose.

The purpose is stated immediately: *ilā Firʿawna wa-malaʾihi* — *to Pharaoh and his notables*. The signs are not for Moses, who is already certain; they are now designated as the evidence to be presented at court.

Note the audience specification. The signs are addressed to Pharaoh *and his malaʾ* — the council, the elite, the power structure. The Qur'an is consistent in this: the confrontation is never with the ruler alone but with the entire order that sustains him. When the magicians are summoned (in the fuller accounts at 7:111 and 20:58), it is the *malaʾ* who organize the response. And when the reckoning comes, it is Pharaoh *and his hosts* who drown together (v. 40).

**"Verily They Are an Iniquitous People"**

*Innahum kānū qawman fāsiqīn*. The word *fāsiq* is from *f-s-q*, which in its root sense means to go out, to exit — used of a date emerging from its husk, and metaphorically of a person who exits the bounds of obedience.

The commentators pause over the tense and the structure: *kānū* — they *were* — indicating a settled, established condition rather than a momentary lapse. This is not a description of people who might be persuaded; it is a diagnosis of a people who have exited the bounds.

Yet — and this is the point the commentators stress — God *still sent them the two proofs*. The verse holds together two things that human reasoning often separates: God's knowledge of how a people will respond, and His sending of the message anyway.

This is one of the most delicate areas of Islamic theology, and the tradition's position is precise. God's foreknowledge does not compel the response. He knew they would reject, and He sent nonetheless, and their rejection was entirely their own act. The Qur'an states both sides:

> *"And had We willed, We would have sent unto every town a warner. So obey not the disbelievers, but strive against them thereby with a great striving."* (Qur'an 25:51–52)

> *"And if thy Lord had willed, whoever is on the earth would have believed, all of them together. Would you then compel people until they are believers?"* (Qur'an 10:99)

The sending of proof to people who will reject it is not futility; it is justice. It establishes that they were addressed, that they saw, and that they chose. And it leaves the door open for those within that structure who *will* respond — and indeed some of them did: the magicians believed (7:120–126), the wife of Pharaoh believed (66:11), and a man from Pharaoh's own people concealed his faith (40:28).

The commentators note this as one of the lessons about *daʿwah*: the message is addressed to a structure, and God knows which individuals within it will respond. The preacher does not know, and therefore preaches to all.

**The Signs and the Nature of Prophetic Authority**

This verse is one of the primary texts for the Islamic doctrine of *muʿjizah* — the miracle that authenticates a prophet. The scholars of creed defined it precisely: a *muʿjizah* is an extraordinary event, performed by a claimant to prophethood, that occurs at his hands as a demonstration of his truthfulness, and that others are unable to produce the like of.

Three conditions are derived, and all three are visible in this verse.

*It is from God*, not from the prophet: *min rabbika* — *from thy Lord*. Moses did not produce the sign; God did, in response to Moses' obedience.

*It is connected to the claim*: the sign appears at the moment the prophet is being sent with a message, and it validates that message.

*It cannot be matched*: the commentators note that the encounter with the magicians in Sūrah Ṭā Hā and al-Aʿrāf is precisely the demonstration that the Egyptians' best practitioners could not produce the like.

The Qur'an's own term for such signs is *āyah* — a sign pointing beyond itself — and the tradition insists on the direction of the pointing. A miracle is not a display of power; it is a *proof of a message*. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was given the Qur'an as his standing miracle, and it is a *sign* in exactly this sense: a text whose inimitability points to its Author rather than to the man who recited it.

**Practical Fruit**

The verse teaches, first, about *the form divine help takes*. Moses was given two signs, both performed with objects he had with him. God's help typically arrives as a transformation of what is at hand rather than as a delivery from elsewhere.

Second, it teaches about *fear management*. The remedy prescribed to a frightened prophet was not an explanation but a posture: draw in your arms, gather yourself, stand steady. Anyone who has had to compose himself before a difficult confrontation can use this.

Third, it teaches about *the purpose of evidence*. The signs were given *to Pharaoh and his notables* — evidence is for those who need to be convinced, and its presentation is an act of justice rather than of self-display.

And fourth, it teaches something about *the limits of evidence*. The signs are called *burhānān* — conclusive proofs — and the people they were sent to are called *fāsiqīn* — people who have exited the bounds. The two statements sit in the same sentence. Conclusive evidence is not the same as compelling acceptance, and the sūrah will show, at verse 36, exactly what a hard heart does with a conclusive proof: it calls it sorcery.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:33

> **He said, "My Lord! Verily I have killed a soul among them, and I fear that they will slay me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fugitive's Objection**

With the signs given and the destination named, Moses raises his first difficulty. And it is not a difficulty ofeloquence, doctrine, or courage. It is a difficulty of *outstanding criminal liability*: he is a wanted man in Egypt.

*Qāla rabbi innī qataltu minhum nafsan fa-akhāfu an yaqtulūn*. The structure of the sentence is noteworthy. He does not say "I am afraid to go." He says *I killed a soul among them*, states the consequence he fears, and leaves the conclusion to the One he is addressing.

The commentators read the whole exchange of verses 33–35 as Moses' response to the mission, and they note its character carefully. This is not refusal. Moses never says *send someone else* or *I will not go*. What he does is lay out the concrete obstacles as he sees them, and ask for what he needs in order to carry the command out.

The tradition has identified this as the correct manner of responding to a command that is beyond one's capacity: not defiance, not false confidence, but an honest statement of the impediment together with a request for the means.

**"I Have Killed a Soul Among Them" — The Word *Nafs* Again**

The word *nafs* — soul, self, person — is the same word the Qur'an uses in its legislation on homicide:

> *"Whosoever slays a soul, unless it be for a soul or for corruption in the land, it is as though he had slain mankind altogether."* (Qur'an 5:32)

By using *nafs* rather than *rajul* (a man), Moses frames his act in the gravest available terms. The commentators note this as evidence of his sincerity: even at the moment of asking to be spared the consequences, he does not minimize what he did. He does not say "I struck a man who was beating an Israelite"; he says *I killed a soul*.

This is consistent with his conduct at verse 16, where he confessed *I have wronged my own soul* without any mitigating explanation. A decade in Midian has not dulled the moral accounting; if anything, it has made him more precise about it.

**The Two Fears: Retribution and Credibility**

The commentators distinguish two things Moses may have feared, and both are discussed in the commentaries.

*The first is straightforward*: that the Egyptians would kill him for the earlier death. This was the literal situation — he had fled a capital charge, and returning to Egypt meant returning to a jurisdiction where a sentence had been decided (v. 20).

*The second is more subtle*, and al-Rāzī develops it: Moses feared that if he was killed before delivering the message, the message would die with him. The mission was not about him; it was about a people enslaved. A dead messenger delivers nothing.

That second fear explains the shape of God's answer in verse 35. God does not say "you will not be killed" in the sense of an absolute guarantee of safety forever; He says *they shall not reach you* — that is, they will not be able to harm you *until the mission is accomplished*. The protection promised is functional: sufficient to deliver the message.

The commentators note the parallel with the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, to whom God said:

> *"O Messenger, convey that which has been sent down unto thee from thy Lord; and if thou dost not, thou hast not conveyed His message. And God will protect thee from mankind."* (Qur'an 5:67)

The same structure: a command to deliver, and a guarantee of protection *for the purpose of delivery*.

**Is This Fear a Defect? The Question of Prophetic Courage**

The scholars raise the question directly: is it appropriate for a prophet to express fear about a mission God has commanded?

The answer in the tradition is unambiguous, and it rests on the Qur'an's own presentation of the prophets. Fear is part of human nature, and the prophets — precisely because they were fully human — experienced it fully. What the Qur'an censures is not the emotion but the *withdrawal from the command* that fear might produce.

Moses did not withdraw. He stated the fear and continued the conversation. The commentators contrast this with the Qur'an's censure of those who used fear as a reason to stay home:

> *"And a group of them asked leave of the Prophet, saying, 'Our houses are exposed' — though they were not exposed. They desired nothing but to flee."* (Qur'an 33:13)

The difference is precisely what Moses did next. He did not ask to be excused; he asked for Aaron.

The Prophet ﷺ made the same distinction in his own practice. At Uḥud, when the Muslim position was exposed, he did not deny the danger; he consulted, he took the defensive position that the situation required, and he fought. And the Qur'an describes the correct response to a command that frightens:

> *"Those to whom the people said, 'Truly the people have gathered against you, so fear them' — but it increased them in faith, and they said, 'God is sufficient for us, and excellent is the Guardian.'"* (Qur'an 3:173)

**Divine Law on Homicide and the Fear of Retribution**

Because the verse turns on a killing, the commentators discuss the law of homicide as it bears on Moses' situation, and their discussion illuminates the verse.

The killing at verse 15 was unintentional in its outcome: Moses struck with his fist (*wakaza*), intending to drive off, and the man died. Islamic law, as revealed to the community of Muhammad ﷺ, classifies this as *qatl al-khaṭaʾ* or *shibh al-ʿamd* — unintended killing or quasi-intentional killing — and prescribes expiation and blood-money rather than retaliation:

> *"It is not for a believer to kill a believer, except by mistake. And whosoever kills a believer by mistake, then let him free a believing slave and pay blood-money to his family, unless they remit it as charity."* (Qur'an 4:92)

Moses lived before that legislation, and the law of his time and place was Pharaoh's, which recognized no such distinction. His fear was therefore entirely reasonable: in Egypt, the death of an Egyptian at the hands of an Israelite raised in the palace was not going to be processed as accidental.

The commentators add a further point about *the rights of others*. In Islamic law, the rights of creatures are not erased by repentance to God. Even with full *tawbah*, a person who has taken a life or property owes restoration or its equivalent in this world. This is why the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever has wronged his brother in respect of his honor or anything else, let him seek his release from it today, before there is no dinar and no dirham."* (al-Bukhārī 2449)

The commentators note that Moses' situation included an unresolved claim against him in the world, and that his fear reflects a correct understanding of that: repentance settles the account with God, not the account with Pharaoh's courts.

**The Wisdom of the Delay: Ten Years of Exile**

There is a providential reading of this verse that the tradition has developed, and it connects back to the whole Midian episode.

Why did God not call Moses immediately after his flight? Why ten years?

One answer the commentators give is that the *legal* situation in Egypt needed to change, and it did: the reports transmitted in the commentaries hold that the Pharaoh who had sentenced Moses died during Moses' absence, and that a successor reigned when Moses returned. The Qur'an does not state this, and al-Rāzī notes it as transmitted report rather than revelation.

A second answer is more theologically substantial: Moses needed the ten years. The man who killed in anger at verse 15 was not yet the man who could stand before Pharaoh, endure a decade of plagues and negotiations, and then lead a fractious people through a wilderness for forty more. The exile was not a punishment to be served out; it was the formation required.

And a third answer, which the spiritual commentators emphasize, is that the fear itself had to be *spoken to God*. A man who carries unconfessed dread into a mission will eventually break under it. Moses voiced it here, at the threshold, and received in reply not a rebuke but an answer (v. 35).

**Lessons for Anyone Facing a Task With a Complicated Past**

The verse has a peculiarly modern application, and the commentators' principles translate directly.

Many people carry a past that makes a present calling feel impossible: a criminal record, a ruined reputation, a broken relationship in the place they are being sent back to, a failure that everyone remembers. The instinct is either to refuse the calling or to pretend the past is not there.

Moses did neither. He named it — *I killed a soul among them* — to the only One who could do anything about it, and then he continued the conversation about how the mission might be accomplished.

The structure of the response is instructive and repeatable: state the obstacle accurately, without minimization or dramatization; specify what you would need in order to proceed; and leave the resolution with God. That is precisely what Moses does across verses 33 and 34 — obstacle, then request — and it is why verse 35 is an answer rather than a rebuke.

There is also a lesson in *what he did not ask for*. He did not ask to be excused. He did not ask for the past to be undone. He did not ask for a guarantee of safety. He asked for his brother. The request was for the means to do the task, not for the removal of the difficulty.

The tradition has always read that as the signature of sincerity: the person who genuinely intends to obey asks *how*, not *whether*.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:34

> **And my brother, Aaron, is more eloquent than me in speech. So send him with me as a helper to confirm me, for I fear they will deny me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Request: A Partner, Not an Escape**

*Wa-akhī Hārūnu huwa afṣaḥu minnī lisānan fa-arsilhu maʿiya ridʾan yuṣaddiqunī innī akhāfu an yukadhdhibūn*.

The commentators note immediately what this request is not. Moses did not say *send Aaron instead of me*. He said *send him with me*. The particle *maʿiya* — *with me* — is the key: he was asking for reinforcement, not substitution.

This is the clearest evidence of Moses' sincerity in the whole exchange. A man looking for a way out asks to be replaced. A man looking for a way *through* asks for help.

**"My Brother, Aaron" — Kinship Named First**

*Wa-akhī Hārūnu*. He says *my brother* before he says the name, and before he says anything about the qualification. The commentators note the order: kinship, then name, then competence.

The Islamic tradition attaches great weight to this, and to the institution it establishes. Aaron was, by the Qur'an's account, also a prophet — the two were sent together:

> *"And We gave him, out of Our mercy, his brother Aaron, a prophet."* (Qur'an 19:53)

The Prophet ﷺ made the same request of the same God for the same reason when he prayed:

> *"And appoint for me a helper from my family — Aaron, my brother. Strengthen through him my back, and associate him in my task, that we may glorify Thee much and remember Thee much. Truly Thou art ever Seeing us."* (Qur'an 20:29–35)

That these words are quoted in the Qur'an as the words of Moses, and that the Prophet ﷺ used almost exactly the same supplication when asking God to strengthen his own mission through ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, is one of the sūrah's clearest links between the two messengers. When the Prophet ﷺ prayed:

> *"O God, strengthen Islam with the more beloved to You of these two men: Abū Jahl or ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb"* (al-Tirmidhī 3681; Ibn Mājah 93 — and it was ʿUmar)

— he was following the pattern of verse 34.

**"More Eloquent Than Me in Speech" — Honest Self-Assessment**

*Huwa afṣaḥu minnī lisānan* — *he is more eloquent than me in tongue*. The word *afṣaḥ* is from *f-ṣ-ḥ*, clarity of expression, purity of speech.

The commentators discuss the nature of Moses' difficulty. The Qur'an and the reports speak of an impediment in his speech — a stammer or a speech difficulty that the tradition links to an incident in his childhood with Pharaoh, when he pulled the king's beard or took hold of it, and was nearly killed for it. The reports add that his tongue had been touched by a live coal in the test that established his innocence. These are transmitted accounts and not revelation; al-Rāzī records them with his usual caution.

What the Qur'an itself establishes is only what Moses says about himself: *my breast is constrained and my tongue is not fluent*:

> *"He said, 'My Lord, expand for me my breast, and ease for me my task, and loosen the knot from my tongue, that they may understand my speech. And appoint for me a helper from my family — Aaron, my brother.'"* (Qur'an 20:25–29)

The commentators draw several lessons from Moses' candor.

First, he knew his own weakness and said so without shame. The tradition regards accurate self-knowledge as the beginning of wisdom and false confidence as a spiritual disease. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever among you sees in his brother something that pleases him, let him pray for blessing for him"* — and, more pointedly, he taught the supplication: *"O God, as You have made my outward form beautiful, make my character beautiful."* (Aḥmad 24351)

Second, he did not treat his weakness as disqualifying. A defect in the instrument is not a defect in the message. The tradition has always read this verse as a refutation of the idea that effective religious work requires polished communicators. The Qur'an makes the point explicitly elsewhere:

> *"And Moses said, 'My Lord, I have no power over anyone except myself and my brother; so distinguish between us and the iniquitous people.'"* (Qur'an 5:25)

and, in the voice of the Qur'an itself:

> *"Say: 'If the ocean were ink for the words of my Lord, the ocean would be spent before the words of my Lord were spent.'"* (Qur'an 18:109)

Third — and this is the subtle point — Moses asked for the *complement* to his weakness rather than the *correction* of it. He did not ask God to heal his speech. He asked God to send Aaron. Many believers pray for their deficiencies to be removed; Moses prayed for the deficiency to be *covered* by someone else's strength, and God granted that request instead.

The commentators note the divine economy in this: God granted the request for Aaron (v. 35), and in 20:27 He also granted the easing of the tongue. But the principle stands: sometimes God answers by supplying a partner rather than by removing the limitation. The Body works that way, and the tradition has always drawn the lesson for communal work — God distributes gifts so that His servants need one another.

**"Send Him With Me as a Helper to Confirm Me"**

*Fa-arsilhu maʿiya ridʾan yuṣaddiqunī*. The word *ridʾ* means a helper, a support, one who backs another up — from a root meaning to prop or support something.

The purpose clause is the important part: *yuṣaddiqunī* — *that he may confirm me*, *corroborate me*, *attest to my truthfulness*.

The commentators note the function Moses is describing. Aaron was not to be a co-equal messenger with a separate message; he was to be a *corroborating witness* to Moses' message. This is a legal concept as much as a rhetorical one: a claim is strengthened by a second witness.

The parallel passage makes the same point:

> *"And my brother, Aaron — he is more eloquent than me in speech. So send him with me as a support to confirm me."* (Qur'an 20:29–31)

The structural role of Aaron in the mission, as the Qur'an presents it across the sūrahs, is exactly this: he spoke when Moses could not, he stood beside him before Pharaoh, and he was his deputy over Israel when Moses went to the Mount:

> *"And Moses said to his brother Aaron, 'Take my place among my people, and set things right, and follow not the way of the workers of corruption.'"* (Qur'an 7:142)

**"For I Fear They Will Deny Me" — The Second Fear**

*Innī akhāfu an yukadhdhibūn*. This is a different fear from the one in verse 33. There he feared for his life; here he fears for the *reception of the message*.

The commentators note the progression in Moses' two objections, and it is psychologically precise. The first fear was about himself — *they will kill me*. The second is about the mission — *they will deny me*. He has moved from self-concern to concern for the task.

The connection between the eloquence request and the fear of denial is direct: Moses reasoned that the message was true but that its truth could be obscured by imperfect delivery, and he wanted the delivery to be as strong as possible. Al-Rāzī frames it as Moses' recognition that a truth badly presented may be rejected for the wrong reasons, and that the messenger is responsible for the presentation as well as the content.

This is the basis of the Islamic science of *daʿwah* methodology. The Qur'an states the principle:

> *"Call unto the way of thy Lord with wisdom and beautiful exhortation, and argue with them in the best manner."* (Qur'an 16:125)

and:

> *"And speak to people good words."* (Qur'an 2:83)

The Prophet ﷺ was given a prayer for exactly this purpose, and he asked God for it at the beginning of his mission:

> *"O God, there is no ease except what You make easy, and You make the difficult easy if You will."* (Ibn Ḥibbān; ṣaḥīḥ)

and, in the narration concerning his own eloquence:

> *"I am the most eloquent of the Arabs, though I am one of the Quraysh."* (Reported in several collections with varying chains; the well-attested statement is his saying: *"I have been given the keys of eloquent speech."*)

**The Institutional Lesson: Shared Leadership**

This verse is one of the classical texts on the Islamic principle of *shūrā* — consultation — and on collaborative leadership.

The commentators note that the greatest prophet of his age, speaking directly with God, asked for a partner. If Moses needed Aaron, the tradition reasons, no one else is exempt from the need for counsel and assistance.

The Prophet ﷺ institutionalized this in his own practice. He consulted his companions at Badr and Uḥud, despite being the recipient of revelation; and the Qur'an commanded him to do so:

> *"And consult them in the affair; and when you have resolved, then trust in God."* (Qur'an 3:159)

The scholars drew from this verse and its companions the rule that consultation is recommended even — indeed especially — for those who are most capable, because its value lies not only in the quality of the advice obtained but in the strength and commitment it generates in the community.

The practical form Moses' request takes is worth copying. He did not merely ask for "someone to help"; he identified a specific person, named the specific quality that person had, and stated the specific function he would serve. That is the structure of a good request — and, in modern terms, of a good staffing plan.

**Application**

Three things carry directly into ordinary life.

*Know your limitation and name it.* Moses did not hide his speech difficulty and did not pretend it away. The person who cannot acknowledge his weak points cannot staff around them.

*Ask for the complement, not the cure.* God sometimes removes the limitation and sometimes sends a person who has what you lack. Both are answers. A person who insists on being healed before he will work may wait a long time, while the work goes undone.

*Request help in a way that strengthens the mission rather than diluting responsibility.* Moses remained the one commanded, the one who would stand before Pharaoh, and the one whose staff would be thrown. Aaron confirmed him. He did not replace him. Asking for help does not mean asking to be relieved of the task.

And the verse's final clause carries the warning that makes the request urgent: *I fear they will deny me*. Truth does not automatically prevail. It must be delivered, and the delivery is part of the obligation. Moses, who had the strongest possible claim on God's support, still took that seriously enough to ask for a partner.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:35

> **He said, "We shall strengthen thine arm through thy brother, and We shall grant an authority for both of you, such that they shall not reach you. With Our signs, you and those who follow you shall be the victors."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer: Every Request Granted, and More**

The divine response is a single sentence answering both of Moses' difficulties, and the commentators note the structure: each element of verses 33 and 34 is addressed in turn, and each answer exceeds the request.

Fear of death (v. 33) → *they shall not reach you*.
Need for a partner (v. 34) → *We shall strengthen thine arm through thy brother*... *We shall grant an authority for both of you*.
Fear of rejection (v. 34) → *With Our signs, you and those who follow you shall be the victors*.

Nothing Moses asked for was refused, and nothing was merely granted at the level requested. The tradition draws the rule: God answers the petition of His servant, and His answer carries more than the petition contained.

**"We Shall Strengthen Thine Arm Through Thy Brother"**

*Sanashuddu ʿaḍudaka bi-akhīka*. The idiom is worth unpacking, because it is physical and specific.

*Shadda* means to bind, to make firm, to tighten. *ʿAḍud* is the upper arm — the part of the body that does the heavy lifting, the seat of strength in Arabic idiom. To *strengthen the arm* of someone is to increase his capacity for action.

Al-Ṭabarī glosses it simply: *We shall help thee and strengthen thee through thy brother*. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the phrase is an idiom for support in any undertaking.

But the physical concreteness is meaningful. Moses' arm was the arm that held the staff — the instrument of both signs. The strengthening is therefore connected to the *work*: the hand that would be thrust into the bosom and the arm that would cast the staff are the arm being strengthened.

There is also a subtle wordplay the commentators note. *Ashuddu* — strengthen — shares a root with *shadīd* (strong) and with the *shadd* that binds things together. The two brothers are to be *bound* to one another. The strengthening is not merely additive, as though two men's strength were summed; it is a binding that makes the pair more than the sum.

Theologically, this verse establishes a principle about the means God uses: He strengthens His servants *through* one another. The brotherhood of believers is not a sentiment but an instrument. The Qur'an states it as a defining characteristic of the community:

> *"And those who have believed, emigrated, and striven in the way of God, and those who gave shelter and helped — those are the believers in truth."* (Qur'an 8:74)

And the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The believer to the believer is like a building, each part strengthening the other"* — and he interlaced his fingers. (al-Bukhārī 481; Muslim 2585)

and:

> *"The similitude of the believers in their mutual love, mercy, and compassion is that of a single body: when one limb complains, the whole body responds with sleeplessness and fever."* (al-Bukhārī 6011; Muslim 2586)

**"We Shall Grant an Authority for Both of You"**

*Wa-najʿalu lakumā sulṭānan*. The word *sulṭān* is from *s-l-ṭ*, meaning power, dominion, and — critically in Qur'anic usage — *authority backed by evidence*, a warrant.

The commentators distinguish several senses of *sulṭān* in the Qur'an, and all of them are operative here.

It means *proof* — the decisive argument or sign that establishes a claim. The Qur'an uses it in this sense when it demands evidence for false worship:

> *"Or have you a clear authority (sulṭān)? Then bring your book, if you are truthful."* (Qur'an 37:156–157)

It means *delegated power or dominion* — the authority to act. The Qur'an:

> *"And We had certainly established them in that wherein We have not established you."* (Qur'an 46:26)

And, in the darker sense, it means the *power of influence* that one being exercises over another, as in Satan's disavowal:

> *"And I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me."* (Qur'an 14:22)

In this verse, the commentators read *sulṭān* as including both the *evidential* authority — the two signs, which no one in Egypt could match — and the *protective* authority, which the next clause spells out. The pair would arrive at Pharaoh's court carrying something the court could not refute and could not overpower.

The detail that *both* of them receive it — *lakumā*, the dual — matters. Aaron was not a subordinate without standing; he was given authority in his own right, as the Qur'an confirms elsewhere:

> *"And We gave Moses and Aaron the criterion, and a light and a reminder for the reverent."* (Qur'an 21:48)

> *"And We verily gave Moses and Aaron the criterion, and a light..."* (cf. 23:45: *"Then We sent Moses and his brother Aaron with Our signs and a manifest authority"*)

**"Such That They Shall Not Reach You"**

*Fa-lā yaṣilūna ilaykumā*. The guarantee is precise, and the commentators insist on its precision.

It does **not** say that no harm would ever come to them. It says that *they* — Pharaoh and his people — would not be able to *reach* them, to get to them, to lay hands on them. The protection is from Egyptian power specifically, and it is tied to the mission.

The commentators stress two things about this. First, it is a real guarantee, and the narrative bears it out: Moses and Aaron walked into Pharaoh's court repeatedly, performed the signs, endured the magicians' contest, delivered warnings through years of plagues, and were never arrested or killed. The Qur'an records Pharaoh's impotent threats in other sūrahs:

> *"He said, 'If you take a god other than me, I will surely place you among the imprisoned.'"* (Qur'an 26:29)

> *"He said, 'Have you believed in him before I gave you leave? Indeed, he is your chief who taught you magic; so I will surely cut off your hands and your feet on opposite sides, and I will surely crucify you all.'"* (Qur'an 7:123)

Those threats were made and not carried out — because of this verse.

Second, the guarantee is *functional rather than absolute*. God protected them for the purpose of delivering the message, and the tradition has always read the promise that way. The Prophet ﷺ was likewise promised protection *for the conveyance*:

> *"O Messenger, convey that which has been sent down unto thee from thy Lord... And God will protect thee from mankind."* (Qur'an 5:67)

— and he was indeed protected through twenty-three years of mission, though he was wounded at Uḥud, starved during the boycott, and harmed in many ways. The promise was kept exactly as stated: the message was delivered, and nothing prevented its delivery.

This distinction is pastorally critical, and the commentators make it often. God's protection of His servants is real, but it is not a promise of a comfortable life; it is a promise that nothing will defeat the purpose for which He sent them. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Know that victory comes with patience, that relief comes with distress, and that with hardship comes ease."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad)

and he said, when the people of Ṭāʾif stoned him:

> *"O God, to You I complain of my weakness, my lack of resources, and my insignificance before people..."* (reported in the sīrah literature with varying chains)

The guarantee of verse 35 was fulfilled in the teeth of real suffering, and that is what guarantees in this religion look like.

**"With Our Signs, You and Those Who Follow You Shall Be the Victors"**

*Bi-āyātinā antumā wa-man ittabaʿakumā al-ghālibūn*. The closing clause is the answer to Moses' second fear — the fear of being denied.

Note the instrument: *bi-āyātinā* — *by Our signs*. Victory is attributed neither to Moses' eloquence nor to Aaron's nor to any political or military resource, but to the signs. The commentators stress this: the triumph of the messengers is a triumph of the truth they carry, not of their personal capacities.

Note also the scope: *you and those who follow you*. The victory is shared with the community of believers — the enslaved Israelites who would follow Moses out of Egypt. This is consistent with the sūrah's opening promise in verse 5, where the oppressed were to be made *imams* and *heirs*. The promise made there is now guaranteed here.

And note the word *al-ghālibūn* — *the victors, the ones who overcome*. It is a present-active form indicating a settled outcome: you are the ones who will overcome. The commentators point out that this is stated in the present tense of certainty while the confrontation has not yet begun, and they cite it as an instance of the Qur'an's habit of describing future divine acts in the language of accomplished fact.

**Theological Note: Victory Promised, Victory Defined**

The commentators are careful about what this victory was and was not, because the sūrah itself makes the point at verse 40.

Moses and those who followed him were *not* victorious in Egypt in any conventional political sense. They did not overthrow the regime and rule in its place; the regime destroyed itself, and the Israelites left. What they had was: the deliverance of a people, the destruction of a tyranny, the vindication of the message, and the inheritance promised at verses 5–6.

That is the Qur'anic definition of victory, and the tradition has never altered it. The Prophet ﷺ was told in a Makkan verse:

> *"And Our word has already gone forth to Our servants, the messengers: that they would surely be helped, and that Our hosts would surely be the victors."* (Qur'an 37:171–173)

— and this was revealed at a time when the Muslims were being tortured and boycotted. The victory promised was the triumph of the message and the establishment of the community, not immediate dominion.

The commentators draw the practical conclusion: the believer measures success by whether the truth has been delivered and upheld, not by whether the immediate struggle went his way. A messenger may be killed, exiled, or defeated politically, and still be among *al-ghālibūn*, because the signs he carried outlive him.

**Application**

The verse answers the two questions every person facing a daunting task asks: *am I adequate to this?* and *will I be safe?*

To the first, God's answer is *I will strengthen your arm through your brother* — the adequacy is supplied, and it is supplied through people. The practical instruction is to identify the Aaron in your situation and ask for him by name, as Moses did.

To the second, the answer is *they shall not reach you* — the protection is real, but it is bounded by the mission. The believer is not promised immunity; he is promised that nothing can defeat the purpose for which he was sent. That is enough, and the tradition has found it sufficient for fourteen centuries.

And the final clause answers the deepest fear of all — the fear that the work will come to nothing. *With Our signs, you and those who follow you shall be the victors.* The outcome was settled before Moses left the valley, and the settling of it came from the One who had just identified Himself in verse 30 as *Lord of the worlds*.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:36

> **But when Moses brought them Our clear signs, they said, "This is naught but fabricated sorcery. We heard this not among our fathers of old."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Confrontation Compressed Into One Verse**

The whole drama of Moses before Pharaoh — the summons to court, the contest with the magicians, the years of signs — is compressed here into a single sentence of narration and a single sentence of response.

*Fa-lammā jāʾahum Mūsā bi-āyātinā bayyinātin qālū mā hādhā illā siḥrun muftaran wa-mā samiʿnā bi-hādhā fī ābāʾinā al-awwalīn*.

The commentators note the deliberate economy. The sūrah's interest is not in the sequence of events — which is narrated at length in al-Aʿrāf, Ṭā Hā, and al-Shuʿarāʾ — but in the *structure of rejection itself*. What did the powerful do when conclusive evidence arrived? They called it sorcery and appealed to ancestral custom. Those two moves, and only those, are recorded here.

This is the sūrah's method throughout: it isolates the moral mechanism rather than narrating the spectacle. What follows in verses 37–42 is an anatomy of unbelief, not a chronicle of Egypt.

**"Our Clear Signs" — *Bayyināt***

The word *bayyināt* is from the root *b-y-n*, the same root that gave us *al-kitābu al-mubīn* in verse 2. It means clear, evident, self-demonstrating — signs whose meaning is not ambiguous and whose origin is not in doubt.

The commentators stress the adjective because the charge that follows is inherently self-refuting. One does not dismiss *clear* evidence by calling it magic; one dismisses it that way precisely because it cannot be explained otherwise. Al-Rāzī makes the point sharply: the accusation of sorcery is an admission that the event occurred. Nobody calls a failed trick "sorcery."

This reading is confirmed by the fuller accounts, where the magicians — the professionals, the people who would know — immediately recognized the difference between what Moses did and what they did:

> *"Then the magicians fell down prostrate. They said, 'We believe in the Lord of the worlds, the Lord of Moses and Aaron.'"* (Qur'an 26:46–48)

The experts believed. The court did not. That asymmetry is the sūrah's point, and it is repeated throughout the Qur'an as the signature of ideological refusal: those who know the field recognize the truth, and those with an interest at stake deny it.

**"Fabricated Sorcery" — The Two-Part Charge**

*Siḥrun muftaran*. Two words, and the commentators parse both.

*Siḥr* — sorcery, magic, illusion. Attributing a divine sign to sorcery accomplishes three things at once: it explains the event without conceding its source, it discredits the messenger, and it licenses the audience to dismiss him without investigation. It is, in short, the perfect dismissal — and the commentators note that it has never gone out of use.

*Muftaran* — fabricated, invented, forged — from *f-r-y*, to cut or fashion something falsely. The word carries the sense of deliberate manufacture: someone made this up.

The charge is therefore: this is a trick, and Moses knows it is a trick. The commentators note the malice in this. It is one thing to say "we do not understand what happened"; it is another to say "he invented it," which asserts knowledge of the messenger's inner state — an assertion no one can make and which the speaker does not believe.

The Qur'an records this form of denial as standard across the prophets:

> *"And they said, 'O you to whom the Remembrance has been sent down, you are surely mad!'"* (Qur'an 15:6)

> *"Thus did no messenger come to those before them but that they said, 'A sorcerer or a madman.'"* (Qur'an 51:52)

The recurrence is the point. The tradition reads these verses as establishing that rejection has a *fixed grammar*, and that the grammar is the same in every age: dismiss the evidence, impugn the messenger, and appeal to what we have always believed.

**"We Heard This Not Among Our Fathers of Old" — The Argument From Ancestry**

The second objection is the more interesting, because it is the more common.

*Wa-mā samiʿnā bi-hādhā fī ābāʾinā al-awwalīn* — *we heard not of this among our fathers of old*.

The commentators identify this as *taqlīd* — blind imitation of ancestral precedent — and they place it at the center of the Qur'an's critique of unbelief. Al-Rāzī's treatment of this verse is explicit: the point of the verse is to condemn conformity to the religion of one's forefathers purely because they held it.

The Qur'an states the objection in its purest form and answers it in the same breath:

> *"And when it is said to them, 'Follow what God has sent down,' they say, 'Nay, but we follow that which we found our fathers practicing.' What! Even if their fathers understood nothing and were not rightly guided?"* (Qur'an 2:170)

> *"And thus did We not send before thee any warner into a town, but that its affluent ones said, 'Truly we found our fathers upon a way, and we are following in their footsteps.'"* (Qur'an 43:23)

> *"And when they commit an indecency, they say, 'We found our fathers doing it, and God commanded us thus.' Say: 'Truly God commands not indecency. Do you say concerning God that which you know not?'"* (Qur'an 7:28)

The structure of the Qur'anic response is always the same two-part move: first, that the ancestors were themselves fallible mortals, and second, that following them does not transfer their merit to you.

**Why Ancestry Is So Powerful**

The psychological realism of this verse deserves attention, and the commentators provide it.

Ancestral custom is not merely an idea; it is an identity. To abandon the religion of one's fathers is to abandon one's people, one's place in the social order, and one's sense of continuity with the dead. This is why the objection is framed in the first person plural throughout — *we heard not*, *our fathers* — because what is at stake is membership, not opinion.

The sūrah itself will name this motive directly, a few verses later, when the Quraysh give their real reason for rejecting the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"And they say, 'If we follow guidance along with thee, we shall be snatched away from our land.'"* (Qur'an 28:57)

That is the same motive as here, stated more honestly: we cannot afford to be cut off from our people.

The commentators note a further element: the objection from ancestry is typically raised by those who *benefit* from the existing order. Pharaoh's court was not defending Egyptian theology out of piety; it was defending a political structure in which the court's status rested on the religious order it administered. Moses' monotheism was not a rival *opinion*; it was a rival *source of authority*, and therefore a threat.

**The Logical Structure of the Two Objections**

Set the two charges side by side and an interesting tension appears, and the commentators have noted it.

The first charge — *sorcery* — implies that something extraordinary happened.

The second charge — *we never heard this before* — implies that the *content* of Moses' message was novel, and novelty is treated as disqualifying.

These two are in tension. If it is sorcery, it is not new (Egypt had magicians in abundance). And if it is merely new, it is not sorcery. The court produced both charges because it needed both: one to explain the signs and one to dismiss the doctrine.

The Qur'an regularly exposes this kind of incoherence in the arguments of rejecters, and the commentators collect the instances:

> *"Nay, but they denied that which they comprehended not, and whose fulfillment had not yet come to them. Thus did those before them deny."* (Qur'an 10:39)

> *"And they say, 'Why is this Qur'an not sent down to a great man from one of the two cities?'"* (Qur'an 43:31) — an objection that tacitly concedes the Qur'an's greatness.

The lesson the tradition draws is methodological: the arguments of unbelief do not need to be *consistent* with one another, because they are not produced by reason. They are produced by the will, and reason is then employed to dress them.

**The Modern Shape of "We Heard This Not Among Our Fathers"**

The commentators' diagnosis transfers with uncomfortable precision.

Ancestral custom in the modern world appears as inherited ideology, national myth, professional consensus, family prejudice, and — most powerfully — as the ambient assumptions of a culture that no one remembers adopting. The form of the objection is identical: *this is not what we have always done; this is not what respectable people believe; this is new, and newness is disqualifying.*

The Qur'an's counter-principle is not that novelty is good. It is that *truth is not determined by age or by majority*:

> *"And if you obey most of those on earth, they will lead you astray from the way of God. They follow nothing but conjecture, and they do nothing but guess."* (Qur'an 6:116)

> *"Say: 'Bring your proof, if you are truthful.'"* (Qur'an 2:111)

The demand is for *burhān* — evidence — and the sūrah has already used that word at verse 32 for what Moses was given. The criterion is not how old a belief is but what supports it.

**The Consequence of the Charge**

Finally, note what the charge cost those who made it. The verse says they called *clear signs* sorcery. The commentators point out the mechanism of self-harm: to preserve their position, they had to disable their own capacity to recognize truth.

The Qur'an describes this as a real, progressive condition:

> *"And We shall turn their hearts and their eyes away, even as they did not believe at first, and We shall leave them in their transgression, wandering blindly."* (Qur'an 6:110)

> *"And when they deviated, God made their hearts deviate."* (Qur'an 61:5)

The tradition is careful that this is requital and not compulsion: the deviation comes first, and the sealing is the consequence of it, not the cause. The person who refuses the first sign finds the second one harder to see. Al-Rāzī states it as: denial is a habit, and habits form the soul.

That is the real stakes of this verse. The men in Pharaoh's court were not merely rejecting a message; they were, in that moment, making themselves into the kind of people who could not receive one. The sūrah will describe where that process ends in verse 42 and, with terrifying economy, in verse 78.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:37

> **Moses said, "My Lord knows best who brings guidance from Him, and whose end is the Abode. Truly the wrongdoers will not prosper."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**How a Prophet Answers Mockery**

The commentators note immediately what Moses did *not* do. He did not defend his signs, did not debate the charge of sorcery, did not produce further evidence on the spot, and did not answer the appeal to ancestral custom with a counter-argument.

He said three things: that God knows who is guided; that God knows whose end is the Abode; and that wrongdoers do not prosper.

The structure is worth analyzing, because it is the Qur'an's model response to ideological rejection, and it appears again and again.

**"My Lord Knows Best Who Brings Guidance From Him"**

*Rabbī aʿlamu bi-man jāʾa bi-al-hudā min ʿindihi*. The first clause transfers the *verdict* from the audience to God.

The commentators read this as the definitive answer to the charge of sorcery. Moses is not claiming to be right; he is appealing to the only Judge whose assessment matters. The question "who really brings guidance?" is not settled by a court in Egypt, or by a majority, or by ancestry, or by eloquence. It is settled by God, and His knowledge is complete.

The Arabic uses the comparative form *aʿlamu* — *most knowing* — which the commentators note is not a comparison between two knowers but an intensive: He knows best, and no one else knows at all in this matter. The Qur'an uses the same construction for the question of who is truly guided:

> *"Say: 'Each awaits; so await. And you will know who are the companions of the straight path and who is rightly guided.'"* (Qur'an 20:135)

> *"And they say, 'Who is better in religion than one who submits his face to God, being a doer of good, and follows the creed of Abraham, a man of pure faith?'"* (Qur'an 4:125)

There is a rhetorical point here as well that the commentators enjoy. The court had claimed to know — *we heard this not among our fathers* — and Moses' answer is that the claim to know is itself the disputed thing. He does not argue with their conclusion; he disputes their competence to reach one.

**"And Whose End Is the Abode"**

*Wa-man takūnu lahu ʿāqibatu al-dār*. The word *ʿāqibah* means the end, the outcome, the final consequence of a course of action — not merely what comes last in time but what a thing *results in*.

*Al-dār* — the Abode — is the Hereafter. The Qur'an uses the phrase *dār al-ākhirah* regularly for the abiding world:

> *"And the Abode of the Hereafter is better for those who are reverent. Will you not understand?"* (Qur'an 6:32)

Al-Rāzī notes a subtlety in this verse: *al-dār* is used here in its *beatific* sense — the good end, the Garden — because the opposite end is not called an *abode* in the same honorific way. The phrase therefore carries a quiet claim: the good outcome belongs to someone, and the question is to whom.

This is the second transfer. Moses moves the court's attention from the present verdict to the final one. They would judge him today; but the only judgment that determines anything is the one at the end.

The Qur'an states this principle as a consolation to every messenger and every believer who has been dismissed:

> *"And those who were arrogant will say to those who were oppressed, 'Did we turn you away from guidance after it had come to you? Nay, but you were guilty.'"* (Qur'an 34:32)

and:

> *"And the end is for the reverent."* (Qur'an 7:128; 11:49; 13:35; 28:83 — this sūrah returns to it at its close)

The frequency of that refrain across the Qur'an, and its appearance four times, is itself part of the consolation. The tradition has always read the repetition as God's repeated assurance to those who lose the argument in this world.

**"Truly the Wrongdoers Will Not Prosper"**

*Innahu lā yufliḥu al-ẓālimūn*. The final clause is a universal statement, and its form is emphatic: *innahu* with a negated verb covering all *ẓālimūn*.

The word *falāḥ* — rendered *prosper* — means success, attainment, flourishing, the achieving of what one is reaching for. Its opposite is not merely failure but *khasārah*, loss — the condition of a person who has spent his life on what does not deliver.

The term *ẓālimūn* here is comprehensive. The scholars note that *ẓulm* in the Qur'an has three graded senses, and the word's force changes by context:

*Ẓulm* as *shirk* — the greatest wrong, because it places worship where it does not belong:

> *"And Luqmān said to his son... 'O my son, ascribe no partners unto God; truly ascribing partners is a tremendous wrong.'"* (Qur'an 31:13)

*Ẓulm* as *wronging others* — oppression, theft, slander, violation of rights:

> *"The way [of blame] is only against those who wrong people and transgress in the land without right."* (Qur'an 42:42)

*Ẓulm* as *wronging oneself* by sin:

> *"Then of them are some who wrong themselves..."* (Qur'an 35:32)

In this verse, the commentators take *al-ẓālimūn* in the first and second senses together: those who reject God's signs and those who oppress His creatures — which is precisely the combination the sūrah has described in Pharaoh from the opening verse.

**The Logic of the Statement**

There is a logical claim embedded in *the wrongdoers will not prosper*, and the commentators have defended it against an obvious objection, because the objection is real: wrongdoers visibly *do* prosper. Pharaoh ruled for decades. Korah's wealth was the envy of a nation (v. 79). Tyrannies have lasted for centuries.

The tradition's answer has several components, and they are worth setting out because they constitute the Islamic doctrine of prosperity.

First, *falāḥ* in the Qur'an is an eschatological category before it is a worldly one. To *prosper* is to attain the good that does not end; whatever is attained for a season and then lost is not *falāḥ* by definition. The Qur'an makes this explicit in Korah's own story, where his wealth and his end are set side by side (vv. 76–81).

Second, the *apparent* prosperity of the wrongdoer is itself a form of deferment that has a name in the Qur'an: *istidrāj*, gradual enticement toward destruction.

> *"And let not those who disbelieve think that the respite We grant them is good for their souls. We grant them respite only that they may increase in sin; and theirs is a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

> *"So when they forgot that by which they had been reminded, We opened unto them the gates of all things, till, even as they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly; and lo! they were in despair."* (Qur'an 6:44)

And the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"When you see God giving a servant what he loves of this world while he persists in disobedience, that is only istidrāj."* Then he recited: *"So when they forgot that by which they had been reminded, We opened unto them the gates of all things..."* (Aḥmad; graded ḥasan)

Third, the *inner* condition of the wrongdoer is not prosperous even when his circumstances are. The Qur'an's Pharaoh is the proof: a man with absolute power who spends his reign hunting children, fearing dreams, and being publicly humiliated by a shepherd. The sūrah will describe his court's final state in verse 42 as *repugnant* — a description of inward ugliness that manifests outwardly, as al-Rāzī notes.

And fourth, the claim is a statement about *the end*, and the end is the only frame in which the verdict is final. The sūrah itself supplies the demonstration five verses later.

**Why This Is the Right Response**

The commentators draw a practical method from Moses' three sentences, and it is a method the tradition has recommended to anyone who is mocked for his religion.

*Do not enter the argument on the opponent's terms.* The charge of sorcery was unwinnable by debate, because the court was not weighing evidence. Moses did not try to prove he was not a sorcerer.

*Refer the verdict to God.* The question is not who wins the exchange but who is with God. That is settled by Him, not by the room.

*Move the frame to the end.* Every confrontation in this world is decided in a longer frame than the confrontation itself. The believer's advantage is that he knows the frame.

*State the principle without personalizing it.* Moses did not say "you will not prosper." He said *the wrongdoers will not prosper* — a universal statement from which the court could draw its own conclusion. This is the Qur'anic etiquette of admonition: state the law, let the listener apply it.

The Prophet ﷺ followed exactly this method. When the Quraysh demanded miracles, he was told:

> *"Say: 'Glory be to my Lord! Am I aught but a mortal messenger?'"* (Qur'an 17:93)

and when they mocked him, the Qur'an instructed:

> *"And indeed We know that thy breast is constrained by what they say. So glorify the praise of thy Lord and be among those who prostrate."* (Qur'an 15:97–98)

**Application**

For anyone who has been dismissed, ridiculed, or professionally sidelined for holding a religious conviction, this verse supplies an unusual kind of comfort — not the comfort of vindication in the same room, but the comfort of a transferred verdict.

The wrongdoer's prosperity is real for a season and is not what it appears. The believer's loss is real for a season and is not what it appears. And the interval between the two is measured by a God who *knows best who brings guidance from Him, and whose end is the Abode*.

The sūrah is about to demonstrate the principle in the most dramatic way available, and it will do so in four verses.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:38

> **Pharaoh said, "O notables! I know of no god for you other than myself. So kindle for me a fire, O Hāmān, for clay, and make me a tower, that haply I may behold the God of Moses, though truly I think he is among the liars."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim of Divinity, Stated Publicly**

*Wa-qāla Firʿawnu yā ayyuhā al-malaʾu mā ʿalimtu lakum min ilāhin ghayrī*. With this verse, the sūrah reaches the theological center of the entire Moses narrative.

The declaration is addressed to the *malaʾ* — the council of notables — and the commentators note the significance of that audience. This was not a private delusion muttered in a palace chamber. It was a public, constitutional claim, made to the men who administered Egypt: there is no god for you other than me.

The phrasing *mā ʿalimtu* — *I know of no* — has been discussed at length. Some commentators read it as a literal claim: I am the only god. Others read it as an assertion of exclusive authority: I recognize no authority over you but my own. The two readings converge, because in the ancient Egyptian order they were the same claim — pharaoh was understood as divine, the incarnate son of the sun god, and his will was law.

The Qur'an states Pharaoh's claim in its most brazen form elsewhere:

> *"And he gathered and called out, and said, 'I am your lord most high.'"* (Qur'an 79:24)

and, in the version addressed to his court:

> *"And Pharaoh said, 'O notables, I know of no god for you other than myself...'"* (Qur'an 28:38) — with the parallel at 40:36.

The commentators note that the claim is *political theology*, and that its function was to place Pharaoh beyond every check. If the king is the source of law, no law binds him. If the king is god, disobedience is not crime but sacrilege.

**"Kindle for Me a Fire, O Hāmān, for Clay"**

*Fa-awqid lī yā Hāmānu ʿalā al-ṭīni*. The command is specific and technological: fire the clay — bake bricks. This was not a metaphor. Construction in the Nile valley used mud-brick, and a monumental tower would require fired brick or kiln-fired brick for its upper courses.

The commentators note the detail as evidence of the account's realism. Al-Rāzī points out that Pharaoh specifies the *material process* — fire upon clay — because he is giving an engineering order, not uttering a poetic boast.

*Wa-ijʿal lī ṣarḥan* — *and make me a tower*, or palace, or lofty structure. The word *ṣarḥ* denotes a tall, prominent, elevated building; the lexicographers gloss it as a palace or any lofty edifice. The same word is used of Solomon's hall of glass:

> *"It was said to her, 'Enter the palace (al-ṣarḥ).' But when she saw it, she supposed it to be a pool of water."* (Qur'an 27:44)

**"That Haply I May Behold the God of Moses"**

*Laʿallī aṭṭaliʿu ilā ilāhi Mūsā*. The word *aṭṭaliʿu* means to ascend in order to look down upon, to gain a vantage over. Pharaoh intends to climb above and inspect.

Was this serious, or was it mockery? The commentators divide, and the division is worth examining.

*The mockery reading.* Al-Rāzī argues strenuously that this was rhetorical — a public sneer intended to reduce Moses' God to a physical object that could be located in the sky and inspected from a height. On this reading, Pharaoh is saying, in effect: *your God is supposedly up there; let me build a ladder and check.*

*The literal reading.* Other commentators took him at his word: that a man who had convinced himself of his own divinity might indeed think a rival god could be reached by climbing.
