The two readings are not incompatible, and the commentators note that mockery often contains a real anxiety. Pharaoh's court had just watched the magicians defect. The signs had been performed and could not be explained away in the room. A public jest about climbing to heaven was, among other things, a way of reasserting control over a situation that had slipped.

**The Legend of the Arrow, and Why Al-Rāzī Rejected It**

The commentaries transmit a popular account: that Pharaoh actually built the tower, climbed it, shot an arrow into the sky, and that the arrow fell back stained with blood — which Pharaoh then presented as evidence that he had killed the God of Moses.

Al-Rāzī's response is famous and deserves quoting in substance, because it is the tradition's critical faculty at its best. He says the report is not one that "a sane person" could accept, and he raises objections that any reader can verify: how could a structure be built to reach the sky? And if it were, how could a man standing on it shoot an arrow and have it return stained with blood — and who could possibly be deceived by such a trick? He further notes that the story attributes to Pharaoh a degree of stupidity incompatible with the shrewdness the Qur'an itself attributes to him elsewhere.

Al-Ṭabarī transmits the report more neutrally, as he transmits everything, but notes that nothing in the Qur'an requires it. The sound position is that the Qur'an records the *order* to build; whether anything was built is unknown, and the story of the arrow is legend.

The methodological lesson generalizes: the classical commentarial tradition is not credulous by default. Its best practitioners recorded reports and then evaluated them against reason, revelation, and the demands of the narrative.

**Theology of the Divine Elevation: *Fawqiyyah* Without Place**

The verse raises a creedal question the scholars addressed carefully: does Pharaoh's talk of *ascending* to God imply that God is up in the sky, in a place?

The Sunni position, as formulated by the early scholars and elaborated by Ibn Taymiyyah, Ibn al-Qayyim, and the later tradition, holds:

God is *above* His creation in a manner befitting His majesty — *fawqiyyah* is affirmed, because the Qur'an and Sunnah affirm it:

> *"The Merciful, established upon the Throne."* (Qur'an 20:5)

> *"Do you feel secure that He Who is in the heaven will not cause the earth to swallow you?"* (Qur'an 67:16)

> *"And to Him ascend the good words."* (Qur'an 35:10)

And the Prophet ﷺ asked a slave woman, *"Where is God?"* and she indicated upward, and he said, *"Free her, for she is a believer."* (Muslim 537)

But this "above" is not *containment*. God is not inside the sky; the sky and everything else are held by Him. The early scholars said: *God was, and there was no place; and He is now as He ever was* — before creation there was no "above," and His being above creation is a relation that began with creation, not a change in God.

The crucial distinction the tradition insists upon is between *ʿuluww* — exaltedness, highness of rank and of being — and *makān* — location. Pharaoh's error was not that he thought God was high; it was that he thought God was *located* somewhere he could reach by climbing. That is the error of every theology that makes God a very large object within the universe.

The verse thus mocks Pharaoh with his own words. He who claimed to be *most high* (79:24) sought to climb to the One who truly is Most High, and could not construct a ladder long enough to reach the bottom of His creation.

**"Though Truly I Think He Is Among the Liars"**

*Wa-innī la-aẓunnuhu mina al-kādhibīn*. The clause is revealing, and the commentators dwell on it.

Note the tense and the particle: *innī la-aẓunnuhu* — *truly I do think him* — with emphatic *inna* and *la*. The word *ẓann* means conjecture, opinion, supposition — it is not knowledge. Pharaoh says, in effect: I have formed the opinion that Moses is lying.

The commentators note the rhetorical structure of the whole verse, because it is a study in intellectual dishonesty. Pharaoh:

Asks for a tower to *behold* the God of Moses — which presupposes the possibility of His existence.

And in the same sentence declares that he *thinks* Moses is lying — which denies it.

This is *ẓann* in the precise Qur'anic sense: a position held without evidence, asserted with conviction, and deployed because it is useful. The Qur'an condemns it throughout:

> *"And they follow naught but conjecture, and conjecture avails naught against the truth."* (Qur'an 53:28)

> *"And most of them follow naught but conjecture. Truly conjecture avails naught against the truth."* (Qur'an 10:36)

There is a deep psychological observation here that the spiritual commentators developed. Pharaoh had *seen* the signs. The court had seen them. The magicians had recognized them. What Pharaoh produced in response was not an argument but a *mood* — *I think he is lying* — because a mood requires no defense and cannot be refuted.

The tradition notes that this is the last refuge of the person who has lost the argument but will not concede: I just don't think so.

**The Tyrant's Logistics: What the Verse Reveals About Power**

One more observation deserves attention, because the Qur'an places it here deliberately.

Pharaoh's response to a challenge to his theology was to order *construction*. He did not summon Moses for debate; he summoned bricks. Faced with an argument he could not answer, he commissioned a building.

The commentators note this as the characteristic move of institutional power everywhere: when the claim cannot be refuted, build something. A monument, a program, a committee, a media campaign, an edifice large enough to change the subject. The tower is the archetype of the vanity project as a substitute for an answer.

And the sūrah's next verses show where that leads. The tower was never finished — or was finished and used for nothing. The owner of it drowned in a sea he had spent his life believing he controlled.

**Application**

For the reader, this verse is the definitive portrait of *istikbār* — the arrogance that rejects truth — and the tradition asks each person to check himself against it.

Its signature symptoms are visible in the verse. It claims ultimacy: *no god for you other than me*. It converts every challenge into a project rather than a question. It uses ridicule where argument is unavailable. And it substitutes conjecture for knowledge while speaking in the language of certainty.

The cure the tradition prescribes is the opposite of all four: to recognize a source of authority above oneself, to let a challenge be a question rather than a project, to argue rather than sneer, and to say *I do not know* where one does not know.

The Prophet ﷺ identified the root of the disease in a single sentence:

> *"Arrogance is rejecting the truth and despising people."* (Muslim 91)

Pharaoh's verse says both: he rejected the truth in the clause about Moses being a liar, and he despised his people in the claim that he was their only god.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:39

> **And he and his hosts waxed arrogant upon the earth, without right, and they thought they would not be returned unto Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict: Arrogance and Denial of Return**

*Wa-stakbara huwa wa-junūduhu fī al-arḍi bi-ghayri al-ḥaqqi wa-ẓannū annahum ilaynā lā yurjaʿūn*.

Two crimes are named in one sentence, and the commentators have long noted the causal relationship between them. The first is *istikbār* — arrogance, the claim to a greatness one does not have. The second is the denial of the return — the belief that there is no reckoning. And the second is the *enabler* of the first: a person who truly believed he would stand before his Maker would not behave this way.

**"Waxed Arrogant" — *Istakbara* and Its Two Components**

The root *k-b-r* gives greatness. The form *istakbara* means to seek greatness, to claim greatness, to *consider* oneself great. The commentators note the grammar: it is not merely *being* great but the reflexive claiming of it, and the Qur'an uses the same form for Iblīs:

> *"And when We said to the angels, 'Prostrate before Adam,' they prostrated, except for Iblīs; he refused and waxed arrogant, and he was among the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 2:34)

The connection is not incidental. The tradition has always taught that arrogance is the primordial sin — the first sin in existence and the root of the rest. The Prophet ﷺ defined it precisely:

> *"Arrogance is rejecting the truth and despising people."* (Muslim 91)

That definition has two halves, and this verse shows both. Pharaoh rejected the truth that Moses brought, and he despised the people he enslaved.

The Prophet ﷺ also stated the consequence:

> *"No one who has an atom's weight of arrogance in his heart will enter Paradise."* A man said, "But a man likes his garment to be fine and his sandals to be fine." He said: *"Truly God is beautiful and loves beauty. Arrogance is rejecting the truth and despising people."* (Muslim 91)

The clarification in that ḥadīth is theologically important: the prohibition is not on excellence, beauty, or dignity. The Prophet ﷺ himself wore fine garments on occasion. What is condemned is the *disposition* — the inward claim to a status above the truth and above other people.

**"Upon the Earth" — Where the Arrogance Was Exercised**

*Fī al-arḍ*. The commentators note the phrase's resonance. The earth is God's, and it is the place of testing; to be arrogant *upon the earth* is to behave as an owner in a house that belongs to someone else.

The Qur'an uses the same phrase for the arrogant in every age:

> *"Thus does God seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant."* (Qur'an 40:35)

> *"Truly those who dispute concerning the signs of God without any authority having come to them — there is naught in their breasts but pride, which they shall never attain."* (Qur'an 40:56)

The word *arḍ* also carries a quiet irony. The earth is what will swallow Korah (v. 81), and it is the earth that will be changed on the Day of Resurrection (14:48). Those who are arrogant *upon* it are standing on something that will outlast them and that will eventually testify against them.

**"Without Right" — *Bi-ghayri al-Ḥaqq***

*Bi-ghayri al-ḥaqqi* — *without right*, *without truth*, *without warrant*.

The commentators pause over this qualifier, and it is one of the most theologically loaded phrases in the verse. It concedes, in principle, that greatness *can* be claimed with right — that is, that there is such a thing as legitimate authority.

Islam does not teach that all greatness is false. God is *al-Kabīr*, the Greatest, and *al-Mutakabbir*, the One whose greatness is real. And God delegates real authority to human beings: kingship, governance, knowledge, wealth, and fatherhood are all real forms of greatness when they are received as trusts rather than seized as possessions.

The Prophet ﷺ was told:

> *"And lower thy wing to the believers."* (Qur'an 15:88)

— and he was also, at the same time, the acknowledged leader of a state. Greatness held as a trust is not arrogance; greatness claimed as a possession is.

The distinction is drawn precisely in the Qur'an's account of Solomon, who had more power than Pharaoh and was not arrogant:

> *"And he said, 'My Lord, forgive me and grant me a dominion such as will not befit anyone after me. Truly Thou art the Bestower.'"* (Qur'an 38:35)

and of Dhū al-Qarnayn, who was given authority over the earth and attributed it entirely to God:

> *"Truly We established him in the earth and gave him a way to everything."* (Qur'an 18:84)

Pharaoh's crime was not that he had power; it was that he held it *without right* — that is, without acknowledging the Giver, without accepting limits, and without accountability.

**"And They Thought They Would Not Be Returned Unto Us"**

*Wa-ẓannū annahum ilaynā lā yurjaʿūn*. This is the root, and the commentators are emphatic about it.

The verb is *ẓannū* — *they thought*, *they supposed*, *they assumed*. As at verse 38, the Qur'an uses the vocabulary of conjecture for the creed of the arrogant. They did not *know* that there was no return; they assumed it, and built a civilization on the assumption.

The return (*marjiʿ*) is one of the three certainties that the Qur'an presents as the foundation of the moral life, alongside God's oneness and prophethood. It is asserted throughout the Book and denied by those who wish to live without accountability:

> *"Did you then think that We created you in jest, and that unto Us you would not be returned?"* (Qur'an 23:115)

> *"Truly he thought that he would never return."* (Qur'an 84:14)

> *"And they say, 'There is naught but our life in this world; we die and we live, and nothing destroys us save time.' But they have no knowledge of that; they do but conjecture."* (Qur'an 45:24)

The last verse is the crucial one, because it identifies the denial of the return as *conjecture dressed as knowledge*.

**The Causal Logic: Why Denying the Return Produces Arrogance**

The commentators spell out the connection that the verse assumes, and it is worth stating in full because it is the practical argument of the entire sūrah.

If there is no return, then there is no reckoning. If there is no reckoning, then power is the only real measure. If power is the only measure, then the powerful are entitled to what they can take. And if the powerful are entitled to what they can take, then enslaving a people, killing their children, and declaring oneself divine are not crimes but exercises of sovereignty.

Every step in that chain is rational *given* the first premise. This is why the Qur'an treats the denial of the Hereafter as the foundational error rather than as one error among many. It is the premise from which all the others follow.

The converse is also true, and the Qur'an argues it explicitly:

> *"Or did those who commit evil deeds think that We would make them like those who believe and work righteousness — equal in their life and their death? Evil is what they judge!"* (Qur'an 45:21)

> *"Shall We then treat those who submit as the guilty? What ails you? How do you judge?"* (Qur'an 68:35–36)

If the two are treated alike, then justice is not real, and if justice is not real, then God is not just. The Qur'an rejects this as unworthy of the Creator:

> *"God bears witness that there is no god but He — and the angels and the possessors of knowledge — upholding justice. There is no god but He, the Mighty, the Wise."* (Qur'an 3:18)

**"He and His Hosts" — The Architecture of Complicity Again**

As at verse 6 and verse 8, the Qur'an names not only Pharaoh but *junūduhu* — his hosts, his armies, his apparatus.

The commentators note the consistency. Arrogance in the Qur'an is never a private vice; it is always structural. The hosts shared the assumption and participated in the conduct, and they will share the consequence (v. 40).

This raises the question of collective guilt that the tradition handled with care, as discussed at verse 6. The principle is that each soul is accountable for its own choices:

> *"No bearer of burdens bears the burden of another."* (Qur'an 6:164)

But those who *execute* oppression choose to do so, and the Qur'an's scenes of the Resurrection show the followers and the followed contending:

> *"They will say, 'Our Lord! We obeyed our leaders and our great ones, and they led us astray from the way. Our Lord, give them double the punishment and curse them with a great curse.'"* (Qur'an 33:67–68)

The tradition's practical conclusion for the ordinary person inside an unjust structure is that complicity is a choice made daily, and that the least acceptable option is the one most commonly chosen — to go along while telling oneself that the decision was made elsewhere. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whosoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand; if he cannot, then with his tongue; and if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith."* (Muslim 49)

**The Psychology of the Unaccountable Life**

There is a further observation embedded in the verse, and the spiritual commentators developed it at length.

The assumption that one will not be returned produces a very particular psychology: a life organized around the horizon of death as a wall rather than as a door. If death is the end, then everything must be extracted before it arrives — pleasure, power, security, legacy. This generates a permanent, low-grade desperation that no amount of acquisition quiets, because the acquisitions are measured against an absolute end.

The Qur'an describes the resulting condition as a kind of fever:

> *"Know that the life of this world is but play and diversion and adornment and boasting among you and rivalry in wealth and children."* (Qur'an 57:20)

And it describes the alternative — the life lived toward a return — as one with a fundamentally different texture:

> *"Whoever hopes for the meeting with God, truly the term appointed by God is coming; and He is the Hearing, the Knowing."* (Qur'an 29:5)

The tradition's pastoral counsel is that remembrance of the return is not morbid but *calming*, because it restores proportion. A person who knows he will give an account can afford to be patient, can afford to lose, and does not need to secure everything at once.

**Application**

The verse's warning applies at every scale, and the tradition has never restricted its reference to ancient Egypt.

On the personal scale: any area of life in which a person behaves as though there were no accountability is a small Pharaohate. It may be a business, a household, a career, or an appetite. The diagnostic question the verse supplies is simple — where in my life do I act as though I will not be returned?

On the social scale: any institution that denies an external standard of justice is, by the verse's logic, on its way to arrogance. The denial need not be verbal. It is enough to behave as though the only measure of a decision's worth is whether it can be enforced.

And the verse supplies the corrective to the whole sūrah's drama. Pharaoh's tower, Korah's treasure, and the notables' conspiracies were all constructed on a single assumption — *we will not be returned*. The sūrah will spend its remaining verses demonstrating what happens when that assumption meets reality.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:40

> **So We seized him and his hosts, and cast them into the sea. So behold how the wrongdoers fared in the end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The End of the Story in One Sentence**

*Fa-akhadhnāhu wa-junūdahu fa-nabadhnāhum fī al-yammi fa-nẓur kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu al-ẓālimīn*.

In eleven Arabic words, the greatest empire of its age is finished. The sūrah has spent thirty-nine verses building toward this sentence, and the sentence itself is almost casual in its brevity. The commentators note this as one of the Qur'an's most deliberate rhetorical effects: the destruction of Pharaoh is not narrated as a battle, because it was not one. It is narrated as a disposal.

**"We Seized Him" — *Akhadhnā* and the Divine Grasp**

*Fa-akhadhnāhu* — *so We seized him*. The verb *akhadha* is ordinary Arabic for taking, but when the Qur'an uses it of God's dealing with the guilty, it carries the sense of an arrest that cannot be resisted. The same verb is used for the punishment of earlier peoples:

> *"So each We seized for his sin: among them were those upon whom We sent a storm of stones, and among them were those whom the Cry seized..."* (Qur'an 29:40)

> *"And thy Lord is not one to destroy the towns unjustly while their people are doing right."* (Qur'an 11:117)

The commentators note the phrase's compactness: no account of the pursuit, the parting of the sea, or the closing of the water is given here. The sūrah has already made its point about Pharaoh's power, and the destruction is reported as the simple, inevitable consequence of that power meeting its Author.

**"And Cast Them Into the Sea"**

*Fa-nabadhnāhum fī al-yammi*. The verb *nabadha* means to fling, to cast away, to throw something aside — the word used for discarding a worthless object.

The commentators remark on the verb's contempt. Pharaoh, who ordered infants thrown into the river (v. 7 in effect, and explicitly at 7:127), was himself *thrown* — and the word the Qur'an uses for him is the word used for refuse. The correspondence is not subtle, and it is characteristic of the Qur'an's treatment of the arrogant: the punishment mirrors the crime.

Moses was cast into the river as an infant by his mother's obedient hand and was delivered. Pharaoh was cast into the sea by God's hand and was not.

*Al-yamm* is the sea — a word used in the Qur'an specifically for the sea of the Exodus:

> *"And We revealed to Moses: 'Travel by night with My servants; strike for them a dry path through the sea.'"* (Qur'an 20:77)

> *"And We parted the sea, and We drowned the people of Pharaoh while you were looking on."* (Qur'an 2:50)

> *"Then We saved Moses and those with him, all together; then We drowned the others."* (Qur'an 26:65–66)

**Why the Sea?**

The commentators note the providential fit, because it is too exact to be incidental.

Egypt was the Nile civilization. Its wealth, its agriculture, its transport, its religion, and its sense of security all rested on water. The Nile was the source of the abundance that made Pharaoh's arrogance materially possible. And the instrument of his destruction was water — the same element, turned against him.

Moses had been cast into the *river* as an infant and was saved by it; Pharaoh's people were destroyed by the sea. The water that delivered the oppressed drowned the oppressor. The Qur'an states the parallel explicitly elsewhere:

> *"And We drowned the people of Pharaoh while you were looking on."* (Qur'an 2:50)

The tradition has drawn from this the principle that the means of a person's pride frequently become the means of his fall. Korah will be destroyed by the earth that produced his treasure (v. 81). The wealthy man of the two gardens will be destroyed by his own orchard (18:32–42). The pattern is stated generally:

> *"And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 3:54)

— where the "plotting" attributed to God means, as the creed insists, that He brings about consequences through means the plotters did not anticipate, and that no moral blame attaches to Him for it.

**"Behold How the Wrongdoers Fared in the End" — The Qur'an's Most Repeated Invitation**

*Fa-nẓur kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu al-ẓālimīn*. The commentators note that this refrain, in one form or another, recurs dozens of times in the Qur'an, and they ask why.

The answer they give is that the Qur'an is not primarily in the business of *informing* the reader about history; it is in the business of *training* him to read it. The imperative *unẓur* — *look*, *consider*, *observe* — is a command to develop a faculty.

The refrain appears in the same or similar form throughout:

> *"Say: 'Travel through the earth and see how the end of the guilty was.'"* (Qur'an 27:69)

> *"Have they not traveled in the land and seen how was the end of those before them? God destroyed them utterly; and for the disbelievers is the like thereof."* (Qur'an 47:10)

> *"Say: 'Travel through the land and observe how He originated creation; then God brings about the final bringing-forth.'"* (Qur'an 29:20)

The tradition made *iʿtibār* — taking lesson through comparison — a formal discipline on the basis of these verses. The method is: identify the pattern in a past case, and then apply it to a present one. The scholars of *ʿilm al-tārīkh* and of *siyar* (the study of states and their rise and fall) treated it as the Qur'anic foundation of their fields, and Ibn Khaldūn's analysis of the rise and fall of dynasties stands in this lineage.

**The Word *ʿĀqibah* — The End That Is Also the Meaning**

The word rendered *end* is *ʿāqibah*, and the commentators are precise about its sense. It does not mean merely *what came last*. It means the *outcome*, the *upshot*, the thing to which a course of action actually led.

This distinction matters enormously for the Qur'an's argument. The *last* thing that happened to Pharaoh's reign was not the drowning; it was a long reign of power. The *ʿāqibah* was the drowning, because the drowning is where his course of action actually terminated in the only frame that counts.

The Qur'an makes the same point about wealth and children:

> *"Your wealth and your children are only a trial; and with God is a mighty reward."* (Qur'an 64:15)

— that is, apparent goods are not necessarily the *outcome* of a life.

This is why the Qur'an's argument for the Hereafter frequently takes the form of an *ʿāqibah* comparison. It asks the reader to judge by outcome rather than by appearance, and it insists that the outcome is not visible within a single lifetime:

> *"Do they not see that We come to the land, diminishing it at its borders?"* (Qur'an 13:41)

**Theological Note: Justice, Timing, and the Problem of Delay**

The verse raises the question that every thoughtful reader asks, and the commentators address it directly: why did God wait?

Pharaoh enslaved a people for generations, killed their children, and declared himself divine. Moses' mission lasted years. Where was God during all of it?

The tradition's answer has several parts.

*God's timing is not our timing, and it is not arbitrary.* The Qur'an states the principle of respite:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve think that the respite We grant them is good for their souls. We grant them respite only that they may increase in sin."* (Qur'an 3:178)

The respite is not indifference; it is the completion of the case against them, and the extension of the opportunity to turn back. Pharaoh's own story proves the point: he was given years of signs, warnings, and negotiations, and he was offered the chance to release Israel after each plague.

*The delay is itself part of the trial of the believers.* The Qur'an says to the believers under persecution:

> *"Or did you suppose that you would enter the Garden while there had not yet come to you the like of what came to those who passed away before you? Affliction and adversity befell them... until the Messenger and those who believed with him said, 'When is the help of God?' Truly the help of God is near."* (Qur'an 2:214)

*And the destruction, when it comes, is not vengeance but justice.* The tradition is emphatic that God does not punish without prior warning, and the Qur'an states it as a universal rule, repeated twice in this very sūrah:

> *"And thy Lord never destroys towns until He sends a messenger to their mother city."* (Qur'an 28:59)

The sūrah will state that rule twenty verses after this one, and its placement there is a deliberate answer to the reader's unspoken question about the timing of verse 40.

**"The Wrongdoers" — The Category, Not Just the Man**

The refrain says *al-ẓālimūn*, the wrongdoers, plural, and not "Pharaoh." The commentators note the generalization: this is a category, and Pharaoh is its most complete instance.

The verse is therefore a promise as well as a report. Every reader who has been wronged is being told that wrongdoing has a structural end, and every reader who is wronging is being told the same. The tradition has always read such verses in both directions, and the Prophet ﷺ warned his own community against the assumption that they were exempt:

> *"Beware of oppression, for oppression will be darkness on the Day of Resurrection."* (Muslim 2578)

and, in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"O My servants, I have forbidden oppression for Myself, and I have made it forbidden among you, so do not oppress one another."* (Muslim 2577)

The Prophet ﷺ also spoke of the historical pattern with specific reference to his own community's future, warning that the nations before them were destroyed when a noble person among them committed theft and was left unpunished while a weak person was punished for the same:

> *"Those before you were destroyed only because when a noble among them stole, they let him go, and when a weak one among them stole, they imposed the punishment on him. By God, if Fāṭimah the daughter of Muhammad stole, I would cut off her hand."* (al-Bukhārī 3475; Muslim 1688)

**Application: Learning to Read for the Outcome**

The verse's most transferable instruction is the command *unẓur* — look.

In practice, this means developing the habit of evaluating a course of action by where it terminates rather than by how it is going. Applied to a career, a relationship, a business practice, a political movement, or a personal habit, the question is the same: what is the *ʿāqibah* of this?

The Qur'an's confidence is that the *ʿāqibah* of wrongdoing is knowable in advance, because it is built into the structure of things and not merely imposed at the end. Wrongdoing corrodes the one who does it, isolates him, makes him dependent on escalating measures, and brings him into collision with forces he does not control. Pharaoh's story is not a fable about a magical punishment; it is a demonstration of what a tyranny does to itself.

And for the person who is suffering under one: the verse is the sūrah's clearest statement that the tyrant's end is not a hope but a pattern. The command *behold* is addressed to the reader who can see it — and the sūrah's remaining verses will show that the bystanders saw it too, and said, in the morning after Korah's destruction: *Alas! It seems the disbelievers will not prosper* (v. 82).

That is what *iʿtibār* produces: not a private satisfaction but a public recognition that the world is governed, and that the governance is just.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:41

> **And We made them imams calling unto the Fire. And on the Day of Resurrection they will not be helped.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mirror of Verse 5**

The commentators have always read this verse against verse 5, and the comparison is one of the sūrah's most deliberate structures.

There, of the oppressed Israelites: *We desired to be gracious to those who were oppressed in the land, and to make them **imams**, and to make them the heirs.*

Here, of the oppressors: *We made them **imams** calling unto the Fire.*

The same word is used for both, and the symmetry is the theology. Leadership is a reality in both directions. Every human being is a leader of something — a household, a field, a institution, a circle of friends, a following — and the Qur'an's judgment on leadership is not whether one leads but *where one leads people to*.

**"We Made Them Imams" — The Passive of Consequence**

*Wa-jaʿalnāhum aʾimmah*. The verb *jaʿala* — to make, to appoint — is used here as it was at verse 5, and the commentators are careful about its sense in each place.

At verse 5, the making was a gift of grace. Here, it is a *consequence*: God did not commission Pharaoh to call people to the Fire; Pharaoh chose that, and God made the consequence of his choice into a permanent reality. The distinction is standard in the tradition's treatment of verses where God "makes" someone do something: where the act is obedience, the making is grace (*tawfīq*); where the act is sin, the making is the decreeing of its consequence (*khidhlān*, the withdrawal of aid, and *iqāmat al-ʿāqibah*, establishing the outcome).

The scholars of creed formulated the rule precisely:

> *"Whatever God wills is, and whatever He does not will is not. But His will is not His pleasure, and His decree is not His command."*

Pharaoh is called an imam calling to the Fire because he *is* one — because that is what his life accomplished and what his example does to those who follow it.

**"Calling Unto the Fire" — *Yadʿūna ilā al-Nār***

The phrase is active and present-tense: *they call*, continuously. The commentators note what this implies about posthumous influence.

Al-Rāzī's reading is that they were foremost in the beliefs and actions that lead to the Fire, and they called others to them — and that the calling does not stop with their deaths. The example of a tyrant continues to recruit. The propagandists of an oppressive order continue to make its case long after the order has fallen.

This is the doctrine of *daʿwah* applied in the negative, and it is one of the most sobering ideas in the tradition. The Prophet ﷺ stated it in both directions in a single ḥadīth:

> *"Whoever calls to guidance has a reward like the rewards of all who follow him, without diminishing anything from their rewards; and whoever calls to misguidance bears a sin like the sins of all who follow him, without diminishing anything from their sins."* (Muslim 2674)

And he said:

> *"Whoever sets a bad precedent in Islam will bear its sin and the sin of whoever acts upon it after him, without diminishing anything from their sins."* (Muslim 1017)

The commentators drew a direct practical conclusion from this verse and that ḥadīth: influence is not neutral, and a person is accountable not only for his own deeds but for the deeds his example generates in others. This is why the tradition placed such emphasis on the company one keeps and the precedent one sets, and why the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"A person is upon the religion of his close friend, so let each of you look to whom he befriends."* (Abū Dāwūd 4833; al-Tirmidhī 2378 — graded ḥasan)

**Why the Fire Is Named Rather Than Simply Punishment**

The commentators note the specificity. The verse says they call to *al-nār* — the Fire — not merely to error or to sin.

This is consistent with the Qur'an's method elsewhere: the destination is named so that the follower may know where the road leads. The road signs are there:

> *"O you who believe, obey God and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you... That is better and fairer in the end."* (Qur'an 4:59)

> *"And whosoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him and follows other than the way of the believers, We shall leave him to what he has chosen, and We shall expose him to Hell — an evil journey's end."* (Qur'an 4:115)

The naming of the destination is an act of mercy in the present tense: the reader of this verse is being warned while warning is still useful.

**"And on the Day of Resurrection They Will Not Be Helped"**

*Wa-yawma al-qiyāmati hum lā yunṣarūn*. The negation is absolute: no help, from any quarter, on that Day.

The commentators identify the kinds of help that will be unavailable, and the enumeration is itself the warning.

*No intercessor.* In this world, the powerful can be shielded by advocates, connections, and influence. The Qur'an repeatedly states that on that Day, the ordinary machinery of patronage ceases:

> *"And fear a Day when no soul shall avail another soul at all; nor shall intercession be accepted from it; nor shall any compensation be taken from it; nor shall they be helped."* (Qur'an 2:48)

> *"O you who believe, spend from that which We have provided you before a Day comes in which there is no bargaining, no friendship, and no intercession."* (Qur'an 2:254)

*No wealth.* The Qur'an:

> *"Truly those who disbelieve — if they possessed all that is in the earth, and the like of it with it, to ransom themselves from the punishment of the Day of Resurrection, it would not be accepted from them."* (Qur'an 5:36)

*No followers.* This is the specific relevance for the *imams calling to the Fire*. In this world they had armies; on that Day, those armies will be adversaries. The Qur'an describes the scene:

> *"When those who were followed disown those who followed, and they see the punishment, and all ties between them are severed."* (Qur'an 2:166)

*And no power of their own.* The verse's passive construction (*lā yunṣarūn* — they will not be helped) implies that they cannot help themselves either, which the sūrah will state explicitly of Korah at verse 81: *he had no party who could help him against God, nor could he help himself.*

**Theological Note: Who *Will* Be Helped?**

The commentators complete the picture by noting that the Qur'an promises help to others on that Day, and the contrast defines the condition.

> *"Truly We shall help Our messengers and those who believe, in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses shall stand forth — the Day when their excuses will not avail the wrongdoers, and theirs will be the curse, and theirs the evil abode."* (Qur'an 40:51–52)

> *"God is the Protector of those who believe; He brings them out of darkness into light."* (Qur'an 2:257)

The condition of being helped is therefore *walāyah* — alliance with God — which is established in this life by faith and obedience, and which cannot be manufactured on the Day itself. The tradition's formulation is that the deeds are the credentials, and the credentials must be acquired before the gate.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

Structurally, verse 41 begins the sūrah's first explicit turn to the Hereafter in the Moses narrative. Verses 40–42 form a triad: the destruction in this world (v. 40), the leadership to the Fire and the absence of help (v. 41), and the curse in this world and disgrace in the next (v. 42).

The commentators note the completeness of the judgment. Tyranny's consequences are threefold and none of them is deferred to a single venue: it is destroyed in history, it is exposed in the Hereafter, and it leaves a stain that outlives it in this world's memory.

**Application: The Question of What You Are Leading People Toward**

The verse's most piercing practical use is the question it puts to everyone.

*Imām* in the Qur'anic sense is not an office; it is a direction. A parent is an imam to his children. A teacher is an imam to his students. A manager is an imam to his team. A person with a public platform, a large account, or even simply a circle of friends who take their cues from him is an imam to those people.

The question the verse raises is not whether one leads — one does — but *to what*. The categories here are stark: calling to the Fire or calling to what saves from it. And the terrifying feature of the calling is that it continues after the caller is gone. Habits, opinions, fashions, and permissions set in motion by a person propagate in people he will never meet.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the positive version of this in a ḥadīth that the commentators set beside this verse:

> *"When a person dies, his deeds come to an end except for three: ongoing charity, knowledge that is benefited from, or a righteous child who prays for him."* (Muslim 1631)

Three continuing legacies — and the verse before us is their exact negative.

There is also a word of caution for the *follower*. If the leaders one is following lead to what the Qur'an calls the Fire, then on the Day they will not be helped — and, by the Qur'an's own account elsewhere, neither will those who followed them knowingly:

> *"And they will say, 'Our Lord! Truly we obeyed our leaders and our great ones, and they led us astray from the way.'"* (Qur'an 33:67)

The excuses of that Day are recorded in the Qur'an so that they can be examined now, while there is still time to stop making them.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:42

> **And We caused them to be pursued by a curse in this world. And on the Day of Resurrection they will be among the repugnant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Curse That Follows**

*Wa-atbaʿnāhum fī hādhihi al-dunyā laʿnatan*. The verb *atbaʿa* means to cause to follow, to send in pursuit, to make something trail behind someone. The image is of a pursuing shadow.

The commentators note the choice of verb. The curse does not merely fall on Pharaoh's people; it *follows* them. It is a consequence that tracks them through history.

Al-Ṭabarī identifies the curse's content: the successive plagues and humiliations that Egypt suffered during Moses' mission, and then the destruction that ended it. The Qur'an enumerates those afflictions elsewhere:

> *"So We sent upon them the flood, and the locusts, and the lice, and the frogs, and the blood — distinct signs, yet they waxed arrogant and were a guilty people."* (Qur'an 7:133)

And al-Qurṭubī adds the second, lexical sense of *laʿnah*: distance from God and from every good. The root *l-ʿ-n* in Arabic usage carries the sense of being driven away, expelled, cut off from mercy and from the company of the good.

The two senses belong together. A curse in the Qur'anic sense is not a magical imprecation; it is the *condition* of being distanced — from God's aid in this life, from His mercy in the next, and from the good opinion of humanity in between.

**The Historical Curse: A People Remembered for Their Crime**

There is a third sense of the curse that the commentators note, and it is the one most visible to us: the permanence of infamy.

Four thousand years later, Pharaoh is the archetype of the tyrant in three continents' worth of moral vocabulary. The Qur'an itself makes him the type:

> *"And Pharaoh — the one of the stakes — who transgressed in the land and spread corruption therein; so thy Lord poured upon them a scourge of punishment."* (Qur'an 89:10–13)

> *"God sets forth the parable of those who disbelieve: the wife of Noah and the wife of Lot... And the wife of Pharaoh..."* (Qur'an 66:10–11)

The tradition has drawn from this a law of reputation: oppression is remembered, and the memory is part of the punishment. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The worst of people in the sight of God on the Day of Resurrection will be... a man who was given authority and used it to oppress."* (Muslim; the wording varies across collections)

and:

> *"There is no one who is given authority over ten or more and does not govern them with justice but that he will... face God bound [in accountability] on the Day of Resurrection."* (Reported in several collections; the meaning is well-attested)

The commentators note that the Qur'an's own rhetoric participates in this: by naming Pharaoh repeatedly across fourteen centuries of recitation, the Book itself is the vehicle of the curse's continuation. The tyrant's name is recited in every mosque in the world, every day, as a warning.

**"And on the Day of Resurrection They Will Be Among the Repugnant"**

*Wa-yawma al-qiyāmati hum mina al-maqbūḥīn*. The word *maqbūḥ* is from *q-b-ḥ*, meaning ugliness, repulsiveness, loathsomeness.

The commentators — al-Rāzī especially — dwell on this word, because the Qur'an normally describes the state of the condemned in terms of *punishment* rather than *appearance*. Here it describes them as *ugly*.

Al-Rāzī's explanation has become the standard: on the Day of Resurrection, inward ugliness becomes outward. The moral condition that was invisible in this world — a soul deformed by arrogance, cruelty, and refusal — will be manifested physically, because on that Day the reality of each person is no longer concealed by the body's temporary beauty or by social appearance.

This is a striking and consistent theme in the Qur'an's eschatology:

> *"The Day when hearts and eyes will be convulsed."* (Qur'an 24:37)

> *"And you will see the guilty on that Day bound together in fetters, their garments of pitch, and their faces enveloped by the Fire."* (Qur'an 14:49–50)

> *"And whosoever is blind in this world will be blind in the Hereafter, and further astray from the way."* (Qur'an 17:72)

The last verse supplies the principle: the blindness that matters is the blindness of the heart in this life, and its consequence is the blindness of the next.

**The Symmetry: Beauty as the Reward of the Righteous**

The tradition always reads this verse against its counterpart, because the Qur'an does. If the condemned are *maqbūḥīn*, the saved are described in terms of radiance and beauty:

> *"Faces on that Day shall be radiant, gazing upon their Lord."* (Qur'an 75:22–23)

> *"You will recognize in their faces the glow of bliss."* (Qur'an 83:24)

> *"Thou wilt see them... with the mark of prostration on their faces."* (Qur'an 48:29)

And the Prophet ﷺ gave the mechanism in a ḥadīth qudsī and in his own instruction:

> *"Faith is naked, and its garment is God-consciousness, its adornment is modesty, its fruit is knowledge, and its perfection is good deeds."* (Reported in various collections with weak chains; the meaning is sound)

More firmly attested is his statement:

> *"God does not look at your forms or your wealth, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds."* (Muslim 2564)

The commentators reconcile the two by observing that the ḥadīth is about *this* world's valuation — God does not assess a person by his appearance now — while the verse is about the *next* world's manifestation, where the inner reality becomes the outward form.

**The Pastoral Logic of the Verse**

Why does the Qur'an describe the condemned as *repugnant* rather than only as suffering?

The commentators give a reason that is worth pondering, because it addresses a real difficulty. Human beings are naturally moved by suffering and unmoved by abstractions. If the Qur'an said only "they will be punished," a reader might feel pity and identify with them. By describing what they will *be*, the verse establishes that the punishment is not an external penalty imposed on a neutral person but the manifestation of what that person has made of himself.

This is the consistent Qur'anic theology of the Fire. It is not an arbitrary sentence; it is the *consequence taking visible form*. The Qur'an says of the guilty:

> *"Nay, but what they used to do has rusted over their hearts."* (Qur'an 83:14)

and:

> *"And they will say, 'Had we but listened or understood, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.'"* (Qur'an 67:10)

The tradition's formulation, repeated by al-Ghazālī and others, is that the Fire is not something God sends to people from outside; it is what their own choices have brought them to, unveiled.

**The Practical Argument: What Arrogance Does to a Face**

There is an everyday observation embedded in this verse that the spiritual commentators did not miss.

Human beings have all seen it: a face hardened by a lifetime of contempt; eyes that have lost the capacity to be glad for another person; a bearing that cannot stoop. Long before the Resurrection, the soul writes itself on the features. The verse says that on that Day nothing will be hidden — which implies that in this life much *is* hidden, and that the hiding is temporary.

The Prophetic tradition contains a corresponding promise for those who cultivate the opposite:

> *"Whoever humbles himself before God, God will raise him."* (Muslim 2588)

and the hadith of the Prophet ﷺ describing the reward of those who forgive:

> *"Charity does not decrease wealth; God increases His servant in honor when he forgives; and no one humbles himself before God except that God raises him."* (Muslim 2588)

**The Two-Stage Judgment**

Finally, note the verse's structure, because it completes a pattern that runs through the sūrah.

Punishment in *this world* — the curse that follows.
Punishment in the *next* — the repugnant state.

The commentators note that this twofold structure is typical of the Qur'an's treatment of the destroyed peoples, and that it answers a question that troubles many readers: if the Hereafter settles everything, why did Pharaoh suffer in this life at all?

The tradition's answer draws on several principles. The worldly punishment demonstrates that God governs history and not only the end of it — which is the sūrah's own argument from verse 5 onward. It relieves the oppressed within a timeframe they can see, which is a mercy to them. It removes the tyrant's capacity to do further harm. And it serves as a *sign* to later generations, which is precisely why the Qur'an recites it:

> *"And We made them a precedent and an example for later generations."* (Qur'an 43:56)

> *"So We seized him and his hosts and cast them into the sea — and he was blameworthy."* (Qur'an 51:40)

**Application**

For those who hold any kind of authority: the verse states that the consequence of oppression is not only deferred. It attaches in this life — as restlessness, as isolation, as the collapse of trust around a person, as the infamy that outlives the office. The tradition has never taught that tyrants escape in this world; it teaches that their escape is impossible in any world.

For those who are oppressed: the verse is part of a consolation the sūrah builds deliberately. The oppressor's apparent immunity is temporary, and the reckoning has already begun in ways that may not be visible yet.

And for everyone: *maqbūḥ* is the warning about what a person becomes. The choices of a lifetime are not merely *rewarded* or *punished*; they are *formative*. The Qur'an's eschatology is not a courtroom drama imposed on a static self. It is the unveiling of what the self has become, and the making of that self is happening now, in the ordinary decisions of every day.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:43

> **And indeed We gave unto Moses the Book—after We had destroyed the former generations—as a [source of] insight for mankind, and guidance and mercy, that haply they may reflect.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pivot From Story to Argument**

With verse 43, the sūrah turns. The Moses narrative proper — oppression, birth, flight, marriage, call, confrontation, destruction — is complete at verse 42. What follows is the sūrah's *use* of that narrative: the argument that the Prophet Muhammad's knowledge of these events is itself proof of his prophethood.

The commentators mark verse 43 as the hinge, and its content is transitional: it names the Book given to Moses, places it in history, and describes its purpose. All three elements prepare for verses 44–46, where the argument proper begins.

**"We Gave Unto Moses the Book"**

*Wa-laqad ātaynā Mūsā al-kitāba*. The Book is the Torah (*al-Tawrāh*), which the Qur'an names explicitly elsewhere:

> *"Truly We sent down the Torah, wherein is guidance and light; the prophets who submitted judged thereby for those who were Jews..."* (Qur'an 5:44)

> *"And We wrote for him upon the tablets an admonition concerning everything, and a detailing of all things."* (Qur'an 7:145)

The commentators note the Qur'an's consistent doctrine of prior revelation: God sent scriptures before the Qur'an, and belief in them is part of Islamic faith:

> *"Say: 'We believe in God, and in that which has been sent down unto us, and that which was sent down unto Abraham and Ishmael and Isaac and Jacob and the tribes, and that which was given to Moses and Jesus and the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and unto Him we submit.'"* (Qur'an 3:84)

This is one of the six articles of faith as the Prophet ﷺ defined them, in the ḥadīth of Gabriel:

> *"Faith is that you believe in God, and His angels, and His Books, and His messengers, and the Last Day, and in the decree — its good and its evil."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8)

The tradition draws two conclusions that are worth stating explicitly, because they are frequently confused.

*First*, Muslims are required to believe that a Torah was revealed to Moses by God. That obligation is unconditional.

*Second*, the texts currently in the hands of Jews and Christians are, in the Islamic view, scriptures that have been subject to alteration over time. The Qur'an states this in several places:

> *"So woe to those who write the Book with their own hands and then say, 'This is from God,' that they may sell it for a small price."* (Qur'an 2:79)

> *"And among them is a group who distort the Scripture with their tongues, that you may suppose it to be from the Scripture, though it is not from the Scripture."* (Qur'an 3:78)

The classical position, as formulated by Ibn Taymiyyah, Ibn Kathīr, and others, is nuanced rather than sweeping: the corruption (*taḥrīf*) occurred in transmission, interpretation, and in the addition and omission of particular passages, not necessarily in the wholesale replacement of the text. The Jews and Christians of the seventh century possessed a scripture that still contained much truth, which is why the Qur'an could say to them:

> *"O People of the Book, you have no basis until you uphold the Torah and the Gospel and what has been sent down to you from your Lord."* (Qur'an 5:68)

The Qur'an is thus described as *muhaymin* — a guardian and criterion — over what came before it:

> *"And We have sent down unto thee the Book in truth, confirming the scripture that came before it and a guardian over it."* (Qur'an 5:48)

**"After We Had Destroyed the Former Generations"**

*Min baʿdi mā ahlaknā al-qurūna al-ūlā*. The commentators identify the destroyed generations: the peoples of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb — the nations whose stories fill the middle sūrahs of the Qur'an.

Why does the verse mention the timing? The commentators give two related reasons.

*Chronologically*, it situates the Torah in sacred history: it came after the great destructions, to a people being reconstituted after the deliverance from Egypt.

*Theologically and rhetorically*, it sets up an argument. The Qur'an's point is that after God had destroyed those earlier peoples — and destroyed their knowledge with them — He gave a Book that preserved guidance in durable form. The contrast is between civilizations that vanished and left only ruins (v. 58 of this sūrah) and a revelation that endures.

The deeper argument, which the sūrah will make explicit at verses 44–46, is this: there was a very long gap between Moses and Muhammad ﷺ, and during that gap knowledge of the earlier events was lost and distorted among the Arabs. And yet the Qur'an recites them accurately. Where did that knowledge come from?

**"As a Source of Insight" — *Baṣāʾir***

*Baṣāʾir* is the plural of *baṣīrah*, from the root *b-ṣ-r*, sight, perception, insight. The commentators note the richness of the word in Qur'anic usage, and they connect it with the related word *baṣāʾir* in other passages:

> *"There have come to you insights (baṣāʾir) from your Lord."* (Qur'an 6:104)

> *"Say: 'This is my way; I call to God with insight (baṣīrah), I and whosoever follows me.'"* (Qur'an 12:108)

> *"And We gave him [Moses] clear portents (baṣāʾir)."* (Qur'an 17:102)

Al-Qurṭubī observes that *baṣīrah* in Arabic, like "insight" in English, is both a *faculty* — the capacity to see truly — and an *instrument* — that by which one sees truly. The Torah was both: it gave Israel the capacity to discern and it was itself the thing discerned.

The commentators also note what this implies about the nature of scripture. Revelation is not primarily a list of rules; it is an instrument of perception. It changes what a person can *see*. This is why the Qur'an repeatedly describes itself as *nūr*, light:

> *"O mankind, there has come to you a proof from your Lord, and We have sent down to you a clear light."* (Qur'an 4:174)

> *"O you who believe, fear God and believe in His Messenger; He will give you a double portion of His mercy, and will appoint for you a light by which you shall walk."* (Qur'an 57:28)

A person in a dark room with a lamp does not learn facts about the furniture; he sees the furniture. Revelation functions the same way, and the tradition has always used this analogy.

**"And Guidance and Mercy"**

*Wa-hudan wa-raḥmatan*. Two more purposes, and the commentators distinguish them.

*Hudā* — guidance — is the direction: the path, the law, the instruction that tells a person where to go and what to do.

*Raḥmah* — mercy — is the quality of the gift: that God gave it at all, and that its content is suited to human capacity and conducive to human flourishing.

The pairing recurs throughout the Qur'an, and it is one of the standard descriptions of revelation:

> *"And We sent down in the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers."* (Qur'an 17:82)

> *"Say: 'It is, for those who believe, a guidance and a healing.'"* (Qur'an 41:44)

> *"And We have sent thee not, save as a mercy unto the worlds."* (Qur'an 21:107)

The commentators note the implication for Islamic law: the Law is *mercy* before it is burden. The objectives of the Law (*maqāṣid al-sharīʿah*), as the scholars from al-Juwaynī and al-Ghazālī to al-Shāṭibī formulated them, are the preservation of religion, life, intellect, lineage, and property — and every one of these is a mercy.

**"That Haply They May Reflect"**

*Laʿallahum yatadhakkarūn*. The verb is from *dh-k-r*, remembrance — the same root as *dhikr*, the remembrance of God, and as *tadhkirah*, reminder, which is one of the Qur'an's names for itself:

> *"Nay, it is a reminder; so whosoever wills shall remember it."* (Qur'an 74:54–55)

> *"And We have indeed made the Qur'an easy to remember; so is there any who will remember?"* (Qur'an 54:17)

The commentators note the significance of the verb's form. *Yatadhakkarūn* is not "they may learn" but "they may remember" — and the Qur'an's consistent claim is that the truths it presents are not new information but a *recovery* of what the human being already knows at the level of his original constitution:

> *"And when thy Lord took from the Children of Adam, from their loins, their progeny and made them bear witness concerning themselves: 'Am I not your Lord?' They said: 'Yes, we bear witness.'"* (Qur'an 7:172)

The tradition built the doctrine of *fiṭrah* on this and similar texts. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Every child is born upon the natural constitution (fiṭrah); it is his parents who make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian."* (al-Bukhārī 1358; Muslim 2658)

Revelation, on this account, does not implant a foreign idea; it removes the rust from a knowledge already present (cf. 30:30, *the natural constitution of God upon which He has originated mankind*).

**The Particle *Laʿalla* Again**

As at verse 22, the commentators address the particle rendered *haply* — *laʿalla*.

The standard position, repeated by al-Qurṭubī and others, is that *laʿalla* used of God does not indicate uncertainty in Him; it indicates either a real outcome (He gave the Book *so that* they would remember) or a statement framed from the human point of view, since the remembering depends on the recipients' response, which God has willed to leave to their choice.

The nuance matters for theology. If the verse said *so that they will remember*, it would imply that all of Israel was compelled to remember, which the narrative contradicts. By using *laʿalla*, the Qur'an states the *purpose* of the gift while leaving the *response* undetermined — which is exactly the relationship between divine will and human responsibility that the tradition maintains.

**The Argumentative Function of the Verse**

Finally, note how the verse sets up the sūrah's central argument.

It establishes that Moses received a Book — that is, that there was a written revelation in the distant past.

It establishes that the intervening generations were destroyed, with their knowledge lost.

It establishes that the Book was a *baṣāʾir* — an instrument of insight — given to humanity at large and not merely to Israel.

And it establishes that people are expected to *reflect*.

All four of these converge in verses 44–46, where the Qur'an turns to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and asks the Quraysh how an unlettered man in Makkah came to know any of this. The argument runs, in outline: you have no scripture, you have no knowledge of these events, and your ancestors were destroyed with theirs — and yet this man recites the account exactly. Either he was there (he was not), or he learned it from a book (he could not read), or God told him.

The commentators from al-Zamakhsharī to al-Rāzī identify this as the *iʿjāz* argument from unseen knowledge (*al-akhbār al-ghaybiyyah*) — one of the Qur'an's principal claims to divine origin, and the one this sūrah develops most fully.

**Application**

Three practical conclusions follow.

*Scripture is a lens, not only a law book.* Its first effect is on perception. The person reading the Qur'an should expect to see the world differently — not merely to accumulate rulings.

*Knowledge can be lost.* The destroyed generations had knowledge of God and lost it. The tradition's constant warning is that knowledge is preserved by transmission, practice, and teaching, and lost by neglect:

> *"God does not take away knowledge by snatching it from the servants, but He takes it away by the death of the scholars, until, when no scholar remains, people take ignorant leaders who are asked and give rulings without knowledge, thus going astray and leading others astray."* (al-Bukhārī 100; Muslim 2673)

*And revelation arrives after the loss.* God did not leave humanity without a Book. The verse names the pattern — destruction, then scripture — and the tradition reads it as a standing promise: when knowledge is lost, God sends it again.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:44

> **And thou wast not on the western side when We decreed unto Moses the Commandment, and thou wast not among the witnesses.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument Begins: You Were Not There**

*Wa-mā kunta bi-jānibi al-gharbiyyi idh qaḍaynā ilā Mūsā al-amra wa-mā kunta mina al-shāhidīn*.

With this verse the sūrah opens its central proof, and the commentators have always treated verses 44–46 as one of the Qur'an's most carefully constructed arguments for the truth of Muhammad's prophethood.

The form is a *negative proof*, and its elegance is worth noting. The Qur'an does not argue "Muhammad is a prophet, therefore believe what he says." It argues: here are facts about the distant past that you, his contemporaries, accept; he could not have acquired them by any natural means available to a man in his situation; therefore his knowledge is from the One who was there.

**"Thou Wast Not on the Western Side"**

*Bi-jānibi al-gharbiyyi* — *on the western side*. The commentators explain the reference from the Qur'an's own parallel accounts:

> *"And thou wast not on the side of the Mount when We called out."* (Qur'an 28:46)

> *"And We called unto him from the right side of the Mount, and We brought him near in communion."* (Qur'an 19:52)

> *"Verily I am thy Lord; so take off thy shoes. Truly thou art in the sacred valley of Ṭuwā."* (Qur'an 20:12)

The western side is the side of the mountain at which Moses stood — the direction from which the call came. Al-Ṭabarī identifies it as the western side of the Mount relative to Moses' approach.

The point of naming the location is precision. The Qur'an is not saying "you were not in the region"; it is saying *you were not standing at the exact spot where the commandment was given*. The specificity is part of the argument's force: this is not vague legend, and it is being narrated with a topographical exactness that the Arabs of Makkah could not check and that Muhammad ﷺ had no source for.

**"When We Decreed Unto Moses the Commandment"**

*Idh qaḍaynā ilā Mūsā al-amra*. The verb *qaḍā* means to decree, to decide, to settle a matter definitively. The *Commandment* — *al-amr* — is a comprehensive word meaning the affair, the commission, the matter at hand.

Al-Ṭabarī reads it as the giving of the mission: the moment at which God commanded Moses to go to Pharaoh.

Al-Qurṭubī and others, following a report from Ibn ʿAbbās, extend it to the giving of the Torah and the laws — the whole commission, including the tablets.

The commentators note that *al-amr* is deliberately comprehensive, because the argument does not depend on which moment is meant. Whatever was decreed to Moses at that place, Muhammad ﷺ was not there, and yet he knows it.

**"And Thou Wast Not Among the Witnesses"**

*Wa-mā kunta mina al-shāhidīn*. The word *shāhid* means a witness, one who is present and observes. The negation is total: you were not among those present at all — not at the event, not in the generation, not in the chain of transmission.

This is the crux. The Qur'an's argument is not merely that Muhammad ﷺ could not have been at Sinai. It is that he had no access to *anyone* who was. He was born in Makkah some two thousand years after Moses, in a region without Jewish settlement and without a scriptural tradition of that event's details.

**The Historical Setting: Makkah and the Absence of Scriptural Knowledge**

The commentators supply the context that the argument assumes, and it is important for understanding the verse's force in its original setting.

Makkah in the early seventh century was a trading city with no Jewish or Christian population of scholarly standing, no synagogue, no scriptural school, and no institutional knowledge of the Israelite prophets. The Quraysh followed the religion of their forefathers — idolatry centered on the Kaʿbah — with a thin monotheistic residue inherited from Abraham. There was no body of Arabic literature containing the Moses narrative.

The Qur'an states this explicitly, in the terms the tradition calls *al-ummiyyah* — the unlettered state of the Prophet ﷺ and of his people:

> *"And thou didst not recite before it any book, nor didst thou write it with thy right hand; for then the falsifiers would have doubted."* (Qur'an 29:48)

> *"He it is Who sent among the unlettered (al-ummiyyīn) a messenger from among themselves, reciting to them His signs and purifying them and teaching them the Book and the wisdom, though before that they were in manifest error."* (Qur'an 62:2)

The Prophet ﷺ's illiteracy is a matter of consensus in the tradition and is reported in his own words:

> *"We are an unlettered nation (ummatun ummiyyah): we neither write nor calculate."* (al-Bukhārī 1913; Muslim 1080)

The argument of this verse therefore runs: an illiterate man from an unscriptured city, with no contact with the People of the Book and no access to their texts, narrates an event two millennia old with topographical precision. Explain that.

**The Three Explanations and Their Refutation**

The commentators enumerate the possible explanations and show that the Qur'an's argument eliminates each.

*He was there.* Refuted by this verse: *thou wast not on the western side*.

*He learned it from people.* Refuted by verse 45: *thou dwelt not among the people of Midian* and by the historical fact of his isolation. The Qur'an records that his opponents themselves could not sustain this charge:

> *"And indeed We know that they say, 'It is only a human being who teaches him.' The tongue of him they refer to is foreign, while this is a clear Arabic tongue."* (Qur'an 16:103)

— a refutation that proceeds by pointing out that the foreigner they accused was a non-Arab who could not have produced Arabic eloquence of this order.

*He read it in a book.* Refuted by 29:48, quoted above, and by the consensus that he never learned to read or write.

What remains is revelation. That is the structure the commentators call *ilhām* no — *iʿjāz bi-l-ghayb*, the inimitability established through knowledge of the unseen.

**The Doctrine of *Waḥy* Established by This Verse**

Theologically, the verse establishes the Islamic doctrine of revelation with unusual clarity, and the commentators spell out its components.

*The source is God.* The knowledge came from the One who decreed the Commandment to Moses, since no one else was present.

*The recipient is a human being.* The verse addresses *thou* — a man in Makkah.

*The mode is revelation.* God taught him what he could not have learned:

> *"The Most Merciful has taught the Qur'an; He created man and taught him clear expression."* (Qur'an 55:1–4)

> *"We shall make thee recite, and thou shalt not forget, save what God wills."* (Qur'an 87:6–7)

> *"Move not thy tongue therewith to hasten it. Truly upon Us is its collection and its recitation. So when We recite it, follow thou its recitation. Then truly upon Us is its exposition."* (Qur'an 75:16–19)

That last passage is especially relevant, because it establishes that both the *wording* and the *meaning* were given by God — a point the tradition insisted upon against those who held that the Prophet ﷺ supplied the meaning himself.

**The Pastoral Purpose: Consolation as Well as Proof**

The commentators note that this verse and the two that follow are addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in the second person, and that their function is not only to convince the Quraysh but to strengthen him.

The structure *thou wast not* — repeated three times in verses 44, 45, and 46 — is a reassurance. God is saying: you have no natural relationship to this knowledge, and that is precisely the proof that I am the One who gave it to you. Your lack of access is not a deficiency in your credentials; it *is* your credential.

The tradition has drawn a general principle from this, and it is one of the most consoling in the religion: what God asks of a person is never dependent on what he happens to possess. The orphan, the unlettered, the person without lineage, education, wealth, or connections — these are not disqualifications in the divine economy, because the source of the work is God and not the servant's equipment.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Truly God does not look at your forms and your wealth, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds."* (Muslim 2564)

and the Qur'an states the principle generally:

> *"And it may be that you dislike a thing while it is good for you, and it may be that you like a thing while it is bad for you. And God knows, and you know not."* (Qur'an 2:216)

**Application: The Argument From Unlikely Knowledge**

The sūrah's argument is unusual among proofs of prophethood in that it is *checkable in principle* and does not depend on accepting a prior premise. Any observer can verify: this man could not have known this.

The tradition has generalized the form. Whenever someone produces knowledge, insight, or capacity that his circumstances cannot account for, the Qur'anic question is where it came from — and the believer's answer is that all capacity is a gift, whether it arrives by ordinary learning or by direct divine teaching.

This produces a specific spiritual disposition: *the aware dependence*. A person who recognizes that his knowledge, his skills, his opportunities, and even his understanding of religion are given rather than owned is protected from the arrogance that destroyed Pharaoh. The tradition's formulation is the Prophet's supplication:

> *"O God, benefit me by what You have taught me, and teach me what will benefit me, and increase me in knowledge."* (al-Tirmidhī 3599; Ibn Mājah 3833)

And there is a final application for the modern reader, who is often told that the Qur'an's narratives are borrowed from Jewish and Christian sources. The verse's own answer is that borrowing requires access, and the argument is about access. The burden of explanation lies with anyone who claims that an illiterate merchant in an unscriptured city acquired this knowledge from a source no one has ever identified.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:45

> **But it is We Who brought into being the generations, and lives grew long for them. And thou dwelt not among the people of Midian, reciting unto them Our signs. But it is We Who sent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Negative: You Did Not Live Among Them**

*Wa-lākinnā anshaʾnā qurūnan fa-taṭāwala ʿalayhimu al-ʿumuru wa-mā kunta thāwiyan fī ahli Madyana tatlū ʿalayhim āyātinā wa-lākinnā kunnā mursilīn*.

The verse continues the argument begun in verse 44, and it adds a crucial element: an explanation of *why* the knowledge was lost to the Arabs in the first place, and a second negative proof concerning Moses' years in Midian.

**"We Brought Into Being the Generations, and Lives Grew Long for Them"**

*Anshaʾnā qurūnan* — *We brought into being generations*. The verb *anshaʾa* means to originate, to raise up, to produce. *Qurūn* is the plural of *qarn*, a generation, a people of a single era.

*Fa-taṭāwala ʿalayhimu al-ʿumuru* — *and the ages grew long for them*, or *lives became lengthy upon them*. The verb *taṭāwala* suggests stretching out, extending over a long span.

The commentators offer two readings of this clause, and both are needed.

*The historical reading.* Between Moses and Muhammad ﷺ there stretched many centuries and countless generations. The long passage of time is precisely what makes the transmission of accurate knowledge so unlikely — and therefore what makes the Qur'an's accurate knowledge so significant. Al-Rāzī takes the clause in this sense: the stretch of time between the two prophets explains the loss and distortion of the earlier revelation.

*The moral reading.* Al-Qurṭubī notes the Qur'an's consistent association of long life with heedlessness:

> *"Has not the time come for those who believe that their hearts should be humbled to the remembrance of God and to the truth that has come down, and that they should not be like those who were given the Book before, whose term was prolonged so that their hearts hardened?"* (Qur'an 57:16)

The mechanism is described twice in this verse's own sūrah-adjacent literature. Long stretches of ease and distance from the original event produce three effects in sequence: forgetfulness, then the substitution of inherited custom for revelation, then the hardening that makes correction unwelcome.

The tradition has elaborated this into one of its most sober observations about religious communities. Knowledge decays through time unless it is actively transmitted, and the default trajectory of a community left to itself is loss, not preservation. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God does not take away knowledge by snatching it from the servants, but He takes it away by the death of the scholars, until, when no scholar remains, people take ignorant leaders who are asked and give rulings without knowledge, thus going astray and leading others astray."* (al-Bukhārī 100; Muslim 2673)

And he described the specific mechanism of distortion in a famous warning:

> *"You will follow the ways of those who came before you, hand-span by hand-span and cubit by cubit, until if they entered a lizard's hole you would enter it too."* They said, "O Messenger of God, [do you mean] the Jews and the Christians?" He said, *"Who else?"* (al-Bukhārī 3456; Muslim 2669)

**"And Thou Dwelt Not Among the People of Midian"**

*Wa-mā kunta thāwiyan fī ahli Madyana*. The verb *thawā* means to stay, to reside, to lodge — with a connotation of settled residence rather than a visit.

This is the second negative proof, and it targets a different chapter of Moses' life: the decade or more he spent in Midian, among a people, married into a family, herding flocks.

The argument's force depends on the sūrah having just narrated that episode in detail — verses 22 through 28. The Qur'an has told the Quraysh about the wells, the two women, the father's offer, the eight or ten years, and the departure. Now it says: you did not live there either.

The commentators note the completeness of the elimination. Verse 44 ruled out presence at Sinai. This verse rules out residence in Midian. Verse 46 will rule out presence at the Mount. Between the three, every location where the narrative's knowledge could have been acquired is excluded.

**"Reciting Unto Them Our Signs"**

*Tatlū ʿalayhim āyātinā* — *reciting to them Our signs*. The commentators note what this clause establishes about the *content*.

It is not merely that Muhammad ﷺ did not live in Midian. It is that he did not *receive* anything there — he neither taught them nor learned from them. The verb *talā* (recite) is the same verb used for Qur'anic recitation throughout, and its use here is deliberate: the only revelation Muhammad ﷺ ever recited was the one sent down to him, and it was not given in Midian.

Al-Rāzī observes the subtle economy of this: to have *learned* the Moses story from the people of Midian, a person would have had to reside among them for years; and if Muhammad ﷺ had done that, the Quraysh would have known it, because his life before his call was public, documented, and unremarkable. He had traveled to Syria twice as a merchant; he had never gone to Midian; and his entire biography was known to his people, who called him *al-Amīn*, the trustworthy, before they called him anything else.

**"But It Is We Who Sent"**

*Wa-lākinnā kunnā mursilīn*. The verse ends where the argument must end: not with a denial but with an affirmation.

*Kunnā mursilīn* — *We have been sending* — is a present-progressive construction in the Arabic idiom, indicating God's continuous practice of sending messengers. The commentators note the plural of majesty and the continuous form: the sending is God's established way.

The structure of the whole passage is worth noting, because it is the structure of the Qur'an's proofs generally:

Negative: you were not there.
Negative: you did not live among them.
Positive: **but We sent**.

The tradition has always insisted that the Qur'an's arguments are never purely negative. Dismantling a false explanation is a preparation for the true one, not a substitute for it. The verse does not say "therefore the story is mysterious"; it says *We sent* — God has been sending messengers, and He sent this one.

**The Doctrine of the Continuity of Prophethood**

The clause *We have been sending* establishes one of the foundations of Islamic belief: that God has never left humanity without guidance.

> *"And there is no community but that a warner has passed among it."* (Qur'an 35:24)

> *"Then We sent Our messengers in succession; every time there came to a community its messenger, they denied him."* (Qur'an 23:44)

> *"And We sent messengers before thee, of some of whom We have told thee, and of some of whom We have not told thee."* (Qur'an 40:78)

That last verse is significant for the creed, because it establishes the proper attitude toward messengers not named in the Qur'an: believe in those named specifically, and believe in principle in the others without asserting their identities.

The tradition's position, as the scholars formulated it, is that the number of prophets is known only to God. A well-known ḥadīth reports the figure:

> *Abū Dharr asked, "O Messenger of God, how many prophets were there?" He said, "One hundred and twenty-four thousand." He asked, "How many messengers among them?" He said, "Three hundred and thirteen, a goodly number."* (Aḥmad; Ibn Ḥibbān — graded ḥasan by some scholars, and the number is widely cited in the tradition)

Whether or not one accepts that specific narration's chain, the principle it expresses is Qur'anic: God has sent many messengers, to many peoples, in many eras.

**The Theological Point About Time**

There is a subtler argument in this verse that the commentators draw out, and it concerns the nature of historical knowledge.

The verse says: generations arose, lives grew long — and the implication is that over that span, knowledge was lost. This is a claim about the *fragility of human transmission*, and the Qur'an makes it repeatedly. The destroyed peoples' knowledge went with them. The Torah was distorted. The Gospels were altered. The Arabs inherited almost nothing.

Against that fragility, the Qur'an sets the permanence of its own transmission, and the contrast is one of its standing arguments:

> *"Truly We sent down the Remembrance, and truly We shall preserve it."* (Qur'an 15:9)

The commentators note that the promise of preservation is unique to the Qur'an among the scriptures named in it, and they have pointed to the historical fact of the Qur'an's transmission — memorized in full by millions, in every generation, with chains of recitation going back to the Prophet ﷺ — as the fulfillment.
