This is the background against which the verse's argument has its full force. If knowledge decays by default, then accurate knowledge of ancient events in an unscriptured society is either a miracle or an anomaly. The Qur'an says it is revelation.

**Application: Why the Argument Matters for the Modern Reader**

The modern reader approaches this verse with a different set of assumptions than the Quraysh had, and the argument needs restating in those terms.

The modern instinct is to assume that any ancient text is the product of its environment — that the Qur'an's narratives came from the Jewish and Christian material "in the air" of late antique Arabia. The verse's answer is that this is an empirical claim about transmission, and that transmission requires contact.

The scholarly questions the verse raises are therefore historical and specific: where, exactly, was this material available? In what language? Through whom? And by what route did it reach an unlettered man in Makkah who never studied with anyone, whose every journey is recorded, and whose opponents — who were actively searching for an explanation — could not produce one?

The tradition's answer is that no such route has ever been identified, and that the burden of proof lies with those who assert one.

For the believer, the argument's value is not primarily polemical. It establishes a way of reading the Qur'an: as speech that originates outside the human environment in which it appeared. That is what makes it *guidance* in the full sense — not the wisdom of seventh-century Arabia, but the address of the Lord of the worlds to everyone who hears it.

And the verse's final clause supplies the disposition in which it should be received: *We have been sending*. The God who sent to Moses at the western side is the God who sent to Muhammad ﷺ in Makkah, and the God who sends now is the One whose Book is in your hands.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:46

> **And thou wast not on the side of the Mount when We called out. But [thou art sent as] a mercy from thy Lord that thou mayest warn a people unto whom no warner has come before thee, that haply they may remember.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Negative: The Mountain Itself**

*Wa-mā kunta bi-jānibi al-Ṭūri idh nādaynā wa-lākin raḥmatan min rabbika li-tundhira qawman mā atāhum min nadhīrin min qablika laʿallahum yatadhakkarūn*.

The third and final location is excluded. Verse 44 excluded the western side where the Commandment was decreed. Verse 45 excluded Midian. This verse excludes *jānib al-Ṭūr* — the side of the Mount — where the call itself was made.

The commentators note the ascending structure. The three verses move outward from the specific spot of the commission (44), to the years of preparation (45), to the moment of the call (46). Every stage of Moses' formation that the sūrah has narrated is covered, and at every stage the Prophet ﷺ is declared absent.

**"On the Side of the Mount" — *Al-Ṭūr***

*Al-Ṭūr* is the Mount — Sinai, the mountain of the divine encounter, which the Qur'an honors by oath:

> *"By the Mount, and by a Book inscribed on parchment unrolled."* (Qur'an 52:1–3)

> *"And by the Mount of Sinai, and by this secure land."* (Qur'an 95:2–3)

The word is used in the Qur'an both as a common noun for a mountain and as the proper name of that mountain, and the commentators note that its appearance here ties this verse to the whole complex of Sinai passages:

> *"And We raised above them the Mount, in acceptance of their covenant."* (Qur'an 4:154; 2:63; 7:171)

The specificity matters for the argument in the same way it did at verse 44: this is not "you were not in the desert." It is *you were not at the side of the Mount when We called*.

**"When We Called Out" — Which Call?**

*Idh nādaynā* — *when We called*. The commentators discuss which call is meant, and the discussion is substantive.

*The majority view*, transmitted by al-Ṭabarī, is that this refers to Moses' initial investiture — the call from the tree described in verses 30–35 of this sūrah, where God said *O Moses, truly I am God, Lord of the worlds*.

*A second view*, recorded by al-Qurṭubī, is that it refers to the giving of the Law after the destruction of the calf, described at 7:155 and 2:55, when Moses was summoned to the Mount for forty nights.

*A third view*, transmitted by al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī from some of the early scholars, is that the call was addressed not to Moses but to the community of Muhammad ﷺ — that God called out to them while they were still in the loins of their forefathers, saying words to the effect of: *O community of Muhammad, I gave to you before you asked of Me, and I answered you before you called upon Me.*

This third reading is remarkable, and the commentators treat it with care. Its basis is the covenant verse:

> *"And when thy Lord took from the Children of Adam, from their loins, their progeny and made them bear witness concerning themselves: 'Am I not your Lord?' They said: 'Yes, we bear witness.'"* (Qur'an 7:172)

On this reading, the community of Muhammad ﷺ was addressed at Sinai in their unborn state, and the mercy of sending Muhammad ﷺ is the historical unfolding of an address made at the mountain. Al-Rāzī records the view without endorsing it as the primary sense, and the tradition has generally treated it as a spiritual interpretation (*ishārī*) rather than the plain meaning.

The plain meaning remains: God called to Moses at the Mount, and Muhammad ﷺ was not there.

**"But [Thou Art Sent As] a Mercy From Thy Lord"**

*Wa-lākin raḥmatan min rabbika*. The commentators note the grammatical construction carefully: the word *raḥmatan* is in the accusative, and the sentence has an elision. The sense is: *but [We sent thee] as a mercy from thy Lord* — or *[We did that] as a mercy*.

The word *raḥmah* is one of the most theologically loaded in the Qur'an. Its root *r-ḥ-m* gives *al-Raḥmān* and *al-Raḥīm* — the two names that open every sūrah but one — and the womb (*raḥim*), the organ of mercy in the Arabic imagination.

The identification of the Prophet ﷺ himself as mercy is made explicitly elsewhere and is one of the foundations of Islamic theology:

> *"And We have not sent thee, save as a mercy unto the worlds."* (Qur'an 21:107)

The commentators note that the Qur'an applies the word to the Prophet ﷺ, to the Qur'an, and to God Himself — and that the three are not separate. The Prophet is mercy because he brought the Qur'an, and the Qur'an is mercy because it is from the Merciful.

The tradition has drawn out what kind of mercy this is. It is not merely compassion; it is the gift that meets the actual need. For a people without scripture and without a warner, the mercy *is* the warning:

> *"And We sent down in the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers; but it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss."* (Qur'an 17:82)

**"That Thou Mayest Warn a People Unto Whom No Warner Has Come Before Thee"**

*Li-tundhira qawman mā atāhum min nadhīrin min qablika*. The verb *andhara* — to warn — is the technical term for the prophet's function in its negative aspect (*nadhīr*, warner, paired always with *bashīr*, bearer of glad tidings).

The claim is historically specific and theologically significant: the Arabs had received no warner before Muhammad ﷺ.

The commentators discuss this carefully, because the Qur'an elsewhere indicates that Abraham and Ishmael were in Makkah and that the Arabs inherited some residue of their religion:

> *"And [mention] when Abraham raised the foundations of the House, with Ishmael: 'Our Lord, accept it from us...'"* (Qur'an 2:127)

The reconciliation the tradition adopted is that no *messenger* had been sent to the Arabs as a people since Ishmael — that the interval between Ishmael and Muhammad ﷺ was an interval of *fatrah*, a gap in prophecy, during which the Arabs retained fragments of Abrahamic monotheism but had received no scripture and no prophet of their own.

The Prophet ﷺ described exactly this gap:

> *"I am the closest of people to Jesus son of Mary in this world and the next."* They asked, "How so?" He said: *"The prophets are paternal brothers — their mothers are different but their religion is one. And there was no prophet between me and him."* (al-Bukhārī 3443; Muslim 2365)

The *fatrah* is a recognized category in Islamic theology, and the scholars discussed the status of those who lived in such an interval — concluding that they are not punished until a message reaches them, on the basis of this sūrah's own verse 59 and the general principle:

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

**The Warning That Is Itself the Mercy**

The commentators note the apparent paradox and resolve it: God sent Muhammad ﷺ as a *mercy* in order to *warn*.

The logic is the physician's. A doctor who knows of a disease and does not tell the patient is not kind. The warning *is* the mercy, because the warning creates the possibility of escape. The alternative — leaving a people without a warner — is what the Qur'an describes as abandonment.

The Qur'an makes the point unforgettably:

> *"O you who believe, save yourselves and your families from a Fire whose fuel is people and stones."* (Qur'an 66:6)

And the Prophet ﷺ described his own function in a similitude that the commentators have always cited:

> *"The similitude of me and what God has sent me with is that of a man who came to a people and said, 'O my people, I have seen an army with my own eyes, and I am the plain warner to you; so save yourselves!' A group of them obeyed him and departed at night, proceeding slowly, and they were saved; and a group of them denied him and stayed in their places, and the army came upon them in the morning and destroyed them utterly."* (al-Bukhārī 7283; Muslim 2283)

The parable is the whole theology of warning in miniature, and its emotional register is exactly this verse's: the warner comes because the army is coming, and the people did not know.

**"That Haply They May Remember"**

*Laʿallahum yatadhakkarūn*. As at verse 43, the closing purpose is *remembrance*.

The commentators note the significance of the verb's form and its recurrence. The Qur'an's aim is not persuasion in the sense of producing new information; it is the awakening of what is already known. The message addresses a *forgetfulness*, not an ignorance — a condition the Qur'an names repeatedly as the root of the human problem:

> *"And be not like those who forgot God, so He made them forget their own souls."* (Qur'an 59:19)

> *"And they forgot God, so He forgot them."* (Qur'an 9:67)

The tradition's corresponding practice is *dhikr* — remembrance — which the Qur'an commands more often than almost anything else:

> *"O you who believe, remember God with much remembrance, and glorify Him morning and evening."* (Qur'an 33:41–42)

> *"Truly in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* (Qur'an 13:28)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The similitude of the one who remembers his Lord and the one who does not is that of the living and the dead."* (al-Bukhārī 6407)

**The Practical Structure of the Argument**

Verses 44–46 together form one of the Qur'an's most elegant proofs, and it is worth setting out the skeleton because it is eminently repeatable in other contexts.

*Step one*: identify a fact that everyone accepts (Muhammad ﷺ was not at Sinai, did not live in Midian, and was unlettered).
*Step two*: identify a knowledge that cannot be explained by that fact (he narrates these events precisely).
*Step three*: eliminate every natural explanation (presence, residence, reading, instruction from a human teacher).
*Step four*: state the remaining explanation (God sent him).
*Step five*: name the purpose of the sending (warning, as mercy).

The tradition has always presented this as a *rational* proof rather than a demand for blind acceptance — which is why the Qur'an's challenges to produce a sūrah like it (2:23, 10:38, 11:13, 17:88) are addressed to people who rejected it. The argument assumes the opponent's rationality.

**Application: The Mercy of Being Warned**

For the modern reader, the most transferable content of this verse is the identification of warning with mercy.

Every culture has mechanisms for avoiding bad news: euphemism, distraction, the refusal to open the letter, the unread test result. The Qur'an's position is that the person who brings the bad news is doing the kindest thing available, and that a community without such people is in the gravest danger.

This has direct implications for how believers speak to one another. The tradition's rule, derived from this and similar verses, is that *naṣīḥah* — sincere counsel — includes the unwelcome kind, and that silence in the face of a known harm is a form of betrayal. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The religion is sincere good counsel."* (Muslim 55)

And the sūrah's own logic supplies the limit: the warner warns, and the response is the hearer's. *Laʿallahum yatadhakkarūn* — perhaps they will remember. The warner is not responsible for the outcome, only for the delivery. That is the consolation of everyone who has ever spoken an unwelcome truth at cost to himself.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:47

> **And [We did so] lest there be an affliction that would befall them because of that which their hands have sent forth at which they should say, "Our Lord! If only Thou hadst sent a messenger unto us, we would have followed Thy signs and been among the believers!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Removes the Last Excuse**

*Wa-lawlā an tuṣībahum muṣībatun bi-mā qaddamat aydīhim fa-yaqūlū rabbanā lawlā arsalta ilaynā rasūlan fa-nattabiʿa āyātika wa-nakūna mina al-muʾminīn*.

The syntax is famously compressed, and the commentators supply the elision. The sense is: *Were it not that they would, when afflicted, say "why did You not send us a messenger?" — [We would have punished them immediately, or We would not have sent you].*

The verse therefore states the *purpose* of the sending in negative form: God sent Muhammad ﷺ so that no one would have this excuse left.

**The Grammar of the Elided Clause**

The commentators, including al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ṭabarī, discuss what is being suppressed, and their discussion is substantive.

*The first reconstruction*: "Were it not that they would have this excuse, We would have destroyed them for their sins without sending a messenger." On this reading, the verse teaches that the sending of the Prophet ﷺ *averted* an immediate punishment that would otherwise have fallen — a reading consistent with the sūrah's own next verse, which shows the Quraysh rejecting him anyway, and with the Qur'anic principle that punishment follows the arrival and rejection of a messenger.

*The second reconstruction*: "We sent you so that they should have no argument." This is the reading that connects directly to:

> *"Messengers bearing glad tidings and warning, lest mankind have any argument against God after the messengers. And God is Mighty, Wise."* (Qur'an 4:165)

Both readings are held by the tradition, and they are complementary. The sending removes the excuse, and the removal of the excuse is what makes subsequent judgment just.

**The Islamic Doctrine: No Punishment Before a Messenger**

This verse articulates one of the most important and most consoling doctrines in Islamic theology: God does not punish a people to whom no messenger has come.

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

> *"And thy Lord never destroys towns until He sends a messenger to their mother city, reciting unto them Our signs. And We never destroy towns, save when their people are wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 28:59 — the sūrah states it again twenty verses later)

> *"And We sent not a messenger save in the tongue of his people, that he might make [the message] clear to them."* (Qur'an 14:4)

The scholars developed from these verses the doctrine of *ahl al-fatrah* — the people of the interval between messengers — and the related question of those who have never heard the message of Islam at all.

The positions of the schools, summarized:

*The Ashʿarī position* (and the one attributed to the majority of Sunni scholars): those who never received the message are not punished for failing to believe it, because the obligation requires a messenger. Some Ashʿarīs held that the obligation to know God's existence arises from reason alone, while the obligation to follow specific revelation requires a messenger.

*The Māturīdī position*: reason can know God, and those who never received revelation are accountable for that much and not for the specifics.

*Ibn Taymiyyah's position*, developed in his discussion of *ahl al-fatrah* and in his treatise on the fate of the children of the polytheists: those who never received the message in this life will be tested on the Day of Resurrection, and whoever obeys in that test will enter the Garden, while whoever disobeys will enter the Fire. He based this on narrations to that effect and on the justice of God.

Ibn al-Qayyim summarized the positions and the relevant ḥadīth at length, and the consensus that emerged is this: God does not punish anyone who had no access to the message, and the details of how He deals with such people belong to Him. The believer's duty is to affirm the justice and to refrain from speculating about individuals.

The commentators note that this is one of the places where Islamic theology is most explicitly *merciful in its restraints*. The religion does not permit anyone to say "such-and-such a person is in the Fire" without revelation, and it positively forbids it for those who never received a message.

**"An Affliction... Because of That Which Their Hands Have Sent Forth"**

*Muṣībatun bi-mā qaddamat aydīhim*. The word *muṣībah* means an affliction, a calamity — from a root meaning to strike, to hit the mark. The Qur'an regularly attributes affliction to human action:

> *"Whatever affliction befalls you, it is because of what your hands have earned; and He pardons much."* (Qur'an 42:30)

> *"Corruption has appeared on land and sea because of what the hands of men have earned, that He may let them taste something of what they have done, that haply they may return."* (Qur'an 30:41)

The phrase *what their hands have sent forth* is one of the most frequent in the Qur'an, and the commentators note what it is teaching: deeds are not merely events; they are *dispatches*. What a person does in this life goes ahead of him and awaits him.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the saying its practical weight in a ḥadīth that the tradition has always quoted beside such verses:

> *"Truly, when the believer dies, he is shown his place in the Fire that he would have occupied had he disobeyed, and it is said to him: 'This was your place, had you disobeyed, but God has given you this [in the Garden] instead.' And when the disbeliever dies, he is shown his place in the Garden that he would have occupied had he believed, and it is said to him: 'This was your place, had you believed, but God has given you this [in the Fire] instead.'"* (Reported in the collections; the meaning is well-attested)

**The Excuse Itself: A Record of Human Self-Deception**

Now consider the excuse the verse quotes, because the Qur'an quotes it more than once and its repetition is the point.

*"Our Lord! If only Thou hadst sent a messenger unto us, we would have followed Thy signs and been among the believers!"*

The Qur'an records this identical plea in several places, always from the mouths of the condemned:

> *"And they will say, 'Had we but listened or understood, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.' So they will confess their sin..."* (Qur'an 67:10–11)

> *"And they will cry out therein, 'Our Lord, bring us out; we will work righteousness, other than what we used to do!' 'Did We not give you lives long enough that whoever would remember might remember therein? And there came to you the warner. So taste [the punishment], for the wrongdoers have no helper.'"* (Qur'an 35:37)

> *"And were you not to be blamed, We would have sent a messenger... but you denied."*

The commentary tradition's observation is devastating: these are the same people who, when the messenger *did* come, said *this is fabricated sorcery* (v. 36) and *two sorceries supporting one another* (v. 48).

The verse therefore exposes the structure of excuses. The plea *if only a messenger had come* is not a statement about evidence; it is a statement about desire. The speaker does not want a messenger; he wants an escape. The Qur'an exposes exactly this in the confrontation at the Fire:

> *"And if you could but see when they are made to stand before the Fire and say, 'Would that we were sent back! Then we would not deny the signs of our Lord, and we would be among the believers.' Nay, but that which they used to conceal before has become manifest to them. And if they were sent back, they would return to that which they were forbidden; and truly they are liars."* (Qur'an 6:27–28)

The final clause of that verse is the key: *and truly they are liars*. God states that the promised repentance is false, and that if returned they would revert. The excuse was never about the missing messenger.

**The Historical Setting: The Quraysh and the Longed-For Prophet**

The commentators note a specific historical detail that sharpens the verse: the Arabs of Makkah had in fact been *asking* for a messenger.

The Qur'an records their claim:

> *"And they say, 'Why is this Qur'an not sent down to a great man from one of the two cities?'"* (Qur'an 43:31)

> *"And [remember] when they said, 'O God, if this is indeed the truth from Thee, then rain down upon us stones from heaven, or bring us a painful punishment.'"* (Qur'an 8:32)

More tellingly, the Quraysh used to say, in the period before the call, that the People of the Book had been given scriptures and prophets while they had not, and that if a prophet were sent to them they would be more guided than anyone. The Qur'an cites this against them:

> *"And they swore by God their most solemn oaths that if a warner came to them, they would be more guided than any of the communities; but when a warner came to them, it increased them in naught but aversion."* (Qur'an 35:42)

That verse is the exact fulfillment of the scenario this verse describes, and the commentators cite it as the commentary on it. The excuse was pre-empted by their own oath — and when the warner came, they broke it.

**Theological Note: Justice as the Removal of Excuses**

The verse establishes what the tradition calls the *qāṭiʿ al-ʿudhr* principle — the cutting off of excuses.

God's justice, in the Qur'an's presentation, is not merely the giving of what is deserved. It is the *demonstration* that what is deserved was deserved. The entire apparatus of revelation — the messengers, the scriptures, the signs, the warnings — exists partly to make the eventual judgment undeniable to the one being judged.

This is why the Day of Resurrection is described in the Qur'an as a day of *argument and counter-argument*, in which the condemned are given the floor and are silenced by their own words:

> *"And they will say to their skins, 'Why did you bear witness against us?' They will say, 'God has made us speak, as He makes all things speak.'"* (Qur'an 41:21)

> *"This Day We seal their mouths, and their hands speak to Us, and their feet bear witness to what they used to earn."* (Qur'an 36:65)

The tradition draws a sober conclusion: the purpose of the message is not only to guide those who will be guided, but to leave the rest without a word to say. And every person who hears the message in this life has had that door closed behind him.

**Application: The Excuses We Manufacture**

The verse's most searching use is as a mirror.

The human talent for conditional piety is one of the most consistent features of the moral life: *if only I had been born in a better family; if only I had been taught properly; if only I had more time; if only the people around me were serious; if only I had been given clearer signs.*

The Qur'an's answer, given here and at 6:27–28, is that the conditional form is evidence of the problem rather than a description of it. The excuse describes an obstacle that the person would remove, and then — the Qur'an says plainly — would not remove it.

The practical discipline the tradition recommends is the reversal: rather than inventorying what one lacks, act on what one has. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"If the Hour is established and one of you has a shoot in his hand, let him plant it if he can."* (Aḥmad 12512; al-Bukhārī in al-Adab al-Mufrad — graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

And the Qur'an's own formulation of the matter is stark:

> *"O you who believe, why do you say what you do not do? It is most hateful to God that you say what you do not do."* (Qur'an 61:2–3)

The verse before us ends the conversation about what might have been. The messenger came. The signs were recited. The condition was fulfilled. What remains is the response — and the sūrah's next verse shows what the Quraysh's response was.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:48

> **But when the truth came to them from Us, they said, "Why was he not given the like of that which was given to Moses?" Did they not disbelieve in that which was given to Moses aforetime? They said, "Two sorceries, supporting one another." And they said, "Truly we are disbelievers in each."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Moment of Truth, and the Evasion**

*Fa-lammā jāʾahumu al-ḥaqqu min ʿindinā qālū lawlā ūtiya mithla mā ūtiya Mūsā*.

This is the verse the entire preceding argument has been building toward. The messenger came. The signs were recited. The excuses were removed (v. 47). And the response was not acceptance, nor even outright refusal — it was a *demand for a different messenger*.

The commentators note the structure of the evasion, and it is one of the most recognizable patterns in human argument. The Quraysh did not say "we reject this." They said *why was he not given what Moses was given?* — a question that sounds pious, shifts the burden onto God, and postpones decision indefinitely.

**The Content of the Demand**

What exactly were they asking for? The commentators record several readings, all transmitted from the early authorities.

*Miracles.* Al-Qurṭubī transmits that they meant visible miracles of the Mosaic kind — the staff, the white hand, the parting of the sea. The Qur'an records the same demand in many places:

> *"And they say, 'We will not believe in thee until you cause a spring to gush forth from the earth for us, or you have a garden of date palms and grapes... or you make the heaven fall upon us in fragments, as you claim...'"* (Qur'an 17:90–92)

*A scripture all at once.* Al-Rāzī transmits this reading: they wanted a book revealed in a single act, as they understood the Torah to have been, rather than in portions over twenty-three years. The Qur'an records this demand:

> *"And the disbelievers say, 'Why was the Qur'an not sent down upon him as a single whole?' Thus [it is sent in parts], that We may strengthen thy heart thereby, and We have rehearsed it with distinct rehearsal."* (Qur'an 25:32)

*A messenger of higher status.* The Qur'an records this too:

> *"And they said, 'Why was this Qur'an not sent down to a great man from one of the two cities?'"* (Qur'an 43:31)

All three readings share the same structure: a demand for a messenger more to their specifications.

**The Refutation: "Did They Not Disbelieve in That Which Was Given to Moses Aforetime?"**

*Fa-lammā jāʾahumu al-ḥaqqu min ʿindinā qālū... a-wa-lam yakfurū bi-mā ūtiya Mūsā min qablu*. The Qur'an's answer is a single, devastating question.

You ask why Muhammad ﷺ was not given what Moses was given. But did you believe in what Moses was given? The demand was never about evidence; you rejected the evidence you already acknowledge.

The commentators note that this is a *reductio*. The demand for more signs presupposes that signs are what is preventing belief. But the Quraysh revered Moses — they invoked him constantly — and they did not follow the Torah. Their problem is not a shortage of signs.

The same argument is made elsewhere with even more precision:

> *"And they said, 'Why are signs not sent down upon him from his Lord?' Say: 'The signs are only with God, and I am only a plain warner.' Is it not sufficient for them that We have sent down unto thee the Book, which is recited to them? Truly in that is a mercy and a reminder for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 29:50–51)

That verse states the tradition's position on miracles plainly: the standing miracle of the Prophet ﷺ is the Qur'an, and it is sufficient. The commentators note that the miracles of earlier prophets were suited to their peoples — Moses' people were masters of magic, so his signs were of that kind; the Arabs were masters of language, so the sign given to Muhammad ﷺ was a Book of unmatched Arabic. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"There was no prophet among the prophets except that he was given signs by which people believed; and what I have been given is a revelation that God has revealed to me, so I hope that I will have the most followers among them on the Day of Resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 4981; Muslim 152)

**"Two Sorceries, Supporting One Another"**

*Qālū siḥrāni taẓāharā*. The word *siḥrān* is the dual of *siḥr* — two sorceries. The verb *taẓāharā* means to support one another, to back each other up, to be mutually reinforcing.

The commentators discuss what the "two" are, and their readings are worth setting out.

*The Torah and the Qur'an.* This is the reading transmitted by al-Ṭabarī from several early authorities: the Quraysh, having dismissed the Qur'an as sorcery, then dismissed the Torah as sorcery too, and said the two corroborate each other — meaning the accusation covers both revelations.

*The Torah and the Gospel.* Also transmitted, on the reading that the two Books known to the Arabs were those.

*Two sorcerers.* The word *siḥrān* can be read as *sāḥirān* — two sorcerers. On this reading, the two are Moses and Muhammad ﷺ, or Moses and Aaron, or Jesus and Muhammad ﷺ. Al-Qurṭubī records all of these.

The commentators note the self-defeating character of the charge in every reading. If two revelations separated by two thousand years and two vastly different communities "support one another," that is evidence of a common source, not of collusion. The charge of collusion requires contact — and the sūrah has just spent three verses proving that no contact was possible (vv. 44–46).

**The Historical Background: The Quraysh Consulting the Jews**

The commentaries transmit a well-known historical detail that explains the verse's specific form, and it is reported in the standard works of *sīrah* and *asbāb al-nuzūl*.

The Quraysh, unable to defeat the Prophet ﷺ in argument, sent a delegation to the Jewish scholars of Madinah — who had scriptures and knowledge of the prophets — and asked them how to test him. The Jews suggested three questions: about the young men who slept in the cave, about the traveler who reached the ends of the earth, and about the soul. The first two are answered in Sūrah al-Kahf (18:9–26, 18:83–98) and the third at 17:85.

Al-Qurṭubī transmits that this consultation is the background of the present verse as well, and that the Jewish scholars instructed the Quraysh to ask why Muhammad ﷺ had not been given what Moses was given — knowing that the question would be impossible to satisfy and would create the appearance of a difficulty.

Al-Rāzī's comment on this is sharp: the question was cynical from the start. The people asking it did not intend to believe if it were answered; they intended to have a question. And the Quraysh's own conduct proved it, since when the Qur'an answered their other challenges, they did not believe either.

The tradition has drawn a permanent lesson from this about the nature of some religious objections: the test is not whether the question is answered but whether the questioner intends an answer.

**The Final Clause: "Truly We Are Disbelievers in Each"**

*Wa-qālū innā bi-kullin kāfirūn*. The verse ends with a total rejection: *we are disbelievers in every one of them*.

The commentators note what this reveals. The previous clause at least paid the revelations the compliment of calling them powerful — *two sorceries supporting one another*. This clause drops the pretense and states the truth: the rejection is comprehensive and is not based on the merits of anything.

The Qur'an's diagnosis is therefore complete. The objection (*why was he not given what Moses was given*) was a performance. The reality is stated here: *we are disbelievers in each*.

**The Logic of the Whole Verse**

Set out the argument as the commentators do, and its structure is worth internalizing because it applies to every generation.

*Premise one:* You demand a sign like Moses'.
*Premise two:* You reject what Moses brought.
*Conclusion:* The demand is not about signs.

The Qur'an generalizes the point elsewhere:

> *"And even if We had sent down to them the angels, and the dead spoke to them, and We gathered all things before them face to face, they would not believe, unless God willed."* (Qur'an 6:111)

That verse is the definitive statement of the principle, and the tradition has always cited it to explain why the Qur'an sometimes *refuses* to grant demanded signs. A sign granted to a person who has already decided against belief does not produce belief; it produces a harder heart and a heavier reckoning.

The Prophet ﷺ was told:

> *"And nothing prevents Us from sending the signs, save that the ancients denied them."* (Qur'an 17:59)

**The Theological Question: Why Not Simply Give Everyone What They Demand?**

The commentators address the obvious objection, and the answer has several parts.

*Signs are given for those who might believe.* The Qur'an's signs are directed at the receptive, and their purpose is mercy, not coercion.

*Compelling belief would destroy the test.* If the evidence were irresistible, faith would not be faith, and the whole structure of moral responsibility would collapse. The tradition states it as: belief is praiseworthy precisely because it is a response to evidence that *can* be refused.

*The standing sign suffices.* For this community, the sign is the Qur'an, and the challenge to produce its like remains open to anyone, at any time:

> *"And if you are in doubt concerning what We sent down upon Our servant, then bring a sūrah like it, and call your witnesses apart from God, if you are truthful. But if you do not — and you will never do so — then fear the Fire whose fuel is people and stones, prepared for the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 2:23–24)

That verse makes the challenge permanent and universal, and the tradition's scholars have written extensively on the *iʿjāz* — the inimitability — that makes it unanswerable.

**Application**

The verse is the Qur'an's anatomy of the *disingenuous objection*, and its diagnostic is portable.

The signature is a demand that sounds like a search for evidence but is structured so that it cannot be satisfied. The demand is always for something *other* than what has been given. The demander has no history of accepting comparable evidence. And when the demand is met — as it sometimes is — the goalposts move.

The tradition's counsel for the person who encounters such objections is not to refuse to answer but to identify the real question. The Qur'an answers the Quraysh's challenges repeatedly and at length — in this sūrah, in al-Kahf, in al-Isrāʾ. It is not evasive. But it also names what the challenges are, so that no one mistakes them for inquiry.

And for the person who catches himself making them: the diagnostic question of this verse is direct. Is there a version of this question whose answer I would accept? If not, the question is not about evidence, and the sūrah's next verse supplies the answer to what it *is* about — the following of caprice (v. 50).

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:49

> **Say, "So bring a book from God that provides better guidance than these two, that I may follow it, if you are truthful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counter-Challenge**

*Qul faʾtū bi-kitābin min ʿindi Allāhi huwa ahdā minhumā attabiʿhu in kuntum ṣādiqīn*.

The commentators note the rhetorical turn with some admiration. The Quraysh had demanded that God produce a messenger to their specifications (v. 48). God's response through His Prophet is to turn the demand around and make it *reasonable*: bring a book from God that is better guided than these two, and I will follow it.

The shift is from an impossible demand to a fair one. You cannot dictate what God must send. But you can certainly produce a better book, if you have one.

**"Say" — The Prophet as Recipient of the Argument**

The verse opens with *qul* — *say* — as do more than three hundred verses of the Qur'an. The commentators note what this establishes: the argument is not the Prophet's invention, and he is not speaking for himself. He is instructed in the exact reply.

This is part of the Qur'an's own self-presentation, and the tradition cites it as one marker of the revelation's origin: a man being told what to say in reply to his own opponents, including replies he could not have anticipated.

Compare the directness of the challenge with the Prophet's own character as reported in the tradition. ʿĀʾishah described him:

> *"He never took revenge for himself; but when the limits of God were violated, he would take revenge for the sake of God."* (al-Bukhārī 6853; Muslim 2327)

The challenge issued here is not personal retaliation; it is an argument given to him to deliver.

**"A Book From God" — The Condition That Cannot Be Met**

*Bi-kitābin min ʿindi Allāhi*. The challenge specifies three things: a *book* (not an argument, not a poem, not a philosophy), *from God* (i.e., with a credible claim to revealed origin), and *better guided* than the two scriptures already named.

The commentators note the precision of the conditions, and they point out that the challenge is *generous*. It does not say "bring a book equal to these two." It says *better guided* — which, the tradition observes, is a deliberately easy standard to state and an impossible one to meet, because the criterion is superior guidance rather than superior beauty, and no human composition can claim revelatory authority at all.

The recurrence of this challenge throughout the Qur'an is one of the Book's most striking features:

> *"And if you are in doubt concerning what We sent down upon Our servant, then bring a sūrah like it... But if you do not — and you will never do so — then fear the Fire."* (Qur'an 2:23–24)

> *"Or do they say, 'He has invented it'? Say: 'Then bring a sūrah like it, and call upon whomever you can apart from God, if you are truthful.'"* (Qur'an 10:38)

> *"Or do they say, 'He has invented it'? Say: 'Then bring ten sūrahs like it, invented, and call upon whomever you can apart from God, if you are truthful. And if they do not respond to you, then know that it is sent down by the knowledge of God.'"* (Qur'an 11:13–14)

> *"Say: 'If mankind and the jinn gathered together to bring the like of this Qur'an, they could not bring the like of it, even if they helped one another.'"* (Qur'an 17:88)

The tradition's scholars collected these verses into the discipline of *iʿjāz al-Qurʾān* — the doctrine of the Qur'an's inimitability — and wrote enormous works on it, from al-Bāqillānī's *Iʿjāz al-Qurʾān* and al-Rummānī's treatise to al-Jurjānī's *Dalāʾil al-Iʿjāz* and al-Zarkashī's *al-Burhān*.

**"That I May Follow It" — The Extraordinary Offer**

*Attabiʿhu* — *I will follow it*. The commentators pause over this, and the pause is justified.

The Prophet ﷺ, who received revelation, is instructed to say that he would follow a better book if one were produced. The offer is unconditional: *if you are truthful*.

This is one of the clearest proofs in the Qur'an that the religion is not tribal, personal, or national. The criterion is not "who brought it" but "is it from God and is it better guided." The commentaries note that this verse refutes, in advance, any claim that the Qur'an's authority rests on the identity or the ethnicity of the messenger rather than on the revelation itself.

The general principle is stated throughout the Qur'an:

> *"Say: 'O People of the Book, come to a word common between us and you: that we worship none but God.'"* (Qur'an 3:64)

> *"And argue with the People of the Book only in the best manner — except those among them who do wrong — and say, 'We believe in that which has been sent down to us and that which has been sent down to you; our God and your God is One, and unto Him we submit.'"* (Qur'an 29:46)

And the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The prophets are paternal brothers: their mothers are different but their religion is one."* (al-Bukhārī 3443; Muslim 2365)

The tradition has drawn from this verse a permanent attitude: the believer follows *truth*, and if a better-guided book from God existed, he would be obliged to follow it. Since none does, he follows this one — not out of partisanship but because this is what the evidence supports.

**"If You Are Truthful" — The Challenge Returns the Burden**

*In kuntum ṣādiqīn*. The closing condition places the burden where it belongs.

The commentators note the logic. The Quraysh's objection in verse 48 was, in form, a claim: that a better messenger and a better revelation should have been sent. A claim carries a burden. The Qur'an's response is to say: produce it.

This is the standing rule of the Qur'an's polemic, and it is stated as a general principle:

> *"Say: 'Bring your proof, if you are truthful.'"* (Qur'an 2:111; 27:64)

> *"Or have you a clear authority? Then bring your book, if you are truthful."* (Qur'an 37:156–157)

The tradition's formulation is that the one who asserts bears the burden, and that the assertion of a negative ("this is not from God") is as much in need of support as the assertion of a positive.

**The Historical Reality: The Challenge Was Never Met**

The commentators — and, more broadly, the tradition's historians — note the empirical fact: the challenge was issued in Makkah and Madinah, repeatedly, to a people whose entire cultural prestige rested on eloquence, and it was not met.

The Qur'an records that some attempted to respond and produced what the tradition regards as risible material — the most famous being the attempts attributed to Musaylimah, whose compositions are recorded in the histories and are, by consensus of the tradition, beneath comparison. The scholars noted that if the Arabs — who could recognize a superior poem at fifty paces and who spent years of their lives on poetry — had been able to meet the challenge, they would have, because nothing else would have ended the dispute so decisively and cheaply.

Instead they chose war. The commentators regard that choice as evidence: people do not fight a battle over something they could have settled with a paragraph.

**The Doctrine of *Iʿjāz*: What the Tradition Actually Claimed**

Because this verse is one of the foundations of that doctrine, it is worth stating what the tradition claimed and did not claim.

*The claim is not merely literary.* While the Arabs' initial confrontation with the Qur'an was on literary grounds — and the Qur'an's Arabic remains, by the consensus of Arabic critics Muslim and non-Muslim, at the summit of the language — the doctrine of *iʿjāz* as the scholars formulated it includes several dimensions:

*Linguistic inimitability*: the Qur'an is neither poetry nor prose (*lā shiʿr wa-lā nathr*), and its register cannot be reproduced.

*Informational inimitability*: its reports of the unseen — past events unknown to its audience (as in verses 44–46), and future events that came to pass:

> *"The Romans have been defeated in the nearest land; and they, after their defeat, will be victorious within a few years."* (Qur'an 30:2–4)

*Legislative comprehensiveness*: the way it establishes a complete moral and legal order without contradiction:

> *"Do they not then reflect upon the Qur'an? Had it been from other than God, they would surely have found within it much discrepancy."* (Qur'an 4:82)

*Transformative effect*: what it produced in its first hearers — a point the tradition regarded as evidence, since a text that turns a tribal society into a civilization in a generation is not ordinary.

And the tradition was careful about a negative point: the *iʿjāz* attaches to the Qur'an as it was revealed, in its Arabic. Translations are interpretations of the meaning, not the Qur'an itself, which is why the prayer and the recitation are in Arabic and why translations are always called *translations of the meanings*.

**Application: The Challenge's Standing Form**

The verse's most useful everyday application is the standard it sets for religious disagreement.

The question is never *who* is speaking, or *where* he comes from, or *what* community he belongs to. The question is: does he have a better book from God? The tradition's confidence is that the question can be asked without fear, because the answer has been the same for fourteen centuries.

There is a second application for the believer's own intellectual life. The phrase *I will follow it, if you are truthful* models a genuine openness: a willingness to be shown better guidance, combined with an insistence that the one claiming better guidance produce it. That combination — intellectual humility about one's conclusions and intellectual rigor about claims — is exactly the posture the tradition calls *inṣāf*, fairness.

The Prophet ﷺ embodied it. When a man came to him and confessed adultery, insisting on the punishment despite the Prophet's attempts to find a way out for him, the Prophet ﷺ neither refused to hear the claim nor accepted it without the legally required repetition. And when the Qur'an instructed him to say *bring your proof*, he said it.

And there is a final, quieter point. The challenge is addressed to people who had every incentive to meet it and could not. The sūrah's next verse draws the conclusion that follows when the challenge is not met.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:50

> **And if they respond not to thee, then know that they follow only their caprices. And who is more astray than one who follows his caprice without guidance from God? Surely God guides not wrongdoing people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Diagnosis After the Challenge Fails**

*Fa-in lam yastajībū laka fa-aʿlam annamā yattabiʿūna ahwāʾahum*.

The verse opens with a condition: *if they do not respond to you*. The commentators note that this is not hypothetical in the Qur'an's own framing — it is the predicted and actual outcome. The challenge of verse 49 was issued, and it was not met. What follows is the diagnosis of *why*.

The diagnosis is one word: *ahwāʾahum* — their caprices, their desires, their whims. The singular is *hawā*, a word the commentators unpack carefully because its range in the Qur'an is wide and its meaning is precise.

**The Word *Hawā*: From Appetite to Idolatry**

The root *h-w-y* carries the sense of falling, descending, being drawn downward. From it comes *hawāʾ* — air, the space through which things fall — and *hawā* — desire, because desire is what pulls a person down.

The Qur'an uses the word in this moral sense throughout, and its usage escalates to one of the most startling statements in the Book:

> *"Have you seen the one who takes his caprice (hawāhu) as his god? And God has led him astray despite knowledge, and set a seal upon his hearing and his heart, and placed a covering over his sight. Who then will guide him after God? Will you not remember?"* (Qur'an 45:23)

That verse is the terminus of the idea, and the commentators regard it as the definitive Qur'anic account of what unbelief actually *is*. The disbeliever is not, in the Qur'an's analysis, primarily someone who has examined the evidence and drawn a negative conclusion. He is someone whose desires have taken the position that belongs to God.

The gradations the tradition drew from the word's usage:

*Hawā as appetite* — the ordinary pull toward what one wants:

> *"And as for him who feared the standing before his Lord and restrained his soul from caprice — truly, the Garden will be his refuge."* (Qur'an 79:40–41)

*Hawā as a source of error in judgment* — the distortion of reasoning by wanting:

> *"And follow not caprice, lest it lead you astray from the way of God."* (Qur'an 38:26)

*Hawā as idolatry* — when the want becomes the object of worship:

> *"Have you seen the one who takes his caprice as his god?"* (Qur'an 45:23)

The Prophet ﷺ warned about the third with a severity that surprised his companions:

> *"Nothing under the sky is worshipped more greatly in God's sight than a desire that is followed."* (Reported in the commentaries; the meaning is supported by 45:23)

and he said:

> *"None of you truly believes until his desire (hawāhu) follows that which I have brought."* (al-Nawawī, *al-Arbaʿīn*, no. 41 — a ḥadīth whose chain has been debated but whose meaning is anchored in 45:23 and 4:65)

**"Who Is More Astray Than One Who Follows His Caprice Without Guidance From God?"**

*Wa-man aḍallu mimmani ittabaʿa hawāhu bi-ghayri hudan mina Allāhi*. The rhetorical question is the Qur'an's strongest form of assertion.

The commentators parse the comparison carefully. The claim is not merely "such a person is astray" but *no one is more astray*. The superlative is deliberate, and the reason given is precise: *without guidance from God* — *bi-ghayri hudan min Allāh*.

The qualification matters enormously. Desire is not condemned as such; the human being is created with desires, and the Qur'an affirms the goodness of what is lawful:

> *"Say: 'Who has forbidden the adornment of God which He has brought forth for His servants, and the good things of provision?' Say: 'These are, in the life of this world, for those who believe...'"* (Qur'an 7:32)

What is condemned is desire *operating without* guidance — that is, desire as the *source* of the moral standard rather than as something submitted to one.

The tradition's formulation is therefore: guidance is the criterion, and desire is what must be measured against it. A person with strong desires and clear guidance is in a better position than a person with mild desires and no guidance, because the first has a standard and the second has only preferences.

The commentators note the logic of the superlative. A person who follows desire *despite* guidance is in error. But a person who follows desire *as* his guidance — who has replaced the standard with his own wanting — has no reference point left by which he could ever discover he was wrong. That is why he is *more astray* than anyone.

**"Surely God Guides Not Wrongdoing People"**

*Inna Allāha lā yahdī al-qawma al-ẓālimīn*. The verse closes with a statement that has generated careful theological discussion, and the discussion is important.

The apparent difficulty: if God does not guide them, how are they responsible for not being guided?

The tradition's answer, developed by the scholars of creed and stated by al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī, distinguishes two kinds of guidance (*hudā*):

*Hudā al-dalālah wa-l-irshād* — the guidance of *showing the way*, which God gives to everyone without exception. He sent messengers, revealed books, and made the signs clear. This guidance is universal and no one is denied it:

> *"And as for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness to guidance."* (Qur'an 41:17)

*Hudā al-tawfīq wa-l-ithbāt* — the guidance of *enabling and confirming*, by which God opens a person's heart and makes him accept. This is given to those who seek it, and withheld from those who persist in wrongdoing as a consequence of their own choice:

> *"And when they deviated, God made their hearts deviate."* (Qur'an 61:5)

> *"So when they turned away, God turned their hearts away."* (Qur'an 61:5, and cf. 4:155)

The order in those verses is crucial and the tradition insists upon it: the deviation comes first, and the divine turning is the consequence. God does not prevent anyone from being guided; He confirms a person in the direction that person has chosen, and the confirmation is itself justice — the person who has said "no" forty times is not wronged by being left with his "no."

The commentators add a third element: the withholding of *tawfīq* is also a warning in the present tense. The verse is recited to people who can still change.

**The Connection to Verses 48–49**

Read verses 48–50 together, and the sūrah has performed a complete diagnosis, which the commentators lay out in four steps.

*Step one*: They asked for a different messenger (v. 48).
*Step two*: They had already rejected the revelations they claimed to revere (v. 48).
*Step three*: They were challenged to produce something better and could not (v. 49).
*Step four*: Therefore, their objection was never about evidence (v. 50) — it is *hawā*.

This is the Qur'an's standard account of rejection, and it appears with the same structure elsewhere:

> *"And even if We had sent down to them the angels, and the dead spoke to them, and We gathered all things before them face to face, they would not believe, unless God willed. But most of them are ignorant."* (Qur'an 6:111)

**The Psychology: How Desire Disguises Itself as Reason**

The spiritual commentators developed the practical psychology of this verse at length, because it is the most useful thing in it.

The mechanism they describe is that desire rarely announces itself. It presents as prudence, as principled objection, as concern for others, as intellectual rigor, as loyalty. The Quraysh's objection sounded like a theological difficulty: *why was he not given the like of what was given to Moses?* Only the unmet challenge of verse 49 exposed it as desire.

The tradition's proposed test is diagnostic rather than theoretical: *would I accept this if it went my way?* If the objection disappears the moment the outcome favors me, the objection was not the reason.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the tradition its standard prayer against this condition:

> *"O God, inspire me with right guidance and protect me from the evil of my own soul."* (al-Tirmidhī 3483 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

and he taught:

> *"O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140; Ibn Mājah 3834)

And the Qur'an's own prayer for the same condition:

> *"Our Lord, let not our hearts deviate after You have guided us, and grant us mercy from Your Presence. Truly You are the Bestower."* (Qur'an 3:8)

**Modern Application: The Industry of *Hawā***

The verse is, in the modern setting, almost unnervingly exact.

The traditional forms of *hawā* — appetite, tribal loyalty, status, comfort — remain. But late modern life has added something new: an entire economic sector devoted to identifying desire and feeding it back to the person at scale, at speed, and with a precision no one's own self-knowledge can match.

The Qur'an's diagnosis predicts the outcome. A system that takes whatever a person already wants and returns it to him endlessly, in a form that feels like his own judgment, produces a person who *cannot* be guided, not because he has rejected guidance but because he has lost the ability to recognize anything that contradicts him. He is, in the Qur'an's term, one who follows his caprice *without guidance from God* — and the superlative of this verse applies.

The tradition's remedies are the ones the Qur'an prescribes: an external standard to which the self is submitted rather than consulted; a community that can say no; regular practices that interrupt the feedback loop (the five daily prayers being the primary one); and the constant invocation against the self's own devices.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Hasten to good deeds before trials come like a portion of the dark night: a man will wake as a believer and enter the evening as a disbeliever, or enter the evening as a believer and wake as a disbeliever, selling his religion for some worldly gain."* (Muslim 118)

That is the description of a person whose standard has become his appetite, and the remedy is to fix the standard before the trials arrive.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:51

> **And indeed We have caused the Word to reach them, that haply they may reflect.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Short Verse With a Long Reach**

*Wa-laqad waṣṣalnā lahumu al-qawla laʿallahum yatadhakkarūn*.

One sentence, and the commentators note that its brevity is deceptive. It is at once a statement about the revelation's completeness, a statement about God's justice, and a restatement of the sūrah's purpose.

**"We Have Caused the Word to Reach Them" — *Waṣṣalnā***

The verb *waṣṣala* is from *w-ṣ-l*, connection, joining, continuity. Al-Ṭabarī glosses it as *We have elaborated the Word for them* — that is, We have set it out fully and in connected sequence. Al-Qurṭubī takes it as *We have completed the Word for them*, meaning nothing essential has been left unsaid.

Both readings are in play, and the commentators note that the word implies both *completeness* and *connection*: the revelation has been delivered as a continuous, whole, sufficient message, not as fragments whose meaning is inaccessible.

The word *al-qawl* — the Word — is used here for the Qur'an, as it is elsewhere:

> *"And We have certainly brought them a Book which We have detailed with knowledge — a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 7:52)

> *"And thy Lord's Word has been fulfilled in truth and in justice; none can change His Words."* (Qur'an 6:115)

The commentators note that *al-qawl* in the Qur'an frequently means the divine decree as well as the divine speech, and that both senses are appropriate here: the revelation has reached them, and the argument has been completed.

**"To Them" — Who?**

*Lahum* — *to them*. The commentators identify the referent from the context: the Quraysh primarily, and the People of the Book secondarily, since verse 48's objection involved both. Al-Qurṭubī notes both possibilities and inclines to the Quraysh, since the sūrah is Makkan and the immediate addressees are the Prophet's own people.

The point the commentators stress is that the Word has *reached* them. This is the condition that makes judgment just, and it is the theme the sūrah has been building since verse 43: the removal of every excuse.

**The Theological Claim: Sufficiency and Clarity of the Message**

The verse makes a claim that the tradition has always treated as foundational: the revelation is complete and sufficient. Nothing necessary for guidance was withheld.

> *"This day I have perfected for you your religion, and completed My blessing upon you, and have chosen for you as religion submission (islām)."* (Qur'an 5:3)

> *"And We have sent down unto thee the Book as an exposition of everything, and a guidance, and a mercy, and glad tidings for those who submit."* (Qur'an 16:89)

The commentators were careful about the scope of those claims. *An exposition of everything* does not mean the Qur'an contains every empirical fact; it means it contains everything necessary for the guidance of human beings to their ultimate good. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī both make this qualification, and the tradition has held it consistently: the Qur'an is sufficient for *guidance*, and the Sunnah is its authoritative exposition, while the empirical and practical sciences are left to human investigation.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the sufficiency in the most quoted sermon of his life:

> *"I have left you upon a clear path: its night is like its day; no one deviates from it after me except one who is destroyed."* (Ibn Mājah 5; Aḥmad — the well-known statement from the Farewell Sermon tradition)

and:

> *"I have left among you two things; you will never go astray as long as you hold fast to them: the Book of God and my Sunnah."* (Mālik, *al-Muwaṭṭaʾ*; al-Ḥākim — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**"That Haply They May Reflect" — *Yatadhakkarūn***

As at verses 43 and 46, the purpose clause is *remembrance*. Its third occurrence in eight verses is itself part of the sūrah's argument, and the commentators note the emphasis.

The Qur'an's claim, repeated here, is that the obstacle is not information. The Word has reached them. What remains is that they *remember* — that is, that the knowledge which is already present in them be awakened and connected to what they have heard.

This is why the Qur'an so often addresses its hearers as people who *know* and have *forgotten*:

> *"And thus do We detail the signs, and that the way of the guilty may become clear."* (Qur'an 6:55)

> *"Say: 'Unto God belongs the conclusive argument; had He willed, He would surely have guided you all.'"* (Qur'an 6:149)

The tradition's pastoral method follows from this: the preacher's task is not primarily to supply new data but to *remind*. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever guides to something good has a reward like the reward of the one who does it."* (Muslim 1893)

and the Qur'an describes itself by that function:

> *"So remind; you are only a reminder. You are not a taskmaster over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

That last verse is the limit the tradition has always observed: the reminder is delivered, and the response is not the messenger's to produce.

**The Structure of the Sūrah's Argument to This Point**

Verses 43–51 form a complete unit, and the commentators summarize its movement:

*Verse 43*: Moses was given a Book after the earlier generations were destroyed.
*Verses 44–46*: You were not present at any of it; God sent you as a mercy to a people with no warner.
*Verse 47*: This was done so that no one would have the excuse that no messenger came.
*Verse 48*: When the messenger came, they demanded a different one.
*Verse 49*: They were challenged to produce a better book and could not.
*Verse 50*: Therefore they follow caprice, not evidence.
*Verse 51*: Nevertheless, the Word has been delivered to them in full, so that they may remember.

The argument is closed. What remains — verses 52 through 55 — is a brief, luminous aside on those who *did* respond.

**Theological Note: The Word Reached Even Those Who Reject It**

The commentators draw an important distinction from this verse. The Word's *reaching* someone is not the same as that person's *accepting* it.

This distinction protects two doctrines at once. It protects God's justice: no one is judged for rejecting what never reached him (v. 47, v. 59). And it protects human responsibility: the Word's arrival does not compel acceptance, and a person may hear it fully and still refuse (v. 50).

The tradition expresses this in its doctrine of *al-ḥujjah* — the conclusive argument. Once the Word reaches a person in a form he can understand, the argument is established and the responsibility attaches. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"By Him in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, no one of this community hears of me — whether Jew or Christian — and then dies without believing in what I was sent with, but that he will be among the inhabitants of the Fire."* (Muslim 153)

The ḥadīth's condition is *hears of me* — the message must arrive. The scholars therefore discussed what counts as a message's arrival in each case, and they held that the *distorted* representations of Islam that reach some people do not necessarily constitute the arrival of the message in a recognizable form.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*First, the message is complete.* The believer is not waiting for a further revelation, a missing book, or a secret teaching. The Word has been connected and delivered, and the tradition's task is to understand and live it, not to supplement it. This is the foundation of the Islamic rejection of religious innovation (*bidʿah*) in matters of creed and worship:

> *"Whoever introduces into this affair of ours something that is not from it, it is rejected."* (al-Bukhārī 2697; Muslim 1718)

*Second, the failure is in remembrance, not in information.* Most people who are far from God are not short of information about Him. They are short of remembrance. That is why the tradition prescribes *dhikr* — the regular, structured remembering — as the ordinary maintenance of the religious life:

> *"And remember your Lord within yourself, humbly and in awe, and without loudness of voice, morning and evening; and be not among the heedless."* (Qur'an 7:205)

*Third, the reminder has a limit.* The one who reminds is not a taskmaster. The tradition has drawn from this the proper emotional posture of anyone engaged in calling others to God: full effort in delivery, and release of the outcome. The Qur'an states it as the consolation of every messenger:

> *"So remind; you are only a reminder. You are not a taskmaster over them — save him who turns away and disbelieves, and God will punish him with the greatest punishment. Truly unto Us is their return; then truly upon Us is their reckoning."* (Qur'an 88:21–26)

The Word has reached them. The sūrah now turns, for four verses, to those who received it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:52

> **Those unto whom We gave the Book before it, they are believers in it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Light After the Argument**

The sūrah has spent nine verses on rejection. Now, in four verses, it turns to those who believed — and the commentators note that the contrast is mercy as much as argument. The Qur'an does not leave its hearers with only a picture of refusal; it shows the alternative immediately.

*Alladhīna ātaynāhumu al-kitāba min qablihi hum bihi yuʾminūn* — *those to whom We gave the Book before it, they are believers in it*.

**Who Are They?**

The commentators identify the referent from the sūrah's context: the People of the Book — Jews and Christians — who embraced Islam when the Qur'an came.

Al-Ṭabarī names the specific individuals the tradition remembers: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, who was a Jewish scholar of Madinah; Salmān al-Fārsī, who had been a Zoroastrian and had traveled through Christian circles seeking the truth; and members of the Christian communities of Syria and Abyssinia. Al-Qurṭubī adds the names transmitted in the early sources: Baḥīrah the monk, Waraqah ibn Nawfal, and the Abyssinian Christians who returned with the emigrants.

The commentators note two readings of the phrase *from before it* — *min qablihi*.

*Before the Qur'an.* That is, they were given a scripture previously, and when the Qur'an came they believed in it.

*Before Muhammad's appearance.* Some commentators read it as: they believed in the coming of a final prophet before he appeared, on the basis of their own scriptures, and then recognized him when he came.

Both readings describe the same people, and the second explains the first: the reason they believed in the Qur'an is that their own Book had prepared them for it.

**The Theological Principle: Continuity of Revelation**

The verse is one of the primary Qur'anic texts on the relationship between Islam and the earlier scriptures, and the commentators draw several conclusions from it.

*The earlier scriptures were from God.* The verse says *We gave them the Book* — the same Giver. The tradition's position, as noted at verse 43, is that the Torah and the Gospel were genuine revelations.

*Faithfulness to an earlier revelation leads to the later one.* This is the verse's most striking implication. These people believed in the Qur'an *because* they had taken their own Book seriously, not in spite of it. The Qur'an states the principle elsewhere:

> *"Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered prophet, whom they find written with them in the Torah and the Gospel, who enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong..."* (Qur'an 7:157)

> *"And when there came to them a Book from God, confirming what was with them — even though before that they had been praying for victory over those who disbelieved — yet when there came to them that which they recognized, they disbelieved in it."* (Qur'an 2:89)

That second verse is the negative counterpart to this one, and the two together establish the Qur'an's criterion: the sincere believer in an earlier revelation *recognizes* the later one, because the source is the same. The one who rejects it does so not out of fidelity to his scripture but out of something else, and 2:89 names it plainly — they had been expecting this very messenger and praying for victory *through* him, and when he came they denied him.

**The Reward of Those Who Believed**

The commentators connect this verse directly to verse 54, where these people are promised *a reward twice over*. Two reasons are given in the tradition for the doubling, and both are transmitted from the early authorities:

*They believed in the foretelling and then in the fulfillment.* That is, they believed in a prophet they had not seen, on the basis of their scripture's promise, and then believed again when he actually came. Two acts of faith in two different circumstances.

*They believed in their own prophet and then in Muhammad ﷺ.* Two messengers, two loyalties maintained rather than one abandoned for the other.

The Prophet ﷺ confirmed the principle in a ḥadīth that the tradition cites constantly beside this verse:

> *"Whoever believes in his prophet and then lives to see me and believes in me, he has a double reward."* (Reported in the sources with varying chains; the meaning is anchored in 28:54 and 57:28)

and the Qur'an states the same promise for the People of the Book who believe:

> *"O you who believe, fear God and believe in His Messenger; He will give you a double portion of His mercy, and will appoint for you a light by which you shall walk, and will forgive you; and God is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 57:28)

**The Question of the Salvation of Non-Muslims**

Because this verse and its companions bear on a question the modern world asks constantly, the tradition's position deserves a careful statement.

The classical position, as formulated by the scholars of creed and law:

*Before the mission of Muhammad ﷺ*, the valid religion was the one brought by the prophet of the time. The People of the Book were obligated to follow the Torah and the Gospel as they had them.

*After the mission of Muhammad ﷺ*, his message abrogates all previous legislation, and salvation requires following it. The evidence is the ḥadīth quoted at verse 51:

> *"By Him in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, no one of this community hears of me — whether Jew or Christian — and then dies without believing in what I was sent with, but that he will be among the inhabitants of the Fire."* (Muslim 153)

*Those who never heard the message* are in a different category, as discussed at verse 47. They are not punished for what never reached them.

*The nuance the scholars insisted upon* is that the *arrival of the message* must be genuine. Al-Ghazālī, and after him a long line of scholars, distinguished between those who hear the name of Islam in a distorted form and those to whom the actual message has been conveyed with its evidence. The latter are responsible; the former are in God's hands.

Ibn Taymiyyah addressed the question at length and his position — that those who never received the message will be tested on the Day of Resurrection — has been influential, though other scholars held different views. The one thing the tradition has never permitted is for any human being to declare a specific individual damned.

The commentators also note the pastoral balance the Qur'an itself maintains here. In the same sūrah that states *truly we are disbelievers in each* (v. 48), it states that some of the People of the Book *are believers in it* (v. 52). The rejection was not universal, and the Qur'an is careful to say so.

**The Sūrah's Purpose in This Verse**

Why does a Makkan sūrah about Moses include this aside about believing Jews and Christians?

The commentators give two reasons.

*It answers the objection of verse 48.* The Quraysh had said *two sorceries supporting one another*, dismissing both the Torah and the Qur'an. The Qur'an's reply is that the two scriptures' agreement is exactly what the people who actually know the Torah recognize. The experts confirm; only the ignorant deny.

*It establishes a criterion for the Prophet's truth.* The Qur'an repeatedly appeals to the People of the Book as witnesses:

> *"Say: 'Is it not a sign for them that the scholars of the Children of Israel know it?'"* (Qur'an 26:197)

> *"And those to whom We gave the Book rejoice in what has been sent down to thee."* (Qur'an 13:36)

The argument is that those with scriptural knowledge are in a position to verify, and that those who had it, accepted.

**Application**

The verse's most practical content is the principle that *sincerity in what you have leads you to what comes next*.

These people were not religious shoppers. They were serious adherents of a tradition who had taken it seriously enough to recognize its fulfillment when it arrived. The tradition's lesson is that fidelity to lesser light is what prepares a person for greater light, and that the person who is unfaithful to what he already knows will not be given more:

> *"And those who strive in Our cause, We shall surely guide them upon Our ways."* (Qur'an 29:69)

> *"And as for those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and gives them their reverence."* (Qur'an 47:17)

There is also a warning embedded here for religious people of every tradition. The people described in 2:89 had the same scripture as those in this verse, and they rejected. The difference was not information; it was what the Qur'an calls *the love of what they had been given*. The scholars named the disease: a vested interest in one's existing position.

And the verse holds out, for anyone in any community, the promise it makes: whoever takes whatever light he has and follows it will be led to the fuller light, and will be counted among those who receive *a reward twice over*.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:53

> **And when it is recited unto them, they say, "We believe in it; verily it is the truth from our Lord. Truly we were submitters even before it."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response of the Recognizers**

*Wa-idhā yutlā ʿalayhim qālū āmannā bihi innahu al-ḥaqqu min rabbinā innā kunnā min qablihi muslimīn*.

The verse describes the *moment* of recognition, and the commentators have always treated it as one of the most moving in the Qur'an: a person hearing the recitation and saying, in effect, this is what I already knew.

**"When It Is Recited Unto Them" — The Power of Recitation**

*Wa-idhā yutlā ʿalayhim*. The verb is the passive of *talā* — to recite, to read aloud, to follow in sequence. The same verb appeared at verse 3 (*We recite unto thee*) and at verse 45 (*reciting unto them Our signs*).

The commentators note what the Qur'an is claiming about the *mode* of the message's arrival. It is not summarized, paraphrased, or argued; it is *recited*. The sound of it is part of its effect.

The tradition has enormous material on this point. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Beautify the Qur'an with your voices, for a good voice increases the Qur'an in beauty."* (al-Bayhaqī; Abū Dāwūd 1468 with varying chains)

> *"He is not one of us who does not chant the Qur'an [with a melodious voice]."* (al-Bukhārī 7527)

And the Qur'an itself describes the physical effect of the recitation on those with knowledge:

> *"God has sent down the best of discourse, a Book consistent and oft-repeated, whereat shiver the skins of those who fear their Lord; then their skins and their hearts soften to the remembrance of God."* (Qur'an 39:23)

> *"And when they hear what has been sent down unto the Messenger, you see their eyes overflowing with tears because of what they recognize of the truth."* (Qur'an 5:83)

That last verse is the direct parallel to this one, and the commentators read them together. The response of the People of the Book who believed was, characteristically, *weeping* — because they recognized something they had been looking for.

**"We Believe in It" — Faith Spoken and Declared**

*Qālū āmannā bihi*. The commentators note the form: the response is *spoken*. Faith is not treated as a private interior state that may or may not be expressed. It is confessed.

This is consistent with the Islamic definition of faith, which the tradition derived from the ḥadīth of Gabriel:

> *"Faith is that you believe in God, and His angels, and His Books, and His messengers, and the Last Day, and in the decree — its good and its evil."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8)

and the Prophet's companion definition:

> *"Faith is a word upon the tongue, a belief in the heart, and an action with the limbs."* (The wording is transmitted in the creedal literature; its content is the position of the People of Ḥadīth, against the Murji'ah who restricted faith to inward assent)

The scholars debated the precise relationship between inward conviction and outward confession, and the majority position that emerged — that of the People of Ḥadīth and later the Māturīdiyyah — is that faith is *in the heart and on the tongue*, with deeds as its fruit and its perfection.

**"Verily It Is the Truth From Our Lord"**

*Innahu al-ḥaqqu min rabbinā*. Two claims in one clause, and the commentators separate them.

*It is the truth.* The word *al-ḥaqq* is one of the divine names and denotes reality, truth, and right. The claim is that what has been recited corresponds to what is — it is not a plausible fiction, an inspired myth, or a noble invention.

*It is from our Lord.* The ownership claim. *Rabbunā* — *our Lord* — is significant: these people already stood in a relationship with God, and they identify the new revelation as coming from that same Lord. They did not experience the Qur'an as a foreign religion arriving from outside; they experienced it as their own Lord speaking again.

The commentators point out that this is the distinguishing mark of genuine recognition. The person who truly knows God receives further truth from Him as *continuity*, not as *invasion*.

The Qur'an states this principle for the messengers generally:

> *"He has prescribed for you as religion that which He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed unto thee, and that which We enjoined upon Abraham and Moses and Jesus: establish the religion and be not divided therein."* (Qur'an 42:13)

**"Truly We Were Submitters Even Before It"**

*Innā kunnā min qablihi muslimīn*. This is the most theologically loaded clause in the verse, and the commentators discuss it at length because it bears on the meaning of *islām* itself.

The word *muslimīn* — submitters — is being used here for people who lived *before* the Qur'an was revealed. How can they be called Muslims?

The tradition's answer, which the commentators state explicitly, is that *islām* in its primary Qur'anic sense is not the name of a historical community but the name of a *posture*: the surrender of oneself to God.

> *"Truly the religion with God is submission (islām)."* (Qur'an 3:19)

> *"And whosoever seeks a religion other than submission (islām), it will never be accepted of him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers."* (Qur'an 3:85)

> *"Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian, but he was a ḥanīf, one who submits (muslim), and he was not among those who ascribe partners."* (Qur'an 3:67)

That last verse is decisive for the question: Abraham, who lived before both Moses and Jesus, is called a *muslim*.
