The word therefore cannot mean "adherent of the religion brought by Muhammad ﷺ" in its primary sense; it means "one who surrenders to God," and every prophet's followers were muslims in that sense.

The scholars expressed the distinction as follows:

*Islām in the general sense*: surrender to God, which is the religion of every prophet and every true follower of every prophet.

*Islām in the specific sense*: the religion revealed to Muhammad ﷺ, which abrogates the previous legislations and is the only acceptable religion after his mission.

The commentators note that the people in this verse were muslims in the first sense before the Qur'an came, and became muslims in both senses when it did. Their prior state was not rejected but *fulfilled*.

**The Question This Raises: Were They Believers Before?**

A further question the commentators raise: what was their status before the revelation reached them?

The answer given is that they were believers in the message available to them, and that God accepted that from them until the new message arrived. This is the principle of *the obligation being proportional to what has been received*:

> *"We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

The tradition's formulation is that God does not require of a people what He has not given them the means to know. The verse before us shows people acting on the light they had, and being given more light as a result — which is exactly the promise:

> *"Those who believe and do righteous deeds — He will guide them by their faith; beneath them rivers will flow in Gardens of bliss."* (Qur'an 10:9)

**The Contrast With the Quraysh**

The sūrah has set two responses side by side, and the commentators note that the contrast is the verse's purpose.

The Quraysh heard and said *two sorceries supporting one another* and *we are disbelievers in each* (v. 48).

The People of the Book heard and said *we believe in it* and *we were submitters before it* (v. 53).

Same message, same city, same era. The difference is not intelligence, information, or even upbringing. The Qur'an attributes the difference to *what the hearer was looking for*.

The commentators developed this into a principle of reception that the tradition has always taught: a person approaches the truth either as a judge or as a seeker, and the same evidence functions entirely differently in the two cases. The seeker's question is *is this true?* The judge's question is *does this suit me?*

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God says: 'I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me.'"* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

and:

> *"Hearts are vessels, and some of them hold more than others. So when you ask of God, ask while you are certain of the answer, for God does not answer a prayer from a heedless, inattentive heart."* (al-Tirmidhī 3479)

**Application**

The verse models the posture that the tradition calls *insāf* — fairness — and it is worth describing in practical terms.

These people had an existing religious identity, a community, a scripture, and probably social cost attached to leaving all of them. What they had *in addition* was a prior commitment to truth itself, which was stronger than their commitment to their own position. When the truth arrived in a form they had not expected and that would cost them, they recognized it anyway.

The practical discipline this implies is an old one in the tradition: cultivate the habit of following evidence *before* the costly case arrives. A person who has never once changed his mind on a small matter will not change it on a large one. The recognition described in this verse was not produced in the moment of hearing; it was produced by a lifetime of preferring truth to comfort, and it was ready when the moment came.

And the reward for that posture is stated in the next verse, in terms the tradition has never stopped quoting: a reward twice over, for having been patient.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:54

> **It is they who will be given their reward twice over for their having been patient. And they repel evil with good, and spend from that which We have provided them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Double Reward**

*Ulāʾika yuʾtawna ajrahum marratayni bi-mā ṣabarū*. The verse opens by identifying the recipients — *those* — and stating their reward: *twice over*, *for that they were patient*.

The commentators, as noted at verse 52, give two complementary reasons for the doubling, and they add a third drawn from the nature of patience itself.

*First*: they believed in a prophet they had not seen on the basis of their scripture's promise, and then believed in him again when he came. Two faiths, two rewards.

*Second*: they held to their own messenger and then to Muhammad ﷺ, maintaining both rather than exchanging one for the other.

*Third*, which the spiritual commentators emphasize: their patience had two objects. They were patient in *keeping* the religion they had when everyone around them had abandoned it, and patient in *accepting* the new one when it cost them their standing. Patience in retention is different from patience in transition, and they exercised both.

The Prophet ﷺ confirmed the principle in a statement the tradition transmits widely:

> *"There are three who will be given their reward twice: a man from the People of the Book who believed in his prophet and then lived to see the Prophet ﷺ and believed in him and followed him — he has a double reward; a slave who fulfills the right of God and the right of his master — he has a double reward; and a man who had a slave girl whom he educated well and taught well, then freed her and married her — he has a double reward."* (al-Bukhārī 97; Muslim 154)

The first of the three is exactly the person in this verse, and the ḥadīth's phrasing — *a man from the People of the Book who believed in his prophet and then believed in me* — is the authoritative gloss on *twice over*.

**"For Their Having Been Patient" — *Ṣabr* as the Condition**

*Bi-mā ṣabarū* — the reward is explicitly *because of* their patience. The commentators note that this makes patience the operative virtue rather than an incidental one.

The Arabic *ṣabr* is a word of unusual range. Its root means to confine, to restrain, to bind. From it, the tradition derived the threefold division that the scholars of ethics formalized:

*Ṣabr ʿalā al-ṭāʿah* — patience *in* obedience: the steadiness to keep praying, fasting, giving, and doing what is required when it is inconvenient and when no one is watching.

*Ṣabr ʿan al-maʿṣiyah* — patience *against* disobedience: the restraint that keeps a person from what he wants and knows he should not have.

*Ṣabr ʿalā al-muṣībah* — patience *under* affliction: the endurance that does not collapse when something is lost, and that does not respond to loss with resentment toward God.

The people in this verse exercised all three. They had kept their religion (obedience), they had refused to participate in what their communities had become (restraint), and they bore the cost of converting (endurance).

The Qur'an promises the reward of patience without specifying a limit:

> *"Truly the patient will be given their reward in full, without reckoning."* (Qur'an 39:10)

— a phrase the commentators have always found remarkable: every other deed is recompensed by measure, and patience is recompensed *without measure*, because its full weight is known only to God.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"No one has ever been given a gift better and more comprehensive than patience."* (al-Bukhārī 1469; Muslim 1053)

and:

> *"How wonderful is the affair of the believer! Truly, all of his affair is good... If something good befalls him, he is grateful, and that is good for him; and if something harmful befalls him, he is patient, and that is good for him."* (Muslim 2999)

**"And They Repel Evil With Good"**

*Wa-yadraʾūna bi-al-ḥasanati al-sayyiʾah*. The verb *daraʾa* means to push away, to repel, to drive off. The construction is striking: they *push away the evil deed with the good deed* — that is, goodness is the instrument by which evil is removed.

The commentators note the grammatical specificity: the good deed is placed in the position of the *tool* (with the preposition *bi-*), and the evil deed in the position of the thing acted upon. The image is physical: evil arrives, and a good deed is used to shove it away.

This is one of the most quoted ethical principles in the Qur'an, and it recurs in several forms:

> *"And the good deed and the evil deed are not equal. Repel [evil] with that which is better; then behold, the one between whom and thee there is enmity shall be as if he were a loyal protecting friend. But none is granted it save those who are patient; and none is granted it save the possessor of a great portion."* (Qur'an 41:34–35)

> *"And those who restrain their anger and pardon people — and God loves the doers of good."* (Qur'an 3:134)

> *"Repel with that which is best. We know best what they attribute."* (Qur'an 23:96)

The commentators note the two conditions attached in 41:35 — patience and *a great portion* (*ḥaẓẓ ʿaẓīm*) — which they read as: this is the conduct of the spiritually advanced, and it is not achieved without struggle.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the principle its most concrete form:

> *"Fear God wherever you are, and follow an evil deed with a good one that will erase it, and treat people with good character."* (al-Tirmidhī 1987 — graded ḥasan)

And he described the *effect* of this conduct, which the Qur'an's phrase *as if he were a loyal protecting friend* also describes:

> *"Shall I not tell you of something by which God raises ranks and erases sins? Performing ablution thoroughly in difficult circumstances, taking many steps to the mosques, and waiting for the prayer after the prayer. That is the ribāṭ [holding the frontier]."* (Muslim 251)

The spiritual logic the commentators drew out deserves stating, because it is not merely sentimental. Repaying evil with good does two things at once: it removes the *escalation* (the enemy has nothing to react to), and it changes the *repayer* (he does not become what he is fighting). The alternative — repaying evil with evil — is a machine that converts both parties into the same thing.

**"And Spend From That Which We Have Provided Them"**

*Wa-mimmā razaqnāhum yunfiqūn*. The third mark is generosity, and the commentators note the phrasing: they spend *from what We provided them*.

The preposition matters, and the tradition has always noticed it. The spending is from *God's* provision, not from what they earned by their own power:

> *"And spend from that whereof He has made you trustees."* (Qur'an 57:7)

This is the Qur'an's standing cure for the possessiveness that makes charity difficult: the recognition that the giver is a steward, not an owner. The commentators note that the same recognition is what made Korah's sin possible in reverse — he said *I have only been given it on account of knowledge I possess* (v. 78), attributing the provision to himself.

The pairing of charity with patience and with repelling evil is not incidental. The three describe the complete moral life in the tradition's scheme: patience is the person's relationship to *hardship*, generosity is his relationship to *wealth*, and repelling evil with good is his relationship to *other people*.

The Qur'an pairs them in the description of the believers elsewhere:

> *"Those who spend in ease and hardship, and restrain their anger, and pardon people — and God loves the doers of good."* (Qur'an 3:134)

> *"And [they are] those who respond to their Lord and establish the prayer, and whose affair is [determined by] consultation among themselves, and who spend from what We have provided them, and who, when wrongdoing afflicts them, defend themselves."* (Qur'an 42:38–39)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Charity does not decrease wealth."* (Muslim 2588)

and:

> *"The upper hand is better than the lower hand; the upper hand is the giver and the lower hand is the taker."* (al-Bukhārī 1429; Muslim 1033)

**The Three Marks as a Self-Examination**

The verse gives the tradition one of its most-used checklists, and the commentators have always applied it as such.

*Am I patient?* Not merely resigned — *ṣabr* is active, not passive; it is the restraint of a person who could do otherwise and does not.

*Do I repay evil with good?* The test is concrete and immediate: the last time someone wronged me, what did I do with it? The tradition's standard is not the absence of a response but the presence of a *better* one.

*Do I spend?* The Qur'an's test of faith is repeatedly financial, because money is the thing human beings find hardest to release:

> *"You will never attain righteousness until you spend from what you love."* (Qur'an 3:92)

**Application: The Reward That Is Doubled**

The verse's promise is specific and the tradition has treated it as an encouragement rather than a curiosity.

What is being promised is that *costly* fidelity is recompensed at a higher rate than comfortable fidelity. The person who held his religion when it cost him his community, and who then accepted further truth when that cost him again, is not merely compensated — he is recompensed twice.

The logic is comprehensible. The reward is proportional to the difficulty borne for God's sake, and the difficulty of these people was double: they were faithful in two eras, under two sets of pressure, with two communities' worth of opposition.

For the modern reader, the application is direct for anyone whose fidelity is expensive — the convert who loses his family, the believer who loses a career opportunity, the person who keeps his practice in an environment where it is costly. The verse does not promise that the cost will be reimbursed in this life. It promises that the recompense is *doubled*, and that the measure of patience is *without reckoning*.

And the verse's structure supplies the condition, stated in the same breath as the promise: they were patient, they repelled evil with good, and they spent. The reward attaches to a *life*, not to a profession of faith.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:55

> **And when they hear idle talk, they turn away therefrom and say, "Unto us our deeds, and unto you your deeds. Peace be upon you! We do not seek out the ignorant."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Mark: Dignified Withdrawal**

*Wa-idhā samiʿū al-laghwa aʿraḍū ʿanhu wa-qālū lanā aʿmālunā wa-lakum aʿmālukum salāmun ʿalaykum lā nabtaghī al-jāhilīn*.

The verse completes the portrait begun at verse 54. The believers described there are patient, generous, and return good for evil. Here, a fourth quality is added: they know how to *leave*.

The commentators note that this is the hardest of the four, and that the Qur'an treats it as a mark of maturity rather than of weakness.

**"Idle Talk" — *Al-Laghw***

The word *laghw* denotes speech that is empty, purposeless, vain — talk that accomplishes nothing and leads nowhere. The lexicographers derive it from a root meaning to speak without effect, and the Qur'an uses it for several kinds of discourse:

*Vain or abusive speech:*

> *"And they who turn away from idle talk (al-laghw)."* (Qur'an 23:3)

> *"And when they pass by idle talk, they pass by with dignity."* (Qur'an 25:72)

*False oaths and careless speech:*

> *"God will not take you to task for the unintentional in your oaths, but He will take you to task for what your hearts have earned."* (Qur'an 2:225) — where the unintentional oath is described as *laghw*.

*Speech that denies or mocks the revelation:*

> *"And those who disbelieve say, 'Do not listen to this Qur'an, and speak idly (al-ghaw) during it, that you may overcome.'"* (Qur'an 41:26)

That last verse is the immediate context here. The Quraysh's strategy included *talking over the recitation* — making noise, mocking, drowning it out. The believers in this verse are those who refuse to be drawn into that.

The commentators note that *laghw* includes not onlymockery but everything that wastes a person's attention: gossip, pointless argument, obscenity, and — in the modern extension the tradition's scholars have drawn — any stream of words that occupies the mind without producing anything.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

and:

> *"Part of a person's good observance of Islam is his leaving alone what does not concern him."* (al-Tirmidhī 2317; Ibn Mājah 3976 — graded ḥasan)

**"They Turn Away Therefrom"**

*Aʿraḍū ʿanhu*. The verb *aʿraḍa* means to turn away, to give one's back, to move off — the same verb used throughout the Qur'an for the act of turning away from *God*, which is the gravest of sins.

The commentators note the deliberate reuse of the verb in a positive sense. *Iʿrāḍ* is blameworthy when its object is the truth; it is praiseworthy when its object is vanity. The moral quality of turning away depends entirely on what one is turning from.

This is a precise and useful principle. The tradition is full of commands to *not* turn away — from the revelation, from the prayer, from the poor, from a person in need. And it is equally full of commands to turn away — from mockery, from obscenity, from suspicion, from what does not concern you.

> *"O you who believe, avoid much suspicion; indeed some suspicion is sin. And do not spy, nor backbite one another."* (Qur'an 49:12)

> *"And when you see those who engage in vain discourse about Our signs, turn away from them until they engage in some other talk."* (Qur'an 6:68)

The verse before us describes people who have mastered the discrimination: their backs are turned to emptiness and their faces to what matters.

**"Unto Us Our Deeds, and Unto You Your Deeds"**

*Lanā aʿmālunā wa-lakum aʿmālukum*. The commentators note what this sentence accomplishes: it ends the dispute without conceding or escalating.

It is not an agreement with what was said. It is a statement that each party is accountable for his own conduct, and therefore that the argument is settled elsewhere and by someone else.

The same formula appears in the Qur'an as the Prophet's own instruction in dealing with those who will not be persuaded:

> *"Say: 'O disbelievers, I worship not what you worship, nor do you worship what I worship... Unto you your religion, and unto me my religion.'"* (Qur'an 109:1–6)

> *"And if they deny thee, say: 'Unto me my deeds, and unto you your deeds. You are innocent of what I do, and I am innocent of what you do.'"* (Qur'an 10:41)

The commentators are careful to note the limits of this formula. It is a statement of *disassociation* in a context where argument has failed, not a statement of relativism. The believers in this verse are not saying "you have your truth and I have mine." They are saying "each of us will answer for himself, and I will not be responsible for what you insist upon."

The tradition has been precise about this distinction, because the formula is frequently misquoted. Sūrah al-Kāfirūn was revealed in Makkah, at a moment when the Quraysh were proposing a compromise — that the Prophet ﷺ worship their gods for a year and they worship his God for a year. The sūrah's answer is a flat refusal, not a pluralist concession. The phrase *unto you your religion* is the *severance* of a negotiation, not its conclusion.

**"Peace Be Upon You!" — *Salāmun ʿAlaykum***

The commentators dwell on this phrase, because its use here is remarkable.

*Salām* is the greeting of the Muslims, and it is also one of the names of God (*al-Salām*, the Source of Peace). To say *salāmun ʿalaykum* to a person who has just been abusive is to invoke peace upon him — to wish him safety and soundness rather than harm.

The tradition views this as the highest form of the conduct commanded at verse 54 (*repel evil with good*), and it notes that the Prophet ﷺ legislated the greeting as a universal practice:

> *"You will not enter Paradise until you believe, and you will not believe until you love one another. Shall I tell you of something which, if you do it, you will love one another? Spread the greeting of peace among yourselves."* (Muslim 54)

And he taught that the greeting is a right:

> *"The rights of a Muslim over a Muslim are five: returning the greeting, visiting the sick, following the funeral, accepting the invitation, and responding to the sneezer."* (al-Bukhārī 1240; Muslim 2162)

The commentators note the specific grace of using it *here*, to people who are hostile. The believers do not curse, do not threaten, and do not even argue. They invoke peace and leave.

There is a ḥadīth that captures the same spirit from the Prophet's own life:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ was not obscene, nor did he seek to be obscene, nor did he shout in the markets, and he did not repay an evil deed with an evil deed; rather he pardoned and overlooked."* (al-Tirmidhī 2016; Aḥmad)

**"We Do Not Seek Out the Ignorant"**

*Lā nabtaghī al-jāhilīn*. The verb *nabtaghī* means to seek, to desire, to aim at. The object is *al-jāhilīn* — the ignorant, from *j-h-l*, which in the Qur'anic vocabulary means much more than lack of information.

The commentators are emphatic that *jahl* in the Qur'an is a *moral* category. Its opposite is not knowledge but *ḥilm* — forbearance, self-possession, the maturity that does not react.

The tradition's definition is well established:

> The Prophet ﷺ said to a man who had behaved badly toward him: *"Know that in me there is ḥilm [forbearance]."* (Reported in the ethical literature)

and the classic statement:

> *"The strong man is not the one who overcomes people by his strength, but the strong man is the one who controls himself while angry."* (al-Bukhārī 6114; Muslim 2609)

So the sentence means: we are not looking for encounters with people who will drag us into conduct beneath us. We do not seek them out. The believers are not claiming superiority over the mockers; they are stating a boundary about where they will stand.

The commentators note the practical wisdom in this, and it is considerable. Most of the damage in a religious life is not done by argument but by *association with people who make you worse*. The tradition's rules of companionship are built on exactly this principle:

> *"A person is upon the religion of his close friend, so let each of you look to whom he befriends."* (Abū Dāwūd 4833; al-Tirmidhī 2378 — graded ḥasan)

> *"The similitude of a good companion and a bad companion is that of the seller of musk and the blower of the bellows: the seller of musk will either give you some or you will buy from him, or you will notice a pleasant smell from him; and the blower of the bellows will either burn your clothes or you will notice a foul smell from him."* (al-Bukhārī 2101; Muslim 2628)

**The Historical Setting: The Report of Abū Jahl**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī transmit the occasion of this verse: certain People of the Book embraced Islam in the presence of Abū Jahl, and when they left the gathering he followed them, heaping abuse on them. They responded only with *peace be upon you*, and this verse was revealed commending them.

The commentators note the structure of the incident. The new converts had every provocation available to them — they had just declared a faith that made them targets, and they were being pursued and insulted by the most powerful man in the opposition. Their response was to invoke peace and withdraw.

That is the model the verse establishes: the believer does not *seek out* confrontation with the ignorant, and when confrontation arrives anyway, he exits it with his dignity and his character intact.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

Structurally, verses 52–55 complete the sūrah's answer to verses 47–51.

The argument there was that the Quraysh's rejection was *hawā* — caprice — rather than a shortage of evidence (v. 50), and that the Word had nevertheless reached them in full (v. 51).

Verses 52–55 demonstrate that the evidence *was* sufficient, by showing people who received it and believed. And they demonstrate what the believers look like: patient, generous, returning good for evil, and refusing to be drawn into vanity.

The contrast is the sūrah's method throughout: Pharaoh and Moses, Korah and the knowledgeable, the mockers and the recognizers.

**Application**

The verse is, for most readers, the most immediately applicable in the sūrah, because the situation it describes is the commonest one.

*Turn away from what is empty.* This is a discipline of attention, and the tradition has always treated attention as a religious matter. The things a person gives his ears and eyes to shape what he becomes.

*Disengage without escalating.* The formula *unto us our deeds, and unto you your deeds* is the Qur'an's model for ending an argument that cannot be won, and it preserves both truth and dignity: truth, because nothing was conceded; dignity, because nothing was thrown.

*Invoke peace on the hostile.* Not because they deserve it — the verse is not making a claim about their deserts — but because the person who invokes peace is not formed by resentment.

*And do not seek out the ignorant.* This is the hardest counsel, because engagement with the hostile is frequently pleasant: it is flattering, it produces a sense of purpose, and it is rewarded by every incentive of modern attention economies. The verse recommends the opposite, and the tradition's own formulation of why is the Prophetic saying:

> *"Whoever remains silent is saved."* (al-Tirmidhī 2501 — graded ḥasan)

The believers described in this verse are not silent because they have nothing to say. They are silent because what they have to say is more important than the argument on offer, and because they have somewhere better to be.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:56

> **Surely thou dost not guide whomsoever thou lovest, but God guides whomsoever He will. And He knows best those who are rightly guided.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Uncle**

*Innaka lā tahdī man aḥbabta wa-lākinna Allāha yahdī man yashāʾu wa-huwa aʿlamu bi-al-muhtadīn*.

The commentators are unanimous about the occasion of this verse, and the story is one of the most poignant in the entire Prophetic biography. It concerns Abū Ṭālib, the Prophet's uncle, the man who had raised him after the death of his grandfather ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, who had protected him through thirteen years of persecution, who had stood by him through the boycott of the Banū Hāshim, and who never embraced Islam.

**The Deathbed Scene**

Al-Bukhārī records the event in his Book of Tafsīr, in the section on this very sūrah, from al-Musayyab ibn Ḥazn:

> *When Abū Ṭālib was on his deathbed, the Messenger of God ﷺ went to him while Abū Jahl was sitting beside him. The Prophet ﷺ said, "O my uncle, say: 'There is no god but God' — a word with which I may argue for you before God." Abū Jahl and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Umayyah said, "O Abū Ṭālib, will you abandon the religion of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib?" They kept pressing him, and the last thing he said to them was that he was upon the religion of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, and he refused to say "there is no god but God." The Prophet ﷺ said, "I shall certainly seek forgiveness for you unless I am forbidden to do so." Then God revealed: "It is not for the Prophet and those who believe to ask forgiveness for the polytheists, even if they be kinsfolk, after it has become clear to them that they are the inhabitants of Hellfire" (9:113). And concerning Abū Ṭālib this verse was revealed: "Surely thou dost not guide whomsoever thou lovest...";* (al-Bukhārī 4772; and cf. Muslim 24, from Abū Hurayrah, with a longer account)

The Muslim version adds the detail of Abū Ṭālib's reply:

> *"Were it not that the Quraysh would blame me, saying that he did it only out of fear of death, I would have comforted your eyes with it."*

The commentators note what is in that sentence. Abū Ṭālib was not arguing; he was not denying; he was explaining that the social cost of conversion at the moment of death would be interpreted as panic, and he would not give his enemies that satisfaction. The reason he gave was a reason of *honor*, and it killed him.

**The Sunni and Shiʿi Readings**

The tradition records two positions on Abū Ṭālib's end, and the commentators note both.

*The Sunni position*, which rests on the ṣaḥīḥ narrations cited above, is that he died without professing the testimony of faith, and that the Prophet ﷺ was forbidden to seek forgiveness for him.

*The Shiʿi position* holds that Abū Ṭālib did in fact embrace the faith of his nephew, and that the reports of his refusal were corrupted by later partisan interest. Shiʿi sources transmit poetry attributed to Abū Ṭālib expressing faith, and they regard the man who protected the Prophet ﷺ for thirteen years and whose son was ʿAlī as necessarily a believer.

The present commentary follows the Sunni sources because the ṣaḥīḥ narrations are explicit, and because the verse's meaning — that guidance is not in any creature's power, including the Prophet's — is unaffected by the historical question. Indeed, the verse is *stronger* on the Sunni reading, which is presumably why al-Bukhārī placed the narration in his commentary on it.

**"Thou Dost Not Guide Whomsoever Thou Lovest"**

The verse's structure is a negation and an affirmation, and the commentators note the exactness of both.

*The negation*: *innaka lā tahdī man aḥbabta* — *you do not guide whom you love*. The verb *tahdī* is in the present tense, indefinite. It is not "you did not guide this man" but "you do not guide anyone." The scope is universal.

The commentators note the word *aḥbabta* — *you loved*. The verse does not say "whom you wish" or "whom you try to reach." It says *whom you love*. The strongest human desire, directed at the person dearest to him, cannot produce guidance.

This is the humbling core of the verse. If the Prophet ﷺ — the best of creation, the man whose every prayer was heard, the man for whose sake God would have granted almost anything — could not guide the uncle who raised him, then no one can guide anyone.

*The affirmation*: *wa-lākinna Allāha yahdī man yashāʾ* — *but God guides whom He wills*. The contrast is absolute: the ability belongs to God, and to no one else in any sense that matters.

**The Theological Framework: Two Kinds of Guidance Again**

As at verse 50, the commentators distinguish the two senses of *hudā*, and the distinction is what makes this verse coherent with the rest of the Qur'an.

*The guidance of showing the way* (*hudā al-dalālah*): this the Prophet ﷺ possessed and exercised perfectly. He conveyed the message with total clarity, and the Qur'an testifies that he did:

> *"And We sent down unto thee the Remembrance, that thou mayest make clear to mankind that which was sent down unto them."* (Qur'an 16:44)

> *"O Messenger, convey that which has been sent down unto thee from thy Lord..."* (Qur'an 5:67)

*The guidance of enabling the heart* (*hudā al-tawfīq*): this belongs to God alone, and this is what the verse negates of the Prophet ﷺ.

The distinction resolves the apparent tension with verses that describe the Prophet ﷺ as a guide:

> *"And truly thou dost guide unto a straight path."* (Qur'an 42:52)

The commentators read that verse in the first sense — he guides by showing, calling, and teaching — and this verse in the second sense — he cannot open a heart.

**The Doctrine of Divine Will and Human Responsibility**

The clause *God guides whom He wills* raised the central question of Islamic theology, and the tradition's answers were developed over centuries with great care.

*The Jabriyyah* held that the human being is compelled, and that his acts are God's acts.

*The Qadariyyah and Muʿtazilah* held that the human being creates his own acts, and that God cannot will what is evil — which required them to say that God does not will disbelief, and therefore that *whom He wills* must be interpreted as *whom He knows will believe*.

*The People of Ḥadīth and the Ashʿariyyah* held the middle position that became normative: God wills everything that occurs, good and evil, in the sense that nothing occurs outside His will; the human being nonetheless *acquires* (*kasb*) his acts and is genuinely responsible for them, because the act is his by intention and choice even though its creation belongs to God.

The position is stated with precision in the creedal literature:

> *"God wills all that exists; nothing occurs in His dominion that He does not will. But His will is not His pleasure, and His decree is not His command. He may will what He does not love, and love what He does not decree."*

The proof-texts for the two sides are both abundant, and the tradition refused to sacrifice either:

> *"And if thy Lord had willed, whoever is on the earth would have believed, all of them together. Would you then compel people until they are believers?"* (Qur'an 10:99)

> *"And We shall turn their hearts and their eyes away, even as they did not believe at first."* (Qur'an 6:110)

> *"Whosoever wills, let him believe; and whosoever wills, let him disbelieve."* (Qur'an 18:29)

The last verse, with its explicit attribution of choice to the human being, is the tradition's standing evidence against any reading of predestination that would make responsibility an illusion.

The practical rule the scholars took from this: the believer affirms both, refuses to speculate beyond what is revealed, and conducts himself *as if* everything depended on his own choice — because it does, in the only sense he can act on.

**"And He Knows Best Those Who Are Rightly Guided"**

*Wa-huwa aʿlamu bi-al-muhtadīn*. The closing clause is a statement about God's knowledge, and the commentators read it as the resolution of the verse's difficulty.

Why does God guide one person and not another? The verse does not say that the difference is arbitrary. It says that *He knows best* who is rightly guided — that is, who will respond to guidance, whose heart is oriented toward it, who is in earnest.

Al-Rāzī's formulation: God's guidance is given in accordance with His knowledge of who will accept it, and that knowledge is not a cause but a description. He does not refuse guidance to someone who would have accepted it; He knows that this person's refusal is real and is his own.

The tradition's corresponding pastoral principle is that the door is never closed in this life. The verse describes Abū Ṭālib's end, not his situation at twenty, forty, or sixty.

**The Consolation in the Verse**

The commentators have always read this verse as consolation before anything else, and it is easy to see why.

The Prophet ﷺ had loved his uncle and had failed to convert him. He had watched the man who protected him die outside the faith. The verse that came down did not say "you failed"; it said *you do not guide* — the inability is structural, not personal. The Prophet ﷺ had done everything a human being can do, and the outcome was never in his hands.

The tradition has applied this to every person who has prayed for someone's guidance and not seen it. The Qur'an has a whole gallery of such people:

Adam and his son:

> *"And recite to them the story of the two sons of Adam in truth..."* (Qur'an 5:27)

Noah and his son:

> *"And Noah called out to his Lord and said, 'My Lord, truly my son is of my family...'"* (Qur'an 11:45–46)

Abraham and his father:

> *"And Abraham's asking forgiveness for his father was only because of a promise he had made to him; but when it became clear to him that he was an enemy to God, he dissociated himself from him."* (Qur'an 9:114)

Lot and his wife:

> *"God sets forth the parable of those who disbelieve: the wife of Noah and the wife of Lot..."* (Qur'an 66:10)

The gallery is the Qur'an's way of saying to every believer: this is not your failure, and it is not new.

**The Report of the Shallow Fire**

The tradition transmits a narration that both confirms Abū Ṭālib's state and discloses the Prophet's own love for him, and it is recorded in the ṣaḥīḥ:

> *Al-ʿAbbās said, "O Messenger of God, have you benefited your uncle at all? For he used to protect you and become angry on your behalf." He said, "Yes. He is in a shallow part of the Fire; and were it not for me, he would be in the lowest depths of it."* (al-Bukhārī 3883; Muslim 209)

The commentators note two things. First, the ḥadīth confirms the Sunni position on his fate. Second, it shows that the Prophet's intercession was granted to the full extent permitted — a *lessening*, even where salvation was not possible. That lessening is the measure of a nephew's love, and the verse is the measure of its limit.

**Application**

For anyone who has loved someone into a faith they would not accept, this verse is the specific consolation. Three things in it are worth carrying.

*The outcome was never yours to produce.* You were responsible for the conveying, not for the opening. The tradition's rule is:

> *"Truly you do not guide whom you love, but God guides whom He wills."*

*Love remains love, and its limits are not its failure.* The Prophet ﷺ did not love his uncle less after this verse. He sought a mitigation for him and was granted one.

*And the door stays open until the last breath.* Abū Ṭālib's refusal was at the very end, and it was his own. No one reading this verse can know what another person's end will be.

The sūrah, which began with a mother placing her child in a river and trusting God with the outcome, now states the same principle at the other end of a life: do everything, love completely, and leave the result to the One who knows best those who are rightly guided.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:57

> **And they say, "If we follow guidance along with thee, we shall be snatched away from our land." Have We not established for them a secure Sanctuary, to which the fruits of all things are brought, as a provision from Our Presence? But most of them know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Honest Objection, Finally Stated**

*Wa-qālū in nattabiʿi al-hudā maʿaka nutakhaṭṭaf min arḍinā*.

After the evasions of verses 48 and 50, the Quraysh finally state a real reason. The commentators note the shift: this is not a demand for a different messenger or a claim about sorcery. It is a statement of political and economic fear.

If we follow this guidance with you, we will be *snatched away from our land* — *nutakhaṭṭaf*, a passive verb meaning to be snatched, seized, plucked away suddenly. The word is used for a bird of prey taking its catch.

The commentators explain the fear precisely. Makkah's position rested on its status as a pilgrimage center. The Kaʿbah drew the tribes of Arabia every year, and that traffic was the city's economy and the Quraysh's prestige. The Quraysh reasoned — correctly, as it turned out — that a religion abolishing the idols would destroy the pilgrimage, and that the tribes would then have no reason to leave Makkah alone.

The Qur'an records the same fear in other sūrahs:

> *"And they said, 'If we follow the guidance with thee, we shall be snatched away from our land.'"* (Qur'an 28:57)

> *"And they were amazed that a warner came to them from among themselves, and the disbelievers said, 'This is a sorcerer, a liar! Has he made the gods [into] one God? Truly this is an amazing thing.' And the notables among them went forth [saying], 'Go and be steadfast to your gods; truly this is a thing desired.'"* (Qur'an 38:4–6)

Note the closing phrase of that passage: *this is a thing desired* — the notables knew exactly what was at stake, and they named it.

**What Makes This Objection Different**

The commentators distinguish this objection from the earlier ones, and the distinction is instructive.

Verses 48 and 50 recorded objections that were *caprice* — the questioner had no intention of being satisfied. This verse records a genuine calculation. The Quraysh were not wrong about the economics: monotheism would disrupt the pilgrimage trade, and the disruption would be severe. Their error was not in the analysis of consequences but in the weighing.

The tradition has therefore treated this verse as the Qur'an's treatment of *the cost of faith* — and specifically of the fear that obedience to God will ruin one's life.

The Qur'an addresses the same fear in its own community:

> *"And among people is he who says, 'We believe in God,' but when he is harmed in God's cause, he takes the harm of people as though it were the punishment of God."* (Qur'an 29:10)

> *"And among people is he who worships God on the edge: if good befalls him, he is content with it; but if a trial befalls him, he turns on his face. He has lost this world and the Hereafter; that is the manifest loss."* (Qur'an 22:11)

That second verse is the definitive portrait of the person who believes conditionally — and the commentators have always read it as the answer to the fear in this one.

**The Answer: "Have We Not Established for Them a Secure Sanctuary?"**

*A-wa-lam numakkin lahum ḥaraman āminan*. The response is a question that redirects the fear to its true object.

The argument, as the commentators lay it out, runs as follows. You fear that following Me will cost you your security and your land. But who *gave* you your security and your land?

The Qur'an uses the emphatic interrogative *a-wa-lam* — *have We not?* — which presumes an affirmative answer the hearers cannot deny.

**The Ḥaram: Makkah's Sacred Enclosure**

The word *ḥaram* means both *sanctuary* and *forbidden* — a place set apart, in which certain acts are prohibited. Makkah had been a sanctuary since the time of Abraham, and the Arabs observed its inviolability with a rigor remarkable in a tribal society where blood feuds could last generations.

The Qur'an describes the sanctuary's privileges:

> *"And they say, 'If we follow the guidance with thee, we shall be snatched away from our land.' Have We not established for them a secure sanctuary...?"* (Qur'an 28:57)

> *"Have they not seen that We have made [Makkah] a secure sanctuary, while people are being snatched away all around them?"* (Qur'an 29:67)

> *"Who has made it a secure sanctuary, and He feeds them against hunger and gives them security against fear."* (Qur'an 106:4)

> *"God has made the Kaʿbah, the Sacred House, a standing [place of devotion] for mankind, and the sacred months, and the sacrificial animals and the garlands — that you may know that God knows whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth."* (Qur'an 5:97)

The commentators note the historical reality behind these verses. Arabia outside the ḥaram was in a state of near-permanent tribal warfare — the *ayyām al-ʿArab*, the "days of the Arabs," were a century of remembered battles. Inside the ḥaram, a man could not be attacked even by his brother's killer. The security was real, was recognized by everyone, and was the precondition of everything the Quraysh had.

The tradition has also noted the peculiar nature of the Qur'an's argument here: it is arguing from a *blessing the Quraysh themselves acknowledged*. They attributed the sanctuary's security to their gods and to their own custodianship. The Qur'an accepts the fact and disputes the attribution.

**"To Which the Fruits of All Things Are Brought"**

*Yujbā ilayhi thamarātu kulli shayʾin*. The verb *yujbā* means to be collected, gathered, brought in — the word used for the collection of taxes and of harvests.

The commentators explain the historical situation. Makkah is a barren valley without agriculture, without water, and with no natural resources. And yet its markets were supplied with the produce of the surrounding regions, because the sanctuary's security made it the one place where trade could be conducted without risk.

The commentators note the rhetorical force. You fear losing your provision if you obey God — but your provision arrives in a valley that produces nothing, and it arrives because of a security that you did not create and cannot maintain. The argument is *a fortiori*: the One who brings fruit into a barren valley from every direction can certainly preserve you if you obey Him.

The Qur'an makes the same argument in the prayer of Abraham, which the tradition has always read as the explanation of Makkah's strange prosperity:

> *"Our Lord, I have settled some of my offspring in a valley without cultivation, by Thy Sacred House, our Lord, that they may establish the prayer. So make the hearts of people incline toward them, and provide them with fruits, that haply they may be grateful."* (Qur'an 14:37)

The commentators note the correspondence: Abraham asked for *hearts to incline* and for *fruits*, and Makkah received both, in a place where neither was natural.

**"As a Provision From Our Presence"**

*Rizqan min ladunnā*. The phrase *min ladunnā* means *from Our Presence, from Ourselves* — direct, personal bestowal, without intermediary.

The commentators stress this phrase because it carries the verse's argument. The provision is not a product of the Quraysh's trading acumen, though they traded. It is not a product of the idols, though they credited them. It comes directly from God, and it comes to a place that has no natural claim on it.

The theological point is the one the Qur'an makes constantly: provision (*rizq*) is God's, and no human arrangement is its ultimate source.

> *"And there is no creature on earth but that its provision is upon God."* (Qur'an 11:6)

> *"Truly God is the Provider, the Possessor of Strength, the Firm."* (Qur'an 51:58)

> *"And in the heaven is your provision, and that which you are promised."* (Qur'an 51:22)

The last verse is often cited by the commentators here, because it locates provision in the *decree* rather than in the *means*.

**"But Most of Them Know Not"**

*Wa-lākinna aktharahum lā yaʿlamūn*. As at verse 13, the closing clause identifies the problem as perception.

The Quraysh could see the sanctuary, the trade, and the security. What they could not see was the Giver. And the commentators note the consequence: a person who does not know the source of his provision will fear its loss in the wrong way. He will protect the *means* — the trade, the treaties, the idols, the alliances — and treat obedience to God as a risk to them.

This is precisely inverted. The means are not the source. The One who brought fruit into a barren valley for the sake of a prayer uttered by Abraham will not withdraw His provision because you obeyed Him.

The tradition's formulation of the practical conclusion is the Prophetic statement:

> *"Know that if the whole nation were to gather together to benefit you with something, they would benefit you only with something that God had already written for you; and if they were to gather together to harm you with something, they would harm you only with something that God had already written against you. The pens have been lifted and the pages have dried."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad)

**The Spiritual Reading**

The commentators — and especially the spiritual tradition, following Ibn ʿAjībah — have applied this verse inwardly, and their application is among the most useful in the sūrah.

On this reading, the *land* from which a person fears to be snatched is whatever he is settled in: a habit, a livelihood, a reputation, a relationship, a position, a comfort. And the objection takes the form: *if I obey God here, I will lose this.*

The verse's answer is the same in every case: who gave you this? And do you think the Giver will impoverish you for obeying Him?

The tradition has an enormous body of testimony on this point, but its scriptural basis is a single promise:

> *"And whosoever is reverent toward God, He will make for him a way out, and will provide for him from whence he does not reckon. And whosoever trusts in God, He is sufficient for him."* (Qur'an 65:2–3)

> *"And whoever emigrates in the way of God will find on the earth many refuges and abundance."* (Qur'an 4:100)

**Application: The Cost-of-Discipleship Question**

This verse will be used by every generation of believers, because the question it answers is permanent.

The form is always the same: *if I do this — wear this, refuse this business, speak this truth, leave this relationship, give this money — I will lose what I have.*

The Qur'an's answer has three parts, and all three are worth carrying.

*Who gave you what you have?* The question relocates the fear. Provision is not self-generated, and the One who gave can sustain.

*Is the thing you fear losing the source, or is it a means?* The Quraysh had confused the ḥaram's security with the idols they credited for it. Modern equivalents are easy to identify: the job, the network, the reputation, the platform. All are means. None is the source.

*And what would the loss actually be?* The Qur'an's promise is not that obedience is never costly — the sūrah's whole narrative says otherwise. Moses lost Egypt. The Prophet ﷺ lost Makkah. The promise is that what obedience costs is never as much as it appears, and that what it purchases is permanent.

The sūrah's next verse turns to the historical demonstration of that final point.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:58

> **And how many a town have We destroyed who exulted in their life of ease. There are their dwellings, uninhabited after them save for a few. And We, truly, are the Inheritor.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Historical Answer to the Fear of Loss**

*Wa-kam ahlaknā min qaryatin baṭirat maʿīshatahā fa-tilka masākinuhum lam tuskan min baʿdihim illā qalīlan wa-kunnā naḥnu al-wārithīn*.

The verse continues directly from verse 57. The Quraysh feared losing their city. The Qur'an's answer is to point at other cities that *were* lost — and to name the reason why.

The commentators note the argument's structure: the thing you fear happened to others, and not because they obeyed God. It happened because they did the opposite.

**"How Many a Town Have We Destroyed"**

*Wa-kam ahlaknā min qaryatin*. The construction *kam* — *how many!* — is exclamatory and is one of the Qur'an's standard openings for this lesson. It appears repeatedly:

> *"And how many a town have We destroyed that exulted in its living! There are their dwellings, uninhabited after them, save a little; and We are the Inheritor."* (Qur'an 28:58)

> *"How many a town have We destroyed! Our might came upon it by night or while they were sleeping at noon."* (Qur'an 7:4)

> *"And how many a town that was wrongdoing did We shatter, and raised up after it another people."* (Qur'an 21:11)

> *"And how many a town more mighty in strength than thy town which has driven thee out have We destroyed; and they had no helper."* (Qur'an 47:13)

The commentators note that these verses are not threats addressed to the Quraysh so much as an invitation to *look around*. The ruins were visible. Every trade caravan from Makkah to Syria passed the remains of the Thamūd at al-Ḥijr, the ruins of the cities of Lot near the Dead Sea, and the traces of ʿĀd in the south.

The Prophet ﷺ is reported to have instructed his companions, when passing the ruins of Thamūd, not to enter them except weeping, and to hurry through:

> *"Do not enter upon these people who were punished except while weeping; and if you do not weep, then do not enter upon them, lest there befall you what befell them."* (al-Bukhārī 4419; Muslim 2980)

**"Who Exulted in Their Life of Ease"**

*Baṭirat maʿīshatahā*. The verb *baṭira* is from *b-ṭ-r*, and the lexicographers are precise about it: it means to be insolent *because of* ease — to be corrupted by comfort. It is not merely to enjoy abundance; it is to enjoy it in a way that forgets the Giver.

The commentators connect this verb to the Qur'an's most fundamental moral psychology. The disaster is not prosperity; the disaster is prosperity *unaccompanied by gratitude*. The Qur'an states the principle:

> *"And had God expanded provision for His servants, they would surely have transgressed in the earth; but He sends down in due measure what He wills."* (Qur'an 42:27)

> *"Nay, truly man transgresses, in that he deems himself self-sufficient."* (Qur'an 96:6–7)

That second verse is the definition. *Istighnāʾ* — the feeling of not needing — is the mechanism by which abundance destroys.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the people of these towns did not know they ought to have been grateful. That is the precise failure: not atheism but *inattention*. They enjoyed the provision without tracing it to its source, and in the absence of gratitude the provision became a poison.

The tradition's corresponding instruction is the Prophetic practice of *shukr* — and the Prophet ﷺ himself, who was promised Paradise, stood in prayer until his feet swelled, and when asked why he did this, said:

> *"Should I not be a grateful servant?"* (al-Bukhārī 1130; Muslim 2819)

**"There Are Their Dwellings, Uninhabited After Them"**

*Fa-tilka masākinuhum lam tuskan min baʿdihim*. The commentators note the demonstrative: *those are their dwellings* — as though pointing at them. The ruins are being indicated in the present tense, to a people who could visit them.

The clause *lam tuskan* — *not inhabited* — is emphatic. Whatever these cities were, they are now places where no one lives. The houses remain and the people are gone.

The Qur'an returns to this image with haunting frequency:

> *"And how many generations before them have We destroyed! Do you perceive a single one of them, or hear from them a sound?"* (Qur'an 19:98)

> *"Have they not traveled in the land and seen how was the end of those before them? They were greater than them in power, and they tilled the earth and inhabited it more than these have inhabited it."* (Qur'an 30:9)

The second verse is the sharper one, because it makes the comparison the Quraysh did not want: those people were *stronger* than you, and they are gone.

**"Save for a Few"**

*Illā qalīlan*. The commentators — al-Qurṭubī especially — note the grammatical point: *qalīlan* here is not referring to a few *people* remaining. It refers to a *little* inhabitation — that is, the ruins are used occasionally, by travelers passing through, as temporary shelter.

The distinction matters for the argument. The verse is not saying a remnant survived; it is saying the place is functionally dead. Travelers camp in the shells of houses. That is all.

The image is one of the Qur'an's most melancholy, and the tradition has always read it as a memento mori for civilizations: buildings outliving builders, and the buildings being used by strangers for a night.

**"And We, Truly, Are the Inheritor"**

*Wa-kunnā naḥnu al-wārithīn*. The verse's closing clause is its theological weight, and the commentators unpack it carefully.

*Al-wārith* — the Inheritor — is one of the divine names. The word means the one who receives what remains when everyone else is gone. Human inheritance is a transfer from the dead to the living within a chain of owners. God's inheritance is different in kind: He is the one who remains when all chains end.

The Qur'an uses the name:

> *"And truly it is We Who give life and cause death, and We are the Inheritor."* (Qur'an 15:23)

> *"And how many a garden and spring did they leave behind, and sown fields and noble stations, and comforts wherein they had rejoiced! Thus it was; and We made another people inherit them."* (Qur'an 44:25–28)

> *"Truly We shall inherit the earth and all who are upon it, and unto Us they shall be returned."* (Qur'an 19:40)

The last verse states the doctrine with full clarity: God inherits everything, because everything returns to Him.

The commentators draw out three implications.

*Nothing is ultimately owned.* Every human holding is a temporary stewardship. The tradition's legal and ethical system is built on this: property is real and protected, but it is held in trust, and the trusts have conditions.

*The apparent permanence of a civilization is an illusion.* The verse is addressed to a people living in a stone city, surrounded by the stone ruins of other people's cities, who had nevertheless concluded that their situation was permanent.

*And the return is to the true Owner.* Every person meets the same end: he leaves everything and returns to God. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The son of Adam says, 'My wealth, my wealth!' But is there anything of your wealth except what you ate and used up, or wore and wore out, or gave in charity and sent ahead?"* (Muslim 2958)

and:

> *"What has been sent ahead goes with a person; what he leaves behind is for others."* (Reported in the ethical literature; the meaning is established)

**The Verse's Argument Against the Fear of Loss**

Put verses 57 and 58 together, and the sūrah's argument is complete, and it is worth stating because it is one of the Qur'an's most practically powerful sequences.

*The fear*: obedience will cost us our city.
*The first answer*: who gave you the city? (v. 57)
*The second answer*: other cities were lost — and they were lost *through disobedience*, not through obedience (v. 58).
*The implied conclusion*: the thing you are trying to protect is the very thing your strategy will destroy.

The commentators note that the argument was vindicated within a generation. Makkah was conquered, the idols were removed, the pilgrimage was restored on monotheistic terms, and the city's prosperity increased rather than diminished. The Quraysh's fear was reasonable and its premise was false.

**Application**

Three conclusions carry into ordinary life.

*Comfort is a test, not a certificate.* The verse's diagnostic word — *baṭirat* — describes a condition that is available in every economic stratum. The tradition's cure is gratitude expressed as use: the blessing employed in obedience.

*Look at ruins.* The Qur'an's method here is empirical and can be copied. Every generation leaves the wreckage of its confident institutions. The discipline of *iʿtibār* — taking warning from comparable cases — is a habit of mind rather than a piece of information.

*And hold property loosely.* We are the Inheritor is not a threat to the prosperous; it is a description of reality that, if believed, liberates a person from the terror of loss. The one who knows that everything returns to God can afford to give, to risk, and to obey.

The sūrah's next verse supplies the justice principle that governs all of this: God does not destroy a town until He has sent it a messenger.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:59

> **And thy Lord never destroys towns until He sends a messenger to their mother city to recite unto them Our signs. And We never destroy towns, save when their people are wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Justice Principle Stated Explicitly**

*Wa-mā kāna rabbuka muhlika al-qurā ḥattā yabʿatha fī ummihā rasūlan yatlū ʿalayhim āyātinā wa-mā kunnā muhlikī al-qurā illā wa-ahluhā ẓālimūn*.

This is one of the most important verses in the Qur'an on the justice of God, and the commentators treat it as a controlling principle that qualifies every narrative of destruction in the Book.

Two negations are stated: God never destroys until He has sent a messenger to their mother city; and He never destroys save when the people are wrongdoers. The first establishes that warning precedes punishment. The second establishes that punishment is never arbitrary.

**"Until He Sends a Messenger to Their Mother City"**

*Ḥattā yabʿatha fī ummihā rasūlan*. The word *umm* means mother, and by extension the origin, the source, the chief place. *Umm al-qurā* — *the Mother of Towns* — is the title the Qur'an gives to Makkah:

> *"And thus have We revealed unto thee an Arabic Qur'an, that thou mayest warn the Mother of Towns and those around it."* (Qur'an 42:7)

> *"That thou mayest warn a people whose fathers were never warned, and so they are heedless."* (Qur'an 36:6)

The commentators note that the majority of Muslim scholars understand *mother city* here as Makkah specifically — a reading supported by the Qur'an's own use of the title and by the fact that the verse was revealed in Makkah to a Makkan messenger.

Others, including some transmitted from Mujāhid and al-Ṭabarī, read the phrase generically: the *mother city* is the principal city of any region, and the verse states a universal rule that God sends messengers to the centers from which the message spreads.

Both readings converge on the same principle, and the commentators note that the generic reading is the one with broader legal and theological reach: God sends His messengers to the *centers*, because from a center the message reaches the peripheries. Al-Qurṭubī observes that this is itself an argument for the wisdom of the sending — that revelation is given at the hubs of human communication rather than in inaccessible places.

**"To Recite Unto Them Our Signs"**

*Yatlū ʿalayhim āyātinā*. The messenger's function is specified: to *recite* the signs — not merely to announce a message in summary, but to deliver the revelation in its recited form.

This is the third time the sūrah has specified the function in this verb (vv. 3, 45, 59), and the commentators note the consistency. The destruction is preceded not by a warning but by a *recitation* — a scripture the people can hear, preserve, and consult.

The implication is that the warning must be *durable and public*. A dream, a feeling, or a private intuition does not establish the argument. The Qur'an requires a recited, preserved scripture:

> *"And We sent not a messenger save in the tongue of his people, that he might make [the message] clear to them."* (Qur'an 14:4)

The last clause of that verse is the key: the purpose of sending in the people's own language is *clarity* — so that no one can say he did not understand.

**"And We Never Destroy Towns, Save When Their People Are Wrongdoers"**

*Wa-mā kunnā muhlikī al-qurā illā wa-ahluhā ẓālimūn*. The second negation is equally emphatic and equally important.

The commentators note the word order: *illā wa-ahluhā ẓālimūn* — *except while their people are wrongdoers*. The construction (*illā* with a circumstantial clause) indicates that the wrongdoing is a *concomitant condition*: at the moment of destruction, the people are in a state of wrongdoing.

This establishes two things at once.

*Punishment is not arbitrary.* It is not a display of power and it is not a matter of divine mood. It corresponds to a real moral condition in the people destroyed.

*And the wrongdoing is real and knowable.* *Ẓulm* here is comprehensive — it includes idolatry (the greatest wrong, per 31:13), oppression of others, and the corruption of the social order. The sūrah has already given the full anatomy of it at verse 4.

**The Parallel Verses**

The commentators set this verse beside its companions, because the doctrine is stated repeatedly and the repetition is itself part of the claim:

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

> *"And thy Lord never destroys towns unjustly while their people are doing right."* (Qur'an 11:117)

> *"And there is no community but that a warner has passed among it."* (Qur'an 35:24)

The second of these is especially important, because it adds the word *bi-ẓulm* — *unjustly* — making explicit that God's destruction of a town is never unjust, since it follows warning and corresponds to wrongdoing.

**The Theological Stakes: The Doctrine of *Ahl al-Fatrah***

This verse is one of the primary sources for the Islamic position on those who lived in an interval between messengers (*ahl al-fatrah*) and, by extension, on everyone who has never received the message.

The commentators and the scholars of creed derived from it the rule that obligation follows the arrival of the message. The tradition's formulation:

*No one is accountable for a revelation that never reached him.* Al-Ghazālī stated the position with characteristic clarity in his discussion of those who never heard of Islam, distinguishing between those who heard the name without the substance and those who received the substance with its evidence — and holding that the former are not held to what they could not have known.

*The scholars' positions on the fate of such people*, as summarized at verse 47, vary: the Ashʿarī majority holding that they are not punished; the Māturīdī position that reason establishes some obligation; and Ibn Taymiyyah's position that they will be tested on the Day of Resurrection. All agree on the negative point established by this verse: God does not punish before sending.

*And the practical consequence for Muslims* is that no one may declare a specific individual damned. The tradition's rule is absolute on this point, and the scholars have always cited the hadith:

> *A man said, "By God, God will not forgive so-and-so." And God said: "Who is he who swears by Me that I will not forgive so-and-so? I have forgiven him and nullified your deed."* (Muslim 2621)

**The Historical Argument: Makkah's Case**

Returning to the sūrah's immediate context: this verse is the final move in the argument with the Quraysh, and the commentators note that it closes the door they had been trying to keep open.

The Quraysh's position, as stated at verse 47 in hypothetical form, was that they had received no scripture and no warner. The sūrah's answer has been: you have one now (vv. 44–46), he was sent precisely so you would have no excuse (v. 47), and he has recited the signs to your mother city (v. 59).

The warning is therefore complete and the argument established. The commentators note that this is why the Qur'an can speak of the consequences of Makkah's rejection with such certainty — the condition has been met.

What makes the verse *merciful* rather than merely ominous is that it states the condition before the outcome. The Quraysh were being told, in effect: this is the rule, and you are now inside it. Nothing has been decided that you cannot change.

**The Relationship Between the Two Conditions**

The commentators note a subtlety in how the verse's two halves work together, and it resolves a question many readers raise.

If God sends a messenger and the people believe, no destruction occurs. If He sends one and they reject, destruction may follow. So the sending is not a sufficient condition for destruction; it is a *necessary precondition* for it.

The second clause supplies the sufficient condition: they are *wrongdoers*. And the wrongdoing in question, in the Qur'an's usage here, is not ordinary sinfulness — every community sins — but the settled, public, structural wrongdoing that follows the rejection of a delivered message.

The tradition's reading is therefore: God does not destroy a people for sin as such; He destroys them for sin *after the message has come and been refused*, which is a different and more serious thing. The Qur'an's destroyed peoples were not merely immoral; they were immoral in the full light of a delivered warning.

**Application**

The verse's main practical content is the discipline it imposes on how believers think about judgment — God's and their own.

*About God's judgment:* the rule is that no one is held to what never reached him. This is a source of enormous comfort for anyone troubled by the question of the unevangelized, and it is a standing refutation of the idea that God punishes on technicalities.

*About our judgments of others:* the rule is that we do not know what has reached a person, in what form, or with what clarity. The tradition's consequent rule — do not declare anyone's destination — follows directly.

*About our own position:* the rule cuts the other way. If the message *has* reached you, then the condition is met and the accountability is real. The verse is not primarily a consolation for the person who has heard; it is a warning to him. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"By Him in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, no one of this community hears of me — whether Jew or Christian — and then dies without believing in what I was sent with, but that he will be among the inhabitants of the Fire."* (Muslim 153)

The condition in that ḥadīth — *hears of me* — is the verse's condition, stated in the form of a warning rather than a rule.

And there is a final, structural point that the commentators drew for communities rather than individuals. God sends to the *mother city* — to the centers. The implication they drew is that the health of a civilization is determined at its centers of influence: its capital, its media, its schools, its markets of ideas. That is where the message must go, and that is where its rejection is most consequential.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:60

> **And whatsoever you have been given, it is an enjoyment of the life of this world, and its ornament. But that which lies with God is better and more lasting. Will you not understand?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Thesis on Wealth, Stated in One Sentence**

*Wa-mā ūtītum min shayʾin fa-matāʿu al-ḥayāti al-dunyā wa-zīnatuhā wa-mā ʿinda Allāhi khayrun wa-abqā a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*.

This verse is the theological premise of the Korah passage that follows (vv. 76–82), and the commentators note that the sūrah places it here deliberately — as a statement of principle before the narrative that illustrates it.

**"Whatsoever You Have Been Given"**

*Wa-mā ūtītum min shayʾin*. The construction is maximally general: *whatever thing you have been given*. No category is excluded — wealth, children, health, beauty, knowledge, position, talent, security, reputation.

The verb is passive: *ūtītum* — *you have been given*. The commentators note the significance. The Qur'an does not say "whatever you have acquired" or "whatever you own." It says *you have been given*, which places the recipient in the position of one who received.

This is the foundation of the Islamic theology of provision that was discussed at verse 57 and that the tradition states flatly:

> *"And there is no creature on earth but that its provision is upon God."* (Qur'an 11:6)

> *"Say: 'Truly the bounty is in the hand of God; He gives it to whom He wills, and God is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing.'"* (Qur'an 3:73)

**"It Is an Enjoyment of the Life of This World, and Its Ornament"**

*Fa-matāʿu al-ḥayāti al-dunyā wa-zīnatuhā*. Two nouns, and the commentators distinguish them.

*Matāʿ* — enjoyment, use, benefit — from a root meaning to profit from something for a time. The word carries an inherent temporariness: *matāʿ* is what you get *use out of*, not what you keep. The Qur'an uses it precisely:

> *"Say: 'The enjoyment of this world is little, and the Hereafter is better for him who is reverent.'"* (Qur'an 4:77)

> *"And the life of this world is naught but enjoyment of delusion."* (Qur'an 3:185)

*Zīnah* — ornament, adornment, decoration. The word implies something *added to* a thing rather than belonging to its substance. Ornament beautifies; it does not constitute. The Qur'an:

> *"Wealth and children are an ornament (zīnah) of the life of this world; but the enduring good deeds are better with thy Lord in reward and better in hope."* (Qur'an 18:46)

That verse is the exact commentary on this one, and the commentators cite it constantly. Wealth is *zīnah* — an adornment, an addition, something that makes the passing scene pleasant. It is not the substance of a person and it is not the purpose of a life.

The two nouns together cover the two ways people go wrong with what they are given: by *using* it as though the use were permanent (*matāʿ*), and by *displaying* it as though the display were the point (*zīnah*).

**"But That Which Lies With God Is Better and More Lasting"**

*Wa-mā ʿinda Allāhi khayrun wa-abqā*. Two comparatives, and the commentators note that they answer the two nouns that preceded.

*Khayr* — *better*. Answering *zīnah*: what is with God is more beautiful and more valuable than anything the world can decorate a life with.

*Abqā* — *more lasting*, from *b-q-y*, to remain. Answering *matāʿ*: what is with God is permanent where worldly enjoyment is temporary.

The comparison is therefore exact and complete. The world's goods lose on both counts: they are inferior in quality and finite in duration. The tradition has always noted that the Qur'an does not say the world's goods are *evil*. It says they are *lesser* and *shorter*.

The commentators cite the parallel verses:

> *"And whatever you have of bounty, it is from God."* (Qur'an 16:53)

> *"Whatever is with you passes away, and whatever is with God remains."* (Qur'an 16:96)

> *"But you prefer the life of this world, while the Hereafter is better and more lasting."* (Qur'an 87:16–17)

The last of these is the closest parallel, and its placement in the sūrah of *al-Aʿlā* — a Makkan sūrah of pure creed — shows how central this comparison is to the Qur'an's entire message.

**Al-Rāzī's Measure of the Comparison**

Al-Rāzī adds a striking observation in his commentary on this verse, transmitted from earlier authorities and echoed widely in the tradition: that this world in relation to the Hereafter is like a speck of dust in relation to the sea.

The tradition's basis for such comparisons is a group of texts, of which the most famous is the ḥadīth:

> *"If this world were worth a gnat's wing to God, He would not give a disbeliever a drink of water from it."* (al-Tirmidhī 2320; Ibn Mājah 4110 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And in the opposite direction, the Qur'an's description of the Hereafter's scale:

> *"No soul knows what joy is kept hidden for them, as a recompense for what they used to do."* (Qur'an 32:17)

The commentators are careful about the meaning of these comparisons. They do not mean the world is worthless in itself — it is the place of testing, and its lawful goods are genuine goods. They mean that in *comparison with what follows*, the disproportion is infinite, and that any rational person who understands the disproportion will order his choices accordingly.

**"Will You Not Understand?"**

*A-fa-lā taʿqilūn*. The closing question uses the verb *ʿaqala* — to reason, to use the intellect, to bind things together.

The commentators note the Qur'an's characteristic move: it closes a comparison with an appeal to reason. The choice between the temporary and the permanent is not presented as a matter of taste or temperament; it is presented as a matter of *arithmetic* that anyone can do.

This is one of the many places where the Qur'an treats faith and reason as allies rather than antagonists. The tradition's scholars made much of it:

> *"Do they not then reflect upon the Qur'an? Or are there locks upon their hearts?"* (Qur'an 47:24)

> *"And We have certainly spread out for mankind in this Qur'an every kind of similitude, that they may remember."* (Qur'an 39:27)

The classical formulation, stated by al-Ghazālī and repeated by the schools, is that revelation is the criterion by which reason's limits are known, and reason is the instrument by which revelation's claims are understood. Neither functions without the other.

**The Logical Structure: A Trade With Known Terms**

The verse is, in form, an invitation to a calculation, and the commentators lay out the terms.

*What is offered*: everything this world can give.
*Its term*: the length of a life, which is decreed and ends.
*What is offered instead*: what is with God.
*Its term*: unending.

The tradition's conclusion is that the comparison admits of only one rational answer — which is why the Qur'an so frequently expresses astonishment at the human preference for the near over the far:

> *"Nay, but you prefer the life of this world, while the Hereafter is better and more lasting."* (Qur'an 87:16–17)

> *"Truly you love this fleeting world, and you forsake the Hereafter."* (Qur'an 75:20–21)

The commentators note that the Qur'an's astonishment is not at people *enjoying* the world. It is at people *preferring* it — that is, spending the permanent on the temporary.

**The Prophetic Guidance on the Same Comparison**

The tradition's most quoted statement of this verse's principle is the ḥadīth:

> *"Be in this world as though you were a stranger or a traveler passing through."* And Ibn ʿUmar used to say: *"When you reach the evening, do not expect to see the morning; and when you reach the morning, do not expect to see the evening. Take from your health for your illness, and from your life for your death."* (al-Bukhārī 6416)

The Prophet ﷺ also gave the comparison in a form that makes the disproportion concrete:

> *"What have I to do with this world? The similitude of me and this world is that of a rider who took shade under a tree, then moved on and left it."* (al-Tirmidhī 2377; Ibn Mājah 4109 — graded ḥasan)

And the counterbalancing statement, which prevents the verse from being read as world-denial:

> *"O God, set right for me my religion, which is the safeguard of my affairs; and set right for me my world, in which is my livelihood; and set right for me my Hereafter, to which is my return; and make life an increase for me in every good, and make death a rest for me from every evil."* (Muslim 2720)

The tradition has always held these together: the world is not the point, and it is not worthless. It is the *place* where the next world is earned, and its lawful goods are means.

**Application: The Comparison as a Decision Rule**

The verse's practical value is that it supplies a *rule* rather than an exhortation, and the rule can be applied mechanically to decisions.

When a choice comes down to a worldly gain at a religious cost, or a religious gain at a worldly cost, the comparison is: which term is longer, and which quality is higher? The verse states the answer in advance.

Applied to the common cases:

*A dishonest income*: more money now, less blessing later. The verse says *better and more lasting*.
*A compromising career*: status for forty years, or integrity forever.
*A missed obligation for a business opportunity*: an hour's gain against an unending account.
*A grudge held*: the temporary pleasure of resentment against the permanent weight of it.

The tradition's discipline is not to refuse the world but to *weigh* it, and the verse supplies the weights.

There is one more application the commentators drew, and it is the subtlest. The verse says *whatever you have been given* — including things that are not wealth at all. Beauty, intelligence, eloquence, health, a good name, a talent, a friendship. All are *matāʿ* and *zīnah*, all are given, and all are temporary. The person who understands this enjoys them without being owned by them, and gives them without feeling that he has lost what was his.

The sūrah's closing passage — verses 76 through 83 — will show what happens to a man who did not understand it.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:61

> **And is he unto whom We made a goodly promise and who receives it, like unto him whom We granted enjoyment in the life of this world and who then, on the Day of Resurrection, will be among those arraigned?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question With an Obvious Answer**

*A-fa-man waʿadnāhu waʿdan ḥasanan fa-huwa lāqīhi ka-man mattaʿnāhu matāʿa al-ḥayāti al-dunyā thumma huwa yawma al-qiyāmati mina al-muḥḍarīn*.

The verse is a rhetorical question of the kind the Qur'an uses when the answer is undeniable: *is X like Y?* The expected response is no, and the reader supplies it.

The commentators note the elegance of the comparison. Two men, two gifts, two ends. The first is *promised* something good and will *receive* it. The second is *granted* something and will be *arraigned*.

The question is not whether the two are equal. It is why anyone would live as though they were.

**The Two Men in the Verse**

The commentators discuss who is being described, and they record two readings.
