*The general reading*: the verse compares *any* believer with *any* disbeliever. The first is the person to whom God has made the promise of the Garden, and who will arrive at it; the second is the person granted worldly enjoyment who will then be brought to account. On this reading, the verse is a universal principle, and it is the reading most commentators prefer.

*The specific reading*: al-Ṭabarī transmits reports identifying the two men as particular individuals — the Prophet ﷺ (with alternative reports naming ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib or Ḥamzah) on one side, and Abū Jahl on the other. These identifications are transmitted rather than revealed, and al-Ṭabarī records them without asserting them.

The commentators note that the specific reading, where sound, is an *instance* of the general one. The principle holds regardless of whether particular individuals are named.

**"A Goodly Promise... Who Receives It"**

*Waʿdan ḥasanan fa-huwa lāqīhi*. The promise is described as *ḥasan* — good, beautiful, excellent. The word covers both the *content* of the promise (the Garden) and its *quality* (it is a promise from One who does not break promises).

The clause *fa-huwa lāqīhi* — *and he will meet it* — uses an active participle indicating certainty. The tradition's commentators stress this: the believer's reward is described as something he will actually *encounter*, not something he merely hopes for.

The Qur'an's promises are stated in this form throughout:

> *"Truly God has purchased from the believers their lives and their wealth, in exchange for the Garden... [It is] a promise binding upon Him in the Torah and the Gospel and the Qur'an. And who is truer to his covenant than God?"* (Qur'an 9:111)

> *"Indeed, God fails not His promise."* (Qur'an 3:9)

The commentators note the practical function of this emphasis: the believer is being given something to *hold onto* in the interval. The promise is not an aspiration; it is a contract.

**"Him Whom We Granted Enjoyment in the Life of This World"**

*Man mattaʿnāhu matāʿa al-ḥayāti al-dunyā*. Note that the verb is the same *mattaʿnā* used in the previous verse for *you have been given*. The worldly portion is called *matāʿ* — temporary use.

And note the attribution: *We granted him*. The disbeliever's wealth is from God too. The commentators are careful on this point, because it establishes one of the harder doctrines of the Qur'an: God gives worldly goods to those who reject Him.

The Qur'an addresses this directly:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve think that the respite We grant them is good for their souls. We grant them respite only that they may increase in sin."* (Qur'an 3:178)

> *"So when they forgot that by which they had been reminded, We opened unto them the gates of all things, till, even as they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly; and lo! they were in despair."* (Qur'an 6:44)

The tradition named this *istidrāj* — gradual enticement toward destruction — and the Prophet ﷺ described it:

> *"When you see God giving a servant what he loves of this world while he persists in disobedience, that is only istidrāj."* Then he recited 6:44. (Aḥmad; graded ḥasan)

The commentators therefore read the *granting* in this verse as a gift with a terrible ambiguity: it is real enjoyment, and it is also a trap for the person who mistakes it for approval.

**"And Who Then, on the Day of Resurrection, Will Be Among Those Arraigned"**

*Thumma huwa yawma al-qiyāmati mina al-muḥḍarīn*. The word *muḥḍarīn* is from *ḥ-ḍ-r*, to bring into the presence of, to summon. Al-Rāzī notes that in Qur'anic usage the term carries a negative connotation: it means being *brought before* a tribunal, summoned to answer. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as being made present at the punishment.

The commentators note the structural balance of the comparison, which is exact:

*The believer*: promised → meets it.
*The disbeliever*: given → summoned.

The believer's future is an appointment. The disbeliever's future is a summons. Both are certain, and they are not remotely the same thing.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Argument**

Verses 60 and 61 together complete the sūrah's first sustained treatment of wealth, and the commentators note the sequence:

*Verse 58*: towns destroyed for exulting in their ease.
*Verse 59*: the justice of that destruction — a messenger came first.
*Verse 60*: the general principle — everything you have is temporary use and ornament; what is with God is better and more lasting.
*Verse 61*: the personal application — two men, two ends, and only one of them is enviable.

And the passage prepares for Korah at verse 76, who is the sūrah's fully drawn specimen of the second man.

**The Theological Point About Wealth and Divine Favor**

The verse raises a question that troubles almost everyone at some point, and the commentators address it directly: if the disbelievers visibly prosper, is prosperity a sign of God's favor?

The tradition's answer is developed across several texts, and it has several components.

*Provision is not graded by faith in this life.* God gives to believers and disbelievers alike, as a test:

> *"And were it not that mankind would become one community [of disbelievers], We would have made for those who disbelieve in the Most Merciful roofs of silver for their houses..."* (Qur'an 43:33)

> *"And whoever turns away from My remembrance — truly his will be a straitened life, and We shall gather him on the Day of Resurrection blind."* (Qur'an 20:124)

That second verse is important, because it identifies the *straitened life* as a spiritual condition that may coexist with abundance.

*The believer's reward is deferred, and that is the design:*

> *"And hasten not [to ask] for them [the punishment]: We count for them a numbering."* — no; rather the principle is stated:

> *"So be patient over what they say, and glorify the praise of thy Lord before the rising of the sun and before its setting."* (Qur'an 20:130)

*And the final accounting is what settles the comparison:*

> *"Do they not see that We come to the land, diminishing it at its borders?"* (Qur'an 13:41)

The Prophet ﷺ gave the tradition its definitive statement on the matter:

> *"This world is a prison for the believer and a paradise for the disbeliever."* (Muslim 2956)

The commentators explain: the believer is restrained here and released there; the disbeliever is released here and restrained there. That single sentence resolves the comparison of this verse completely.

**Application**

Three practical conclusions follow.

*Do not envy on partial information.* The comparison the verse draws requires both terms — the present condition *and* the end. Envy is the error of comparing one person's beginning to another's end, or one person's exterior to another's interior. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Look to those below you [in worldly terms] and do not look to those above you, for that is more likely that you will not belittle the blessing of God upon you."* (al-Bukhārī 6490; Muslim 2963)

*Understand prosperity correctly.* Both prosperity and its absence are tests, and neither is a verdict. The tradition's counsel for the prosperous is gratitude expressed in giving, and for the deprived is patience expressed in dignity.

*And hold the promise.* The first man in this verse is described as one who *will meet* what he was promised. The believer's certainty about the future is not optimism; it is reliance on a promise from One who does not break promises. The practical use of that certainty is that it makes the comparison computable *now*, while there is still time to choose which of the two men one is.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:62

> **And on the Day when He shall call out to them, and say, "Where are My partners, those whom you claimed?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First of Three Summonses**

*Wa-yawma yunādīhim fa-yaqūlu ayna shurakāʾiya alladhīna kuntum tazʿumūn*.

The sūrah now moves into a scene of the Resurrection, and the commentators note that verses 62 through 66 form a single, dramatic unit: three divine questions put to the idolaters, and their inability to answer.

This verse is the first question, and it is repeated almost verbatim at verse 74 — a repetition that the commentators regard as deliberate emphasis rather than redundancy.

**"On the Day When He Shall Call Out to Them"**

*Wa-yawma yunādīhim*. The verb *nādā* means to call out, to cry, to summon audibly. The Qur'an uses it for God's address on that Day:

> *"And on the Day when He will call out to them and say, 'What answer gave you to the message bearers?'"* (Qur'an 28:65)

> *"And the Day He shall call unto them and say, 'Where are My partners?'"* (Qur'an 28:74)

> *"And the Day when He will say, 'Call upon those whom you claimed to be My partners,' and they will call upon them, but they will not respond to them."* (Qur'an 18:52)

The commentators note that the *calling* is itself part of the reckoning. God does not simply sentence; He *addresses* — which means the condemned are given the floor, are asked, and are exposed by their own inability to answer.

This is one of the most consistent features of the Qur'an's eschatology and one of its most theologically loaded. The judgment is public, interrogative, and self-incriminating:

> *"This Day We seal their mouths, and their hands speak to Us, and their feet bear witness to what they used to earn."* (Qur'an 36:65)

> *"And they will say to their skins, 'Why did you bear witness against us?' They will say, 'God has made us speak, as He makes all things speak.'"* (Qur'an 41:21)

**"Where Are My Partners?"**

*Ayna shurakāʾī*. The word *shurakāʾ* — partners, associates — is the Qur'an's technical term for the objects of worship set beside God. The doctrine it protects is *tawḥīd* in its aspect of *ulūhiyyah* — that worship belongs to God alone.

The question *where are they?* is devastating in its form, because it assumes they should be present. The idolaters claimed these beings as God's partners and as their intercessors. Very well — where are they now?

The Qur'an records the same question in several places:

> *"And they have taken gods apart from Him that they might be a source of strength for them. Nay! They will deny their worship and be against them."* (Qur'an 19:81–82)

> *"And when those who ascribed partners see their 'partners,' they will say, 'Our Lord, these are our partners whom we used to invoke apart from Thee.' And they will fling back at them the word: 'Truly you are liars!'"* (Qur'an 16:86)

> *"Then on the Day of Resurrection He will disgrace them and say, 'Where are My partners, those for whose sake you used to oppose [the truth]?'"* (Qur'an 16:27)

**Who Are the "Partners"?**

The commentators are careful to specify the range, because the question is addressed to people whose objects of worship varied.

*Inanimate objects*: idols, stones, trees, celestial bodies. The Qur'an records this directly:

> *"They said, 'We worship them only that they may bring us near to God.'"* (Qur'an 39:3)

*Living beings worshipped*: angels, jinn, and human beings. The Qur'an addresses each:

> *"And on the Day when He will gather them all together, then He will say to the angels, 'Did these worship you?' They will say, 'Glory be to Thee! Thou art our Protector, not them. Nay, but they used to worship the jinn; most of them were believers in them.'"* (Qur'an 34:40–41)

And of Jesus, the Qur'an records a comparable interrogation in Sūrah al-Māʾidah, where he is asked whether he told people to worship him:

> *"And when God said, 'O Jesus son of Mary, did you say to people, "Take me and my mother as gods apart from God"?' He said, 'Glory be to Thee! It is not for me to say what I have no right to...'"* (Qur'an 5:116)

*And objects of ultimate concern that are not gods at all*: wealth, status, tribe, appetite, ideology. This is the extension the commentators drew, and 45:23 supports it:

> *"Have you seen the one who takes his caprice as his god?"* (Qur'an 45:23)

The question *where are My partners?* therefore applies to every case: whatever a person set beside or above God as the effective object of his trust, fear, and obedience will be summoned on that Day and will not answer.

**"Those Whom You Claimed"**

*Alladhīna kuntum tazʿumūn*. The verb *zaʿama* means to claim, to assert — and the commentators note its coloring in Qur'anic usage: it is frequently used for claims that are *false and ungrounded*.

> *"And they say, 'This is naught but a lie invented.'"* (Qur'an 25:4) — uses related vocabulary.

> *"Say: 'Have you considered those whom you invoke apart from God? Show me what they have created of the earth...'"* (Qur'an 46:4)

The claim in question is the one the Qur'an records from the idolaters themselves: that these beings would intercede for them and bring them near to God.

> *"And those who take protectors apart from Him [say], 'We worship them only that they may bring us near to God.'"* (Qur'an 39:3)

> *"And they worship, apart from God, that which neither harms them nor benefits them, and they say, 'These are our intercessors with God.'"* (Qur'an 10:18)

The commentators note the precise mechanism of the error. The idolaters did not deny God — the Qur'an says they acknowledged Him as Creator (*"If you ask them who created the heavens and the earth, they will surely say, 'God,'"* 31:25). Their error was in the *intermediary*: they treated created beings as channels of approach, and then, over time, as objects of worship in their own right.

This is why the Qur'an's argument against idolatry is so often about *intercession* rather than about existence:

> *"Say: 'Unto God belongs intercession entirely.'"* (Qur'an 39:44)

> *"Who is it that can intercede with Him save by His permission?"* (Qur'an 2:255)

**The Knowledge That Arrives Too Late**

Al-Rāzī makes a point that the commentators have repeated ever since, and it is one of the most sobering observations in the sūrah.

On that Day, the idolaters will *know* the truth. They will know that God is one and that their partners were nothing. But this knowledge will not benefit them, because it arrived by necessity rather than by choice.

The distinction is fundamental to the Qur'an's epistemology. Knowledge that comes from *seeing* does not have the same moral weight as knowledge that comes from *seeking*. The whole structure of testing depends on this:

> *"And they will say, 'Had we but listened or understood, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.'"* (Qur'an 67:10)

That is a confession of what they *now* know — and it is useless, because the moment for acting on it has passed.

The tradition's corresponding warning is that faith must be acquired *before* the veil is lifted, because after it is lifted there is nothing left to believe. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Repentance is accepted until the soul reaches the throat"* — no; the relevant text is the Qur'an's own:

> *"Repentance is not for those who do evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says, 'Now I repent' — nor for those who die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 4:18)

and:

> *"The Day when some of the signs of thy Lord come, no soul will benefit from its faith if it had not believed before, or earned good in its faith."* (Qur'an 6:158)

**Application**

The verse's practical use is as a test of what a person actually worships, and the tradition has always applied it that way.

The test is simple and uncomfortable: *in that moment of crisis, what did you call upon?* Every person has an instinctive object of reliance, and it emerges under pressure — in the diagnosis, the loss, the financial collapse, the moment of public exposure. Whatever a person reaches for in those moments is what he has been worshipping, regardless of what he would say if asked.

The tradition's remedy is not to suppress the instinct but to retrain it. The Prophet ﷺ taught specific invocations for distress, and the practice of *duʿāʾ* is, in part, the discipline of turning to God by habit so that one turns to Him by instinct when the instinct is all that is left:

> *"Whoever is afflicted by grief or sorrow and says, 'O God, I am Your servant, the son of Your servant, the son of Your maidservant...' — God will remove his grief and replace it with joy."* (Aḥmad 4318 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And there is a second application concerning intercession itself. The question *where are My partners?* will be asked of anyone who has placed his ultimate trust in a created thing — a leader, a system, a wealth, an ideology, a person. All of them will be unable to answer. Only what is with God remains:

> *"Whatever is with you passes away, and whatever is with God remains."* (Qur'an 16:96)

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:63

> **Those against whom the Word has already come due will say, "Our Lord! These are those whom we perverted. We perverted them as we ourselves were perverse. We disavow [them] before Thee; it was not us whom they worshipped."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to the Summons**

*Qāla alladhīna ḥaqqa ʿalayhimu al-qawlu rabbanā hāʾulāʾi alladhīna aghwaynā aghwaynāhum kamā ghawaynā tabarraʾnā ilayka mā kānū iyyānā yaʿbudūn*.

When God asks *where are My partners?* (v. 62), the ones who answer are not the idols. They are the *leaders* — and their answer is one of the most remarkable passages in the Qur'an, because it is a confession, a defense, and a disavowal all at once.

**"Those Against Whom the Word Has Come Due"**

*Alladhīna ḥaqqa ʿalayhimu al-qawlu*. The phrase *the Word has come due against them* is one of the Qur'an's technical expressions for the sentence of punishment, and the commentators explain its grammar: *ḥaqqa ʿalayhi* means the word *became binding upon*, *was established against* — the language of a verdict that has settled.

The phrase appears throughout the Qur'an:

> *"Truly those against whom the Word of thy Lord has come due will not believe, even if every sign should come to them, until they witness the painful punishment."* (Qur'an 10:96–97)

> *"Thus did the Word of thy Lord come due against those who disbelieved: truly they are the inhabitants of the Fire."* (Qur'an 40:6)

> *"The Word has come due against most of them, for they do not believe."* (Qur'an 36:7)

The commentators are careful about the theology here. The Word coming due is not an arbitrary pre-destination that overrides choice; it is the *confirmation* of what God knew they would freely choose. The tradition's formulation, as noted at verse 50, is that God's knowledge describes rather than compels.

The commentators identify who these are: the leaders of disbelief and misguidance — the ones who *called* rather than merely followed.

**The Three-Part Answer**

The leaders' statement has three clauses, and the commentators parse each.

*First: "These are those whom we perverted."* They identify the followers, and they accept responsibility for the leading astray. The verb *aghwaynā* is from *gh-w-y*, to lead astray, to seduce from the way.

*Second: "We perverted them as we ourselves were perverse."* This is the crucial clause, and the commentators note its function. The leaders are saying: we did not *force* them. We went astray, and they followed. The causal chain is acknowledged but the coercion is denied.

*Third: "We disavow them before Thee; it was not us whom they worshipped."* The disavowal. Whatever they did, they did not *worship us*.

**The Wordplay: *Aghwaynā* and *Ghawaynā***

The Arabic places two forms of the same root in adjacent clauses — *aghwaynāhum* (we led them astray, causative) and *ghawaynā* (we went astray, intransitive) — and the commentators note that the effect is to make the two actions inseparable.

The rhetorical point is precise: the leaders are insisting that their guilt and their followers' guilt are *the same kind*. They led, but the led accepted. They did not possess power over the followers' wills.

The Qur'an states the principle of this elsewhere, in Satan's own disavowal of his followers, which is the model for all such scenes:

> *"And Satan will say, when the matter is decided: 'Truly God promised you a true promise, and I promised you and failed you. But I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me. So blame me not, but blame yourselves. I cannot help you, nor can you help me. Truly I disbelieved in your ascribing me as a partner to God before.'"* (Qur'an 14:22)

That verse is the archetype, and the commentators read the leaders' words here as an imitation of it. If even Satan — the being who has spent existence recruiting — declares *I had no authority over you, save that I called*, then no human leader can claim more.

**"It Was Not Us Whom They Worshipped"**

*Mā kānū iyyānā yaʿbudūn*. This final clause has generated the most commentary, because it seems to contradict the situation.

The followers *did* worship their leaders, in the sense of obeying them against God:

> *"They have taken their rabbis and their monks as lords apart from God, and [also] the Messiah son of Mary — though they were commanded to worship only One God."* (Qur'an 9:31)

The Prophet ﷺ explained that verse to ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim, who objected that the Jews and Christians did not *pray* to their scholars:

> *"Did they not forbid what God had permitted and permit what God had forbidden, and you followed them?"* He said, "Yes." The Prophet ﷺ said: *"That is how you worshipped them."* (al-Tirmidhī 3095; the narration is well known)

So how can the leaders say *it was not us whom they worshipped*?

Al-Rāzī gives the reading the tradition has generally adopted: the leaders mean that the followers' worship was *really* directed at their own desires. They obeyed because obedience suited them, flattered them, or freed them from something. The leaders were the *pretext*; the appetite was the *god*.

This is the same analysis the Qur'an gives at 45:23 — *have you seen the one who takes his caprice as his god?* — and it is consistent with verse 50 of this sūrah, where rejection was diagnosed as *hawā*, desire, rather than as an intellectual conclusion.

The commentators note the terrible insight contained here. The followers thought they were worshipping a leader, a cause, a tradition, or a god. What they were actually worshipping was their own convenience, and the leader knew it all along.

**The Disavowal as the Fulfillment of a Warning**

The Qur'an warned of this moment repeatedly in this life, and the commentators note that the scene's function is to show the warning fulfilled:

> *"When those who were followed disown those who followed, and they see the punishment, and all ties between them are severed."* (Qur'an 2:166)

> *"And they will say, 'Our Lord! Truly we obeyed our leaders and our great ones, and they led us astray from the way. Our Lord, give them double the punishment and curse them with a great curse.'"* (Qur'an 33:67–68)

Note that verse 33:67 is the followers' plea, and this verse is the leaders' answer. Read together, they form a complete dialogue, and the Qur'an has recorded both halves.

The tradition's lesson concerns the nature of *loyalty*. Human alliances are dissolved by self-interest at the moment they become costly. The only alliance that survives the Day is the one made with God:

> *"Friends on that Day will be enemies to one another, except the reverent."* (Qur'an 43:67)

That verse is the general statement of which this scene is the particular instance.

**Theological Note: *Tabarraʾnā Ilayka***

*Tabarraʾnā ilayka* — *we disavow [them] to Thee*. The verb *tabarraʾa* means to declare oneself quit of, to dissociate. The preposition *ilā* — *to Thee* — is significant: the dissociation is made *before God*, as a formal legal act.

The commentators note that this is the same verb believers are commanded to use in this life for the opposite object:

> *"There has been for you a good example in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people, 'Truly we are quit of you and of whatever you worship apart from God. We disbelieve in you; and enmity and hatred have appeared between us and you forever, until you believe in God alone.'"* (Qur'an 60:4)

The difference is the object. Abraham disassociated from the *false* and its people for the sake of God. These leaders disassociate from their *followers* for the sake of themselves. The act looks identical and is opposite, which is one of the Qur'an's most uncomfortable lessons: the same language can express faith or self-preservation, and the difference is invisible from outside.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Leadership is a liability, not only a privilege.* The people speaking here are the ones who led. The tradition's warning about this is severe, and the Prophet ﷺ stated it plainly:

> *"Every one of you is a shepherd and every one of you is responsible for his flock."* (al-Bukhārī 893; Muslim 1829)

and:

> *"Whoever calls to misguidance bears a sin like the sins of all who follow him, without diminishing anything from their sins."* (Muslim 2674)

*Following is a choice.* The leaders' defense — *we went astray and they followed us* — is accepted as fact by the Qur'an and does not reduce the followers' guilt. The tradition's rule is that no one can transfer his moral agency to another. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"You will follow the ways of those who came before you, hand-span by hand-span and cubit by cubit, until if they entered a lizard's hole you would enter it too."* (al-Bukhārī 3456; Muslim 2669)

— and the ḥadīth is a warning precisely because the following is voluntary.

*And the real object of worship deserves examination.* If the leaders can say truthfully *it was not us whom they worshipped*, then the question for every person is what he is actually serving. The tradition's diagnostic is consistent: look at what you obey when it conflicts with what you profess. That is the god, whatever name it goes by.

The sūrah's next verse shows the followers being told to call on their partners — and what happens when they do.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:64

> **It will be said, "Call upon your partners." So they will call upon them, but they will not answer them. And they will see the punishment—if only they had been rightly guided!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Test Applied Publicly**

*Wa-qīla udʿū shurakāʾakum fa-daʿawhum fa-lam yastajībū lahum wa-raʿawu al-ʿadhāba law annahum kānū yahtadūn*.

The verse stages a demonstration. Having been asked *where are My partners?* (v. 62) and having heard their leaders disavow them (v. 63), the idolaters are now told to test the claim directly: *call them*.

The commentators note the judicial character of the scene. This is not mockery; it is the production of evidence. The idolaters' entire religious claim was that these beings could help them. The claim is now tested in the only situation that matters, and it fails.

**"Call Upon Your Partners"**

*Udʿū shurakāʾakum*. The verb *daʿā* means to call, to invoke, to pray to. It is the standard Qur'anic word for *duʿāʾ* — supplication — and the same verb used for calling upon God:

> *"And your Lord says, 'Call upon Me; I will respond to you. Truly those who wax arrogant before My worship shall enter Hell, humbled.'"* (Qur'an 40:60)

The parallel is deliberate and devastating. The identical act — calling — is prescribed for God with a promise of response, and is now prescribed for the false gods with the result that no response comes.

The Qur'an states the contrast as a standing argument:

> *"Say: 'Have you considered: if there came upon you the punishment of God, or there came upon you the Hour — would you call upon other than God, if you are truthful? Nay, but Him alone would you call upon, and He would remove that for which you called upon Him, if He willed, and you would forget what you ascribed [unto Him].'"* (Qur'an 6:40–41)

> *"Or who responds to the distressed one when he calls upon Him, and removes the evil, and makes you inheritors of the earth? Is there a god with God? Little do you remember!"* (Qur'an 27:62)

The commentators note the argument's form: the idolaters *already know* the answer, because every human being calls on God alone in extremity. The Qur'an states this knowledge:

> *"And when harm touches you upon the sea, those whom you invoke vanish, save Him alone."* (Qur'an 17:67)

**"So They Will Call Upon Them, But They Will Not Answer Them"**

*Fa-daʿawhum fa-lam yastajībū lahum*. The failure is complete and is stated in the plainest terms.

The Qur'an records the same failed invocation in several places:

> *"And they will call out therein, 'Our Lord, bring us out; we will work righteousness, other than what we used to do!'"* (Qur'an 35:37) — and no answer comes.

> *"And they will cry out, 'O Mālik, let thy Lord make an end of us!' He will say, 'Truly you will remain.'"* (Qur'an 43:77)

> *"And those who took to themselves protectors apart from Him [will find] that He watches over them... And on the Day when He will gather them all together... Then He will say to the angels: 'Did these worship you?'"* (Qur'an 34:40)

Al-Rāzī adds a subtle observation that the commentators have valued: on that Day, the idolaters *know* already that the answer will not come. They are not calling in hope; they are calling because they were told to, and the calling is itself part of the punishment. The humiliation of invoking what cannot answer, in public, before the creation, is the experience the Qur'an is describing.

This is a profound psychological point. The most terrible moment in the scene is not the punishment that follows; it is the silence after the call.

**"And They Will See the Punishment"**

*Wa-raʿawu al-ʿadhāb*. The word *raʾā* is seeing with the eye. The commentators note the progression: they *called*, nothing *answered*, and then they *saw*. The sequence is important — the failure of the false gods is followed immediately by the appearance of the real consequence.

The tradition has always noted the Qur'an's insistence that on that Day, the punishment is *seen*:

> *"And you will see the guilty on that Day bound together in fetters."* (Qur'an 14:49)

> *"Nay, did you but know with the knowledge of certainty, you would surely see Hellfire; then you would surely see it with the eye of certainty."* (Qur'an 102:5–7)

The last verse gives the tradition its threefold epistemology: *ʿilm al-yaqīn* (knowledge of certainty), *ʿayn al-yaqīn* (the eye of certainty), *ḥaqq al-yaqīn* (the truth of certainty). On that Day, what was known becomes seen.

**"If Only They Had Been Rightly Guided!"**

*Law annahum kānū yahtadūn*. This clause is the most discussed in the verse, because its grammatical structure is elliptical and the elision changes the meaning.

The commentators record several readings.

*Reading one — the apodosis is suppressed*: the sentence means "if only they had been guided [they would have been saved from the Fire]." This is the reading preferred by al-Qurṭubī, and it makes the clause a lament: a statement of what might have been.

*Reading two — the clause completes the preceding statement*: "They would have seen the punishment, if they had been rightly guided." That is, had they been guided in this world, they would have *already seen* — through faith — what they are now seeing with their eyes, and would have feared it in time. Al-Rāzī transmits this reading and finds it powerful: the failure was not of information but of *sight*, and on that Day the sight arrives too late to help.

*Reading three — the subject is the idols, not the idolaters*: "They [the idols] would have seen the punishment, if they had been capable of being guided" — meaning that if these objects had been living beings able to perceive, they too would be facing this. Al-Rāzī records it and notes that it is grammatically possible but contextually less likely.

The commentators generally hold the first two together, and there is no conflict: the clause is simultaneously a regret (*had they been guided, they would have been saved*) and a diagnosis (*had they been guided, they would have seen this coming*).

**The Structure of Verses 62–64**

Taken together, the three verses stage a complete judicial sequence, and the commentators note its formal precision:

*Verse 62*: The claim is named — *where are My partners?*
*Verse 63*: The leaders disavow the followers and name the real object of worship.
*Verse 64*: The claim is tested by direct invocation and fails; the punishment appears; the regret is stated.

The tradition has always read this as the Qur'an's model of how error is exposed: by being *tested*. The false object is asked to do the one thing it was credited with, and cannot.

**The Doctrine of Intercession, Clarified**

Because the verse concerns calling upon beings other than God, the commentators use it to clarify the Islamic doctrine of *shafāʿah* — intercession — which is often misunderstood.

*Intercession is real in Islam.* The Qur'an and Sunnah affirm it, and the believers pray for it constantly:

> *"Who is it that can intercede with Him save by His permission?"* (Qur'an 2:255)

> *"And intercession avails not with Him, save for him whom He permits."* (Qur'an 34:23)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"My intercession will be for the people of major sins among my community."* (Abū Dāwūd 4739; al-Tirmidhī 2435 — graded ḥasan)

and:

> *"For every prophet there is a supplication that is answered, and I have stored my supplication as intercession for my community on the Day of Resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6304; Muslim 199)

*But intercession has conditions*: it is God's, it requires His permission, and it is granted to those He permits and for those He permits. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"I shall be given the banner of praise, and I shall intercede..."* (al-Bukhārī 4712; Muslim 194 — the long ḥadīth of intercession)

*And the crucial difference from the idolaters' claim* is the direction of the plea. The believer asks *God* to accept the intercession of those whom God has authorized. The idolater asks the *intercessor* instead of God, or treats the intercessor as an independent source of help. The Qur'an's question — *where are My partners?* — is directed at precisely that error.

**Application**

The verse's most portable content is the test it proposes, which can be applied in this life.

*In times of extremity, whom do you call upon?* The Qur'an's argument at 17:67 is that the answer is already known: every human being turns to God when the ship is going down. The discipline is to make the emergency posture the ordinary one.

*Test your objects of trust in advance.* The idolaters' claim failed when tested. Most human objects of ultimate trust can be tested the same way and will fail the same way — not because they are worthless but because they are finite. The tradition's counsel is to locate ultimate trust only where it cannot fail.

*And note the order of the verse.* They called, nothing answered, and *then* they saw. The sequence is a warning about the structure of a wasted life: the false reliance holds until the moment it is tested, and the moment it is tested is the moment the consequence appears. There is no interval in which to make other arrangements.

For the reader of this sūrah, the verse also completes a pattern. Pharaoh's court trusted in Pharaoh, and he drowned. Korah trusted in his treasure, and the earth swallowed it. And here, the idolaters trust in their partners, and the partners do not answer. The sūrah has now shown the same failure at the level of a state, a fortune, and a creed.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:65

> **And on the Day when He will call out to them and say, "What answer gave you to the message bearers?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Question**

*Wa-yawma yunādīhim fa-yaqūlu mādhā ajabtumu al-mursalīn*.

Verse 62 asked about *partners* — about what they worshipped. This verse asks about *messengers* — about how they responded to the truth that came to them. The commentators note that the two questions together cover the whole of the creed: what did you worship, and how did you respond to My guidance?

**"What Answer Gave You to the Message Bearers?"**

*Mādhā ajabtumu al-mursalīn*. The word *mursalīn* is the plural of *mursal* — one who is *sent*. The term is technical: a *mursal* is a messenger, distinguished in the tradition's usage from a *nabī* (prophet) by possessing a scripture or a new law, though the Qur'an often uses the two terms interchangeably.

The question is addressed to the *communities*, not to the messengers. The commentators note that the Qur'an records a parallel interrogation of the messengers themselves:

> *"The Day when God will gather the messengers and say, 'What response did you receive?' They shall say, 'We have no knowledge. Truly it is Thou who knowest best the things unseen.'"* (Qur'an 5:109)

That verse is the standard commentary on this one, and al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī both cite it. The structure is symmetrical: the messengers are asked what response they got, and the communities are asked what answer they gave. Both interrogations are public, and both produce the same silence.

The Prophet ﷺ described the scene in a ḥadīth that the tradition has always set beside these verses:

> *"The people will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection barefoot, naked, and uncircumcised."* Then he recited: *"As We began the first creation, We shall repeat it — a promise binding upon Us; truly We shall do it."* (al-Bukhārī 6527; Muslim 2860)

and in the ḥadīth of intercession, the people come to Adam, then Noah, then Abraham, then Moses, then Jesus, and finally to the Prophet ﷺ, each one excusing himself — and the sequence itself is a picture of that Day's accountability.

**Why This Question Is Asked**

The commentators raise a question that is worth stating, because its answer carries the verse's theology.

God knows what answer they gave. He knew it before they gave it. Why ask?

The tradition's answer, consistent across the commentaries, has several parts.

*To establish the case publicly.* The reckoning is not a sentencing but a trial whose record is opened. The Qur'an's scenes of the Resurrection are full of documentation:

> *"And the Book will be placed, and you will see the guilty fearful of what is in it, and they will say, 'Woe to us! What a Book is this! It leaves out nothing small or great but that it has enumerated it.' And they will find what they did present [before them]."* (Qur'an 18:49)

*To make the condemned condemn themselves.* The question is designed to produce a silence that is itself the verdict. They have no answer, and they know it.

*And to vindicate the messengers.* The scene answers the ridicule the messengers endured. Every prophet who was called a liar, a sorcerer, or a madman is vindicated in public, before his own people:

> *"Then We shall question those to whom [messengers] were sent, and We shall question the messengers. Then We shall relate to them with knowledge; and We were not absent."* (Qur'an 7:6–7)

The last clause — *We were not absent* — is the point. God witnessed the whole exchange, and the question is asked so that the witness is entered into the record.

**The Message Bearers: What Was Demanded of Them**

The commentators note that the question is not "did you believe?" but *what answer did you give?* The phrasing implies that some answer was required — that the arrival of a messenger creates an obligation to respond.

This is the doctrine of the *ḥujjah* — the conclusive argument — that the sūrah has been building since verse 47 and stated explicitly at verse 59: no community is destroyed until a messenger has come to it.

The tradition's formulation is that the messenger's arrival converts indifference into a decision. Before he came, a person could be merely uninformed. After he came, the same condition is refusal.

The Prophet ﷺ stated this with maximal clarity:

> *"By Him in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, no one of this community hears of me — whether Jew or Christian — and then dies without believing in what I was sent with, but that he will be among the inhabitants of the Fire."* (Muslim 153)

And the Qur'an states the general form:

> *"Messengers bearing glad tidings and warning, lest mankind have any argument against God after the messengers."* (Qur'an 4:165)

**The Contrast With Verse 62**

The commentators note the movement between the two questions, and it is theologically significant.

*Verse 62*: *Where are My partners?* — the question about *shirk*, the corruption of worship.
*Verse 65*: *What answer gave you to the messengers?* — the question about *takdhīb*, the rejection of guidance.

The two are the primary categories of unbelief in the Qur'an, and the sūrah's closing verses will return to both. The pairing also establishes that the failure is not merely intellectual: it is a failure of *worship* (you directed it wrongly) and a failure of *response* (you refused what was sent).

**Theological Note: The Messengers' Own Answer**

Al-Qurṭubī draws attention to the beauty of 5:109 — *We have no knowledge* — said by the messengers themselves. The commentators explain that on that Day, knowledge belongs to God in a way that silences even the most knowing of His creatures, and that the phrase is an expression of the creature's absolute dependence rather than of ignorance.

The tradition notes the parallel with the Prophet's own statement about the Day:

> *"People will be gathered... and the sun will be brought near until the distress and trouble reaches a level they cannot bear."* (al-Bukhārī 6532; Muslim 2864)

— a ḥadīth that describes the terror of that Day in terms that explain the messengers' answer.

**Application**

The verse's practical value is as a standing question addressed to the living, and the tradition has always used it that way.

*What answer have you given?* Not "what do you intend to answer" or "what answer would you give if asked." The question is in the past tense because the asking will be in the past tense. The response is already made, and it is being made now.

The tradition's corresponding discipline is *muḥāsabah* — self-examination — which the scholars made a formal practice. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said:

> *"Take account of yourselves before you are taken to account; and weigh your deeds before they are weighed for you."* (Reported in the ethical literature; the practice is foundational in the tradition)

*And the answer is not only verbal.* The question is *what answer did you give* — and in the Qur'an's usage, the answer to a messenger is measured by what a person did with the message, not by what he said about it. The commentators note that the question will be answered by the record of a life:

> *"And every person — We have fastened his fate upon his neck, and We shall bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a record which he will find spread open. 'Read your record! Sufficient is your soul this Day as a reckoner against you.'"* (Qur'an 17:13–14)

That is the ultimate form of the answer: a person reads his own life back, and there is nothing left to argue.

For the reader of this sūrah — which has told the story of a messenger sent to Pharaoh, of a people delivered, and of a city that rejected — the question lands with particular force. The sūrah has shown what answer Pharaoh gave (v. 36), what answer the believers among the People of the Book gave (vv. 52–53), and what answer the Quraysh gave (v. 48). The reader is being invited to identify which of those answers is his own.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:66

> **the tidings shall be obscured for them on that Day, and they shall not question one another.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Silence After the Question**

*Fa-ʿamiyat ʿalayhimu al-anbāʾu yawmaʾidhin fa-hum lā yatasāʾalūn*.

The verse describes what follows the divine question of verse 65. The idolaters are asked what answer they gave to the messengers — and they cannot produce one. The Arabic word for their condition is striking: *ʿamiyat* — literally, *it became blind to them*.

**"The Tidings Shall Be Obscured"**

*Fa-ʿamiyat ʿalayhimu al-anbāʾu*. The verb *ʿamiya* means to become blind, to be confused, to be hidden. The word *anbāʾ* is the plural of *nabaʾ* — news, tidings, report, information.

The commentators give two readings, and both are transmitted from the early authorities.

*Al-Qurṭubī's reading*: the *excuses and arguments* they might have offered are obscured from them. That is, they cannot produce a defense. Every plea they might have made is inaccessible to them in that moment.

*Al-Rāzī's reading*: the news of one another is hidden from them — they are so overwhelmed that they lose track of those around them, including the people they had spent their lives with.

Both readings describe the same collapse from different angles: the inability to construct a defense, and the inability to connect with anyone.

The commentators note the appropriateness of the verb. In this life, these people had arguments ready — the appeal to ancestral custom (v. 36), the demand for a different messenger (v. 48), the accusation of sorcery. On that Day, the arguments are *blind*: they cannot find them, because the arguments were never the reason for the rejection.

**"And They Shall Not Question One Another"**

*Fa-hum lā yatasāʾalūn*. The verb *tasāʾala* means to ask one another, to inquire of one another. The Qur'an uses the same verb for the conversations of the people of the Garden:

> *"And they will approach one another, questioning."* (Qur'an 52:25)

and for the exchanges of the condemned:

> *"And some of them will turn to others, questioning one another. They will say, 'Truly we were formerly, among our families, fearful [of God's punishment]...'"* (Qur'an 52:25–26 — the blessed)

> *"And they will draw near unto one another, questioning."* (Qur'an 37:27 — the condemned)

The commentators therefore had to address an apparent difficulty, and they did so carefully and thoroughly, because the issue recurs throughout the Qur'an.

**The Apparent Contradiction and Its Resolution**

The difficulty: some verses say the people will question one another on that Day (37:27, 50, 52:25), while this verse says they will *not* question one another.

The commentators' resolution, stated by al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī and adopted by the tradition, is that the Day has *stages* (*mawāṭin*), and the verses describe different stages.

*In the stage of terror and presentation*, no one asks anyone anything, because the overwhelm is total. This is the condition described here and at:

> *"And when the Trumpet is blown, there will be no ties of kinship among them that Day, nor will they ask of one another."* (Qur'an 23:101)

*In a later stage*, when the reckoning is underway and the people are gathered into their groupings, questioning occurs — both among the blessed (52:25) and among the condemned (37:27, 50), and between the followers and the leaders (33:67, 2:166).

The tradition states the general hermeneutical principle: where two Qur'anic descriptions of the same Day appear to conflict, they are reconciled by reference to the Day's phases, and not by declaring one of them abrogated or figurative.

Al-Rāzī adds a second, complementary resolution: *questioning* in the Qur'an can mean several different kinds of asking — asking for information, asking in order to socialize, asking in order to seek help, and asking in accusation. The verses that deny questioning deny some of these; the verses that affirm it affirm others. A terrified person does not ask for news, but he may well turn on the man who misled him.

**The Same Principle Applied to God's Speaking**

The commentators note that the identical hermeneutic is used for a related question: will God speak to the people on that Day, or not?

Some verses indicate He will not:

> *"Truly God will not speak to them, nor will He look at them on the Day of Resurrection, nor will He purify them."* (Qur'an 3:77)

And others that He will:

> *"And He will say, 'Where are My partners?'"* (Qur'an 28:62, 74)

The resolution is the same: the Day has stages, and the modes of address differ. The righteous are addressed with mercy and are spoken to; some of the guilty are not addressed at all, which is itself described as a punishment worse than what follows.

The tradition has always regarded this as one of the most chilling ideas in the Book: that the worst thing about that Day, for some, is not the fire but the silence of God.

**The Psychological Realism**

The verse is psychologically exact, and the commentators developed it.

Terror severe enough produces a specific effect: the collapse of social connection. A person in catastrophic danger does not converse. He is reduced to his own situation. The Qur'an describes this state elsewhere with great precision:

> *"The Day when a man will flee from his brother, and his mother and his father, and his wife and his children. Every man among them on that Day will have a concern that suffices him."* (Qur'an 80:34–37)

> *"O mankind, fear your Lord! Truly the convulsion of the Hour is a tremendous thing. On the Day you see it, every nursing mother will be distracted from that which she nurses, and every pregnant woman will deliver her burden, and you will see people as though drunk, though they are not drunk — but the punishment of God is severe."* (Qur'an 22:1–2)

The phrase *every man among them on that Day will have a concern that suffices him* is the definitive statement of the condition this verse describes. There is no attention available for anyone else.

The tradition's practical conclusion is that the cure for that isolation must be acquired in this life. The person who has lived for himself arrives at that Day with nothing but himself, and finds it insufficient.

**The Theological Point About Excuses**

The verse makes a specific claim about human self-justification, and the commentators regard it as one of the most important in the sūrah.

The excuses that seemed so compelling in this life — the appeal to ancestry, the sophistication of one's objections, the reasonableness of one's doubts — are *not available* in that moment. Not because they are suppressed, but because they were never real. The person who rejected the truth for a reason will find the reason intact; the person who rejected it for a preference will find that the preference has no vocabulary.

The Qur'an states this with terrible clarity in the exchange of 6:27–28, where the condemned ask to be sent back and promise to believe, and God replies:

> *"And if they were sent back, they would return to that which they were forbidden; and truly they are liars."* (Qur'an 6:28)

The commentators note the function of such verses: they are given in this life so that the excuses can be examined *now*, in the one period in which they can be dismantled.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Build the answer now.* If the tidings will be obscured on that Day, then the defense — such as it is — must be constructed now. The tradition's term is *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* — righteous deed — and the Qur'an's consistent teaching is that the only thing that will answer for a person on that Day is what he sent ahead:

> *"And whatever good you send ahead for yourselves, you will find it with God."* (Qur'an 73:20)

*Do not depend on the crowd.* The people who cannot question one another on that Day are the same people who could not stop consulting one another in this one. The tradition's warning about social proof is constant:

> *"And if you obey most of those on earth, they will lead you astray from the way of God."* (Qur'an 6:116)

*And understand the stages.* The Qur'an's eschatology is not a single tableau but a sequence, and the sequence is meant to be understood. The tradition's scholars worked out the order — the Trumpet, the gathering, the presentation, the reckoning, the scale, the bridge, the intercession, and the abodes — and did so precisely because the Qur'an describes the Day in phases.

The sūrah's next verse opens a door in the middle of all this: the door of repentance, which is the only answer to the question of verse 65 that will be accepted.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:67

> **But for he who repented, believed, and worked righteousness, perchance he will be among those who prosper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Door That Opens in the Middle of the Judgment**

*Fa-ammā man tāba wa-āmana wa-ʿamila ṣāliḥan fa-ʿasā an yakūna mina al-mufliḥīn*.

After three verses of judgment and silence, the sūrah inserts a single verse about repentance. The commentators note the placement and its significance: the door is not closed by the scenes of verses 62–66, because those scenes describe a Day that has *not yet arrived*. For as long as a person is alive, this verse is the operative one.

**Three Conditions in Sequence**

The verse states three things, and the commentators are unanimous that they form a progression rather than a list of alternatives.

*Repented* — *tāba*. The verb is from *t-w-b*, to return, to turn back. Repentance in the Islamic tradition is not an emotion but a reversal of direction, and the scholars specified its conditions precisely, as summarized at verse 16: abandoning the sin, resolving not to return, and restoring the rights of others where rights were violated.

*Believed* — *āmana*. Faith, which in the tradition's definition is conviction in the heart, confession on the tongue, and works with the limbs.

*And worked righteousness* — *wa-ʿamila ṣāliḥan*. Deeds, and specifically *righteous* ones — the adjective *ṣāliḥ* denoting what is sound, fitting, and acceptable.

The commentators note that the three correspond to the whole of the religious life: the past is dealt with by repentance, the interior by faith, and the future by works. A person who has all three has covered every dimension of his existence.

The Qur'an pairs them repeatedly:

> *"And truly I am Most Forgiving unto him who repents, believes, works righteousness, and then remains guided."* (Qur'an 20:82)

> *"Save him who repents, believes, and works righteousness — for such, God will change their evil deeds into good deeds."* (Qur'an 25:70)

> *"And indeed, I am Most Forgiving toward him who repents and believes and does righteousness, then continues in guidance."* (Qur'an 20:82)

The last clause of 20:82 — *then remains guided* — is the tradition's standard addendum: the repentance must persist. A momentary turning followed by a return to the sin is not the repentance this verse describes.

**"Perchance He Will Be Among Those Who Prosper"**

*Fa-ʿasā an yakūna mina al-mufliḥīn*. As at verses 22 and 43, the particle *ʿasā* requires explanation, and the commentators give it.

*Al-Qurṭubī's rule*, which he states in several places: when *ʿasā* and *laʿalla* are used of God, they do not indicate doubt or mere possibility on God's part. They indicate either certainty (what God promises occurs) or a statement framed from the human perspective, where the outcome depends on the recipient's perseverance.

*Al-Rāzī's pastoral addition*: the phrasing is calculated to keep the believer between fear and hope. If the verse said *he will certainly be among the prosperous*, the presumptuous would rely on it. If it said *he will perhaps be*, the despairing would give up. The ambiguity is mercy, because it produces the state the tradition calls *khawf wa-rajāʾ* — fear and hope together — which the scholars held to be the believer's proper condition.

The tradition's classical formulation of the balance:

> *"The believer flies between fear and hope, like two wings of a bird. Fear without hope is despair; hope without fear is presumption."*

The commentators note that the Qur'an itself prescribes both:

> *"And call upon Him in fear and hope. Truly the mercy of God is near unto the doers of good."* (Qur'an 7:56)

*Al-Mufliḥīn* — the prosperous, the successful — is from *f-l-ḥ*, the same root discussed at verse 37, where the Qur'an stated that *the wrongdoers will not prosper*. The two verses are a matched pair: the wrongdoers fail, and the repentant succeed, and the same word measures both outcomes.

**The Verse's Placement After the Judgment**

The commentators note the rhetorical purpose of inserting this verse between verse 66 (silence and terror) and verse 68 (God's exclusive prerogative to choose).

The structure is: judgment described, then mercy offered, then doctrine stated. The mercy is not an interruption of the argument; it is the argument's point.

The tradition has always emphasized that the Qur'an's warnings are *preventive* rather than *retributive*. Every description of punishment in the Book is addressed to living people who can still avoid it. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"By Him in Whose hand is my soul, if you did not sin, God would take you away and bring a people who would sin and then seek God's forgiveness, and He would forgive them."* (Muslim 2749)

And in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"O son of Adam, as long as you call upon Me and hope in Me, I will forgive you for whatever has come from you, and I will not mind. O son of Adam, were your sins to reach the clouds of the sky and you then asked forgiveness of Me, I would forgive you."* (al-Tirmidhī 3540; Aḥmad)

**Repentance and the Question of Timing**

The commentators attach a condition that the Qur'an states elsewhere and that the tradition has never softened: repentance is available until death, and not after.

> *"Repentance is not for those who do evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says, 'Now I repent' — nor for those who die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 4:18)

> *"And when death comes to one of them, he says, 'My Lord, send me back, that I may work righteousness in that which I left behind.' Nay! It is only a word that he speaks."* (Qur'an 23:99–100)

The Prophet ﷺ described the closing of the door:

> *"God accepts the repentance of a servant as long as the soul has not reached the throat."* (al-Tirmidhī 3537; Ibn Mājah 4253 — graded ḥasan)

and:

> *"God extends His hand during the night so that the sinner of the day may repent, and extends His hand during the day so that the sinner of the night may repent, until the sun rises from its west."* (Muslim 2759)

The tradition has always read the last ḥadīth as establishing that the door is open *now*, without exception, and the "rising of the sun from the west" as one of the major signs after which repentance no longer avails.

**Theological Note: Can Repentance Be Genuine Without Works?**

The verse's third condition — *worked righteousness* — raised a question the commentators addressed: is a repentance that produces no change in conduct real?

The tradition's answer, consistent across the schools, is that genuine repentance necessarily produces works, and that a claim of repentance unaccompanied by any change is not the repentance this verse promises for. The evidence is the pairing itself: the Qur'an almost never mentions faith without works.

> *"By time! Truly man is in loss — save those who believe and work righteousness, and counsel one another to truth and counsel one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

That sūrah is the tradition's proof-text for the rule: faith without works, and works without faith, are both loss; the two together are the condition.

The commentators are careful, however, not to make works a *price*. The person who repents is forgiven by God's grace; the works are the evidence and the fruit, not the payment. The tradition states it as:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds."* They said, "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said, *"Not even me, unless God envelops me in His mercy and grace."* (al-Bukhārī 5673; Muslim 2816)

**Application**

For the reader, this verse is the sūrah's most direct offer, and its structure is usable.

*Repent* — deal with the past honestly, without minimization, and with the intention not to return.
*Believe* — establish the interior state, and keep it established.
*Work righteousness* — let the change become visible in conduct.
*And hope without presuming* — the *perchance* is not a loophole but a posture.

The tradition's counsel is to repeat this cycle continuously rather than to perform it once. The Prophet ﷺ, who was forgiven, sought forgiveness constantly:

> *"By God, I seek forgiveness from God and turn to Him in repentance more than seventy times a day."* (al-Bukhārī 6307)

And he taught his community a formula that compresses this verse's three conditions into a life:

> *"O God, You are my Lord; there is no god but You. You created me and I am Your servant, and I am upon Your covenant and promise as much as I am able. I seek refuge in You from the evil of what I have done. I acknowledge Your blessing upon me, and I acknowledge my sin; so forgive me, for none forgives sins but You."* (al-Bukhārī 6306)

That supplication — the *sayyid al-istighfār*, the master prayer of forgiveness — is the tradition's daily form of this verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:68

> **And thy Lord creates what He will, and chooses; no choice have they. Glory be to God and exalted is He above the partners they ascribe!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of Divine Choice**

*Wa-rabbuka yakhluqu mā yashāʾu wa-yakhtāru mā kāna lahumu al-khiyaratu subhāna Allāhi wa-taʿālā ʿammā yushrikūn*.

The commentators identify this as one of the primary Qur'anic texts on God's exclusive prerogative to choose — particularly in the matter of prophethood, which is the sūrah's subject throughout.

**The Historical Occasion**

The commentators are unanimous that this verse answers a specific objection, and they record two versions of it.

*The Quraysh's objection:* that a man of greater wealth and social standing than Muhammad ﷺ would have been more appropriate for prophethood. The Qur'an records this:

> *"And they said, 'Why was this Qur'an not sent down to a great man from one of the two cities?'"* (Qur'an 43:31)

The two cities were Makkah and Ṭāʾif, and the "great men" referred to were the wealthiest and most powerful men in each.

*The Jews' objection:* that the prophet should have been a Jew rather than an Arab — an objection the Qur'an records in several forms:

> *"And when there came to them a Book from God, confirming what was with them... yet when there came to them that which they recognized, they disbelieved in it."* (Qur'an 2:89)

> *"And they say, 'Why was he not given the like of that which was given to Moses?'"* (Qur'an 28:48 — in this very sūrah)

The verse's answer to both objections is the same and is stated in three words: *God chooses*.

**"Thy Lord Creates What He Will, and Chooses"**

*Wa-rabbuka yakhluqu mā yashāʾu wa-yakhtāru*. Two verbs, and the commentators distinguish them.

*Yakhluqu* — He creates, He originates. This is God's act of bringing into existence whatever He wills.

*Yakhtāru* — He chooses, He selects, He elects. This is God's act of singling out from among what He has created.

The sequence matters: creation precedes choice, and choice is an act within creation. God is not selecting among co-eternal alternatives; He creates everything and then elects among His creatures whom He wills for what He wills.

The commentators note the word *rabbuka* — *thy Lord* — with which the verse opens. The address to the Prophet ﷺ is a consolation: the One who chose you is your Lord, and the choice is His to make.

**"No Choice Have They"**

*Mā kāna lahumu al-khiyaratu*. The negation is emphatic: it is not for them to choose.

Al-Qurṭubī discusses the grammatical form at some length, because it bears on the verse's scope. The construction *mā kāna lahum* uses the verb *kāna*, which can indicate past tense — and al-Qurṭubī notes that a literal reading might suggest that they had no choice *in the past* but would have one in the future, which is not the intended meaning. He therefore follows the grammarians in holding that *kāna* here indicates the *state* of the matter rather than a temporal frame: the absence of choice is a permanent feature of the creature's position, not a temporary one.

The commentators draw the distinction precisely. Human beings have real choice in *what they do* — they are commanded, they obey or disobey, and they are rewarded or punished accordingly. What they do not have is choice in *what God decrees*: who is born where, to whom, with what capacities; who is sent as a messenger; what the terms of revelation are; and how the unseen is apportioned.

The verse is therefore not a denial of human agency. It is a denial of human *veto*.

**Al-Ṭabarī's Alternative Reading**

The commentators record a grammatical variant that al-Ṭabarī preferred, and it is worth stating because it shows the tradition's tolerance for interpretive diversity within fixed bounds.

Al-Ṭabarī read the verse as: *"Thy Lord creates what He will, and chooses the choicest of what they have"* — taking *mā* as relative rather than negative. On this reading, the verse means that God selects the best of what human beings possess for Himself, and the sense is one of honor rather than exclusion.

Al-Qurṭubī records al-Ṭabarī's preference and disagrees, on the grounds that the negative reading is the majority one, fits the context of the objection (who deserves to be prophet?), and is supported by the parallel usage elsewhere.

The tradition's practice here is instructive: a respected imam's reading is recorded, the disagreement is stated with reasons, and neither is treated as heretical.

**What God Chooses: The Classical Enumerations**

The commentators asked what, specifically, this verse says God chooses, and their answers cover the range of the doctrine.

*Prophets and messengers.* The primary referent in context, and the one the majority adopt:

> *"God chooses messengers from among the angels and from among mankind."* (Qur'an 22:75)

> *"Say: 'Had God willed, I would not have recited it unto you, nor would He have made it known to you.'"* (Qur'an 10:16)

*Intercession.* Al-Qurṭubī records this reading: that no one may intercede except by God's choice:

> *"Say: 'Unto God belongs intercession entirely.'"* (Qur'an 39:44)

*And everything whatever.* Some commentators read the verse at its widest: that in all matters, the decision belongs to God and not to the creature:

> *"And it is not for a believing man or a believing woman, when God and His Messenger have decided a matter, to have any choice in their affair."* (Qur'an 33:36)

That verse — revealed concerning the Prophet's arrangement of a marriage for Zaynab bint Jaḥsh — is the clearest statement of the principle in its general form, and the commentators cite it as the verse's natural expansion.

**The Theological Framework: *Qadar* and Human Responsibility**

As throughout the sūrah, the commentators use this verse to state the tradition's position on divine decree.

*God wills and creates everything*, including the acts of His creatures:

> *"God created you and what you do."* (Qur'an 37:96)

> *"And God has created you and what you make."*

*The creature acts, chooses, and is responsible*:

> *"Whosoever wills, let him believe; and whosoever wills, let him disbelieve."* (Qur'an 18:29)

*And the two are reconciled* by the doctrine of *kasb* — acquisition — formulated by the Ashʿarī school and by the equivalent formulations of the Māturīdiyyah: God creates the act, and the human being acquires it by his intention and will, and is therefore accountable for it.

The tradition's pastoral rule, repeated by every school: believe both, do not try to resolve the tension by denying either side, and act as though everything depends on you while trusting that everything is decreed. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Work, for everyone is facilitated toward that for which he was created."* Then he recited: *"As for him who gives and is reverent, and affirms the best — We shall ease him toward ease. But as for him who is miserly and deems himself self-sufficient, and denies the best — We shall ease him toward difficulty."* (al-Bukhārī 4949; Muslim 2647)

**The Spiritual Reading**

Ibn ʿAjībah and the spiritual commentators take the verse inwardly, and their reading is among the most practically useful in the tradition.

On this reading, the verse describes the proper relationship between the servant's will and God's. The creature's *khiyarah* — his power of choosing — is not denied but relocated: it is exercised in *submitting* to what God has chosen rather than in dictating to God what He should choose.

Ibn ʿAjībah quotes the saying of his master Abu'l-Ḥasan al-Shādhilī:

> *"Forsake the directing of things; and if you must direct something, then direct yourself not to direct things."*

The commentators note the practical content of this. Most of a person's suffering comes from trying to manage outcomes that were never his to manage — what other people do, what the future holds, what God has decreed. The discipline is to make one choice well (submission) rather than to make a thousand choices badly (control).

The tradition's formal expression of this is *istiqāmah* — steadfastness in what has been chosen for you — and the Prophet's instruction:

> *"Say: 'I believe in God,' and then be steadfast."* (Muslim 38)

**"Glory Be to God, and Exalted Is He Above the Partners They Ascribe"**

*Subhāna Allāhi wa-taʿālā ʿammā yushrikūn*. The verse closes with the formula of *tanzīh* — the declaration of God's absolute transcendence.

*Subhān Allāh*: glory be to God — the word denotes removing from God every imperfection and every likeness to His creation.

*Taʿālā*: exalted, high above — the same root as the *istikbār* condemned at verse 39, here applied to God in the only way it can properly be applied.

*ʿAmmā yushrikūn*: above what they associate with Him.

The commentators note that the closing formula is not decorative. The verse has just asserted God's exclusive right to choose, and that assertion is exactly what *shirk* denies — because the idolater's claim is, in the end, a claim to choose: to decide what shall be worshipped, how, and through what intermediary.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Do not dictate to God.* The objections this verse answers — a prophet should have been richer, should have been from our people, should have come with different signs — are all versions of the same error: telling God what He should have done. The tradition's cure is the discipline of accepting the decree, including the parts of it one would not have chosen.

*Do not despise what God has chosen.* If God chose an orphaned, unlettered merchant of Makkah as the final messenger, then the criteria by which human beings rank one another are not His criteria. The tradition has always drawn from this the dignity of the lowly:

> *"O mankind, truly We have created you from a male and a female, and We have made you into peoples and tribes that you may know one another. Truly the most noble of you in the sight of God is the most reverent of you."* (Qur'an 49:13)

*And choose what He has chosen.* The servant's freedom is exercised in alignment, not in negotiation. The tradition's most concise statement of this is the verse's own closing movement: from *no choice have they* to *glory be to God* — the recognition of God's exclusive prerogative is not a grievance but worship.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:69

> **And thy Lord knows what their breasts conceal and what they disclose.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Follow-Up to the Doctrine of Choice**

*Wa-rabbuka yaʿlamu mā tukinnu ṣudūruhum wa-mā yuʿlinūn*.

The verse follows directly on verse 68's assertion that God alone chooses. The commentators note the connection: having established that the choice is His, the verse establishes that He *knows* — and therefore that His choice is not arbitrary, and that the objections to it are seen for what they are.

**"What Their Breasts Conceal"**

*Mā tukinnu ṣudūruhum*. The verb *akanna* means to conceal, to hide, to keep secret within. The noun *ṣudūr* is the plural of *ṣadr*, the breast — and the commentators note that in Qur'anic usage, the breast is consistently identified as the seat of the concealed self: intentions, doubts, resentments, envy, secret beliefs, and everything a person does not say.

The Qur'an uses this vocabulary with remarkable frequency, and the commentators collected the instances:

> *"Truly God knows whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth; and God is Knower of all things."* (Qur'an 49:16)

> *"And He knows what is in the heavens and the earth, and He knows what you conceal and what you disclose; and God knows whatsoever is in the breasts."* (Qur'an 64:4)

> *"And truly We have created man, and We know what his soul whispers to him; and We are nearer to him than his jugular vein."* (Qur'an 50:16)

> *"And know that God comes between a man and his heart, and that unto Him you shall be gathered."* (Qur'an 8:24)

That last verse is the most theologically charged of the group, and the commentators gave it careful treatment: God's knowledge and power are such that He stands between a person and his own heart — meaning that no thought is hidden from Him and no intention is outside His governance.

**"And What They Disclose"**

*Wa-mā yuʿlinūn* — *and what they make public*. The pairing of the concealed and the disclosed is the Qur'an's standard formulation for the completeness of divine knowledge, and it recurs constantly:

> *"And whether you conceal what is in your breasts or disclose it, God knows it; and He knows whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth."* (Qur'an 3:29)

> *"And thy Lord knows what their breasts conceal and what they disclose."* (Qur'an 27:74 — the exact parallel to this verse)

The commentators note the rhetorical completeness: nothing a person is falls outside the pair. What he hides and what he shows exhaust the categories, and God knows both.

**The Specific Referent: The Quraysh's Hidden Motives**

The commentators identify what was being concealed in this particular context, and it is the sūrah's own diagnosis.

The Quraysh's *outward* objection was religious: they asked for a different messenger (v. 48), accused the revelation of sorcery (v. 48), and cited ancestral custom (v. 36).

What their *breasts concealed* was, as al-Rāzī puts it, enmity toward the Prophet ﷺ and contempt for God's choice of him. And as verse 57 had put it more bluntly, fear of losing their position:

> *"If we follow guidance along with thee, we shall be snatched away from our land."* (Qur'an 28:57)

The commentators note the precision of the Qur'an's exposure. The objections were not honest questions about evidence; they were the public form of a private refusal, and the verse states that God sees the private one.

This is the Qur'an's consistent method with insincerity, and it recurs in every generation's form of the same posture:

> *"And when they meet you, they say, 'We believe'; but when they are alone, they bite their fingertips at you in rage. Say: 'Die in your rage! Truly God knows whatsoever is in the breasts.'"* (Qur'an 3:119)

**Theological Note: The Doctrine of Divine Knowledge**

The verse is one of the foundations of the Islamic doctrine of God's knowledge (*ʿilm*), and the commentators specify what it establishes.

*God's knowledge is comprehensive.* Nothing is outside it — not the hidden, not the manifest, not the past, not the future, not the possible:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them but He. And He knows whatsoever is on the land and in the sea; not a leaf falls but that He knows it."* (Qur'an 6:59)

> *"And they ask thee concerning the Spirit..."* — no; the relevant text is:

> *"Say: 'Whether you conceal what is in your breasts or disclose it, God knows it.'"* (Qur'an 3:29)

*God's knowledge is not acquired.* It is an eternal attribute. The tradition is emphatic on this point against those who held that God knows things only after they occur:

> *"Truly God is Knower of the unseen things of the heavens and the earth. Truly He knows whatsoever is in the breasts."* (Qur'an 35:38)

*And God's knowledge is the basis of the reckoning.* What He knows is what will be presented:

> *"On the Day when every soul will find present what it has done of good, and what it has done of evil..."* (Qur'an 3:30)

**The Pastoral Double Edge**

The commentators note that this verse cuts in two directions, and the tradition has always preached both.

*For the hypocrite and the concealed sinner*, it is a warning. Nothing is hidden. The sin that no human being knows about is fully known, and the intention behind the good deed is equally visible:

> *"And they will say to their skins, 'Why did you bear witness against us?'"* (Qur'an 41:21)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Truly God does not look at your forms and your wealth, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds."* (Muslim 2564)

*For the believer who suffers unseen*, it is a consolation of the first order. The patience no one noticed, the charity no one saw, the restraint no one credited, the intention no one understood — all of it is known and all of it is recorded.

> *"And whosoever does good, be it the weight of an atom, shall see it; and whosoever does evil, be it the weight of an atom, shall see it."* (Qur'an 99:7–8)

The tradition has always placed enormous weight on the *niyyah* — intention — for exactly this reason. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Deeds are only by intentions, and every person shall have only what he intended."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907)

The ḥadīth is the first in al-Bukhārī's collection precisely because it establishes that the inner life is the real object of the divine gaze.

**The Practice of *Ikhlāṣ***

The verse is one of the primary motivations for *ikhlāṣ* — sincerity — which the tradition defines as purifying the intention so that the deed is for God alone.

The scholars treated sincerity as the most difficult of the spiritual disciplines precisely because the defect lies in the *breast* — in what is hidden — and therefore cannot be corrected by external effort alone. The tradition's diagnosis of the disease is famous:

> The Prophet ﷺ said: *"The thing I fear most for you is the lesser shirk."* They asked, "What is the lesser shirk, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Showing off (riyāʾ). God will say on the Day of Resurrection, when people are rewarded for their deeds: 'Go to those for whom you used to show off in the world, and see whether you find any reward with them.'"* (Aḥmad 23680 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The commentators note that *riyāʾ* is the specific vice this verse targets: a person does the visible deed for a human audience while the breast holds something else. God sees the breast.

The tradition's remedy is not to abandon good deeds but to guard the intention, and the scholars prescribed specific practices: concealing voluntary acts where possible, being wary of the pleasure of being seen, and returning to the intention before, during, and after the act.

**Application**

Verse 69 is short, and its practical content can be stated in one sentence: live as though everything were visible, because it is.

The tradition drew two habits from it.

*The first is inward honesty with God.* Since the breast is known, the only coherent response is to stop maintaining a gap between what one shows and what one is. That gap — the tradition's term is *nifāq*, hypocrisy — is the condition this verse exposes, and it is cured by bringing the interior into line with the exterior.
