*The second is the consolation of the unnoticed.* Every person carries acts of obedience that no one saw and pain that no one acknowledged. This verse says that neither is lost. The tradition's constant counsel to the discouraged is a version of this: God sees, and that is enough.

And there is a third, quieter application for those who have been *misjudged*. The Quraysh's public objections were believed by their audience; their concealed motives were known to God. For anyone whose intentions have been misread, this verse is the reassurance that the record kept by the One who knows is the record that will be read out.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:70

> **And He is God; there is no god but He! To Him belongs praise at the beginning and at the end. To Him belongs judgment, and unto Him shall you be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Creedal Center**

*Wa-huwa Allāhu lā ilāha illā huwa lahu al-ḥamdu fī al-ūlā wa-al-ākhirati wa-lahu al-ḥukmu wa-ilayhi turjaʿūn*.

The commentators regard this verse as one of the most comprehensive creedal statements in the Qur'an, and they note the sūrah has placed it at the exact center of its argument: after the declaration of God's exclusive choice (v. 68) and knowledge (v. 69), and before the signs of night and day (vv. 71–73).

Four claims are made, and each is a pillar.

**"And He Is God; There Is No God But He"**

*Wa-huwa Allāhu lā ilāha illā huwa*. The declaration is the first half of the testimony of faith (*shahādah*), and the commentators note its two-part structure: an affirmation and a negation.

*The affirmation*: *He is God* — *Allāh*, the proper name of the Divine Essence, the name that belongs to no other being and that no Muslim hears as anything but the name of the One.

*The negation and re-affirmation*: *there is no god but He* — *lā ilāha illā huwa*. The formula does not merely say "there is one God"; it denies every object of worship and then excepts God alone.

The commentators stress the grammatical point. *Lā ilāha* is an absolute negation of *ilāh* — anything worshipped, anything treated as the ultimate object of trust, fear, hope, and obedience. The exception *illā huwa* then reinstalls God alone in that position.

This is the heart of *tawḥīd*, and the tradition's scholars divided it into three aspects, each with its own textual basis:

*Tawḥīd al-rubūbiyyah* — the oneness of lordship: God alone creates, sustains, and governs:

> *"Say: 'Who provides for you from the heaven and the earth?...' They will say, 'God.'"* (Qur'an 10:31)

*Tawḥīd al-ulūhiyyah* — the oneness of worship: God alone is to be worshipped. This is the aspect the prophets were principally sent to establish, and it is the aspect the idolaters of every age have denied while affirming the first:

> *"And We sent no messenger before thee but that We revealed to him: 'There is no god but I, so worship Me.'"* (Qur'an 21:25)

*Tawḥīd al-asmāʾ wa-l-ṣifāt* — the oneness of names and attributes: God is described by what He has described Himself with, without likening Him to creation, without denying the attribute, and without asking how:

> *"There is nothing like unto Him; and He is the Hearing, the Seeing."* (Qur'an 42:11)

The verse before us states the second aspect directly and implies the first and third.

**"To Him Belongs Praise at the Beginning and at the End"**

*Lahu al-ḥamdu fī al-ūlā wa-al-ākhirah*. The commentators discuss the meaning of *al-ūlā* and *al-ākhirah* here, and they record two readings.

*The temporal reading*: *al-ūlā* is this world (the first) and *al-ākhirah* is the Hereafter (the last). On this reading, the verse states that praise belongs to God in both lives — now and forever.

*The attribute reading*: *al-Awwal* and *al-Ākhir* are divine names — *the First* and *the Last* — and the verse means that praise belongs to Him who was before all things and will be after all things:

> *"He is the First and the Last, and the Outward and the Inward; and He is Knower of all things."* (Qur'an 57:3)

The commentators note that both readings are true and that the verse's deliberate wording holds them together. The words are the same words the Qur'an uses for the two lives, and they are also two of God's names.

The theological content is that praise is *not circumstantial*. God is praised in prosperity and adversity, in this world and the next, and the praise is not a response to His gifts but a recognition of who He is. The tradition expresses this in the formula the Prophet ﷺ taught:

> *"Praise be to God in every circumstance."* (Ibn Mājah 3803 — graded ḥasan)

And in his own practice: when something pleased him he said *al-ḥamdu lillāhi alladhī bi-niʿmatihi tatimm al-ṣāliḥāt* ("praise be to God by Whose blessing good things are completed"), and when something displeased him he said *al-ḥamdu lillāhi ʿalā kulli ḥāl* ("praise be to God in every circumstance").

The commentators also note the correspondence with the opening of the Fātiḥah — *praise be to God, Lord of the worlds* — and with the closing of the people of the Garden:

> *"And their last call will be: 'Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds!'"* (Qur'an 10:10)

Praise opens and praise closes, and the One praised is the same.

**"To Him Belongs Judgment"**

*Wa-lahu al-ḥukmu*. The word *ḥukm* has a range that the commentators enumerate: judgment, decision, rule, authority, wisdom, governance.

*In this world*: all legitimate authority is a function of His authority. The Qur'an states the principle that governance belongs to God and is delegated, not owned:

> *"Judgment belongs to none but God. He has commanded that you worship none but Him."* (Qur'an 12:40)

> *"And whosoever does not judge by what God has sent down — those are the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 5:44)

*In the Hereafter*: God judges directly, and no delegation remains:

> *"And God will judge between you on the Day of Resurrection concerning that wherein you used to differ."* (Qur'an 22:69)

> *"The sovereignty on that Day will belong to God; He will judge between them."* (Qur'an 22:56)

The commentators note the serious implication of this clause for human claims to ultimate authority. Every legal system, every ruler, every institution that claims to be the final arbiter of good and evil is asserting a *ḥukm* that this verse reserves for God. The tradition's position is that human judgment is legitimate precisely to the extent that it derives from and submits to His.

**"And Unto Him Shall You Be Returned"**

*Wa-ilayhi turjaʿūn*. The closing clause completes the verse's arc: from His exclusive divinity, to His exclusive praise, to His exclusive judgment, to the return of everything to Him.

The word *turjaʿūn* — *you will be returned* — is the standard Qur'anic term for the Resurrection, and its recurrence is one of the Book's constants:

> *"Truly unto your Lord is the return."* (Qur'an 96:8)

> *"And unto Him is the return of all of you — the promise of God in truth."* (Qur'an 10:4)

> *"O mankind! Truly you are laboring toward your Lord with [great] exertion, and you will meet Him."* (Qur'an 84:6)

The commentators note that the return is the keystone of the entire moral structure established by this verse. If nothing returns to God, then His judgment is not final and His praise is not universal. The return is what makes both true.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Overall Structure**

The commentators note that verse 70 is the sūrah's theological summit, and that everything in the sūrah can be read as an exposition of it.

*He is God* — established against Pharaoh's claim (*I am your lord most high*, 79:24; *I know of no god for you other than myself*, 28:38).
*Praise at the beginning and the end* — established through the stories of deliverance, which begin in terror and end in praise.
*His is the judgment* — established through the destruction of Pharaoh (v. 40) and the coming destruction of Korah (v. 81).
*And to Him you will be returned* — established through the scenes of the Resurrection at verses 62–66, and stated again at the very last verse of the sūrah (v. 88).

**Application**

The verse is a complete creed in four lines, and the tradition has used it in three ways.

*As worship.* The verse's statements are recited constantly in the Muslim's day — in the prayer, in the remembrance after it, in the morning and evening invocations. The believer repeats them because repetition forms the heart.

*As a test of priorities.* The clause *to Him belongs praise at the beginning and at the end* settles the question of where gratitude is due, in both directions of fortune. The tradition's practice is to praise in both, and the discipline of doing so is what dissolves both arrogance and despair.

*As a check on authority.* *To Him belongs judgment* is a standing limitation on every human claim to final say — including one's own. The tradition has applied it to rulers, to scholars, to cultures, and most importantly to the self: my judgment is not the final judgment, and my assessment is not the verdict.

And the closing clause supplies the horizon within which all of the sūrah's dramas are to be read: *unto Him shall you be returned*. Pharaoh's power, Korah's treasure, Moses' mission, the Prophet's exile, the Quraysh's objections, and the reader's own life are all moving toward the same return.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:71

> **Say, "Have you considered: if God should make night come over you unceasingly until the Day of Resurrection, what god other than God would bring you light? Will you not, then, listen?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First of the Two Signs of Time**

*Qul a-raʾaytum in jaʿala Allāhu ʿalaykumu al-layla sarmadan ilā yawmi al-qiyāmati man ilāhun ghayru Allāhi yaʾtīkum bi-ḍiyāʾin a-fa-lā tasmaʿūn*.

The sūrah now turns from doctrine to a different kind of argument: an argument from ordinary experience. The commentators note the method — the Qur'an asks its hearers to imagine the removal of something so familiar that no one thinks about it, and then asks what would happen.

**"Have You Considered" — The Qur'an's Method of Defamiliarization**

*A-raʾaytum* — *have you seen?*, *have you considered?* The phrase recurs throughout the Qur'an as the opening of an argument from experience:

> *"Say: 'Have you considered: if your water were to become sunken [into the earth], who would bring you flowing water?'"* (Qur'an 67:30)

> *"Say: 'Have you considered: if God took away your hearing and your sight, and set a seal upon your hearts, what god other than God would restore them to you?'"* (Qur'an 6:46)

The commentators note the psychological technique. Human beings do not perceive what is constant. The person who has never lost the alternation of night and day has never *noticed* it, and gratitude requires noticing. By asking the hearer to imagine its removal, the Qur'an restores the perception.

This is one of the most frequently employed Qur'anic methods, and the tradition has always regarded it as an argument from *gratitude* rather than from *design* alone: the point is not only that the arrangement shows a Designer, but that it shows a Giver to whom thanks are owed.

**"If God Should Make Night Come Over You Unceasingly"**

*In jaʿala Allāhu ʿalaykumu al-layla sarmadan*. The word *sarmad* means perpetual, unceasing, without break. The commentators note that the word carries a philosophical resonance in later Islamic thought, where *sarmad* came to denote a specific kind of eternity — timeless duration as opposed to beginningless duration (*azal*) — and they are careful to note that in the Qur'anic text the word simply means *continuous without interruption*.

The hypothetical is stark: imagine that night never ends, until the Resurrection.

The commentators note the physical consequences that the argument assumes: no agriculture, no travel, no work of any kind that requires sight; the collapse of the food supply within a season; and, in the ancient world, the end of everything that made organized life possible. The Qur'an states the arrangement it is asking about elsewhere:

> *"And it is He Who has made the night for you as a garment, and sleep for rest, and has made the day for rising."* (Qur'an 25:47)

> *"And We have made the day for livelihood."* (Qur'an 78:11)

The second of those verses is the direct counterpart: the day is the *maʿāsh*, the time of earning a living. Remove it andlivelihood ends.

**"What God Other Than God Would Bring You Light?"**

*Man ilāhun ghayru Allāhi yaʾtīkum bi-ḍiyāʾin*. The question has a logical structure the commentators lay out.

*The premise*: the alternation of night and day is real and is beneficial.
*The observation*: the idols the Quraysh worshipped did not produce it, and no human being can.
*The conclusion*: the One who produces it is the One who deserves to be worshipped.

The Qur'an makes the argument explicitly in the parallel passages:

> *"Say: 'Have you considered that which you invoke apart from God? Show me what they have created of the earth; or do they have any share in the heavens?'"* (Qur'an 46:4)

> *"Say: 'Who provides for you from the heaven and the earth? Or who owns hearing and sight? And who brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living? And who governs the affair?' They will say, 'God.' Then say: 'Will you not then be reverent?'"* (Qur'an 10:31)

The last verse is the key to the method: the Qur'an asks a question the idolaters *already know the answer to* — they conceded that God created and governed — and then asks why they worship others anyway.

**"Will You Not, Then, Listen?"**

*A-fa-lā tasmaʿūn* — *will you not hear?* The commentators note the correspondence between the sense invoked and the sign described: the verse is about the *absence of light*, and the faculty it appeals to is *hearing*.

Verse 72 will be about the absence of night, and the faculty it appeals to is *sight*. The pairing is deliberate, and the commentators regard it as a small instance of the Qur'an's rhetorical precision: the sense of each verse matches the condition described in it.

The appeal to hearing is also the Qur'an's standard idiom for *receiving the message*:

> *"And if you obey most of those on earth, they will lead you astray from the way of God."* (Qur'an 6:116)

> *"Have they not heard the word, or has not the reminder come to them?"* — the usage is frequent.

The tradition notes the distinction the Qur'an maintains between *hearing* (*samʿ*) and *listening with acceptance* (*istimāʿ*). The verse is not asking whether the Quraysh had auditory capacity; it is asking why they do not *hear* in the sense that matters. The Qur'an states the distinction itself:

> *"Truly you cannot make the dead hear, nor can you make the deaf hear the call when they turn their backs in retreat."* (Qur'an 27:80)

**The Cosmic Arrangement as a Standing Sign**

The commentators place this verse within the large body of Qur'anic verses on the alternation of night and day, and they note that the Qur'an returns to it constantly — a fact that is itself part of the argument:

> *"Truly in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and in the alternation of night and day, are signs for those possessed of understanding."* (Qur'an 3:190)

> *"And it is He Who made the night and the day in succession for him who desires to remember or desires gratitude."* (Qur'an 25:62)

> *"He causes the night to enter into the day, and causes the day to enter into the night, and has made subject the sun and the moon, each running to an appointed term. That is God, your Lord; His is the sovereignty."* (Qur'an 35:13)

The last clause of that verse is the conclusion the sūrah is drawing: *His is the sovereignty*.

**The Scientific Reading and Its Limits**

Modern readers often notice that the Qur'an's descriptions of the cosmic order correspond to what science has since established — the earth's rotation, the gradual interpenetration of night and day, the regularity of celestial motion.

The commentators' tradition has always read such verses in two registers, and both are legitimate:

*As signs of meaning.* The primary purpose is not to teach astronomy but to awaken gratitude and to establish that the order is given rather than necessary. The Qur'an's argument is that the arrangement *could have been otherwise* — which is precisely what the hypothetical in this verse establishes — and that its being as it is points to a chooser.

*As objects of study.* The Qur'an commands observation of the natural order, and the Islamic scientific tradition developed within that frame:

> *"Say: 'Travel through the land and observe how He originated creation.'"* (Qur'an 29:20)

> *"Do they not look at the camels, how they are created? And at the heaven, how it is raised?"* (Qur'an 88:17–18)

The caution the tradition maintains is that scientific readings of Qur'anic verses should not be asserted as the verse's *meaning*, since the meaning is fixed while scientific understanding changes. The signhood is permanent; the interpretation of the mechanism is provisional.

**Application**

The verse's most transferable content is the discipline of *imagined removal*, which the tradition has always found to be one of the most effective spiritual exercises.

*Take something constant and imagine it gone.* Health, sight, a relationship, a steady income, the ability to sleep, the freedom to pray in public, clean water, a functioning mind. Most people can do this exercise for ten seconds and feel the shock of a gratitude they had not been experiencing.

The Prophet ﷺ prescribed the practice in a specific form:

> *"Whoever among you wakes up secure in his dwelling, healthy in his body, having his food for the day — it is as though the whole world has been gathered for him."* (al-Tirmidhī 2346; Ibn Mājah 4141)

And he gave the morning and evening formula that pairs blessing with its removal:

> *"O God, whatever blessing has come to me or to any of Your creation this morning is from You alone; You have no partner. To You belongs praise and to You belongs thanks."* (Abū Dāwūd 5073; Ibn Mājah 3870)

There is a second application concerning the *scale* of the sign. The alternation of day and night is the most public, most universal fact of human experience — available to every person in every century and of every level of education. The Qur'an's choice of it as proof establishes that the evidence for God is not reserved for specialists. Every human being has access to a sign he cannot explain away, and the question at the end of the verse is therefore fair: *will you not listen?*

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:72

> **Say, "Have you considered: if God should make day come over you unceasingly until the Day of Resurrection, what god other than God would bring you night, that you might rest therein? Will you not, then, see?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Complementary Sign**

*Qul a-raʾaytum in jaʿala Allāhu ʿalaykumu al-nahāra sarmadan ilā yawmi al-qiyāmati man ilāhun ghayru Allāhi yaʾtīkum bi-laylin taskunūna fīhi a-fa-lā tubṣirūn*.

The verse is the mirror of verse 71, and the commentators note the deliberate symmetry. Verse 71 imagined perpetual night and asked who would bring light. This verse imagines perpetual day and asks who would bring rest.

The two verses together establish the argument in its completeness: the alternation itself is the sign, and neither half of the cycle is dispensable.

**The Structure of the Pair**

The commentators lay out the correspondence, and it is exact:

*Verse 71*: perpetual night → who brings *light* (ḍiyāʾ)? → *will you not listen?*
*Verse 72*: perpetual day → who brings *night for rest*? → *will you not see?*

As noted at verse 71, the sense invoked in each verse matches the condition described: darkness makes hearing the primary faculty, and light makes sight primary. The Qur'an is drawing attention to the fact that both faculties, like both halves of the day, are given.

**"That You Might Rest Therein"**

*Taskunūna fīhi* — *that you may be still, at rest, settled in it*. The verb *sakana* means to be still, to dwell, to find repose. From it come *sukūn* (stillness) and *sakīnah* (tranquility).

The commentators note that the Qur'an identifies *rest* as the specific purpose of the night, and they set this verse beside its many parallels:

> *"And it is He Who has made the night for you as a garment, and sleep for rest, and has made the day for rising."* (Qur'an 25:47)

> *"And of His signs is your sleep by night and by day, and your seeking of His bounty. Truly in that are signs for a people who hear."* (Qur'an 30:23)

> *"And We have made your sleep for rest, and We have made the night a covering, and We have made the day for livelihood."* (Qur'an 78:9–11)

The last of those is the clearest statement of the division of labor: *sleep for rest* (*subātan*), *night as covering* (*libāsan*), *day for livelihood* (*maʿāshan*).

**The Argument From Rest**

There is a distinct argument in this verse that the commentators draw out, and it is worth stating because it is not the same as the argument of verse 71.

Verse 71's argument was about *production* — without light, nothing can be done.

This verse's argument is about *recovery* — without darkness, nothing can be repaired.

The commentators note the Qur'an's precision here. Rest is not merely the absence of work; it is a distinct good with its own purpose. Sleep is described as a sign in its own right:

> *"And among His signs is your sleep by night and by day."* (Qur'an 30:23)

The tradition has drawn from this the principle that rest is not idleness but a *created purpose* — that the human being was made to need restoration, and that the need is itself part of the design rather than a defect.

The modern confirmation of this is substantial: sleep is now understood to be essential to memory consolidation, immune function, metabolic regulation, and psychological health. The commentators would not have known the mechanisms, but they knew the argument: a world without rest is unlivable, and the One who built rest into the structure of time is the One who deserves gratitude.

**"Will You Not, Then, See?"**

*A-fa-lā tubṣirūn*. As at verse 71, the question is not about optical capacity but about perception.

The Qur'an uses this verb for the recognition of signs:

> *"Do they not look at the camels, how they are created? And at the heaven, how it is raised? And at the mountains, how they are set up? And at the earth, how it is spread out? So remind; you are only a reminder."* (Qur'an 88:17–21)

> *"Have they not looked at the sky above them — how We have built it and adorned it, and it has no rifts?"* (Qur'an 50:6)

The tradition's position, stated throughout the commentaries, is that *baṣar* (sight) and *baṣīrah* (insight) are related in Arabic because they are related in reality: the same faculty that registers the sign should register what the sign points to. The failure to do so is described in the Qur'an as a condition of the heart rather than of the eye:

> *"Truly it is not the eyes that are blind, but blind are the hearts within the breasts."* (Qur'an 22:46)

**The Cumulative Argument of the Two Verses**

Set together, the two verses make an argument by *exhaustion of alternatives*, and the commentators lay it out.

*If the night were perpetual*, you could not work, and you would die.
*If the day were perpetual*, you could not rest, and you would die.
*The cycle is therefore necessary*, and it is not self-explaining: nothing in the nature of a rotating planet requires that the rotation produce conditions suited to human life, work, and recovery.

The Qur'an's argument is not that the alternation is *improbable* in a statistical sense; it is that the alternation is *beneficial in a way that matches human need exactly*, and that such matching is the signature of intention.

The tradition's technical formulation is *dalālat al-ʿināyah* — the argument from providential fit — developed by the theologians: the world is arranged so as to serve the creatures living in it, in ways that exceed what bare existence would require, and that excess is evidence of a purpose.

**The Spiritual Reading: The Night and Day of the Soul**

The spiritual commentators — Ibn ʿAjībah prominently, and the Sufi tradition generally — read these two verses as describing the inner life, and their reading has become standard in the tradition's spiritual literature.

On this reading, the *night* and the *day* are the alternating states of contraction (*qabḍ*) and expansion (*basṭ*) that a person experiences on the path: times of dryness, obscurity, and difficulty, and times of clarity, warmth, and ease.

The principle they drew is stated in the commentaries and repeated in the manuals:

*Perpetual expansion corrupts.* The person who is always in a state of ease becomes bold, presumptuous, and eventually careless. The tradition's term is *basṭ* without *qabḍ*, and it is described as a spiritual danger.

*Perpetual contraction corrupts differently.* The person who is always in a state of difficulty becomes despairing, timid, and eventually gives up.

*And the alternation is the means of growth.* It is through the cycle — difficulty that humbles, ease that encourages, difficulty that deepens, ease that serves — that a person matures.

The Qur'anic basis for this reading is the pair of verses about expansion and contraction, and the tradition's understanding that they describe spiritual states:

> *"Truly with hardship comes ease; truly with hardship comes ease."* (Qur'an 94:5–6)

The repetition in that sūrah is the point: the difficulty is one, and the ease with it is doubled.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the inward version of this counsel:

> *"How wonderful is the affair of the believer! Truly, all of his affair is good... If something good befalls him, he is grateful, and that is good for him; and if something harmful befalls him, he is patient, and that is good for him."* (Muslim 2999)

**Application**

Three practical conclusions follow.

*Give thanks for the ordinary.* The alternation of day and night is the most reliable thing in human experience and therefore the least noticed. The verse's exercise — imagine it gone — is the cure, and it is repeatable daily.

*Honor the need for rest.* The Qur'an presents rest as a divine arrangement rather than a personal weakness. The tradition has accordingly treated adequate sleep, the cessation of work on the day of congregation, and the night's portion for worship as parts of a divinely ordered rhythm rather than as options.

*And trust the alternation of your own states.* For anyone in a season of difficulty, this verse is the assurance that the night is part of the design and not a malfunction of it. The person in whom the two states alternate is being worked on, not abandoned.

The sūrah's next verse draws the conclusion from both signs.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:73

> **Out of His Mercy He made for you night and day, that you may rest therein, and that you may seek of His Bounty, and that haply you may give thanks.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conclusion of the Argument From Time**

*Wa-min raḥmatihi jaʿala lakumu al-layla wa-al-nahāra li-taskunū fīhi wa-li-tabtaghū min faḍlihi wa-laʿallakum tashkurūn*.

Verse 73 closes the pair begun at verse 71. Verses 71 and 72 established the argument by removing each half of the cycle in turn. This verse states what the cycle is *for*, and the commentators note that it opens with the word that gives the whole arrangement its character: *raḥmah* — mercy.

**"Out of His Mercy"**

*Wa-min raḥmatihi*. The preposition *min* indicates source and cause: it was *from* His mercy, and *by reason of* His mercy, that the night and day were made as they are.

The commentators are emphatic about this, because it excludes a rival reading. The alternation of night and day could have been presented as an instance of divine *power* — and it is that too. But the Qur'an here attributes it to mercy, which means that the arrangement is not merely an exhibition of ability but a gift suited to need.

The tradition's theology of mercy is one of the most developed and most consoling in Islam, and it rests on texts like this one:

> *"And My mercy encompasses all things."* (Qur'an 7:156)

> *"Your Lord has prescribed mercy upon Himself."* (Qur'an 6:54)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"When God created the creation, He wrote in a Book — which is with Him, above the Throne: 'Truly My mercy precedes My wrath.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3194; Muslim 2751)

and:

> *"God has one hundred parts of mercy, of which He sent down one part between the jinn, mankind, the animals, and the insects — by it they show compassion to one another, and by it the wild animal shows compassion to its young. And God has kept back ninety-nine parts of mercy with which He will show mercy to His servants on the Day of Resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6000; Muslim 2752)

The commentators note the structural point: the ordinary operations of the world — sunrise, sleep, the seasons, the growth of crops — are *mercy*, not merely mechanism. A person who sees the sunrise as a mercy and one who sees it as a rotation are looking at the same event, and the first is seeing more.

**"That You May Rest Therein"**

*Li-taskunū fīhi* — *that you may be still in it*. As at verse 72, the verb is *sakana*, and the commentators note that the rest in question has two dimensions.

*Physical rest*: sleep, the cessation of labor, the repair of the body.

*And the rest of the world itself*: the night's stillness is what makes the daytime possible. Agriculture, human metabolism, and the ecology of the planet all run on cycles that require the dark phase.

The tradition has noted the Qur'an's combination of *sakīnah* — tranquility — with the night in several places:

> *"And it is He Who has made the night for you as a garment, and sleep for rest."* (Qur'an 25:47)

> *"And by the night when it covers."* (Qur'an 92:2)

**"And That You May Seek of His Bounty"**

*Wa-li-tabtaghū min faḍlihi* — *and that you may seek of His bounty*. The word *faḍl* means bounty, grace, superabundance — what is given beyond what is owed.

The commentators note the phrase's significance for the Islamic theology of work and provision.

*Provision is God's, and seeking it is commanded.* The verse joins the two without tension: the bounty is *His*, and the seeking is *yours*. This is the Qur'an's consistent position against both the person who thinks provision comes from his own effort and the person who thinks effort is unnecessary:

> *"And when the prayer is concluded, disperse in the land and seek of the bounty of God, and remember God much, that you may prosper."* (Qur'an 62:10)

> *"It is He Who has made the earth subservient to you; so walk in its tracts and eat of His provision."* (Qur'an 67:15)

*Work is worship when it is done within the limits.* The tradition's position, developed at length by the jurists and the spiritual writers, is that earning a lawful living is an obligation and a form of worship, and that the marketplace is a legitimate arena of religious life.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"No one eats better food than that which he eats from the work of his own hands; and the prophet of God, David, used to eat from the work of his own hands."* (al-Bukhārī 2072)

and:

> *"Whoever goes to sleep exhausted from the work of his own hands goes to sleep forgiven."* (Reported in the ethical literature)

*And the day is the assigned time for it.* The verse's structure assigns rest to the night and seeking to the day, and the tradition has used this to establish the rhythm of a Muslim's life: work in the day, family and rest in the night, and worship distributed through both.

**"And That Haply You May Give Thanks"**

*Wa-laʿallakum tashkurūn*. The verse's purpose clause, and the third occurrence of the gratitude theme in four verses (vv. 71–73).

The commentators note the sequence: mercy → the arrangement → rest and provision → *gratitude*. The whole cosmic order is described as moving toward a single response.

This is the Qur'an's standard teleology, and the tradition has made it explicit:

> *"And God brought you forth from the wombs of your mothers while you knew nothing, and He gave you hearing and sight and hearts, that haply you may give thanks."* (Qur'an 16:78)

> *"And it is He Who has made the night and the day in succession for him who desires to remember or desires gratitude."* (Qur'an 25:62)

The tradition's definition of gratitude, as noted earlier, is not a feeling but a use: the employment of the blessing in the obedience of the Bestower. The threefold scheme the scholars used:

*Shukr bi-l-qalb* — gratitude of the heart: recognizing that the good is from God.
*Shukr bi-l-lisān* — gratitude of the tongue: saying *al-ḥamdu lillāh* and speaking of the blessing.
*Shukr bi-l-jawāriḥ* — gratitude of the limbs: using the blessing in obedience and not in rebellion.

The Prophet ﷺ embodied all three, and the tradition records:

> *"Shall I not be a grateful servant?"* (al-Bukhārī 1130; Muslim 2819)

And he taught the specific response to a blessing:

> *"Whoever is given something and says, 'Praise be to God' (al-ḥamdu lillāh), has given thanks for it."* (Reported in the ethical literature)

**Theological Note: Mercy as the Default Description of Creation**

The commentators draw a general doctrine from the verse's opening phrase, and it is one of the most contested and most important in Islamic theology.

The question: is this world, as created, *good*? Is creation an act of mercy or a fall?

Islam's answer, on the basis of verses like this one, is that creation is mercy and that the world as God made it is good. There is no doctrine of original sin in Islam, no fall that corrupted the created order, and no teaching that the material world is intrinsically evil:

> *"Who created death and life, that He might try you — which of you is best in deed."* (Qur'an 67:2)

> *"Truly We created man in the best of forms; then We reduced him to the lowest of the low — save those who believe and work righteousness."* (Qur'an 95:4–6)

The second verse is the Islamic account of the human condition: created in the best form, reduced by sin, and restored by faith and works. The reduction is *acquired*, not inherited.

The tradition has drawn from this a characteristic optimism about the world: the material creation is to be enjoyed within limits, the body is to be cared for, beauty is a divine attribute (*God is beautiful and loves beauty*, Muslim 91), and the ordinary goods of life are *faḍl* — bounty — rather than snares.

**Application**

The verse supplies a complete daily framework, and the tradition has used it that way.

*Begin from mercy, not from mechanism.* The sunrise is rotation and it is mercy. The person who can hold both is living in the world the Qur'an describes.

*Work and rest are both obedience.* Neither is the religious life and neither is a distraction from it. The day for seeking, the night for stillness, and both within the limits God set.

*And close the loop with gratitude.* The verse's purpose clause is the point of the arrangement: *that haply you may give thanks*. The tradition's practical counsel is to make gratitude *verbal* and *frequent*, because the tongue teaches the heart.

There is one more application that the commentators drew specifically for the sūrah's argument. The Quraysh's objection had been, in essence, that following the guidance would cost them their security and their provision (v. 57). The answer of verses 71 through 73 is that the entire economic and physical order of their lives — the cycle that makes work possible at all — was already a gift from the One they were refusing. The provider they feared losing to was the provider they had.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:74

> **And on the Day when He shall call out to them, He will say, "Where are My partners, those whom you claimed?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Repeated**

*Wa-yawma yunādīhim fa-yaqūlu ayna shurakāʾiya alladhīna kuntum tazʿumūn*.

This verse is identical in wording to verse 62, and the commentators treat the repetition as one of the sūrah's most deliberate devices rather than as a redundancy.

**Why Repeat It?**

The classical commentators offer several explanations, and they are complementary.

*Emphasis through repetition.* The question is the central accusation of the Day, and the Qur'an repeats it for the same reason it repeats so much: the human heart requires repetition to register what it would rather not hear.

*Two different audiences.* Some commentators hold that the first occurrence (v. 62) is addressed to one group and this one to another — the leaders at verse 62 and the followers here, or vice versa.

*Two different contexts.* The first occurrence came after the discussion of guidance and misguidance (vv. 56–61) and before the interrogation about the messengers. This occurrence comes after the argument from creation and lordship (vv. 68–73). The commentators note that the second context changes the question's force: after verses 68–73 have established that God alone creates, chooses, and knows, and that the night and day are His mercy, the question *where are My partners?* is now unanswerable on the hearers' own premises.

*And a structural device.* The sūrah's closing movement (vv. 62–75) is built as a ring: the question about partners (v. 62), the question about messengers (v. 65), the offer of repentance (v. 67), the assertion of God's exclusive prerogatives (vv. 68–70), the signs in creation (vv. 71–73), and then the question about partners again (v. 74), followed by the witness from every community (v. 75).

The commentators note the effect of the ring structure: the argument that began with the exposure of false worship returns to it after establishing everything that makes it indefensible.

**The Force of the Question in Its Second Context**

Al-Rāzī's observation, developed by later commentators, is worth stating plainly.

At verse 62, the question *where are My partners?* could be answered, in principle, by a person stubborn enough: he might claim his gods were absent, or powerless at that moment, or that he had been misled.

By verse 74, after the sūrah has established:
* that God creates what He wills and chooses, and creatures do not choose (v. 68);
* that He knows what is concealed and what is disclosed (v. 69);
* that there is no god but He, that praise is His in both lives, that judgment is His, and that all return to Him (v. 70);
* and that the night and the day, which no idol produced, are His mercy (vv. 71–73);

— after all that, the question has no available answer at all. The idolater has been given every premise and has no conclusion left.

The commentators note that this is the Qur'an's standard method in its arguments with the idolaters: it does not simply assert monotheism, it dismantles the grounds of polytheism one at a time until the position has nothing to stand on.

**"Those Whom You Claimed"**

*Alladhīna kuntum tazʿumūn*. As at verse 62, the verb *zaʿama* carries the coloring of a claim without foundation.

The commentators note the persistent grammatical detail: the claim is described in the *past* — *you used to claim*. On that Day, the claiming has stopped. The idolaters no longer assert anything, because the assertion is no longer available to them.

This is the same phenomenon the Qur'an describes elsewhere in the scenes of the Resurrection:

> *"Then they will have no temptation but to say: 'By God, our Lord, we were not those who ascribed partners [unto God].' See how they lie against themselves! And that which they used to invent has forsaken them."* (Qur'an 6:23–24)

The tradition has always noted the psychological precision of that verse: even at the Resurrection, the first impulse is denial, and the denial collapses immediately.

**The Question as a Mirror for the Reader**

The commentators drew a homiletic use from this verse that the tradition has repeated in every generation: the question *where are My partners?* should be asked by a person of himself now, while the answer can still change something.

The examination is concrete. What are the partners?

*The overt ones*: anything or anyone invoked, trusted, feared, or obeyed in the place of God.

*The subtle ones*, which are the ones the tradition's spiritual writers spent most of their energy on:

*Riyāʾ* — performing good deeds for the approval of others:

> The Prophet ﷺ said: *"The thing I fear most for you is the lesser shirk — showing off."* (Aḥmad 23680)

*Reliance on means rather than on the Giver of means:*

> *"And whoever trusts in God, He is sufficient for him."* (Qur'an 65:3)

*The treatment of one's own judgment as final:*

> *"Have you seen the one who takes his caprice as his god?"* (Qur'an 45:23)

*And fear of creatures rather than of the Creator:*

> *"So fear them not, but fear Me, if you are believers."* (Qur'an 3:175)

The tradition's diagnostic for all four is the same and is drawn from this verse: whatever you call upon in the moment of need is what you have actually been worshipping, and the question will be asked about it.

**Theological Note: The Nature of *Shirk* in Islam**

The verse is one of the primary texts in the tradition's treatment of *shirk*, and the commentators specify what Islam means by it.

*Shirk is the one unforgiven sin* if unrepented:

> *"Truly God forgives not that partners be ascribed unto Him, but He forgives what is less than that unto whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 4:48)

The commentators are careful about this verse's meaning and about a common misunderstanding. It does not mean that a person who has committed *shirk* and then repents is unforgiven — repentance erases it, and the Qur'an says so repeatedly:

> *"Say: 'O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, despair not of the mercy of God. Truly God forgives all sins.'"* (Qur'an 39:53)

What the verse means is that the person who *dies* in a state of *shirk* without repenting is not forgiven.

*Shirk has grades.* The tradition distinguished:

*Shirk akbar* — major: directing any act of worship to other than God.
*Shirk aṣghar* — minor: showing off (*riyāʾ*), swearing by other than God, and the like. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever swears by other than God has committed shirk."* (Abū Dāwūd 3251; al-Tirmidhī 1535 — graded ḥasan)

*Shirk khafī* — hidden: the subtle forms that even the scrupulous may fall into, including reliance on means over the Giver.

*And shirk is the specific claim that the One God has partners in what belongs to Him alone.* The verse before us states the claim in the exact form it will be refuted: *My partners* — God's own ironic use of their word.

**Application**

The verse's practical content is concentrated in one instruction: identify your partners now.

The tradition's method for doing this is the examination of the heart under pressure, described above, combined with a regular practice of *tajdīd al-niyyah* — renewal of intention — before acts of worship.

There is a second application for the *daʿwah*. The question *where are My partners?* is a question and not a threat, and the tradition has always noted the Qur'an's method here: it asks, it invites the hearer to look, and it lets the evidence speak. The believer arguing with an idolater of any kind is being shown a model in which mockery has no place and in which the argument proceeds by asking rather than by shouting.

And there is a third, for the community. The partners that will be exposed on that Day are not only statues and deities. They are whatever a community has substituted for God as the source of its values, its security, and its identity. The sūrah has named Egypt's version: Pharaoh, his hosts, his treasure, and his tower. Every age has its own.

The next verse supplies the final scene of the whole sequence: the witness drawn from every community, and the demand for proof.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:75

> **And We shall draw forth from every community a witness, and say, "Bring your proof." Then they will know that the truth belongs to God. And that which they used to fabricate will forsake them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Scene of the Sūrah's Judgment Sequence**

*Wa-nazaʿnā min kulli ummatin shahīdan fa-qulnā hātū burhānakum fa-ʿalimū anna al-ḥaqqa lillāhi wa-ḍalla ʿanhum mā kānū yaftarūn*.

This verse closes the block of judgment scenes that began at verse 62. The commentators note the progression: the partners are exposed (62, 74), the messengers' message is examined (65), the excuses collapse (66), and now the witnesses are produced and the evidence is demanded.

**"We Shall Draw Forth From Every Community a Witness"**

*Wa-nazaʿnā min kulli ummatin shahīdan*. The verb *nazaʿnā* means to draw out, to extract, to bring forth — with a sense of pulling something out from among others.

The commentators identify the witness primarily as the prophet sent to that community. The proof-text is the parallel verse:

> *"How will it be when We bring forth a witness from every community, and We bring thee as a witness against these?"* (Qur'an 4:41)

That verse is addressed to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, and it establishes the pattern: every community has a witness, and the final Prophet is the witness over his own.

The Qur'an states the same idea in the chapter of the Pilgrimage, in one of the most frequently cited verses on the subject:

> *"And strive for God with the striving due to Him. He has chosen you and has placed no hardship upon you in the religion — the creed of your father Abraham. He named you Muslims before, and in this [revelation], that the Messenger may be a witness over you, and that you may be witnesses over mankind."* (Qur'an 22:78)

The commentators note the double function established there: the Prophet ﷺ is a witness *over* his community, and the community is a witness *over* mankind. This is the basis of the Islamic doctrine of the *ummatan wasaṭan* — the middle community:

> *"And thus We have made you a middle community, that you may be witnesses over mankind, and that the Messenger may be a witness over you."* (Qur'an 2:143)

Al-Rāzī raises an important difficulty and resolves it, and the resolution matters. Not every community had a prophet actually living among it — there were long intervals and remote regions. Who is the witness for those?

His answer, and the general answer of the tradition, is that the *witness* may include anyone who can be said to bear witness for his community: the righteous, the scholars, those who preserved and transmitted the truth they had received, and — in the case of this community — the community itself.

The commentators note the implication for the believer: every person is potentially a witness, and the question is what he will testify to.

**"And Say, 'Bring Your Proof'"**

*Fa-qulnā hātū burhānakum*. The word *burhān* is the strongest available term for evidence — conclusive, demonstrative proof. It appeared at verse 32 for the two signs given to Moses: *these are two proofs from thy Lord*.

The commentators note the correspondence and its justice. Moses was given a *burhān*. The communities are now asked for theirs. The same standard is applied to both sides, which is the definition of a fair trial.

The demand for proof is the Qur'an's standing challenge, and it is addressed to disbelievers throughout:

> *"Say: 'Bring your proof, if you are truthful.'"* (Qur'an 2:111)

> *"Or have you a clear authority? Then bring your book, if you are truthful."* (Qur'an 37:156–157)

> *"Say: 'Have you considered what you invoke apart from God? Show me what they have created of the earth; or do they have any share in the heavens? Bring me a book [revealed] before this, or some vestige of knowledge, if you are truthful.'"* (Qur'an 46:4)

The last verse is the fullest statement of the demand: bring a book, or bring a trace of transmitted knowledge, or bring a created effect. Failing all three, the claim has no support.

**"Then They Will Know That the Truth Belongs to God"**

*Fa-ʿalimū anna al-ḥaqqa lillāh*. The verb is *ʿalimū* — *they knew*. The commentators note the tense and its implication: this is knowledge acquired at the moment of exposure, and it is knowledge that comes too late to be of use.

As at verse 62, the tradition's point is precise. The knowledge of that Day is not faith. Faith is acceptance of the unseen on the strength of revelation; what comes at the Resurrection is sight, and sight does not produce the moral state that faith produces.

The Qur'an states this repeatedly:

> *"And they will say, 'Had we but listened or understood, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.' So they will confess their sin."* (Qur'an 67:10–11)

> *"And you will see the guilty on that Day... their eyes humbled, abasement overwhelming them. And they used to be called to prostrate while they were sound."* (Qur'an 68:43)

The phrase *al-ḥaqqa lillāh* is theologically dense and worth unpacking. It means:
* that truth *belongs* to God — that is, it is His attribute and His possession, and any claim to truth that opposes Him is by definition false;
* that the truth about everything is *with* God — He is the only one who knows things as they are;
* and that God *is* the Truth — *al-Ḥaqq* being one of His names:

> *"That is because God — He is the Truth, and that which they invoke apart from Him is falsehood; and God — He is the High, the Great."* (Qur'an 22:62)

**"And That Which They Used to Fabricate Will Forsake Them"**

*Wa-ḍalla ʿanhum mā kānū yaftarūn*. The verb *ḍalla* here means to go astray *from* them, to be lost to them, to abandon them. The noun *iftirāʾ* means fabrication, invention, forgery — the same word used at verse 36 for the accusation that Moses' signs were *muftaran*, fabricated.

The commentators note the exact correspondence, and it is one of the sūrah's most satisfying symmetries. The idolaters accused God's signs of being fabricated. On that Day, *their own* fabrications will desert them.

The word *ḍalla* — *went astray from them* — is deliberately chosen: the fabrications do not merely fail; they *leave*. The false gods, the invented doctrines, the comfortable lies, and the self-justifications will not be standing beside the people who made them.

The Qur'an states the same desertion elsewhere:

> *"Then they will have no temptation but to say: 'By God, our Lord, we were not those who ascribed partners.' See how they lie against themselves! And that which they used to invent has forsaken them."* (Qur'an 6:24)

The commentators note what this implies about the relationship a person has with his own falsehoods. In this life, they appear to *serve* him: they justify him, comfort him, and protect him from unwelcome conclusions. On that Day, they will not defend him. They will not even be present.

**Theological Note: The Witness of This Community**

The commentators drew a specific obligation for Muslims from this verse and its companions, and it is worth stating because it is one of the distinctive features of Islamic self-understanding.

The community of Muhammad ﷺ is described as witnesses over mankind (2:143, 22:78). The tradition understood this as an obligation rather than a privilege: to be a witness is to *testify*, and testimony requires that the witness know what he is testifying about, live it, and convey it.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Convey from me even if it be a single verse."* (al-Bukhārī 3461)

> *"Let those of you who are present convey [the message] to those who are absent."* (al-Bukhārī 67; Muslim 1679)

And he warned of the opposite:

> *"Whoever is asked about knowledge and conceals it will be bridled on the Day of Resurrection with a bridle of fire."* (Abū Dāwūd 3658; al-Tirmidhī 2649 — graded ḥasan)

The commentators note the relationship between these two sets of texts: the community that is to witness must transmit, and the transmission is itself part of the testimony.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Acquire the proof before the day it is demanded.* The demand *bring your proof* is addressed to communities regarding their religious claims, and it is answerable in this life from revelation, not from sincerity or tradition. The tradition's entire science of evidence — *uṣūl al-fiqh* and *uṣūl al-dīn* — exists to answer it.

*Understand what will and will not accompany you.* Everything fabricated — every self-justifying story, every convenient doctrine, every identity built on something other than what God said — will desert. What remains is what was real. The tradition's counsel is to build on what will remain.

*And prepare to be a witness.* The witness is drawn forth from every community. Every person in this ummah is a potential witness, and the testimony is given by what a life shows rather than by what a mouth says.

The sūrah now turns to its final narrative: Korah, whose story is the sūrah's complete illustration of everything it has been saying about wealth, power, and the return.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:76

> **Truly Korah was of the people of Moses, but he transgressed against them. And We gave him such treasure that the keys to it would be a heavy burden for a group of strong men. [Remember] when his people said to him, "Exult not! Surely God loves not the exultant."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Final Narrative Begins**

*Inna Qārūna kāna min qawmi Mūsā fa-baghā ʿalayhim wa-ātaynāhu mina al-kunūzi mā inna mafātiḥahu la-tanūʾu bi-al-ʿuṣbati ulī al-quwwati idh qāla lahu qawmuhu lā tafraḥ inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu al-fariḥīn*.

With this verse the sūrah introduces its last great figure: Qārūn, Korah. The commentators note the placement. The sūrah has spent its closing verses establishing the principles — that everything given is temporary use and ornament (v. 60), that the end belongs to God (v. 70), and that the truth will be exposed on the Day (v. 75). Korah is the worked example.

**Who Was Korah?**

The biblical account places Korah in Numbers 16 as a Levite who led a rebellion against Moses and Aaron and was swallowed by the earth. The Qur'an's account here and at 29:39 and 40:24 is consistent with that outline: a wealthy, powerful Israelite who opposed Moses and was destroyed.

The commentators note that the Qur'an's account differs from the biblical one in emphasis. The Bible's Korah leads a *priestly* rebellion over the right to offer incense. The Qur'an's Korah is presented as a *wealthy* man whose wealth was the source of his transgression. The Qur'an's interest is not in the cultic dispute but in the moral psychology of riches.

The name itself is of interest to the commentators: some connect it to the Hebrew *Qōraḥ*, others note the Arabic root *q-r-n* (to join, to amass) and draw the lexical observation that his name matches his activity.

**"He Was of the People of Moses"**

*Kāna min qawmi Mūsā*. The commentators stress this clause, and the stress is significant.

Korah was not Pharaoh. He was not an enemy from outside. He was *of Moses' own people* — an Israelite, a member of the community that had been delivered from Egypt, presumably among those who had crossed the sea.

The implication is one of the sūrah's most pointed: proximity to truth is not possession of it. Membership in the delivered community did not protect him. The tradition has always applied this to its own community with some trepidation, and the Prophet ﷺ warned about it directly:

> *"You will follow the ways of those who came before you, hand-span by hand-span and cubit by cubit, until if they entered a lizard's hole you would enter it too."* They said, "O Messenger of God, [do you mean] the Jews and the Christians?" He said, *"Who else?"* (al-Bukhārī 3456; Muslim 2669)

And he said:

> *"I am your predecessor at the Pool, and I shall testify against some men among you; then they will be taken away from me, and I shall say, 'My Lord, my companions!' And it will be said: 'You do not know what they introduced after you.'"* (al-Bukhārī 7049; Muslim 2297)

The commentators note the sobering content of that ḥadīth: the people taken away from the Prophet's own community are people who knew him.

**"But He Transgressed Against Them"**

*Fa-baghā ʿalayhim*. The verb *baghā* is from *b-gh-y*, and the commentators enumerate its senses here.

*Disbelief.* Some take it as his rejection of faith — that his wealth eventually cost him his religion.

*Contempt for others.* Others take it as the attitude his wealth produced: he looked down on the people around him.

*Mistreating them.* Al-Rāzī mentions mistreatment and the failure to give what was due from his wealth.

*And envy of Moses and Aaron.* The commentators transmit that a part of his transgression was jealousy of the station God had given his two cousins — that he could not bear that Moses received revelation and Aaron the priesthood while he received only money.

The last of these is psychologically the most illuminating, and the tradition has always lingered on it. Korah's problem was not that he lacked; it was that someone else had been given something he had not. Resentment at another's gift is the purest form of ingratitude for one's own.

The Qur'an names *baghy* as one of the great social sins and treats it as a cause of destruction:

> *"O mankind! Your transgression (baghyukum) is only against yourselves."* (Qur'an 10:23)

> *"And thus do We recompense him who transgresses and believes not in the signs of his Lord."* (Qur'an 20:127)

**"And We Gave Him Such Treasure"**

*Wa-ātaynāhu mina al-kunūzi*. The attribution is explicit and theologically crucial: *We gave him*. Korah's wealth was not his acquisition; it was a gift. This is the same verb used at verse 60 — *whatever you have been given* — and the sūrah is now dramatizing that principle.

The commentators note the implication for the doctrine of provision: wealth is a divine grant, and its purpose is a test rather than a reward:

> *"And be aware that your wealth and your children are a trial (fitnah), and that with God is a mighty reward."* (Qur'an 8:28)

> *"Your wealth and your children are only a trial; and with God is a mighty reward."* (Qur'an 64:15)

The word *fitnah* is the key: a trial, a testing, an ordeal that reveals what a person is made of. Wealth is *fitnah* in exactly the sense that a difficult examination is — not because the wealth is bad, but because it discloses.

**"The Keys to It Would Be a Heavy Burden for a Group of Strong Men"**

*Mā inna mafātiḥahu la-tanūʾu bi-al-ʿuṣbati ulī al-quwwah*. The description is one of the most famous in the sūrah, and the commentators parse it carefully.

*Mafātiḥ* is the plural of *miftāḥ* (a key) and of *miftaḥ* (a storehouse, a treasury). The word carries both senses, and the commentators exploit both.

*Tanūʾu* — the verb *nāʾa* means to bend, to be weighed down, to rise with difficulty under a load. The image is of strong men stooping under the weight of the keys.

*Al-ʿuṣbah* is a group, a band — the lexicographers give various numbers, from ten to forty, with the sense being *a company of men banded together*.

The commentators record several readings of what was heavy:

*That the keys themselves were physically numerous and heavy* — so many treasuries that the keys to them were a burden to carry.

*That the treasuries were so numerous that the men carrying the keys were weighed down by the responsibility and the logistics of tracking them.*

*And — the reading al-Rāzī mentions — that the "keys" were keys of *knowledge*: that Korah was versed in so many sciences and crafts that a company of people could not match his expertise. This reading has support in the Qur'an's own usage of keys for knowledge:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them save He."* (Qur'an 6:59)

The majority reading is the literal one, and the tradition has generally preferred it: the wealth was so vast that its mere management was heavy labor. The detail is deliberately chosen to make the point about *scale* — this was not a rich man; this was a fortune beyond ordinary comprehension.

**"Exult Not!" — The Warning His People Gave Him**

*Idh qāla lahu qawmuhu lā tafraḥ*. The commentators note the courage and the charity of the admonition. The people of Korah — the very people he was oppressing — spoke to him for his own sake.

The verb *tafraḥ* — to exult, to rejoice, to be gleeful — requires careful handling, because the Qur'an uses the same root approvingly elsewhere:

> *"Say: 'In the bounty of God and in His mercy — in that let them rejoice (falyafraḥū); it is better than what they amass.'"* (Qur'an 10:58)

The commentators resolve the apparent conflict by distinguishing the *object* of the rejoicing. To rejoice in God's bounty, with gratitude and with the recognition that it is given, is commanded. To rejoice *in the wealth itself* — as a possession, as a proof of one's own merit, as a license — is what is forbidden here.

The parallel verse makes the distinction explicit:

> *"And exult not; truly God loves not the exultant (al-fariḥīn)."* — where the commentators gloss *al-fariḥīn* as *those who are boastful and arrogant in their wealth*.

**"Surely God Loves Not the Exultant"**

*Inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu al-fariḥīn*. The closing clause of the verse states the prohibition in its strongest form: not merely that God disapproves, but that He does not *love*.

The commentators note the Qur'an's usage here. *Love* (*maḥabbah*, *ḥubb*) is attributed to God in the Qur'an both for what He loves and for whom He loves, and the tradition has always treated these as real attributes:

> *"Say: 'If you love God, then follow me; God will love you and forgive you your sins.'"* (Qur'an 3:31)

> *"Truly God loves those who turn to Him in repentance, and He loves those who purify themselves."* (Qur'an 2:222)

> *"And do good; truly God loves the doers of good."* (Qur'an 2:195)

> *"Truly God loves not the arrogant and boastful."* (Qur'an 4:36)

The last of these is the closest parallel, and the tradition has read the two together: the divine love is withheld from the one who lives in his wealth as though he produced it.

**The Structure of Korah's Failure**

The commentators lay out the sequence that this verse and the following ones describe, because it is the sūrah's complete account of how wealth destroys a person:

*Verse 76*: He was given wealth, and he transgressed, and he exulted.
*Verse 77*: His people counseled him — use it for the Hereafter, do not forget this world's share, be good as God has been good to you, and do not corrupt.
*Verse 78*: He refused, and attributed the wealth to his own knowledge.
*Verse 79*: He displayed it, and the crowd envied him.
*Verse 80*: Those with knowledge answered the envy.
*Verse 81*: The earth swallowed him and his house.
*Verse 82*: The enviers understood, the next morning.

The tradition has always read this as the Qur'an's definitive account of wealth's moral dangers, and it is striking that the sūrah devotes as much space to Korah as it does to the entire confrontation with Pharaoh.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Wealth is given, not achieved.* Every person with anything at all is in Korah's initial position. The recognition *We gave him* is the only protection against what Korah became.

*Membership is not safety.* He was of the people of Moses. The tradition's warning is that religious community, family, or history does not substitute for a person's own standing before God.

*And the scale of the gift increases the scale of the test.* Korah's wealth was described as beyond measuring precisely to establish that no amount of provision is large enough to make gratitude unnecessary. The tradition's conclusion is that the test intensifies with the gift:

> *"So let man consider from what he was created: he was created from a gushing fluid."* (Qur'an 86:5–6)

— the Qur'an's standing reminder of origins for anyone who has forgotten them.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:77

> **And with what God has given you, seek after the Abode of the Hereafter, and forget not your portion in this world. And be good, as God has been good to you. Seek not to work corruption upon the earth. Surely God loves not the workers of corruption.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Balanced Economic Verse in the Qur'an**

*Wa-btaghi fī-mā ātāka Allāhu al-dāra al-ākhirata wa-lā tansa naṣībaka mina al-dunyā wa-aḥsin kamā aḥsana Allāhu ilayka wa-lā tabghi al-fasāda fī al-arḍi inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu al-mufsidīn*.

This verse contains the counsel of Korah's people to him, and the commentators have always treated it as one of the most comprehensive statements of the Islamic position on wealth. It has four parts, and each corrects an error that is common in every generation.

**"Seek, With What God Has Given You, the Abode of the Hereafter"**

*Wa-btaghi fī-mā ātāka Allāhu al-dāra al-ākhirata*. The construction is precise: the *instrument* is what God gave you (wealth, position, capacity), and the *object* is the Abode of the Hereafter.

The commentators note two things in this clause.

*The Hereafter is to be sought with wealth.* Not by abandoning wealth but by using it. This forecloses the reading that piety requires renunciation. The tradition's position on monasticism is explicit, and the Prophet ﷺ rejected it when some companions proposed it:

> *Three men came to the houses of the wives of the Prophet ﷺ asking about his worship, and when they were informed, they considered it little and said, "Where are we in comparison to the Prophet ﷺ, who has been forgiven his past and future sins?" One of them said, "As for me, I shall pray all night forever." Another said, "I shall fast every day and never break it." Another said, "I shall keep away from women and never marry." Then the Messenger of God ﷺ came and said: "Are you the ones who said such-and-such? By God, I am the most fearful of God among you and the most conscious of Him; yet I fast and I break my fast, I pray and I sleep, and I marry women. Whoever turns away from my Sunnah is not of me."* (al-Bukhārī 5063; Muslim 1401)

*And the wealth is described as given.* *Fī-mā ātāka Allāhu* — *in what God gave you*. The clause repeats the verb from verse 76 (*We gave him*) in order to press the point: you did not make this, so you may not use it as though you had.

The mechanism by which wealth seeks the Hereafter is *spending*, and the Qur'an legislates it:

> *"And establish the prayer and give the alms; and whatever good you send ahead for yourselves, you will find it with God."* (Qur'an 2:110)

> *"The parable of those who spend their wealth in the way of God is that of a grain which produces seven ears, in every ear a hundred grains."* (Qur'an 2:261)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Charity does not decrease wealth; God increases His servant in honor when he forgives; and no one humbles himself before God except that God raises him."* (Muslim 2588)

**"And Forget Not Your Portion in This World"**

*Wa-lā tansa naṣībaka mina al-dunyā*. This clause is the one the tradition has quoted most often, because it is the Qur'an's explicit rejection of world-denial.

The commentators record two readings, and both are transmitted from the earliest authorities.

*The first reading*: do not neglect to *do good* with your life — do not waste the opportunity of this world by failing to act, to give, and to prepare. This is the reading al-Ṭabarī transmits from several of the Salaf: the *portion in this world* is righteous action done in it.

*The second reading*: do not go to extremes of asceticism. Enjoy what God has made lawful. This reading is transmitted by al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī and is supported by the general body of Qur'anic and Prophetic texts commanding the enjoyment of lawful goods:

> *"Say: 'Who has forbidden the adornment of God which He has brought forth for His servants, and the good things of provision?' Say: 'These are, in the life of this world, for those who believe...'"* (Qur'an 7:32)

> *"O you who believe! Forbid not the good things which God has made lawful for you, and transgress not. Truly God loves not the transgressors. And eat of what God has provided you, lawful and good."* (Qur'an 5:87–88)

The second verse was revealed concerning companions who had resolved to abstain from lawful pleasures, and the Qur'an's correction was direct.

*A third reading*, also transmitted: that the *portion* in this world is one's *grave* — meaning, remember death, and let that remembrance be your share of the world. Al-Qurṭubī records this from some of the Salaf, and it is a spiritual reading rather than a legal one.

The tradition's settled position, combining the readings: Islam forbids both extremes. The wealth that is used for the Hereafter is not thereby withdrawn from this world, and the enjoyment of this world is not thereby detached from the Hereafter. The balanced formula is the one that the commentators quote so often:

> *"Work for this world as though you will live forever, and work for the Hereafter as though you will die tomorrow."*

— a saying attributed variously to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, and to the Prophet ﷺ, and whose chain the ḥadīth scholars have discussed. Al-Qurṭubī quotes it in his commentary on this verse. Whatever its attribution, its content is exactly the verse's.

**"And Be Good, As God Has Been Good to You"**

*Wa-aḥsin kamā aḥsana Allāhu ilayka*. The root *ḥ-s-n* gives *iḥsān* — excellence, beauty, virtue — and the commentators note the threefold resonance: to do good, to do it beautifully, and to do it because of what has been done to you.

The structure of the clause is a *mimetic* command: be good *as* God has been good to you. The commentators stress this as the motive for generosity. The believer gives not from abundance but in imitation of the Giver:

> *"And do good; truly God loves the doers of good."* (Qur'an 2:195)

> *"Is the reward of goodness aught but goodness?"* (Qur'an 55:60)

The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle in its most comprehensive form:

> *"Truly God has prescribed excellence (iḥsān) in all things: so when you kill, kill well; and when you slaughter, slaughter well. Let each of you sharpen his blade and spare suffering to the animal he slaughters."* (Muslim 1955)

And in the ḥadīth that defines the religion's highest station:

> *"[Iḥsān is] that you worship God as though you see Him; and if you do not see Him, then indeed He sees you."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8)

The commentators note the specific application here: the wealthy person's *iḥsān* is generosity — not the payment of a tax but the beautiful use of what he has been given.

**"Seek Not to Work Corruption Upon the Earth"**

*Wa-lā tabghi al-fasāda fī al-arḍi*. The fourth clause names the outward form of Korah's sin: *fasād*.

As at verse 4, the root *f-s-d* means to rot, spoil, go bad. The commentators enumerate what *fasād* means in the context of wealth:

*Miserliness* — withholding what is due.
*Ostentation* — displaying wealth in order to humiliate or to dominate.
*Mistreating people* — using wealth as an instrument of power over them.
*And economic corruption* — cheating in measures, exploiting labor, corrupting markets.

The Qur'an legislates these specifically:

> *"Woe to those who give less [than due]! Those who, when they take a measure from people, take it in full; but when they give by measure or weight to them, they give less."* (Qur'an 83:1–3)

> *"And give full measure when you measure, and weigh with a balanced scale. That is better and fairer in the end."* (Qur'an 17:35)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever cheats us is not one of us."* (Muslim 102)

> *"Give the worker his wage before his sweat dries."* (Ibn Mājah 2443 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**"Surely God Loves Not the Workers of Corruption"**

*Inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu al-mufsidīn*. The verse closes with the same formula as verse 76 — *God loves not* — and the commentators note the repetition: He loves not the exultant (v. 76) and He loves not the corrupters (v. 77). The two vices are the inward and outward faces of the same condition.

**The Verse in the Islamic Economic Tradition**

Because this verse is one of the primary texts of Islamic economic ethics, the tradition's derived positions deserve a summary.

*Property is legitimate and protected.* Islam is not a communistic or a world-denying system. The Qur'an commands the return of trusts and prohibits theft, and the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Truly your blood, your property, and your honor are sacred to one another, like the sanctity of this day of yours, in this month of yours, in this land of yours."* (al-Bukhārī 67; Muslim 1679)

*But property carries obligations.* The *zakāh* is a pillar of the religion and a right of the poor, not a voluntary gift:

> *"And in their wealth is a known right for the beggar and the deprived."* (Qur'an 70:24–25)

> *"Take charity from their wealth to purify them and cleanse them thereby."* (Qur'an 9:103)

*Beyond the obligation lies generosity*, which the Qur'an praises without limit:

> *"They ask thee what they should spend. Say: 'What you spend of good is for parents and kinsfolk and orphans and the needy and the traveler; and whatever good you do, truly God knows it.'"* (Qur'an 2:215)

*And wealth must be acquired lawfully.* The tradition is emphatic that how money is made governs what may be done with it:

> *"O you who believe, consume not your property among yourselves falsely, unless it be trade by mutual consent."* (Qur'an 4:29)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"No flesh nourished by unlawful gain shall enter Paradise; the Fire is more fitting for it."* (Reported in the collections; the meaning is well-attested)

**Application**

The verse is a complete rule for anyone with resources, and its four clauses can be used as a standing examination.

*Am I using what I have for the Hereafter?* The test is concrete: what proportion, what regularity, what priority.

*Have I neglected the world?* The verse forbids both wastefulness and the renunciation of lawful enjoyment. The tradition's balance is difficult and is the mark of a mature religious life.

*Am I good as God has been good to me?* The motive for generosity is gratitude, not obligation alone.

*And is any of my wealth corrupting anything?* The question extends beyond personal conduct to the *use* of one's money, one's investments, one's labor practices, and one's consumption.

The tradition's summary of the whole verse is the Prophetic counsel:

> *"The best of charity is that given by one who has little; and begin with those you support."* (Abū Dāwūd 1676; al-Nasāʾī — graded ḥasan)

and:

> *"O God, set right for me my religion... and set right for me my world, in which is my livelihood... and make life an increase for me in every good."* (Muslim 2720)

Korah was given the counsel in this verse and refused it, and his answer is recorded in the next one.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:78

> **He said, "I have only been given it on account of knowledge I possess." Did he not know that God has destroyed generations before him who were mightier in strength and greater in what they had amassed? And the guilty will not be questioned about their sins.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Condemned Him**

*Qāla innamā ūtītuhu ʿalā ʿilmin ʿindī*.

Six Arabic words. The commentators regard this as the most revealing sentence Korah uttered and the one that sealed his fate. He had been given counsel; he replied with an attribution.

The sūrah immediately answers him with a question, and then with a statement. The structure is: his claim, its refutation, and the principle.

**"I Have Only Been Given It on Account of Knowledge I Possess"**

*Innamā* — *only*, *nothing but* — is a particle of restriction, and the commentators note its force. Korah is not offering one explanation among several; he is excluding every other. *I have been given this only because of what I know.*

The phrase *ʿalā ʿilmin ʿindī* has generated several readings, all transmitted in the commentaries.

*The primary reading*: *on account of knowledge that I possess* — that is, my expertise, my skill, my competence produced this wealth. The commentators transmit reports that Korah was a goldsmith, and others that he was an alchemist — and al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī both record these and both set them aside as unnecessary speculation. The point is the *claim*, not the craft.

*A second reading*, transmitted by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī: read *ʿindī* as *in my opinion* or *in my estimation*, giving: *I have only been given it on account of knowledge — in my opinion*. On this reading, the clause is even more damning, because Korah is not even claiming objective expertise; he is claiming that his own judgment is the measure.

*A third reading*, which the spiritual commentators developed: that the knowledge in question was *knowledge of God's favor toward him* — that is, Korah understood his wealth as evidence that God was pleased with him. This reading connects to the widespread human error the Qur'an addresses elsewhere:

> *"And as for man, when his Lord tries him and honors him and grants him ease, he says, 'My Lord has honored me.' But when He tries him and straitens his provision, he says, 'My Lord has humiliated me.' Nay!..."* (Qur'an 89:15–17)

The *Nay!* in that verse is the Qur'an's direct refutation of the error: wealth is not a verdict.

**What Was Actually Wrong With the Claim?**

The commentators are precise here, because the claim sounds almost reasonable.

The error is not that skill contributed. Skill does contribute; the Qur'an and Sunnah affirm secondary causes (*asbāb*) fully. The error is in the *exclusion* — *innamā*, *only*. Korah attributed the outcome entirely to his own knowledge and removed the Giver from the account.

This is the precise mechanism by which prosperity becomes idolatry, and the tradition has named it *istighnāʾ* — the sense of self-sufficiency:

> *"Nay, truly man transgresses, in that he deems himself self-sufficient."* (Qur'an 96:6–7)

The commentators note the psychological accuracy. A person does not usually deny God in words. He simply stops mentioning Him in the story of how he got here. The wealth has a history — talent, effort, timing, opportunity, health, a stable society, a market, a customer — and every item on that list is given. Korah's error was to take the one item he could call his own and treat it as the whole explanation.

The tradition's counter-principle is stated in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"O My servants, were the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, to stand on a single plain and ask of Me, and I were to give every one what he asked, that would not decrease what I have by more than a needle decreases when it is plunged into the sea."* (Muslim 2577)

And the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Look to those below you [in worldly terms] and do not look to those above you, for that is more likely that you will not belittle the blessing of God upon you."* (al-Bukhārī 6490; Muslim 2963)

**The Refutation: "Did He Not Know That God Has Destroyed Generations Before Him?"**

*A-wa-lam yaʿlam anna Allāha qad ahlaka min qablihi mina al-qurūni man huwa ashaddu minhu quwwatan wa-aktharu jamʿan*.

The answer is a question, and its form is the Qur'an's standard: *did he not know?* — a question implying that the knowledge was available and that he ignored it.

The argument proceeds by *a fortiori*: stronger and wealthier people than Korah were destroyed. The commentators note the two comparisons — *ashaddu quwwatan* (mightier in strength) and *aktharu jamʿan* (greater in amassing) — which cover exactly the two things Korah was priding himself on: his power and his accumulated wealth.
