The argument says: you are not the strongest person who ever lived, and others who were stronger are gone. What makes you think strength protects?

The Qur'an uses this argument repeatedly:

> *"Have they not traveled in the land and seen how was the end of those who were before them? They were greater than them in power, and they tilled the earth and inhabited it more than these have inhabited it."* (Qur'an 30:9)

> *"And how many a town more mighty in strength than thy town which has driven thee out have We destroyed; and they had no helper."* (Qur'an 47:13)

> *"Have they not seen how many generations We destroyed before them, whom We had established in the earth as We have not established you?"* (Qur'an 6:6)

The commentators note the historical basis of the argument: the remains of ʿĀd, Thamūd, and the cities of Lot were visible to the Arabs, and the Israelite community had its own memory of Egypt's destruction. Korah had seen what happened to Pharaoh. He knew. And he still said *I have been given it*.

**"And the Guilty Will Not Be Questioned About Their Sins"**

*Wa-lā yusʾalu ʿan dhunūbihi al-mujrimūn*. This clause has generated more commentary than any other in the verse, because it appears to contradict other Qur'anic texts.

The difficulty: the Qur'an states explicitly that people *will* be questioned:

> *"So by thy Lord, We shall question them all, concerning what they used to do."* (Qur'an 15:92–93)

> *"And stop them; for they are to be questioned."* (Qur'an 37:24)

The commentators' resolutions, which are complementary:

*Al-Ṭabarī's reading*: the guilty will not be questioned about the sins of *others* — each is accountable only for his own. This resolves the apparent contradiction by specifying the object of the question.

*Al-Qurṭubī's reading*: the clause means that God has no *need* to question them, because He already knows their deeds completely. The questioning that occurs is therefore for another purpose — to establish the record publicly and to make the condemned condemn themselves.

*Al-Rāzī's reading*, which the tradition has generally adopted as the comprehensive one: there are *different kinds* of questions. A question may seek information, may be a call to account, or may be a rebuke. God does not ask the first kind (He knows), but He does ask the second and third. The verses that deny questioning deny the first kind; the verses that affirm it affirm the others.

The commentators note that the same hermeneutic applies to the question of whether people will speak to one another on that Day, discussed at verse 66. The Day has stages and modes, and the descriptions are distributed across them rather than contradictory.

The tradition's practical conclusion: the verse's phrase means, in this context, that God does not need to conduct an investigation to establish Korah's guilt. There will be no inquiry, no weighing of evidence, no opportunity to explain. The case is complete.

**The Spiritual Reading: The Danger of Self-Admiration**

The spiritual commentators took this verse as the definitive text on self-admiration (*ʿujb*), and their treatment is worth summarizing because it addresses a condition more common than Korah's wealth.

The mechanism they describe: a person achieves something, and the achievement becomes the explanation of itself. He looks at his own work, his own insight, his own piety, and is *pleased with his own state*. The tradition's diagnosis is that this pleasure is more destructive than open sin, because sin can be repented while self-admiration is invisible to the one who has it.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Three are the destroyers: miserliness obeyed, desire followed, and a person's admiration of his own self."* (Reported in the ethical literature; the meaning is well-attested in the tradition)

And the spiritual writers quoted the saying recorded in the commentaries: *the person who looks at his own achievements with a sense of glory should be counted among the wretched.*

The tradition's prescribed remedy is twofold: the constant recollection that every capacity is given, and the discipline of concealing one's good deeds so that the admiration has less to feed on.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Check the story you tell about your success.* Korah's sentence is the default human autobiography: I worked, I was smart, I earned this. The verse does not deny the work or the smartness. It asks whether the word *only* belongs there.

*Remember what happened to stronger people.* The Qur'an's argument is empirical and generalizable. Every confident person should know the history of confident people who are gone.

*And understand that accountability does not require investigation.* The clause *the guilty will not be questioned* is not a comfort; it is a statement that the case is already closed. For anyone who has been counting on the difficulty of being found out, the verse is the answer: nothing needs to be discovered, because nothing was ever hidden.

The sūrah's next verse shows Korah displaying his wealth and the crowd's reaction — the last stage before the end.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:79

> **And he came forth unto his people in his adornment. Those who desired the life of this world said, "Would that we had the like of that which has been given to Korah. Truly he is a possessor of great fortune!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Display**

*Fa-kharaja ʿalā qawmihi fī zīnatihi*. The commentators note the verb: *kharaja* — he *went out*, he made a public appearance. This was not a private enjoyment of wealth but a *procession*.

The word *zīnah* — adornment, ornament, finery — is the same word used at verse 60 for the world's ornament. The commentators note the connection: Korah came out *in the world's ornament*, which is precisely what the sūrah declared the world's goods to be, and he wore it as though it were substance.

The classical commentaries transmit descriptions of the display — gold, silk, horses, attendants, a retinue of unprecedented size. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record these reports; al-Rāzī records them and notes that they are transmitted elaborations rather than revelation. The Qur'an's own word is sufficient: *zīnah*.

**The Psychology of Display**

The commentators draw out what the verse is describing, and their analysis is exact.

Korah did not need to come out. He was already rich, already secure, already envied. The display was for an audience, and it was *addressed* — it said something to the people watching.

What it said was *innamā ūtītuhu ʿalā ʿilmin ʿindī* (v. 78) in visual form: I have this because I deserve it, and you do not have it because you do not.

The tradition has treated ostentation (*mubāhāh*, *riyāʾ*, *tabdhīr*) as a serious matter for exactly this reason — because display is a form of speech, and what it says is usually a claim about the relative worth of persons.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Eat, drink, give charity, and wear clothes, so long as that does not involve extravagance or conceit."* (al-Nasāʾī; Ibn Mājah 3605 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

> *"Whoever wears a garment of fame in this world, God will clothe him in a garment of humiliation on the Day of Resurrection."* (Abū Dāwūd 4029; Ibn Mājah 3607 — graded ḥasan)

And he described the specific danger of wealth displayed to the poor:

> *"The poor among the emigrants will enter Paradise five hundred years before the rich."* (al-Tirmidhī 2354; Ibn Mājah 4122 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The commentators note that this ḥadīth is not a curse on wealth but a statement about the *delay* that accountability imposes: the wealthy must account, and the accounting takes time.

**"Those Who Desired the Life of This World"**

*Qāla alladhīna yurīdūna al-ḥayāta al-dunyā*. The Qur'an identifies the respondents by their orientation: *those who want the life of this world*.

The commentators note the precision of the description. These were not necessarily disbelievers; many of them were Israelites, members of Moses' community. What they shared was a *direction of desire*: they wanted the world, and therefore they measured by the world's measure.

The Qur'an uses this description as a recurring category:

> *"Whosoever desires the life of this world and its adornment, We will repay them in full for their deeds therein, and they will not be defrauded therein. Those are they for whom there is nothing in the Hereafter but the Fire."* (Qur'an 11:15–16)

> *"Whosoever desires the harvest of the Hereafter, We shall increase for him in his harvest; and whosoever desires the harvest of this world, We shall give him thereof, but he will have no portion in the Hereafter."* (Qur'an 42:20)

The commentators note the crucial distinction these verses establish: wanting this world is not a sin in the abstract — the verse at 42:20 says God *gives* such people what they want. The sin is wanting it *instead*, and measuring everything by it.

**"Would That We Had the Like of That Which Has Been Given to Korah"**

*Yā layta lanā mithla mā ūtiya Qārūn*. The wish is stated in the plainest terms: *if only we had what he has*.

The commentators note that this is *envy* expressed as admiration, and that the two are difficult to distinguish in the moment. The Qur'an's technical term for the vice is *ḥasad* — wishing that another's blessing be removed — and the tradition treated it with great severity:

> The Prophet ﷺ said: *"Beware of envy, for envy consumes good deeds as fire consumes wood."* (Abū Dāwūd 4903; Ibn Mājah 4210)

and:

> *"There has come to you the disease of the nations before you: envy and hatred. And hatred is the shaver — I do not say it shaves hair, but it shaves the religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2510 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

But the tradition also distinguished *hasad* from *ghibṭah* — the wish to have what another has *without* wishing it removed from him. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"There is no envy except in two cases: a man whom God has given wealth and he spends it in the cause of truth; and a man whom God has given wisdom and he judges by it and teaches it."* (al-Bukhārī 73; Muslim 816)

The commentators note the crucial distinction and apply it to the crowd in this verse: their statement was not *ghibṭah* (lawful emulation) because its object was the wealth itself rather than its use, and because — as the next verse will show — they were measuring by appearance rather than by outcome.

**"Truly He Is a Possessor of Great Fortune"**

*Innahu la-dhū ḥaẓẓin ʿaẓīm*. The word *ḥaẓẓ* means a share, a portion, a fortune — and the commentators note the word's use elsewhere in the Qur'an for a *portion of good*:

> *"But none is granted it save those who are patient; and none is granted it save the possessor of a great portion (ḥaẓẓin ʿaẓīm)."* (Qur'an 41:35)

The recurrence is deliberate and ironic, and the commentators point it out. The same phrase — *a great portion* — is used at 41:35 for the person granted the ability to repay evil with good. The crowd here uses it for money.

The sūrah is therefore staging a collision of two vocabularies. In the crowd's vocabulary, *great fortune* means wealth. In the Qur'an's vocabulary, *great fortune* means the moral capacity the crowd does not have.

**The Modern Parallel**

The commentators' analysis transfers with uncomfortable precision to contemporary life, and the tradition's scholars have made the connection explicit in modern writing.

The public display of wealth, the audience that measures by it, the wish to have what is displayed, and the conclusion that the displayer is therefore *fortunate* — this entire sequence is now institutionalized and delivered continuously to every person with a screen.

The Qur'an's diagnosis of the sequence is unchanged. The crowd in this verse were not fooled about the facts — Korah really was wealthy — they were fooled about the *meaning*. They inferred from his wealth that he was fortunate, and the inference is false because it was drawn before the end of the story.

The tradition's corrective is the same one the next verse supplies: judge by outcome, and wait for the outcome.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Examine what you admire.* The crowd's error was not in noticing Korah's wealth but in concluding from it that he was fortunate. The tradition's discipline is to ask what a person does with what he has before concluding anything about him.

*Watch the effect of display on others.* The Qur'an names the crowd's envy as a real consequence of Korah's procession. The tradition has therefore treated modest display as a duty toward the poor, not merely as a personal preference:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him not harm his neighbor."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

*And recognize the voice you are being asked to adopt.* The verse records a crowd's statement in order to record a better answer to it. The believer reading this verse is being offered two possible speeches — the crowd's or the knowledgeable's — and is being asked which one comes out of his mouth when he sees someone with more.

The sūrah supplies the better one immediately.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:80

> **But those who had been given knowledge said, "Woe unto you! The Reward of God is better for those who believe and work righteousness, and none shall receive it save the patient."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counter-Voice**

*Wa-qāla alladhīna ūtū al-ʿilma waylakum thawābu Allāhi khayrun li-man āmana wa-ʿamila ṣāliḥan wa-lā yulaqqāhā illā al-ṣābirūn*.

The sūrah sets two responses side by side within a single scene. The crowd said *would that we had what he has* (v. 79). Those given knowledge said *woe to you*. The commentators note the abruptness of the rebuke and treat it as deliberate: this is not a gentle correction but an alarm.

**Who Were "Those Who Had Been Given Knowledge"?**

The commentators discuss the identification, and they note the range.

*The primary reading*, and the one the majority adopt: these were people of piety, understanding, and religious knowledge among the Children of Israel — the ones who had learned from Moses and who saw through the display. Al-Qurṭubī favors this.

*A transmitted alternative*: that they were the *aḥbār* — the rabbis or scholars of the Children of Israel. Al-Qurṭubī records this reading, and some commentators note the mild anachronism involved, since the term *aḥbār* is more naturally applied to the later Jewish scholarly class.

The commentators make a general point from the identification, and it is one of the tradition's most important principles about knowledge: knowledge is not information but *perception of outcomes*. The people in this verse knew the same facts the crowd knew — Korah's wealth was visible to everyone. What they had that the crowd lacked was the framework for interpreting it.

This is the Qur'an's consistent teaching about knowledge:

> *"Say: 'Are those who know equal to those who do not know?' Truly only those possessed of understanding remember."* (Qur'an 39:9)

> *"And those who have been given knowledge see that what has been sent down to thee from thy Lord is the truth, and guides unto the path of the Mighty, the Praised."* (Qur'an 34:6)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever God intends good for, He gives him understanding of the religion."* (al-Bukhārī 71; Muslim 1037)

and:

> *"The superiority of the scholar over the worshipper is like my superiority over the least of you."* (al-Tirmidhī 2685 — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**"Woe Unto You!"**

*Waylakum*. The word *wayl* is an expression of grief and warning — conventionally rendered *woe*. The commentators note that it is used in the Qur'an both as a curse and as an anguished warning, and that here it is the latter: a cry of alarm at the danger the speakers are in.

The alarm is proportionate to the danger. The crowd was not committing a technical error; they were, in the sūrah's analysis, standing at the edge of a conclusion that would destroy them — the conclusion that wealth is the measure of a person.

The tradition has always noted the courage required for this kind of speech. The knowledgeable spoke against the mood of their own community, in public, at the moment of a spectacle. The Prophet ﷺ described the station of such speech:

> *"The best of struggle is a word of truth spoken before a tyrannical ruler."* (Abū Dāwūd 4344; al-Tirmidhī 2174 — graded ḥasan)

**"The Reward of God Is Better"**

*Thawābu Allāhi khayrun*. The argument is a direct comparison, and the commentators note that it answers the crowd's statement in exactly its own terms.

The crowd had said Korah's possession was *a great portion* (*ḥaẓẓ ʿaẓīm*). The knowledgeable reply: *the reward of God is better* — *khayr*, a comparative. The same measure is being applied, and the verdict is reversed.

The word *thawāb* means reward, recompense, what a person gets in return for his deeds. The commentators note the implication: what God gives is *earned* in a way that Korah's wealth was not — not by merit, but by faith and works.

The comparison rests on the two adjectives the Qur'an has applied to the two things:

*Korah's wealth*: *matāʿ* and *zīnah* — temporary use and ornament (v. 60).
*God's reward*: *khayrun wa-abqā* — better and more lasting (v. 60).

The knowledgeable were quoting the sūrah's own principle back to the crowd.

**"For Those Who Believe and Work Righteousness"**

*Li-man āmana wa-ʿamila ṣāliḥan*. The condition is stated, and the commentators note that it is the same pairing that runs through the entire Qur'an.

This is the decisive point in the verse's argument. The reward of God is *not* for everyone, and it is not for the wealthy as such. It is for those who believe and work righteousness — categories that cut across the economic ones entirely.

The sūrah has already established the comparison at verse 61: the person promised good and the person given worldly enjoyment are not alike. This verse is the same comparison stated in the imperative.

The tradition's texts on this are innumerable; the most concentrated is:

> *"By time! Truly man is in loss — save those who believe and work righteousness, and counsel one another to truth and counsel one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

Note that this sūrah ends with *patience* — the same word the verse before us ends with.

**"And None Shall Receive It Save the Patient"**

*Wa-lā yulaqqāhā illā al-ṣābirūn*. The closing clause is one of the most quoted in the Qur'an, and the commentators unpack it carefully.

The verb *yulaqqā* is passive: *none is granted it*, *none is made to meet it*. The commentators note the implication: the reward is not seized; it is given, and it is given to a specific kind of person.

Why patience specifically? The commentators give several reasons, and they are cumulative.

*Because the reward is deferred.* Everything about the Hereafter requires waiting, and a person who cannot wait will take the immediate substitute instead. The whole of Korah's error was impatience in this sense: he took the portion now and called it the whole.

*Because faith produces opposition.* The believer will be tested, and the testing requires endurance:

> *"Do people think that they will be left to say, 'We believe,' and they will not be tried?"* (Qur'an 29:2)

*Because the crowd's position is the default one.* Patience here includes the patience of *not envying*, of standing in a gathering where everyone is impressed by wealth and refusing to be impressed by it.

*And because the reward is described as requiring the whole of a life.* The commentators quote the ḥadīth:

> *"Whoever is patient, God makes him patient; and no one has been given a gift better and more comprehensive than patience."* (al-Bukhārī 1469; Muslim 1053)

> *"Truly the patient will be given their reward in full, without reckoning."* (Qur'an 39:10)

**The Connection to Verse 54**

The commentators note the echo of verse 54, where the believers among the People of the Book were described as receiving *their reward twice over for their having been patient*. The same pairing — reward and patience — appears there and here, and the sūrah is consistent: reward attaches to patience in every case.

**The Two Vocabularies Again**

The sūrah's method in these two verses is worth naming, because it is the sūrah's method throughout.

The crowd used the vocabulary of *possession*: what he has been given, his great fortune.
The knowledgeable used the vocabulary of *outcome*: reward, better, patience.

The commentators note that this is the distinction the Qur'an is training its readers to make. The first vocabulary is available to anyone and produces envy. The second requires knowledge and produces patience. Everyone in the scene saw the same procession; only one group saw what it meant.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Acquire the second vocabulary.* The knowledge in this verse is not a body of facts but a way of seeing outcomes. The tradition's prescription for acquiring it is the study and practice of the religion, and the company of those who have it.

*Practice the rebuke.* *Woe unto you* is addressed to people who were about to make a catastrophic inference. The tradition's obligation of *naṣīḥah* includes this kind of alarm, and it is owed to people one loves:

> *"The religion is sincere good counsel."* (Muslim 55)

*And understand what patience purchases.* The verse states that the reward of God is *only* given to the patient. Since the reward is the whole point, patience is not an incidental virtue but the necessary condition. The tradition's counsel for acquiring it is to remember what it buys:

> *"Know that victory comes with patience, that relief comes with distress, and that with hardship comes ease."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad)

The sūrah's next verse shows what happened to the man the crowd envied, and how quickly the envy changed.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:81

> **Then We caused the earth to engulf him and his house. And he had no party who could help him against God, nor could he help himself.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The End of Korah**

*Fa-khasafnā bihi wa-bi-dārihi al-arḍa fa-mā kāna lahu min fiʾatin yanṣurūnahu min dūni Allāhi wa-mā kāna mina al-muntaṣirīn*.

In one verse, the man who was envied by his entire community is gone. The commentators note the economy: the procession of verse 79 was described in a single clause, and the destruction is described in a single clause.

**"We Caused the Earth to Engulf Him and His House"**

*Fa-khasafnā bihi wa-bi-dārihi al-arḍa*. The verb *khasafa* means to cause the earth to swallow, to sink, to cave in. It is the same verb used for the fate of the arrogant elsewhere:

> *"Or are you secure that He Who is in the heaven will not cause the earth to swallow you, when lo, it is convulsing?"* (Qur'an 67:16)

The phrase *wa-bi-dārihi* — *and his house* — is significant, and the commentators note it. Not only Korah but his entire establishment: his household, his property, his display. The whole thing went down together.

The commentators draw the correspondence with verse 76's description of his treasure. The earth that had yielded his wealth now consumed it. The instrument of his pride became the instrument of his destruction — the same pattern the sūrah established with Pharaoh and the sea (v. 40).

The tradition has a general principle for this, drawn from many such narratives:

> *"And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 3:54)

— where the "plotting" attributed to God means that He brings about consequences through means the plotters did not anticipate, with no moral blame attaching to Him.

**How It Happened: The Reports and the Doctrine**

The commentaries transmit the account that God gave Moses power over the earth, and that Moses commanded it to swallow Korah. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both record versions of this, and the reports add details: that Korah's house sank in stages, that he was swallowed up to his knees, then his waist, then his chest, and that each time he cried out to Moses for mercy and Moses did not relent.

Al-Rāzī records these reports and makes two methodological points that the tradition has adopted.

*First*, the mechanism is not the point. Whether the earth opened by God's direct command or through Moses' invocation, the agent is God — the verse says *We caused the earth to engulf him*.

*Second*, the reports of staged sinking and of Moses' refusal are transmitted elaborations. The Qur'an states the event and its meaning; the details belong to the category of report that may be mentioned but not insisted upon.

The tradition's caution here is worth noting, because these details are extremely popular in oral storytelling. The rule the scholars applied is that the Qur'an's account is sufficient, and that elaborations must never be presented as if they were revelation.

**"He Had No Party Who Could Help Him Against God"**

*Fa-mā kāna lahu min fiʾatin yanṣurūnahu min dūni Allāh*. The word *fiʾah* means a party, a band, a group that a person can call upon for support — and the commentators note its technical military sense: a group that gives a person strength in battle.

The Qur'an uses the same word in the context of combat:

> *"O you who believe, when you meet those who disbelieve advancing [in battle], turn not your backs to them; and whoever turns his back to them on that day, unless maneuvering for battle or joining another company (fiʾah), has incurred wrath from God."* (Qur'an 8:15–16)

The word choice here is deliberately martial. Korah had, in effect, a standing army of retainers, clients, dependents, and admirers — the people who envied him the day before. And not one of them could do anything.

The commentators note the specific refutation this constitutes. Korah's wealth was, among other things, a *power base*. Money buys protection, and his had bought a great deal of it. The verse states that on the day it mattered, the protection purchased nothing.

The Qur'an says the same of every worldly power base:

> *"And if God touches thee with harm, there is none who can remove it save He; and if He desires good for thee, there is none who can repel His bounty."* (Qur'an 10:107)

> *"Say: 'Who is it that can protect you from God, if He intends harm for you, or intends mercy for you?' And they will find for themselves, apart from God, no protector and no helper."* (Qur'an 33:17)

**"Nor Could He Help Himself"**

*Wa-mā kāna mina al-muntaṣirīn*. The second negation completes the first. He had no helpers, and he could not help himself.

The commentators note the structure: external resources first, then internal. The man who believed himself self-sufficient (*innamā ūtītuhu ʿalā ʿilmin ʿindī*, v. 78) discovered that he had nothing — not from others, and not from himself.

This is the direct answer to verse 78. He said *I* and *my knowledge*. The verse says: no party, and no self.

The tradition has always read this as the definitive exposure of *istighnāʾ* — the claim of self-sufficiency:

> *"Nay, truly man transgresses, in that he deems himself self-sufficient."* (Qur'an 96:6–7)

> *"O mankind! You are the poor in relation to God; and God — He is the Self-Sufficient, the Praised."* (Qur'an 35:15)

**Theological Note: Why This Punishment?**

The commentators raise the question of proportionality, and their answers have several parts.

*The punishment fits the crime.* Korah's wealth came from the earth and was his pride; the earth took him. The Qur'an's punishments characteristically mirror the offense, and the tradition has made this a principle of interpretation.

*The punishment was public.* He had displayed his wealth publicly (v. 79) and his destruction was equally public — which is why verse 82 describes the change in the crowd's mood the next morning.

*And the punishment was preceded by counsel.* Verses 76 and 77 record the warnings he received from his own people, and verse 78 records his refusal. The sūrah has therefore established that the case against him was complete before the sentence.

The tradition notes the parallel with the sūrah's own principle, stated at verse 59: God does not destroy a people until a messenger has come to them. Korah lived among a prophet and received counsel from believers in his own community.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Argument**

The commentators note that Korah's destruction completes a three-part demonstration the sūrah has been constructing.

*Pharaoh* — the tyrant destroyed by water (v. 40).
*The towns* — the comfortable cities destroyed for their ingratitude (v. 58).
*Korah* — the wealthy man destroyed by the earth (v. 81).

Three objects of misplaced trust — political power, civic prosperity, and personal wealth — and each destroyed by the very thing it rested on.

The sūrah's conclusion will be drawn at verse 83: the Abode of the Hereafter belongs to those who desire neither dominance nor corruption.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*The power that money buys is conditional.* Korah's *fiʾah* — his party — was real and large, and it was worth nothing. The tradition's counsel is to build reliance where it cannot be dissolved:

> *"And whoever trusts in God, He is sufficient for him."* (Qur'an 65:3)

*Self-sufficiency is an illusion with a deadline.* Everyone has one; the verse shows what happens when it is tested. The tradition's discipline is to acknowledge dependence before dependence is forced:

> *"You alone we worship, and from You alone we seek help."* (Qur'an 1:5)

*And the end of the envied should be remembered by the envious.* The verse is addressed in the first instance to the crowd of verse 79, and the next verse records what they said when they understood. The tradition's practice is to read the two together, because the moral of the story is not Korah's punishment but the bystanders' correction.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:82

> **And morning found those who had longed to be in his place the day before saying, "Alas! It seems God outspreads and straitens provision for whomsoever He will among His servants. Had God not been gracious to us, He would have caused us to be engulfed [as well]. Alas! It seems the disbelievers will not prosper!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Morning After**

*Wa-aṣbaḥa alladhīna tamannaw makānahu bi-l-amsi yaqūlūna wayka-anna Allāha yabsuṭu al-rizqa li-man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi wa-yaqdiru lawlā an manna Allāhu ʿalaynā la-khasafa binā wayka-annahu lā yufliḥu al-kāfirūn*.

The verse opens with the verb *aṣbaḥa* — *morning found them* — the third significant use of this verb in the sūrah (vv. 10, 18, and here). The commentators note the pattern: in each case, morning is the moment when the reality of yesterday can no longer be deferred.

For Moses' mother, morning brought the terror of separation (v. 10). For Moses, morning brought the fear of discovery (v. 18). For Korah's enviers, morning brought the correction of an entire worldview.

**"Those Who Had Longed to Be in His Place the Day Before"**

*Alladhīna tamannaw makānahu bi-l-amsi*. The commentators note the precision of the description: *the day before*, these same people had said *would that we had the like of what Korah was given* (v. 79).

The sūrah is deliberately placing the two statements twenty-four hours apart. The commentators note the rhetorical effect: a single night separated the envy from its correction, and the people who spoke both sentences were the same people.

The tradition has always drawn from this the lesson about *timing* in judgment. The crowd's statement was not foolish at the time; it was *premature*. They drew a conclusion about Korah's fortune before his story was over, and the story ended the next morning.

The Qur'an states the general principle:

> *"And judge not according to appearances"* — no; the relevant text is:

> *"Perhaps you dislike a thing while God places in it much good."* (Qur'an 4:19)

and:

> *"It may be that you dislike a thing while it is good for you, and it may be that you like a thing while it is bad for you. And God knows, and you know not."* (Qur'an 2:216)

**"Alas! It Seems God Outspreads and Straitens Provision"**

*Wayka-anna Allāha yabsuṭu al-rizqa li-man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi wa-yaqdiru*. This is the first of the two things they understood, and it is the theological center of the verse.

*Yabsuṭu* — He outspreads, He enlarges, He gives abundantly.
*Yaqdiru* — He straitens, He measures, He restricts.

The commentators note the pairing, because it is one of the most important economic doctrines in the Qur'an: provision is apportioned by God, and its distribution is not a function of merit or of effort alone.

The same pairing appears throughout:

> *"God outspreads provision for whomsoever He wills, and straitens [it for whom He wills]."* (Qur'an 13:26)

> *"Truly thy Lord outspreads provision for whomsoever He wills, and straitens [it]. Truly He is, of His servants, Aware and Seeing."* (Qur'an 17:30)

> *"Have they not seen that God outspreads provision for whomsoever He wills and straitens [it]? Truly in that are signs for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 30:37)

> *"Say: 'Truly my Lord outspreads provision for whomsoever He wills of His servants, and straitens [it] for him; and whatsoever you spend, He will replace it.'"* (Qur'an 34:39)

The last verse adds the complementary promise: whatever you spend, He replaces. The commentators have always set that clause beside this one, because together they resolve the believer's relationship to provision: it is apportioned by God, and generosity does not diminish it.

**What the Particle Means**

The word *wayka-anna* has generated detailed grammatical discussion, and the commentators record the readings.

*The majority reading*: the particle expresses realization and regret — *alas!*, *do you not see that?*, *know that*. On this reading, the crowd is stating what they have just learned.

*A reading transmitted by al-Qurṭubī*: that *wayka* is an expression of rebuke addressed to the self — *woe to you!* — as though the speakers were rebuking their own yesterday. On this reading the phrase means: *Woe to you [O self]; know that God outspreads...*

*Another reading*, transmitted by al-Rāzī: that the phrase functions as emphasis — *indeed, God outspreads* — with the regret implied rather than stated.

The commentators note that all three readings converge on the same content: the crowd now knows what the knowledgeable knew (v. 80), and they know it too late to have profited from it as a *choice*.

**"Had God Not Been Gracious to Us, He Would Have Caused Us to Be Engulfed"**

*Lawlā an manna Allāhu ʿalaynā la-khasafa binā*. This is the second thing they understood, and the commentators regard it as the more important.

The clause is remarkable for what it confesses: the enviers understood that they had been *spared*, not that they had been *better*.

The verb *manna* is the same verb used at verse 5 for God's grace to the oppressed Israelites — *We desired to be gracious (namunna) to those who were oppressed*. The commentators note the echo: the same grace that delivered Israel from Pharaoh is here acknowledged as the only thing that kept these people out of the earth.

The theological content is precise and important. These people had envied Korah, which means they had aligned themselves with his values. The verse states that they were not swallowed *only because of God's grace* — not because their hearts were in better condition.

The tradition has always read this as a statement about the nature of divine forbearance:

> *"And if God were to take mankind to task for what they have earned, He would not leave upon it [the earth] a single creature; but He defers them to an appointed term."* (Qur'an 35:45)

> *"And were it not for the grace of God upon you and His mercy, not one of you would ever have been pure; but God purifies whom He wills."* (Qur'an 24:21)

The commentators note the pastoral conclusion: no one is safe because he is better. Everyone is safe because God has been gracious.

**"Alas! It Seems the Disbelievers Will Not Prosper"**

*Wayka-annahu lā yufliḥu al-kāfirūn*. The third thing they understood, and it closes the Korah passage.

The word *kāfirūn* here is from *k-f-r*, to cover, to be ungrateful, to disbelieve. The commentators note that both senses are in play: Korah was ungrateful for what he was given, and his ingratitude was the substance of his unbelief.

*Lā yufliḥu* — *will not prosper* — is the exact negation of verse 67's *perchance he will be among those who prosper* (*mina al-mufliḥīn*). The sūrah is using the same measure for both outcomes, and the crowd is now applying it correctly.

The same formula closed verse 37, where Moses said *truly the wrongdoers will not prosper*. The sūrah has stated the principle three times — through Moses' mouth, through the knowledgeable, and now through the mouths of the enviers themselves. The commentators note the progression: the principle is first announced by a prophet, then taught by the knowing, then confessed by the crowd that denied it.

**What Changed Overnight**

The commentators draw out the structure of the transformation, because it is the verse's main content.

*Yesterday*: they measured by appearance and concluded that Korah was fortunate (*dhū ḥaẓẓin ʿaẓīm*).
*Today*: they measure by outcome and conclude that the reward of God is better.

The facts did not change. Korah's wealth was real on both days. What changed was the frame: the story had an ending they had not seen, and the ending reorganized everything.

The tradition has made this the definitive Qur'anic statement about *perspective in judgment*:

> *"And We did not make the qiblah which you used to face except that We might make evident who would follow the Messenger from him who would turn back on his heels."* (Qur'an 2:143) — no; the more apt text is:

> *"Nay, but you prefer the life of this world, while the Hereafter is better and more lasting."* (Qur'an 87:16–17)

**Theological Note: The Doctrine of *Basṭ* and *Qabd***

The verse's first clause — that God outspreads and straitens provision — is the basis of an important Islamic doctrine about wealth, and the commentators specify what it does and does not mean.

*It does not mean that effort is irrelevant.* The Qur'an and Sunnah command work, and the tradition has always rejected quietism:

> *"And say: 'Work; and God will see your work, and [so will] His Messenger and the believers.'"* (Qur'an 9:105)

*It does not mean that wealth indicates God's pleasure, or poverty His displeasure.* The verse before us is precisely the refutation of that inference — Korah had more than anyone and was destroyed.

*It means that the outcome of effort is decreed, and that the decree is wise and unobjectionable.* The believer works, accepts the outcome, and does not measure himself or others by it.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The Holy Spirit [Gabriel] inspired into me: 'No soul will die until it has completed its allotted term and its provision. So fear God and be moderate in seeking [provision]; and let not the delay of provision drive you to seek it through disobedience to God. For what is with God is only attained through obedience to Him.'"* (al-Ḥākim; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Do not draw conclusions about anyone before the end.* The crowd's error is the commonest one in human judgment, and the sūrah corrects it in the most vivid way available: wait a day.

*Understand your own safety as grace.* *Had God not been gracious to us, He would have caused us to be engulfed* — this is the correct posture of anyone observing another person's ruin. The tradition's formulation is the Prophetic supplication:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the disappearance of Your blessing, from the change of Your protection, from the suddenness of Your vengeance, and from all of Your displeasure."* (Muslim 2739)

*And let the correction come before the catastrophe.* The people in this verse learned the truth the morning after. The believer is being given the same truth now, in the sūrah, in advance of his own versions of it.

The sūrah's next verse draws the moral of the whole Korah passage in a single sentence.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:83

> **That is the Abode of the Hereafter, which We ordain for those who desire neither dominance upon the earth, nor corruption. And the end belongs to the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Moral of the Korah Passage**

*Tilka al-dāru al-ākhiratu najʿaluhā li-alladhīna lā yurīdūna ʿuluwwan fī al-arḍi wa-lā fasādan wa-al-ʿāqibatu li-al-muttaqīn*.

The sūrah states its conclusion from the Korah narrative in one verse, and the commentators regard it as one of the most comprehensive statements in the Qur'an on the relationship between power, wealth, and salvation.

**"That Is the Abode of the Hereafter"**

*Tilka al-dāru al-ākhiratu*. The demonstrative *tilka* points at something distant and exalted — the same gesture used at verse 2 for the Book. The commentators identify the referent as the Garden, which the verse says God *assigns*:

*Najʿaluhā* — *We make it*, *We appoint it*, *We ordain it*. The verb is emphatic in the present tense: this is God's standing practice, not a one-time decision.

The word *dār* means an abode, a dwelling, a permanent residence. The commentators note the contrast the Qur'an draws throughout between *dār al-dunyā* (the near abode) and *dār al-ākhirah* (the final abode), and they note that the Arabic itself makes the argument: *dunyā* is from *d-n-w*, nearness — the lower, the nearer, the immediate; *ākhirah* is from *ʾ-kh-r*, the last, the end, the final.

**"For Those Who Desire Neither Dominance Upon the Earth, Nor Corruption"**

*Li-alladhīna lā yurīdūna ʿuluwwan fī al-arḍi wa-lā fasādan*. This is the verse's central and most-quoted clause, and the commentators parse it with care.

*The word ʿuluww.* From *ʿ-l-w* — height, exaltedness, ascendancy. It is the same root the sūrah used at verse 4 for Pharaoh's self-exaltation (*Pharaoh exalted himself in the land*) and at verse 39 (*they waxed arrogant upon the earth without right*).

The commentators note the correspondence and its significance. The sūrah opened by diagnosing Pharaoh's *ʿuluww* and now closes by making the renunciation of *ʿuluww* the condition of inheriting the Hereafter. The two are exact opposites, and the sūrah has placed them at opposite ends.

*The word fasād.* As at verses 4 and 77, corruption, rot, the spoiling of what is sound.

*And the crucial grammatical feature:* the negation attaches to the *desire* — *lā yurīdūna*, *those who do not want*. The commentators stress this point, because it is easy to misread the verse as requiring the absence of power or wealth.

The verse does not say *those who have no power*. It says *those who do not desire ʿuluww*. The distinction is enormous and has been the tradition's constant teaching:

> *"And We desired to be gracious to those who were oppressed in the land, and to make them imams, and to make them the heirs."* (Qur'an 28:5)

— the same sūrah says that the oppressed were made *imams* and *heirs*, which is a form of elevation. And Solomon, who had dominion beyond any ruler of his age, is one of the Qur'an's great prophets. The possession of authority is not the problem; the *desire for ascendancy* is.

**Al-Rāzī's Reading: Salvation Is by Renunciation, Not by Position**

Al-Rāzī's gloss, quoted by later commentators, is worth stating: the Hereafter is attained not by dominating or corrupting, but by *no longer dominating or corrupting*.

The commentators unpack this in three directions.

*Wealth is permissible; the desire for ascendancy is not.* A person may be wealthy, may hold office, may be influential, and may be among the saved — provided that what he wants from these things is not to be *above* others.

*The desire, not the state, is what is judged.* A poor man may burn with *ʿuluww*; a king may be free of it. The verse judges the heart.

*And renunciation is the inward form of what generosity is outwardly.* The person who gives because he does not need to be above is practicing the verse's condition; the person who gives in order to be admired is practicing the opposite.

The tradition's texts on this are consistent:

> *"That Abode of the Hereafter — We assign it to those who do not seek exaltedness upon the earth or corruption. And the end belongs to the reverent."*

> *"And whosoever is saved from the avarice of his own soul — those are the successful."* (Qur'an 59:9)

> *"And they give preference [to others] over themselves, even though they be in need. And whosoever is saved from the avarice of his own soul — those are the successful."* (Qur'an 59:9)

The phrase *shuhha nafsih* — the avarice of his own soul — is the tradition's term for the inward condition this verse names.

**The Prophetic Teaching on Dominance**

The Prophet ﷺ warned about *ʿuluww* in terms that the commentators have always set beside this verse:

> *"Whoever humbles himself before God, God will raise him."* (Muslim 2588)

> *"Charity does not decrease wealth; God increases His servant in honor when he forgives; and no one humbles himself before God except that God raises him."* (Muslim 2588)

> *"Truly God has revealed to me: Be humble, so that no one boasts over another and no one oppresses another."* (Muslim 2865)

And in the ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"Glory is My lower garment and greatness is My cloak; whoever competes with Me in either of them, I will cast him into the Fire."* (Muslim 2620)

The last narration is the definitive statement of the creedal principle: exaltedness belongs to God alone, and the creature who reaches for it is competing with his Creator.

**"And the End Belongs to the Reverent"**

*Wa-al-ʿāqibatu li-al-muttaqīn*. The closing clause is one of the most frequently repeated refrains in the Qur'an, and the commentators note that its recurrence is itself part of its meaning.

The word *ʿāqibah*, as at verse 40, means the outcome, the upshot — not merely what comes last in time but what a course of action actually results in.

The word *muttaqīn* — the reverent, the God-conscious — is from *w-q-y*, to guard, to protect. *Taqwā* is the state of a person who has placed a guard between himself and what would harm him.

The tradition's classical definition, attributed to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib: *taqwā* is Fear of the Majestic, acting according to the revelation, contentment with little, and preparation for the day of departure.

And the Prophet ﷺ pointed to its location:

> *"Taqwā is here"* — and he pointed to his chest three times. (Muslim 2564)

The refrain appears throughout the Qur'an:

> *"And the end belongs to the reverent."* (Qur'an 7:128)

> *"So be patient; truly the end belongs to the reverent."* (Qur'an 11:49)

> *"And the good end belongs to the reverent."* (Qur'an 13:35)

> *"Truly the end belongs to the reverent."* (Qur'an 28:83)

The commentators note that the refrain appears in this sūrah at the precise moment when every worldly appearance says otherwise. Pharaoh's power, Korah's wealth, and the Quraysh's position all looked permanent. The verse states that the outcome belongs to the people who had none of those things.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Conclusion**

The commentators note that verse 83 is the hinge of the sūrah's ending. Everything after it — verses 84 through 88 — applies the conclusion to the Prophet ﷺ and then to everyone.

The sūrah's final movement:
*Verse 83*: the principle — the Hereafter belongs to those who renounce dominance and corruption.
*Verse 84*: the mechanism — requital is measured and just.
*Verse 85*: the promise to the Prophet ﷺ — you will return.
*Verse 86*: the reminder — the Book was not expected; it was mercy.
*Verse 87*: the instruction — do not be turned away.
*Verse 88*: the creed — there is no god but He; all things perish save His Face.

**Application**

The verse supplies the tradition's standard for ambition, and it is worth stating plainly because it is frequently misunderstood.

*Ambition is not forbidden; domination is.* The believer may seek excellence, position, influence, and wealth. What he may not seek is the *height* itself — the state of being above others, the pleasure of being deferred to, the security of being untouchable.

*The test is the desire, not the circumstance.* The tradition's diagnostic is not "do I have power?" but "what do I want it for, and would I be content without it?"

*And the outcome is already assigned.* *The end belongs to the reverent.* The verse states the conclusion in the present tense, before the events that would appear to confirm or deny it. The tradition's confidence rests on that: the outcome is not in doubt, and the believer may therefore afford to be patient, generous, and free of the need to dominate.

For the sūrah's original hearers — a small, persecuted community in Makkah, watching a wealthy and powerful opposition — this verse was the answer to the question of who was actually winning.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:84

> **Whosoever brings a good deed, he shall have better than it; and whosoever brings an evil deed, those who perform evil deeds shall only be recompensed for that which they have done.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Law of Requital**

*Man jāʾa bi-al-ḥasanati fa-lahu khayrun minhā wa-man jāʾa bi-al-sayyiʾati fa-lā yujzā alladhīna ʿamilū al-sayyiʾāti illā mā kānū yaʿmalūn*.

The sūrah now states the mechanism by which the promise of verse 83 is administered. The commentators note the structural sequence: the destination is named (v. 83), then the rule of the road (v. 84).

**"Whosoever Brings a Good Deed, He Shall Have Better Than It"**

*Man jāʾa bi-al-ḥasanati fa-lahu khayrun minhā*. The verb *jāʾa bi* — *he brought* — is significant, and the commentators dwell on it. A person does not merely *do* a good deed; he *brings* it, as though carrying something to a place.

The image is of a traveler arriving with goods. The deed is what a person has to show when he arrives.

The promise is *khayrun minhā* — *better than it*. The commentators note the comparative and its implication: the reward exceeds the deed.

The Qur'an specifies the excess elsewhere:

> *"Whosoever brings a good deed shall have ten times the like thereof; and whosoever brings an evil deed shall be recompensed only with the like thereof; and they shall not be wronged."* (Qur'an 6:160)

That verse gives the standard measure: a good deed is multiplied tenfold, and an evil deed is recorded once. The tradition has elaborated the multipliers:

> *"The parable of those who spend their wealth in the way of God is that of a grain which produces seven ears, in every ear a hundred grains. And God multiplies for whom He wills."* (Qur'an 2:261)

— giving seven hundred for charity specifically.

And the Prophet ﷺ reported from his Lord:

> *"God has written down the good deeds and the bad deeds... Whosoever intends to do a good deed and does not do it, God writes it down with Himself as a complete good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it down with Himself as ten good deeds, up to seven hundred, up to many times over. And whosoever intends a bad deed and does not do it, God writes it down with Himself as a complete good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it down as one bad deed."* (al-Bukhārī 6491; Muslim 131)

The commentators note the three features of that ḥadīth that bear directly on this verse: the multiplication is real, the intention itself counts, and the ratio is deliberately asymmetrical in the creature's favor.

**"And Whosoever Brings an Evil Deed..."**

*Wa-man jāʾa bi-al-sayyiʾati*. The same verb — *brought* — is used for the evil deed. The commentators note the symmetry: both kinds of deed are carried to the same place and presented.

**"Those Who Perform Evil Deeds Shall Only Be Recompensed for That Which They Have Done"**

*Fa-lā yujzā alladhīna ʿamilū al-sayyiʾāti illā mā kānū yaʿmalūn*. The construction is emphatic: *they shall not be recompensed except for what they used to do*.

The commentators note two things in this clause.

*The restriction* — *illā*, *only*. No one is punished beyond his deeds. This is the Qur'an's guarantee of divine justice, stated in the strongest terms:

> *"And thy Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

> *"Truly God wrongs not [anyone] by so much as an atom's weight; and if there be a good deed, He multiplies it and gives from Himself a mighty reward."* (Qur'an 4:40)

That verse is the exact commentary on this one, and the commentators cite it constantly: the *atom's weight* establishes the justice, and the *multiplication* establishes the generosity.

*And the change of subject.* The verse begins *whosoever brings an evil deed* (singular) and then continues *those who perform evil deeds* (plural). The commentators note this as a deliberate softening: the evil-doer is described as part of a class rather than pointed at individually, which the tradition reads as a mercy even in the statement of justice.

The Qur'an does the same elsewhere:

> *"And whosoever commits a sin, it is only against himself."* (Qur'an 4:111)

**Theological Note: Justice and Generosity in the Divine Economy**

The verse and its companions establish what the tradition came to call the *mīzān* — the balance — and the commentators derived several principles from it.

*Requital is by measure, and the measure is known:*

> *"And We shall set up the scales of justice for the Day of Resurrection, so no soul will be wronged at all; and if there be [even] the weight of a mustard seed, We shall bring it forth. And sufficient are We as reckoners."* (Qur'an 21:47)

*God's justice is exact and His generosity is free.* The tradition distinguished carefully: the punishment is *strictly proportionate* (*jazāʾan wifāqan*, 78:26 — *a fitting recompense*), while the reward is *disproportionate by grace*. The asymmetry is not an inconsistency; it is the definition of the relationship.

*And no one enters the Garden by his deeds alone.* The tradition held this with total consistency alongside the emphasis on works:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds."* They said, "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said, *"Not even me, unless God envelops me in His mercy and grace."* (al-Bukhārī 5673; Muslim 2816)

The commentators reconcile the two positions as follows: deeds are the *necessary condition* and mercy is the *efficient cause*. A person cannot enter without deeds, and he does not enter by them. Both are true, and the tradition refuses to sacrifice either.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

The commentators note the placement of verse 84 immediately after verse 83.

Verse 83 said the Hereafter is appointed for those who renounce dominance and corruption. Verse 84 explains why the renunciation is rational: because the accounting is exact, and because the good is multiplied.

The logic is economic and the sūrah has been building it since verse 60. Every worldly possession is *matāʿ* and *zīnah* — temporary use and ornament (v. 60). What is with God is *better and more lasting* (v. 60). And the exchange rate is stated here: a good deed returns better than itself, and an evil deed returns only itself.

The commentators note the conclusion: in a market where the return on good is multiplied and the return on evil is one-for-one, only a person who does not understand the terms would choose as Korah chose.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Every deed is carried somewhere.* The verb *jāʾa bi* — brought — should govern how a person moves through the day. What am I accumulating to bring?

*The exchange rate favors goodness overwhelmingly.* The tradition's counsel to anyone hesitating over an act of charity, patience, or restraint is simply this verse: the return is ten to seven hundred and beyond, and the loss is one-for-one. The arithmetic is not close.

*And the justice is exact.* The restriction *only for what they did* is a comfort to anyone who has feared that God's judgment might be capricious or disproportionate. The tradition's verse for this fear:

> *"And We did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves."* (Qur'an 16:118)

For the persecuted community in Makkah, this verse carried a specific message: what you are suffering is recorded, and what they are doing is recorded, and neither will be mismeasured.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:85

> **Truly the One Who ordained the Quran for thee shall surely bring thee back to the place of return. Say, "My Lord knows best those who bring guidance, and those who are in manifest error."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Most Personal Verse**

*Inna alladhī faraḍa ʿalayka al-qurʾāna la-rādduka ilā maʿādin qul rabbī aʿlamu man jāʾa bi-al-hudā wa-man huwa fī ḍalālin mubīn*.

The commentators note that this verse is addressed directly to the Prophet ﷺ and contains one of the most specific promises in the Qur'an. Its occasion and its wording have both been discussed at length.

**"The One Who Ordained the Qur'an for Thee"**

*Alladhī faraḍa ʿalayka al-qurʾāna*. The verb *faraḍa* means to ordain, to make obligatory, to decree. It is the word from which *farḍ* — the obligatory act in Islamic law — is derived.

The commentators record two readings of the phrase, and both are transmitted from the earliest authorities.

*Al-Rāzī's reading*: *ordained* means *made its obligations and prohibitions binding*. On this reading, the emphasis is on the *content* — the Qur'an's commands are a charge laid upon the Prophet ﷺ and his community.

*Al-Ṭabarī's reading*: *ordained* here means *bestowed*, *sent down*, *decreed*. On this reading, the emphasis is on the *gift* — the One who gave you the Qur'an.

The commentators note that the two readings are complementary and that the argument of the verse works on both: the One who *commanded* it will see it through, and the One who *gave* it will not abandon the one He gave it to.

**"Shall Surely Bring Thee Back to the Place of Return"**

*La-rādduka ilā maʿādin*. The promise is emphatic: *inna* plus *la-* plus the active participle *rādduka* — *truly, He is certainly returning you*.

The word *maʿād* means a place of return, a destination to which one comes back. The commentators discuss what it refers to, and they record several readings.

*Makkah.* The majority of the commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Rāzī, transmit that this is a promise that the Prophet ﷺ would return to Makkah — the city from which he was being driven — victorious. The reading fits the historical circumstances of the verse's revelation and was fulfilled at the conquest of Makkah in the eighth year after the Hijrah.

*Death and the Resurrection.* Some commentators read *maʿād* as referring to the return to God after death, or to the Resurrection. Al-Qurṭubī transmits this.

*The Garden.* Others read it as the ultimate return to Paradise.

The commentators note that these readings are not exclusive, and the tradition has generally held them together: the promise has an immediate historical fulfillment (Makkah), an inevitable personal fulfillment (death and the Resurrection), and an ultimate fulfillment (the Garden).

**The Occasion: Juḥfah, on the Road to Madinah**

The reports transmitted by al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and others place this verse's revelation at Juḥfah, a place between Makkah and Madinah, during the Prophet's emigration.

The account: having left Makkah under threat of death, traveling by an unfamiliar route, the Prophet ﷺ felt the sadness of departure, and this verse was revealed to console him with the promise that he would return.

The commentators note the deliberate parallel with Moses, which is the sūrah's organizing theme. Moses left Egypt as a fugitive and was returned to it with a mission and a victory. The verse tells the Prophet ﷺ that his story will have the same shape — and indeed, the conquest of Makkah came eight years later, and he entered it with his head bowed in gratitude while his army surrounded him.

The tradition records the fulfillment:

> *On the day of the conquest of Makkah, the Prophet ﷺ entered the city riding his camel, his head bowed so low in humility before God that his beard nearly touched the saddle, reciting Sūrat al-Fatḥ.* (al-Bukhārī 4285; the account is well known in the sīrah)

**The Theological Structure of the Promise**

The commentators draw out the argument embedded in the verse's grammar, and it is worth stating because it is one of the most consoling patterns in the Qur'an.

*The premise*: the One who *ordained* the Qur'an for you — who laid upon you a task of cosmic weight — is the One speaking.
*The conclusion*: He *shall surely bring you back*.

The logic is that the One who gives a mission also guarantees its completion. This is the structure of every divine promise to the prophets:

> *"And We desired to be gracious to those who were oppressed in the land, and to make them imams, and to make them the heirs."* (Qur'an 28:5)

> *"Truly We shall help Our messengers and those who believe, in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses shall stand forth."* (Qur'an 40:51)

> *"O Messenger, convey that which has been sent down unto thee from thy Lord... And God will protect thee from mankind."* (Qur'an 5:67)

The commentators note the practical inference for the believer: the God who commands is the God who enables. A person who has been given a task he cannot carry has also been promised the help to carry it, and the two promises came from the same Speaker.

**"Say: 'My Lord Knows Best Those Who Bring Guidance'"**

*Qul rabbī aʿlamu man jāʾa bi-al-hudā*. The second half of the verse instructs the Prophet ﷺ to transfer the verdict about his mission to God.

The commentators note the connection to verse 37, where Moses said *My Lord knows best who brings guidance from Him*. The sūrah is deliberately echoing Moses' words in the mouth of Muhammad ﷺ — another of the many links the sūrah establishes between the two careers.

The instruction is also the correct response to rejection. When a person is told that his life's work is delusion, the Qur'an's answer is not argument but referral: *My Lord knows best who brings guidance*.

The tradition's corresponding counsel for the believer who is mocked for his religion:

> *"And if they deny thee, say: 'Unto me my deeds, and unto you your deeds. You are innocent of what I do, and I am innocent of what you do.'"* (Qur'an 10:41)

**"And Those Who Are in Manifest Error"**

*Wa-man huwa fī ḍalālin mubīn*. The phrase is carefully worded. Note that it says *those who are in manifest error* rather than *those who are astray* — the error is *mubīn*, clear, evident, unmistakable.

The commentators note the implication: the people opposing the Prophet ﷺ were not in a subtle difficulty. The guidance had been delivered with signs, recited in their own language, by a man whose truthfulness they had attested to for forty years. Their error was manifest, and the verse says so.

**The Verse as a Prayer for Travelers**

The tradition has used this verse in a specific devotional way, and the commentators mention it: it is among the verses recommended for a person setting out on a journey, and particularly for one returning home.

The basis for this practice is the verse's content — it is a promise of return — combined with the general Prophetic guidance on travel supplications:

> *"God is Greatest, God is Greatest, God is Greatest. Glory be to Him Who has subjected this to us, and we could not have accomplished it. And truly unto our Lord we shall return."* (Muslim 1342)

The commentators note that the practice is a lawful devotional use of the Qur'an's words and is not to be treated as a *sunnah* with a specific prescribed form unless a specific Prophetic text supports it.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*The God who commands also returns.* For anyone who has had to leave something — a city, a home, a relationship, a phase of life — this verse is the promise that leaving is not the last word. The tradition's confidence rests on the verse's grammar: the One who ordained is the One who returns.

*Exile may be the form obedience takes.* Neither Moses nor Muhammad ﷺ chose to leave. Both were driven out, and both were returned. The tradition has drawn from this the understanding that departure is sometimes the mechanism by which a promise is fulfilled rather than the obstacle to it.

*And the verdict belongs to God.* *My Lord knows best those who bring guidance* is the answer to every accusation of futility. The tradition's counsel is to say it and continue.

The sūrah's final three verses now turn from promise to instruction, and close with one of the Qur'an's greatest creedal statements.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:86

> **And thou didst not hope that the Book would be delivered unto thee; rather, it was a mercy from thy Lord. So be not a support for the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Thou Didst Not Hope"**

*Wa-mā kunta tarjū an yulqā ilayka al-kitābu illā raḥmatan min rabbika*.

The verse opens by reminding the Prophet ﷺ of something he knew better than anyone: he had not expected revelation.

The commentators note the argumentative function of this reminder. The sūrah has been establishing since verse 44 that the Prophet ﷺ had no natural access to the knowledge he was conveying. This verse adds a psychological dimension: he not only lacked the *means* to acquire the Book, he lacked the *expectation* of receiving it.

The tradition's biographical material confirms what the verse states. The Prophet ﷺ spent his early manhood in Makkah as a merchant, and there is no report of his having sought prophethood, studied with the People of the Book, or expressed any ambition in that direction. The revelation came to him in a cave while he was engaged in solitary retreat — and the ḥadīth records his terror:

> *The angel came to him and said, "Read." He said, "I am not a reader." He said: "Then the angel took hold of me and pressed me until I could bear no more, then released me and said, 'Read.' I said, 'I am not a reader.' He took hold of me and pressed me a second time... and a third time, then released me and said: 'Read in the Name of thy Lord Who created...'"* (al-Bukhārī 3; Muslim 160)

The ʿĀʾishah version adds his return home:

> *...and he returned with it, his heart trembling, and entered upon Khadījah and said, "Wrap me, wrap me!"* (al-Bukhārī 3)

The commentators note the significance: a man who had plotted to acquire the Book would not have responded to its arrival with terror. The fear is itself evidence of the genuineness of the claim, and the verse states the fact.

Al-Rāzī records an alternative grammatical reading: *thou didst not hope that the Book would be delivered unto thee, but only for mercy from thy Lord* — taking *illā* as an exception meaning *except as a mercy*. On this reading, the sense is: you had no expectation of the Book *except* through the mercy of your Lord. Both readings converge on the same doctrine, which the verse states explicitly: it was *raḥmatan min rabbika*.

**"Rather, It Was a Mercy From Thy Lord"**

*Illā raḥmatan min rabbika*. The commentators stress this clause, because it contains the verse's theology.

The Book was not earned, not sought, not merited, and not acquired. It was *mercy*. This is the Islamic doctrine of revelation in a phrase, and the tradition has always held it as a guard against two errors:

*The error of merit* — that a person can earn prophethood or revelation by his striving. The Qur'an states the corrective repeatedly:

> *"Say: 'Had God willed, I would not have recited it unto you, nor would He have made it known to you.'"* (Qur'an 10:16)

> *"God knows best where to place His message."* (Qur'an 6:124)

> *"And thy Lord creates what He wills, and chooses; no choice have they."* (Qur'an 28:68 — in this very sūrah)

*And the error of despair* — that a person is too lowly to be chosen. The verse before us refutes this as well: the Prophet ﷺ had not hoped, and was given. The tradition has made this the ground of its confidence that God raises whom He wills from whatever station He wills.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God does not look at your forms and your wealth, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds."* (Muslim 2564)

And the Qur'an states the principle of unmerited election in its most consoling form:

> *"And We have not sent thee, save as a mercy unto the worlds."* (Qur'an 21:107)

**"So Be Not a Support for the Disbelievers"**

*Fa-lā takūnanna ẓahīran li-al-kāfirīn*. The word *ẓahīr* — a supporter, a backer, one who lends his weight — is the same word Moses used at verse 17 when he vowed: *I shall never be a supporter (ẓahīran) of the guilty*.

The commentators note the deliberate echo, and it is one of the sūrah's most beautiful structural devices. Moses made the vow at the beginning of his career; the final Prophet is given the same instruction at the end of the sūrah. The two messengers are bound together by the same commitment.

**What the Prohibition Means**

The commentators specify the scope of *being a support for the disbelievers*, and their discussion is substantive because the practical questions are real.

*It forbids compromising the religion to accommodate them.* The historical context is the Quraysh's negotiations with the Prophet ﷺ, in which they proposed that he worship their gods for a period and they would worship his God for a period. The response was Sūrat al-Kāfirūn — a flat refusal:

> *"Say: 'O disbelievers, I worship not what you worship, nor do you worship what I worship... Unto you your religion, and unto me my religion.'"* (Qur'an 109:1–6)

*It forbids aiding them against the believers.* The Qur'an legislates this explicitly:

> *"O you who believe, take not the disbelievers as allies instead of the believers."* (Qur'an 4:144)

> *"O you who believe, take not My enemy and your enemy as allies, extending affection to them."* (Qur'an 60:1)

*And it forbids the internal posture of seeking their approval.* The commentators note that the prohibition covers not only formal alliances but the subtle alignment of one's judgments with what the disbelievers would approve of:

> *"And they wish that you would compromise, and then they would compromise."* (Qur'an 68:9)

> *"And had We not strengthened thee, you might have inclined toward them a little."* (Qur'an 17:74)

**What It Does Not Forbid**

The commentators are equally careful about the limits, because the verse is frequently misapplied.

*It does not forbid just dealing with non-Muslims.* The Qur'an commands it:

> *"God does not forbid you, with regard to those who do not fight you because of religion and do not drive you out of your homes, from dealing kindly and justly with them. Truly God loves the just."* (Qur'an 60:8)

*It does not forbid learning from them, trading with them, or living among them.* The Prophet ﷺ traded with the Quraysh before his mission, dealt with the Jews of Madinah throughout it, and accepted the counsel of Salmān al-Fārsī at the Battle of the Trench.

*And it does not forbid calling them to the truth.* The prohibition is on *supporting* them in their disbelief, not on loving their guidance:

> *"And We have not sent thee, save as a mercy to the worlds."* (Qur'an 21:107)

The tradition's distinction, as the jurists formulated it, is between *muwālāh* — alliance in religion, which is forbidden — and *muʿāmalah* — ordinary dealing, which is permitted and often commendable.

**Theological Note: The Prophet's Protection From Error**

The commentators raise the obvious question: was the Prophet ﷺ in danger of becoming a support for the disbelievers?

Their answer draws on the doctrine of *ʿiṣmah*. The prohibition here is not a warning against a real possibility of lapse; it serves three other purposes.

*It establishes the rule for the community.* The addressee is the Prophet ﷺ, but the instruction is binding on every believer, and the tradition has always read it that way.

*It magnifies the gravity of the thing.* If the Prophet ﷺ is told *do not*, then the matter is grave for everyone else.

*And it demonstrates the reality of human will.* The Qur'an addresses the Prophet ﷺ as a moral agent who could have chosen otherwise — which is precisely what makes his obedience meritorious. The tradition has held both: he was protected from error, and his obedience was real and was tested.

The commentators point to 17:74, quoted above, as the clearest statement of this structure: *and had We not strengthened thee, you might have inclined toward them a little*.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Remember that what you have been given was mercy.* Every capacity, every opportunity, every item of guidance a person possesses was not earned. The tradition's discipline is to receive without pride, since pride in a gift is the beginning of its loss.

*Do not lean toward those who oppose the truth.* The prohibition covers the subtle cases — the softening of a judgment because it would be better received, the silence because speaking would cost, the alignment because the other side has the influence. The tradition's rule:

> *"Whosoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand; if he cannot, then with his tongue; and if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith."* (Muslim 49)

*And deal justly with everyone.* The prohibition on alliance is not a license for hostility. Justice, kindness, and fair dealing are owed to all, and the verse's limit is precisely at the point where support for disbelief would begin.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:87

> **And let them not turn thee from the signs of God after they have been sent down unto thee. But call [others] to thy Lord and be not among those who ascribe partners unto God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Instruction to Steadfastness**

*Wa-lā yaṣuddunnaka ʿan āyāti Allāhi baʿda idh unzilat ilayka wa-udʿu ilā rabbika wa-lā takūnanna mina al-mushrikīn*.

The verse instructs the Prophet ﷺ in two movements: a prohibition against being turned away, and a command to call. The commentators note that this is the sūrah's final instruction to the messenger before its closing creedal statement, and that the two halves correspond to the two poles of the religious life — holding fast oneself, and calling others.

**"Let Them Not Turn Thee From the Signs of God"**

*Wa-lā yaṣuddunnaka ʿan āyāti Allāh*. The verb *ṣadda* means to turn away, to avert, to block, to divert. It is the same verb the Qur'an uses for what the opponents of the prophets do to *other people*:

> *"And those who disbelieve say to those who believe: 'Follow our way, and we will bear your sins.'"* (Qur'an 29:12) — no; the more exact usage is:

> *"And when they see a transaction or a diversion, they scatter off to it..."* (Qur'an 62:11)

> *"And they turn away from it and are arrogant."* (Qur'an 32:22 — *yaṣdifūna*)

The verb *ṣadda* appears in the key passage on hindering others from the way of God:

> *"And they hindered [many] from the way of God."* (Qur'an 9:9) — *wa-ṣaddū ʿan sabīli Allāh*

The commentators note the significance of applying the verb here to the *Prophet*. The Quraysh had been preventing others from hearing the message for years; this verse warns that the same pressure could, in principle, operate on the messenger himself.

**How They Tried to Turn Him**

The commentators enumerate the specific pressures the verse addresses, drawing on the historical material transmitted in the sīrah and *asbāb al-nuzūl* literature.

*Persecution and threat.* The boycott of the Banū Hāshim, the physical assaults, the attempt on his life that precipitated the Hijrah.

*Negotiation and compromise.* The offer of wealth, kingship, and marriage into the best families in exchange for abandoning the call — the offer transmitted in the sīrah, in which ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah presented the proposals and the Prophet ﷺ answered him with the opening verses of Sūrat Fuṣṣilat.

*Mockery and social pressure.* The accusations of sorcery, poetry, and madness recorded throughout the Qur'an.

*And the specific attempt recorded for this verse:* al-Qurṭubī transmits that the Quraysh were trying to induce the Prophet ﷺ to praise or magnify their gods — the episode to which the "satanic verses" fabrication is remotely and falsely attached, and which the tradition rejected decisively. The genuine report is the negotiation described above, and the Qur'an's response to it is absolute.

The commentators note the Qur'an's record of the pressure in its own words:

> *"And indeed they were about to tempt thee away from that which We revealed unto thee, so that you might invent against Us something other than it; and then they would have taken thee as a friend. And had We not strengthened thee, you might have inclined toward them a little. Then We would have made thee taste a double [punishment] in life and a double [punishment] after death; and then you would find no helper against Us."* (Qur'an 17:73–75)

That passage is the definitive commentary on this verse, and the commentators read them together.

**"After They Have Been Sent Down Unto Thee"**

*Baʿda idh unzilat ilayka*. The clause adds gravity to the prohibition. The commentators note the argument: it is one thing to lack guidance; it is another to have received it clearly and then be turned from it.

The Qur'an states the principle generally:

> *"And whosoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him and follows other than the way of the believers, We shall leave him to what he has chosen, and We shall expose him to Hell — an evil journey's end."* (Qur'an 4:115)

**"But Call Unto Thy Lord"**

*Wa-udʿu ilā rabbika*. The positive command that answers the prohibition. The commentators note the structure: do not be turned away, and *call*.

The verb *daʿā* — to call, to invite, to summon — is the root of *daʿwah*, and the verse is one of the primary texts establishing the obligation of calling to God.

The tradition has derived a great deal from this and similar commands:

> *"Call unto the way of thy Lord with wisdom and beautiful exhortation, and argue with them in the best manner. Truly thy Lord knows best who has strayed from His way, and He knows best who is guided."* (Qur'an 16:125)

> *"And say: '[This is] the truth from your Lord; so whosoever will, let him believe; and whosoever will, let him disbelieve.'"* (Qur'an 18:29)

> *"Say: 'This is my way; I call to God with insight, I and whosoever follows me. And glory be to God; and I am not among those who ascribe partners.'"* (Qur'an 12:108)

Note that 12:108 combines exactly the two elements of this verse: *I call to God* and *I am not among those who ascribe partners*. The commentators note the parallel and treat the two verses as a single statement of the caller's condition: he calls, and he does not compromise.

**The Obligation and Its Limits**

The tradition's scholars worked out the scope of *daʿwah* from verses like this one, and their conclusions include:

*It is an obligation on the community*, discharged by some on behalf of all (*farḍ kifāyah*), and on every individual to the extent of his capacity:

> *"Convey from me even if it be a single verse."* (al-Bukhārī 3461)

*Its method is wisdom and good counsel*, not coercion:

> *"There is no compulsion in religion. Rectitude has become distinct from error."* (Qur'an 2:256)

*And the caller is not responsible for the outcome*:

> *"So remind; you are only a reminder. You are not a taskmaster over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

The Prophet ﷺ stated the reward and its independence from results:
