> *"By God, that God should guide one man through you is better for you than the best of camels."* (al-Bukhārī 2942; Muslim 2406)

and:

> *"Whoever calls to guidance has a reward like the rewards of all who follow him, without diminishing anything from their rewards."* (Muslim 2674)

**"And Be Not Among Those Who Ascribe Partners Unto God"**

*Wa-lā takūnanna mina al-mushrikīn*. The verse closes with the prohibition of *shirk*, and the commentators note that this is the sūrah's final prohibition before its final declaration (v. 88).

The commentators raise the question that arises here as at verse 86: was the Prophet ﷺ in danger of *shirk*?

Their answer:

*The prohibition establishes the rule for the community.* As with verse 86, the address to the Prophet ﷺ is the strongest available way of stating a universal obligation.

*And it defines the outer limit of the compromise being refused.* The Quraysh's proposal was, in its essence, an invitation to *shirk* — to acknowledge their gods alongside his. The verse names the proposal for what it was.

**Al-Rāzī's Observation on the Audience**

Al-Rāzī notes that some commentators held that the real audience of this verse is the *community of believers* rather than the Prophet ﷺ personally, since the Prophet ﷺ is protected from *shirk* by unanimous agreement.

The tradition's position, held by the People of Ḥadīth and the schools of creed, is that the prophets are protected from *major* sins and from anything that would discredit their message, and that this verse is addressed to them in order to legislate for their communities and to demonstrate the gravity of the matter.

**Application**

Three things carry from this verse.

*Expect pressure, and name it.* The verse assumes that the believer will be pushed — by persecution, by negotiation, by mockery, by the offer of advantage. The tradition's counsel is not to be surprised by the pressure but to recognize it when it arrives.

*Resist by continuing.* The command that answers *let them not turn thee* is *call*. The tradition's observation is that the most effective form of steadfastness is continued activity: the person who keeps doing the work is much harder to divert than the person who merely resolves not to be diverted.

*And keep the two together.* *Call to thy Lord* and *be not among those who ascribe partners* — the call and the purity of the call are a single obligation. The tradition's warning, drawn from this and 12:108, is that a *daʿwah* which compromises the *tawḥīd* it is calling to is not *daʿwah* at all.

The sūrah now closes with the creedal statement toward which all of its narratives have been moving.

---
## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:88

> **And call not upon another god along with God. There is no god but He! All things perish, save His Face. Judgment belongs to Him, and unto Him will you be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Final Word**

*Wa-lā tadʿu maʿa Allāhi ilāhan ākhara lā ilāha illā huwa kullu shayʾin hālikun illā wajhahu lahu al-ḥukmu wa-ilayhi turjaʿūn*.

The sūrah that opened with three unreadable letters closes with the most readable of all statements. The commentators note the arc: from *Ṭā. Sīn. Mīm.* to *there is no god but He*. The Book's mysteries are at its beginning; its demand is at its end.

This verse contains four declarations, and each is among the most consequential in the Qur'an.

**"And Call Not Upon Another God Along With God"**

*Wa-lā tadʿu maʿa Allāhi ilāhan ākhara*. The prohibition is on *calling* — *duʿāʾ* — which the commentators identify as the heart of worship.

The tradition's basis for this identification is the ḥadīth:

> *"Supplication is the essence of worship."* Then he recited: *"And your Lord says, 'Call upon Me; I will respond to you. Truly those who wax arrogant before My worship shall enter Hell, humbled.'"* (al-Tirmidhī 3372; Abū Dāwūd 1479 — from Qur'an 40:60)

The commentators note the prohibition's grammar: *maʿa Allāhi* — *along with*, *together with*. The verse does not say "call not upon a god *instead of* God." It says *along with*. This is the precise formulation of the error the Qur'an is refuting, in every age and in every heart: not the denial of God, but the admission of a second object of trust beside Him.

The tradition's three categories of *shirk*, discussed at verse 74, all fall under this prohibition: the major, in which worship is directed to another; the minor, in which deeds are done for another's approval; and the hidden, in which reliance settles on a means rather than on the Giver of means.

**"There Is No God But He"**

*Lā ilāha illā huwa*. The testimony of faith in its negative-and-affirmative form, as at verse 70.

The commentators note the sūrah's structure: it has stated this twice (vv. 70, 88), and the two statements bracket the sūrah's final movement. In between, the sūrah has shown what *shirk* looks like in practice: Pharaoh claiming divinity (v. 38), the idolaters being asked *where are My partners?* (vv. 62, 74), Korah attributing his wealth to his own knowledge (v. 78), and the crowd measuring a man by his possessions (v. 79).

The sūrah has therefore not merely asserted monotheism; it has shown the shape of every rival claim and then concluded with the assertion.

**"All Things Perish, Save His Face"**

*Kullu shayʾin hālikun illā wajhahu*. This is one of the most quoted, most discussed, and most theologically loaded clauses in the Qur'an, and the commentators have given it more attention than almost any phrase in the sūrah.

*The word wajh.* It means face, countenance, and — by extension in Arabic idiom — the reality of a thing, its essence, what it *is*. The lexicographers note that *wajh* in the Qur'an can denote:

*God Himself* — the Essence. This is the reading transmitted by al-Qurṭubī from several authorities, and it is the most direct.

*The Divine Essence (al-Dhāt)* — reading the phrase as "all things perish except His Self." This is the reading of al-Zamakhsharī and others.

*The Truth or Reality (al-Ḥaqq)* — recorded by al-Thaʿlabī.

*That which is turned toward*, i.e., the aspect of the Divine Reality that faces creation. Al-Rāzī records this reading and finds it elegant: what remains is not the world's face turned toward God but God's face turned toward the world.

*God's religion.* Several commentators, including al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī, transmit the reading that *wajh* here means *that by which one seeks God* — His religion and His worship. On this reading the verse means: everything perishes except what was done for God's sake.

The commentators note that these readings are not mutually exclusive and that the tradition has held several of them together. The safest formulation, and the one the scholars preferred: *wajh* is an attribute affirmed of God in a manner befitting Him, without likening it to the faces of creatures, without denying it, and without asking how.

This is the standard Sunni rule for the *ṣifāt* — the divine attributes — stated in the creedal literature:

> *"We affirm what God affirmed for Himself, without tahrīf (distortion), taʿṭīl (denial), takyīf (asking how), or tamthīl (likening Him to creation)."*

*The word hālik.* It means perishing, being destroyed, passing away. The commentators record several readings of *how* things perish:

*Annihilation.* Some read *hālik* as meaning that created things become literally nothing.

*Becoming unusable.* Al-Rāzī's preferred reading, and the one most commentators adopted: a thing is said to "perish" when it ceases to function as what it was, even though its constituent parts remain. He gives the example of a garment: when it is so worn that it can no longer be worn, one says it has perished — though the threads remain.

This reading connects directly to the Qur'an's account of the transformation of the cosmos:

> *"On the Day the earth shall be changed into other than the earth, and the heavens [too]."* (Qur'an 14:48)

*Essential contingency.* Others read the phrase as stating a permanent truth rather than an event: every created thing is *essentially* perishable (*mumkin al-wujūd*, contingent), dependent for its existence at every instant on its Creator, and only God is necessary in Himself (*wājib al-wujūd*).

The commentators note that this last reading gives the verse its philosophical weight: the world does not merely *end*; it is, in every moment of its existence, held in being by Another.

*And the parallel verse.* The commentators always set this clause beside its twin in Sūrat al-Raḥmān:

> *"All upon it passes away; and there remains the Face of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Honor."* (Qur'an 55:26–27)

**The Ḥadīth of Labīd**

Ibn Kathīr quotes, in his commentary on this verse, the ḥadīth of the pre-Islamic poet Labīd:

> The Prophet ﷺ said: *"The truest words spoken by a poet were those of Labīd: 'Behold! Everything besides God is false (bāṭil).' "* (al-Bukhārī, in the Book of Manners; Muslim 2256)

The poem continues: *"And every enjoyment will without doubt vanish."*

The commentators note several things about this report. First, its function is to show that the truth of this verse is so evident that even a pre-Islamic Arab poet, working with only the residue of Abrahamic monotheism, had grasped it. Second, the Prophet's approval of a single line of poetry is not an endorsement of poetry as a genre — and the Qur'an itself restricts the matter:

> *"And as for the poets, those who stray follow them. Have you not seen that in every valley they roam, and that they say what they do not do? Except those who believe and work righteous deeds and remember God much."* (Qur'an 26:224–227)

And third, the report is one of the tradition's many instances of the Prophet ﷺ quoting approved pre-Islamic wisdom — a practice that reflects the Islamic understanding that truth is one and is recognized wherever it is found.

**The Spiritual Reading: What Does Not Perish**

The spiritual commentators, including al-Qurṭubī's transmitted readings and Ibn Kathīr's, developed the clause *all things perish save His Face* into one of the tradition's most practical disciplines.

On this reading, the *wajh* that remains is *what was done for God's sake*. Everything else — the deed done for reputation, the wealth accumulated for its own sake, the achievement pursued for the self — perishes with the self. Only what was done *for His Face* survives.

The tradition's term for this is *ikhlāṣ* — sincerity — and the verse is its foundation. The classical definition:

> *"I say: 'my intention is to draw near to God' — for the sake of His Face, not for praise, not for status, not for the pleasure of being seen."*

The Prophet ﷺ gave the tradition its most precise statement of the criterion:

> *"Deeds are only by intentions, and every person shall have only what he intended. So whoever emigrated for God and His Messenger, his emigration was for God and His Messenger; and whoever emigrated for some worldly gain or to marry a woman, his emigration was for that which he emigrated to."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907)

And the ḥadīth qudsī that states which deeds survive:

> *"I am the most self-sufficient of partners: whoever does a deed in which he associates another with Me, I leave him and his association."* (Muslim 2985)

The commentators note the practical conclusion and have repeated it in every generation: the verse supplies a daily test. Of anything I am doing — does this have a Face? Is it done toward Him? If not, it is among the things that perish.

**"Judgment Belongs to Him"**

*Lahu al-ḥukmu*. The third declaration, stated identically at verse 70. The commentators note the recurrence: the sūrah has now stated God's exclusive judgment at its theological center (v. 70) and at its conclusion (v. 88).

The word *ḥukm* covers judgment, authority, rule, and wisdom — all of which belong to God alone, as discussed at verse 70.

The commentators note the specific relevance of this clause at the sūrah's close. The sūrah has narrated the judgments of history: Pharaoh drowned, his people destroyed, the towns ruined, Korah swallowed by the earth, and the Prophet ﷺ driven from his home and promised return. The verse states that all of these were *His* judgments, and that none of them was arbitrary.

**"And Unto Him Will You Be Returned"**

*Wa-ilayhi turjaʿūn*. The final words of the sūrah, identical in wording to verse 70's closing clause.

The commentators note the deliberate circularity of the sūrah's ending. It opened with the disconnected letters and the *clear Book*; it narrated oppression and deliverance, arrogance and ruin, exile and promise; it argued from the unseen knowledge of a prophet, from the alternation of night and day, from the fate of cities and of a wealthy man; and it closes by returning the reader to the one fact from which all of it follows: you are going back to Him.

**The Sūrah's Complete Argument**

It is worth tracing, at the end, the arc the sūrah has traced, because its unity is one of the things the commentators remark upon.

*Verses 1–3*: the letters, the Book, and the announcement that a true story is about to be recited.
*Verses 4–42*: the Moses narrative — oppression, the infant in the river, the crime and the flight, Midian, the fire, the mission, the confrontation, and the drowning. God's planning operates through, and against, human planning.
*Verses 43–51*: the argument that the Prophet's knowledge of these events is itself revelation, and the catalog of objections and answers.
*Verses 52–55*: those who recognized the truth when it came, and the marks by which they are known.
*Verses 56–75*: the Makkan objections answered — the limits of guidance, the fear of loss, the ruins of the arrogant, the justice of warning before punishment, the comparison between now and then, the scenes of the Resurrection, and the signs in creation.
*Verses 76–83*: Korah — the complete anatomy of wealth's danger, and the conclusion that the Hereafter belongs to those who renounce dominance and corruption.
*Verses 84–88*: the law of requital, the promise of return, the command to steadfastness, and the final creed.

And the two anchors, one at each end of the argument: *We desired to be gracious to those who were oppressed* (v. 5) and *the end belongs to the reverent* (v. 83).

**Application: Living at the End of the Sūrah**

The verse's practical content, as the tradition has received it, is concentrated in its middle clause.

*Everything perishes; a Face does not.* The believer's task is to attach what he does to what remains. The tradition's discipline is the renewal of intention before every act, and the concealment of the good deed where concealment is possible.

*Judgment belongs to God.* The clause relieves the believer of the burden of settling accounts, and restrains him from assuming the role of judge over others.

*And you are returning.* The final clause is the horizon within which everything in the sūrah is to be read — every tyrant's end, every exile's promise, every wealthy man's test, and every reader's own life.

The sūrah closes here. It has told the story of a mother who placed her child in a river and trusted a promise, of a man who fled a city and was returned to it, of a tyrant who built a tower and was drowned in a sea, and of a rich man whose treasure could not keep him out of the earth. And it has said, in one sentence, what all of it means:

> *There is no god but He. All things perish, save His Face. Judgment belongs to Him, and unto Him will you be returned.*

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ]**
